Coverages and Grothendieck Toposes
Emilio Minichiello*

Abstract
. These notes detail the basics of the theory of Grothendieck toposes from the viewpoint of coverages. Typically one defines a site as a (small) category equipped with a Grothendieck topology. However, it is often desirable to generate a Grothendieck topology from a smaller structure, such as a Grothendieck pretopology, but these require some pullbacks to exist in your underlying category. There is an even more light-weight structure one can generate a Grothendieck topology from called a coverage. Coverages don’t require any limits or colimits to exist in the underlying category. We prove in detail several results about coverages, such as closing coverages under refinement and composition, to be what we call a saturated coverage, which doesn’t change its category of sheaves. We show that Grothendieck topologies are in bijection with saturated coverages. We give an explicit description of the saturated coverage and the Grothendieck topology generated from a coverage. We furthermore give a readable account of some of the most important parts of Grothendieck topos theory, with an emphasis placed on coverages. These include constructing sheafification using the plus construction and also in “one go,” the equivalence between left exact localizations of presheaf toposes and saturated coverages, morphisms of sites using the fully general notion of covering flatness, points of a Grothendieck topos and Giraud’s theorem. We show that Giraud’s theorem is equivalent to Rezk’s notion of weak descent. Also included is a section devoted to many examples of sites and Grothendieck toposes appearing in the literature, and appendices covering set theory and category theory background, localization and locally presentable categories.



1.
Introduction

The theory of sheaves has become a widely-used and powerful tool in modern mathematics. Sheaves were first developed by Jean Leray in a prison camp during World War II, see the wonderful article
[
Mil00
]
for more history of the origins of sheaf theory. Their original area of application was algebraic topology, but before long the influence of sheaf theory spread to other areas, especially algebraic geometry. Grothendieck took the theory of sheaves to new heights, inventing the concept of what is now known as a Grothendieck topology. This notion was then central to the definition of étale cohomology in algebraic geometry, which was itself key to proving the Weil conjectures.

Today, sheaf theory has expanded horizontally, in the sense that it is being applied to ever more areas of mathematics and science
[
Ros22
,
KPR20
,
HG20
,
Cur14
]
, and vertically, in the sense of new developments in the theory of sheaves of spaces, i.e. higher topos theory
[
Lur09
]
.

There are already the wonderful books
[
Joh02
,
MM12
,
Gol14
]
amongst many other resources for the subject of topos theory. So I must justify why I wrote these notes. As a PhD student, I studied diffeological spaces, which are a certain generalization of smooth manifolds. It turns out that the category of diffeological spaces is equivalent to the category of concrete sheaves on the site of open subsets of cartesian spaces (see Example
8.50
).

I wanted to study these diffeological spaces using the modern machinery of higher topos theory as in
[
Sch13
]
. However, there was a certain technical difficulty that presented itself. There are many different sites that one can use that give equivalent categories of diffeological spaces (see Example
8.50
), but only one site (Example
8.29
) had a particular property
1
1

1

Briefly, many more simplicial presheaves tend to be
\infty
-stacks on
(\mathbf{Cart},j_{\text{good}})
than on
(\mathbf{Man},j_{\text{emb}})
, see
[
FSS11
, Remark 3.2.2]
, and also the former site has projective cofibrant Čech nerves for its covering families. This is a very important technical convenience for working with the projective model structure on simplicial presheaves. Furthermore the remarkable results
[
SS22
, Lemma 3.3.29, Proposition 3.3.30]
,
[
Pav22
, Proposition 4.13]
hold for the former site.
that was necessary for the homotopical theory of higher sheaves. Most of the time in differential geometry, one studies a Grothendieck pretopology (Definition
8.11
), from which it is easy to generate the resulting Grothendieck topology. But the site I mentioned above, that I needed for homotopical reasons, was not a Grothendieck pretopology. Instead it was merely a coverage. This made me realize that I really needed to better understand the theory of coverages. However, nearly all of the theory of coverages I could find in the literature was covered in three references
[
Joh02
,
Shu12
,
Low16
]
.

In order to help myself understand this theory enough to write my PhD thesis, (which I was able to achieve, it consisted of the two papers
[
Min24
,
Min24a
]
) I decided to write these notes. It helped me immensely, and I hope it can help you as well. My justification for writing these notes consists of three points:

(1)

most of the references on topos theory are very long, and as a graduate student I found it quite difficult to navigate these massive texts to find the most important points. These notes are meant to be short, and to be readable by motivated graduate students. Hence I have included as many proofs as I could without extending the text massively, and included as many details in the proofs as I could within reason.

(2)

most of the references on topos theory focus extensively on Grothendieck topologies. This is reasonable, as any coverage can be completed to a unique Grothendieck topology. However, in the context of differential geometry, I really needed to be able to manipulate the covering families directly. Doing this with Grothendieck topologies can be a bit cumbersome. I felt that a detailed discussion with careful proofs of closing coverages under desirable properties was missing from the literature.

(3)

many of the most impressive applications of topos theory are to logic and algebraic geometry. I felt it was time to make a text that avoided these well-known examples and focus more on lesser-known areas of application, such as in differential geometry. I included an extensive section of examples (Section
8
) that I hope will be useful and interesting.

For these notes I decided to focus particularly on material and subjects that I had the hardest time pinning down. You will also see this reflected in the appendix material, which consists of three subjects that I found vitally important but also somewhat more difficult to get a handle on. My hope is that these notes will serve as a useful base of material for a young researcher or graduate student to refer back to, and pave the way for learning about homotopical sheaf theory.

Now let us briefly detail the different sections of these notes. In Section
2
we introduce the main objects of study: coverages, presheaves and sheaves. In Section
4
we study how to close arbitrary coverages under two closure operations: refinement closure and composition closure. We prove that closing a coverage under these operations does not change its sheaves. In Section
6
we discuss saturated coverages, which are precisely those coverages that are both refinement closed and composition closed. We show that saturated coverages are in bijection with Grothendieck topologies. In Section
7
we prove the Little Giraud Theorem (Theorem
7.18
), that left exact localizations of presheaf toposes are equivalent to Grothendieck toposes. We also give a detailed construction of sheafification using the plus construction applied twice, and then also explain another construction which “sheafifies in one go.” In Section
8
we provide a long list of examples of sites, Grothendieck toposes and categories of concrete sheaves that show up in the literature. We also discuss several ways to deal with the underlying site being a large category. In Section
9
, we discuss morphisms between sites, using the most general possible notion of covering flatness and prove the Comparison Lemma (Theorem
9.52
). In Section
10
we apply the theory of morphisms of sites to discuss points of sites and corresponding points of Grothendieck toposes. In Section
11
we prove Giraud’s Theorem (Theorem
11.42
), which characterizes the conditions under which a category is a Grothendieck topos, and we show that this is equivalent to the notion of weak descent. We give a little taste of how this generalizes to the case of
\infty
-toposes. Finally we include three appendices: Appendix
A
covers some background on set theory, category theory and presheaf toposes, Appendix
B
covers the theory of localizations of categories, and Appendix
C
covers the theory of locally presentable categories.

1.1.
What’s been left out

The following topics have been left out of these notes.

(1)

There is little to no mention of algebraic geometry in these notes. The reasoning for this is due to my own ignorance on the subject, and because the wonderful resource the Stacks Project
[
Jon25
]
already exists. We highly recommend the reader go there to learn more.

(2)

We do not cover Čech cohomology or sheaf cohomology of sites, you can find that in
[
Jon25
,
Tag 01FQ
]
.

(3)

We do not cover categorical logic or the use of Lawvere-Tierney topologies in these notes. Both of these are important parts of topos theory, and are covered very well in the following references
[
MM12
,
Gol14
,
Joh02
,
Lur18a
,
AB09
]
.

1.2.
What the reader should know

While we collect some helpful prerequisite information in the appendices, in order to keep these notes to a reasonable size, we had to omit certain topics from the appendices and make them assumed knowledge of the reader. Here we list those topics and references for them.

•

Basic category theory: (co)limits, adjunctions, natural transformations, etc.
[
Rie17
]
,

•

Coend Calculus
[
Lor21
]
,

•

Tensoring and Powering
[
Rie14
, Section 3.7]
,

•

the pasting lemma for pullbacks and pushouts
[
Bau12
]
,

•

Cartesian Closure and Local Cartesian Closure
[
Hua22
]
.

1.3.
Conventions and Notation

•

We use a bold font
\mathbf{C}
to denote named categories like
\mathbf{Set}
. We use the font
\mathcal{C}
for unnamed categories.

•

We take as our set-theoretic foundation a pair
\mathbb{U}\in\mathbb{V}
of Grothendieck universes, see Section
A.1
. We call sets that are elements of
\mathbb{U}
small and elements of
\mathbb{V}
large.

•

All categories
\mathcal{C}
are understood to be locally small (have small hom-sets) and all sets are understood to be small sets.

•

We assume the law of excluded middle and the axiom of choice hold.

•

The reader should be warned that we will not always cite the first reference where a result or definition was given. Rather we will sometimes refer to what we consider to be a readable reference which may then itself give the correct attribution of a result or definition.

1.4.
Corrections

This is a living document that will be updated periodically. As with any large non-peer reviewed document, it will certainly contain mistakes and typos. Please reach out by email if you find any such mistakes. I will make sure to mention you here.

For help finding errors, thanks to Quique Ruiz and Cheyne Glass.

2.
Sites and Sheaves

In this section, we introduce the main objects of study of these notes, sites and sheaves.

2.1.
Coverages

The theory of sites is usually presented using Grothendieck topologies or pretopologies
[
MM12
]
. These are particular kinds of collections of covering morphisms. Here we follow
[
Joh02
]
in using coverages. Coverages are more general collections of covering morphisms than Grothendieck (pre)topologies. Their definition is simpler, and apply to more examples, but proving theorems using them is typically more difficult.

Definition 2.1
.

Let
\mathcal{C}
be a category, and
U\in\mathcal{C}
. A
family of morphisms
(or
family
for short) over
U
is a set of morphisms
r=\{r_{i}:U_{i}\to U\}_{i\in I}
in
\mathcal{C}
with codomain
U
. A
refinement
of a family of morphisms
t=\{t_{j}:V_{j}\to U\}_{j\in J}
over
U
consists of a family of morphisms
r=\{r_{i}:U_{i}\to U\}_{i\in I}
, a function
\alpha:I\to J
, which we call the
index map
of the refinement, and for each
i\in I
a map
f_{i}:U_{i}\to V_{\alpha(i)}
, which we call the
i
th
component
of
f
, making the following diagram commute:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{U_{i}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{V_{\alpha(i)}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces U}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f_{i}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces r_{i}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces t_{\alpha(i)}}

If
r
is a refinement of
t
, with maps
f_{i}:U_{i}\to V_{\alpha(i)}
, then we write
f:r\to t
. Let
\text{Fam}(U)
denote the category whose objects are families over
U
and whose morphisms are refinements. We say that
r
refines
t
, and write
r\leq t
if there exists a refinement
f:r\to t
.

Notation 2.2
.

Given any object
U\in\mathcal{C}
, there always exist several families over
U
. Let
\varnothing_{U}
denote the
empty family
over
U
, and let
y_{U}
denote the
maximal family
over
U
, which consists of all those morphisms with codomain
U
. If
f:V\to U
is a morphism, then let
(f)
denote the corresponding singleton family
(f)=\{f:V\to U\}
.

Definition 2.3
.

If
g:V\to U
is map in
\mathcal{C}
and
t=\{t_{j}:V_{j}\to V\}_{j\in J}
is a family on
V
, then let

g_{*}(t)=\{V_{j}\xrightarrow{t_{j}}V\xrightarrow{g}U\}_{j\in J}

denote the
pushforward
of
t
by
g
. This makes
Fam
into a (strict) functor
\text{Fam}_{*}:\mathcal{C}\to\mathbf{Cat}
by setting
\text{Fam}_{*}(g)=g_{*}
and that sends a refinement
f:t\to s
to the refinement
g_{*}(f):g_{*}(t)\to g_{*}(s)
whose components agree with
f
.
We can also pullback families in the following way. Given
g
as above and
r=\{r_{i}:U_{i}\to U\}
a family over
U
, let

g^{*}(r)=\{f:W\to V\,\mid\,gf\text{ factors through a map $r_{i}:U_{i}\to U$ }\}.

This similarly makes
Fam
into a (strict) functor
\text{Fam}^{*}:\mathcal{C}^{\text{op}}\to\mathbf{Cat}
by setting
\text{Fam}^{*}(g)=g^{*}
2
2

2

In fact, if
r
is a family over
U
, then
g^{*}(r)
is a sieve (Definition
3.1
) over
V
. If
h:r\to t
is a refinement, then
g^{*}(r)\subseteq g^{*}(t)
.
.

Remark 2.4
.

In what follows we will often drop the indexing set from a family
r=\{r_{i}:U_{i}\to U\}_{i\in I}
, referencing it by
r
or
\{r_{i}:U_{i}\to U\}
when we wish to make the morphism names and domains known.

Definition 2.5
.

A
collection of families

j
on a small category
\mathcal{C}
consists of a set
j(U)
for each
U\in\mathcal{C}
, whose elements
r\in j(U)
are families of morphisms over
U
. We write
r\in j
to mean that there exists some
U\in\mathcal{C}
such that
r\in j(U)
.

Definition 2.6
.

We say that a collection of families
j
on a small category
\mathcal{C}
is a
coverage
if

•

for every
U\in\mathcal{C}
,
(1_{U})\in j(U)
, and
3
3

3

There are some versions of the definition of coverage in the literature that do not require this axiom, such as
[
Joh02
, Definition A.2.1.9]
, and there are some that do, such as
[
Low16
, Definition A.2.8]
. We include it for several technical reasons that will become more apparent as we go along.

•

for every
U\in\mathcal{C}
,
r\in j(U)
and map
g:V\to U
in
\mathcal{C}
, there exists a family
t\in j(V)
such that
g_{*}(t)\leq r
.

If
j
is a coverage on
\mathcal{C}
, then we call families
r\in j(U)

covering families
over
U
. If a map
r_{i}:U_{i}\to U
belongs to a covering family
r\in j(U)
, then we say that
r_{i}
is a
covering map
. If
\mathcal{C}
is a small category, and
j
is a coverage on
\mathcal{C}
, then we call the pair
(\mathcal{C},j)
a
site
. We also think of
j(U)
as a poset, where
r\leq r^{\prime}
in
j(U)
if both
r
and
r^{\prime}
are covering families over
U
and there is a refinement
f:r\to r^{\prime}
.

Remark 2.7
.

Unravelling Definition
2.6
means that for every
U\in\mathcal{C}
,
r\in j(U)
, and
g:V\to U
, there exists a
t\in j(V)
such that for every
t_{j}:V_{j}\to V
in
t
there exists a map
r_{i}:U_{i}\to U
in
r
and a map
s_{j}:V_{j}\to U_{i}
in
\mathcal{C}
making the following diagram commute:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{V_{j}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{U_{i}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces V}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces U}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces t_{j}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces s_{j}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces r_{i}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g}

Example 2.8
.

Let
j_{\text{epi}}
denote the collection of families on
\mathbf{FinSet}
4
4

4

here we let
\mathbf{FinSet}
denote a small skeleton of the category of finite sets. Explicitly, let
\mathbf{FinSet}
denote the category whose objects are the sets
[n]=\{1,\dots,n\}
with
[0]=\varnothing
and whose morphisms are functions.
where
r=\{r_{i}:S_{i}\to S\}_{i\in I}
belongs to
j_{\text{epi}}(S)
if and only if
r
is
jointly epimorphic
, i.e. that
\sumop\displaylimits_{i\in I}r_{i}:\sumop\displaylimits_{i\in I}S_{i}\to S
is an epimorphism. Given a map
g:T\to S
of sets, pulling back each
r_{i}
gives a function

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{g^{*}(S_{i})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{S_{i}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces T}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces S}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g^{*}(r_{i})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lrcorner}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces r_{i}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g}

and
\sumop\displaylimits_{i\in I}g^{*}(r_{i})
is an epimorphism. Indeed, if
t\in T
, then there exists an
i\in I
and an
s_{i}\in S_{i}
such that
r_{i}(s_{i})=g(t)
. Then
\sumop\displaylimits_{i\in I}g^{*}(r_{i})(t,s_{i})=t
. Thus
j_{\text{epi}}
forms a coverage on
\mathbf{FinSet}
.

Example 2.9
.

Let
X
be a topological space and let
\mathcal{O}(X)
denote the poset of open subsets of
X
, where namely
U\leq V
if and only if
U
and
V
are open subsets of
X
and
U\subseteq V
. Let
j_{X}
denote the collection of families on
\mathcal{O}(X)
such that
j_{X}(U)
is the set of all open covers of an open subset
U
, namely
\{U_{i}\subseteq U\}_{i\in I}\in j_{X}(U)
if
\bigcupop\displaylimits_{i}U_{i}=U
. This collection of families is a coverage. Indeed, suppose that
\{U_{i}\subseteq U\}
is an open cover of
U
and
V\leq U
is an open subset of
U
. Then
\{V\cap U_{i}\subseteq V\}
is an open cover of
V
, and
V\cap U_{i}\leq U_{i}
for every
i\in I
. We call
j_{X}
the
open cover coverage
of
X
, and call
(\mathcal{O}(X),j_{X})
the
site associated with
X
.

Example 2.10
.

Now suppose that
X
is a topological space with topology
\tau
, and let
\mathscr{B}
be a basis for
\tau
in the classical sense, namely
\mathscr{B}\subseteq\tau
is a subcollection of open sets such that the elements of
\mathscr{B}
cover
X
and for every open set
U
and elements
x\in U
, there exists a basis element
B_{x}\in\mathscr{B}
such that
x\in B_{x}
and
B_{x}\subseteq U
. Let us show that
\mathscr{B}
defines a coverage
j_{\mathscr{B}}
on
\mathcal{O}(X)
. If
U\in\mathcal{O}(X)
, then let
j_{\mathscr{B}}(U)
denote those open covers of
U
consisting only of elements in
\mathscr{B}
(and also containing the identity family
(1_{U})
). So if
V\subseteq U
is an open subset, and
\{B_{i}\subseteq U\}\in j_{\mathscr{B}}(U)
, then every point
y\in V
belongs to some
B_{i}
. So for every
y\in V
there exists a basis element
B^{\prime}_{y}\in\mathscr{B}
such that
y\in B^{\prime}_{y}
and
B^{\prime}_{y}\subseteq V\cap B_{i}
. Then
\{B^{\prime}_{y}\}_{y\in V}
forms an open cover of
V
. Thus
j_{\mathscr{B}}
is a coverage on
\mathcal{O}(X)
. In fact, if we let
\mathcal{O}(X,\mathscr{B})
denote the full subcategory of
\mathcal{O}(X)
only consisting of basis elements, then
j_{\mathscr{B}}
also defines a coverage on this category by the same argument.

Example 2.11
.

Given a small category
\mathcal{C}
, there are several canonical coverages one can consider, recall Notation
2.2
:

(1)

let
j_{\text{triv}}
denote the
trivial coverage
, where for every
U\in\mathcal{C}
,
j_{\text{triv}}(U)=\{(1_{U})\}
,

(2)

let
j_{\text{iso}}
denote the
isomorphism coverage
, where for every
U\in\mathcal{C}
,
j_{\text{iso}}(U)
consists of those singleton families
(f)
over
U
where
f
is an isomorphism,

(3)

let
j_{\text{max}}
denote the
maximal coverage
, where for every
U\in\mathcal{C}
,
j_{\text{max}}(U)=\{(1_{U}),y_{U}\}
.

Example 2.12
.

Suppose that
(\mathcal{C},j)
is a site with
U\in\mathcal{C}
. Let
\mathcal{C}_{/U}
denote the slice category, and
\pi_{/U}:\mathcal{C}_{/U}\to\mathcal{C}
denote the projection functor. Let
j_{/U}
denote the collection of families on
\mathcal{C}_{/U}
where if
g:V\to U
is a morphism in
\mathcal{C}
, then
\{f_{i}:r_{i}\to g\}\in j_{/U}(g)
if and only if
\{\pi_{/U}(f_{i})\}\in j(V)
. It is easy to see that
j_{/U}
forms a coverage on
\mathcal{C}_{/U}
. We call
(\mathcal{C}_{/U},j_{/U})
the
slice site
at
U
.

Example 2.13
.

Given a site
(\mathcal{C},j)
and a subcategory
\mathcal{D}\hookrightarrow\mathcal{C}
, we say that a coverage
j^{\prime}
on
\mathcal{D}
is
induced
if every covering family
r^{\prime}
in
j^{\prime}
is of the form
r^{\prime}=r\cap\text{Mor}\,(\mathcal{D})
. If
j^{\prime}
is induced then we write
j^{\prime}=j|_{\mathcal{D}}
, and call it the induced coverage.

2.2.
(Pre)Sheaves

Definition 2.14
.

A
presheaf
on a category
\mathcal{C}
is a functor
X:\mathcal{C}^{\text{op}}\to\mathbf{Set}
. An element
x\in X(U)
for an object
U\in\mathcal{C}
is called a
section
over
U
. If
f:U\to V
is a map in
\mathcal{C}
, and
x\in X(V)
, then we sometimes denote
X(f)(x)
by
x|_{U}
. Given a family
r=\{r_{i}:U_{i}\to U\}_{i\in I}
over
U
, an
intersection square
on
r
is a commutative diagram of the form

(1)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces U_{ij}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{U_{j}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{U_{i}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces U}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces r_{i}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces r_{j}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces u_{i}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces u_{j}}

where
r_{i},r_{j}\in r
and
u_{i}
,
u_{j}
, and
U_{ij}
are arbitrary. An
X
-matching family
over
r
is a collection of sections
\{x_{i}\in X(U_{i})\}_{i\in I}
such that given any intersection square (
1
),
X(u_{i})(x_{i})=X(u_{j})(x_{j})
for all
i,j\in I
. If the presheaf is clear from context we may say that
\{x_{i}\}
is a matching family for
r
.

If
X
is a presheaf on
\mathcal{C}
, and
r
is a family of morphisms on
U
, then let
\text{Match}(r,X)
denote the set of
X
-matching families over
r
. Given a
X
-matching family
\{x_{i}\}
over
r
, an
amalgamation
for
\{x_{i}\}
is a section
x\in X(U)
such that
X(r_{i})(x)=x_{i}
for all
i
.

If
U\in\mathcal{C}
, then let
y(U)
denote the presheaf on
\mathcal{C}
where for
V\in\mathcal{C}
,
y(U)(V)=\mathcal{C}(V,U)
, and if
f:V\to V^{\prime}
is a morphism in
\mathcal{C}
, then
y(U)(f):y(U)(V^{\prime})\to y(U)(V)
is the precomposition map. We call
y(U)
a
representable presheaf
, and more specifically the representable on
U
.

Lemma 2.15
.

Given a presheaf
X
on a category
\mathcal{C}
, suppose that
r=\{r_{i}:U_{i}\to U\}_{i\in I}
and
t=\{t_{j}:V_{j}\to U\}_{j\in J}
are families over
U
and
f:r\to t
is a refinement, with index map
\alpha:I\to J
. If
\{x_{j}\}_{j\in J}
is an
X
-matching family over
t
, then
\{X(f_{i})(x_{\alpha(i)})\}_{i\in I}
is an
X
-matching family over
r
.

Proof.

Suppose we have morphisms
u_{i}:U_{ij}\to U_{i}
and
u_{j}:U_{ij}\to U_{j}
such that
r_{i}u_{i}=r_{j}u_{j}
. Then we have a commuting diagram:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{U_{i}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{U_{ij}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{U_{j}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{V_{\alpha(i)}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces U}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{V_{\alpha(j)}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces u_{i}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces u_{j}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f_{i}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f_{j}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces r_{i}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces r_{j}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces t_{\alpha(i)}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces t_{\alpha(j)}}

and therefore

X(u_{i})X(f_{i})(x_{\alpha(i)})=X(f_{i}u_{i})(x_{\alpha(i)})=X(f_{j}u_{j})(x_{\alpha(j)})=X(u_{j})X(f_{j})(x_{\alpha(j)}),

since
\{x_{j}\}
is a matching family for
t
. Thus
\{X(f_{i})(x_{\alpha(i)})\}
is a matching family for
r
.
∎

Remark 2.16
.

If
f:r\to t
is a refinement of families of morphisms and
\{x_{j}\}
is a matching family for a presheaf
X
over
t
, let
f^{*}\{x_{j}\}
denote the corresponding matching family
\{X(f_{i})(x_{\alpha(i)})\}
. Given
U\in\mathcal{C}
and a presheaf
X
on
\mathcal{C}
, this makes
\text{Match}(-,X)
into a functor
\text{Match}(-,X):\text{Fam}(U)^{\text{op}}\to\mathbf{Set}
by setting
\text{Match}(f)(\{x_{j}\})=f^{*}\{x_{j}\}
. We call
f^{*}\{x_{j}\}
the
pullback matching family
of
\{x_{j}\}
by
f
.

Example 2.17
.

Let us consider the example of the site
(\mathcal{O}(\mathbb{R}^{1}),j_{\mathbb{R}^{1}})
of the real line. The family
\mathcal{U}_{1}=\{(n,n+2)\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{1}\}_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}
forms an open cover of
\mathbb{R}^{1}
. The family
\mathcal{U}_{2}=\{(m,m+3)\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{1}\}_{m\in\mathbb{Z}}
forms another open cover of
\mathbb{R}^{1}
, and there is a refinement
f:\mathcal{U}_{1}\to\mathcal{U}_{2}
with index map
\alpha:\mathbb{N}\to\mathbb{N}
given by the identity map. If
X=y(\mathbb{R})
is the representable presheaf on
\mathbb{R}
, and
\{x_{m}\}
is an
X
-matching family of continuous functions
x_{m}:(m,n+3)\to\mathbb{R}
for
\mathcal{U}_{2}
, then we get the pullback matching family
f^{*}\{x_{m}\}=\{X(f_{n})(x_{\alpha(n)})\}_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}=\{x_{n}|_{(n,n+2)}\}_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}
on
\mathcal{U}_{1}
.

Definition 2.18
.

Given a presheaf
X
on a category
\mathcal{C}
and a family of morphisms
r
on an object
U
in
\mathcal{C}
, there is a canonical map

(2)

\text{res}_{r,X}:X(U)\to\text{Match}(r,X)

which is defined for an element
x\in X(U)
to be the matching family
\text{res}_{r,X}(x)=\{X(r_{i})(x)\}
of
X
over
r
. We say that
X
is
separated
on
r
if
\text{res}_{r,X}
is injective. We say that
X
is a
sheaf
on
r
if
\text{res}_{r,X}
is bijective.

Given a collection of families
j
on
\mathcal{C}
, we say that
X
is (separated) a
j
-sheaf on an object
U\in\mathcal{C}
if
X
is (separated) a sheaf on
r
for every
r\in j(U)
.

Given a site
(\mathcal{C},j)
, we call
X
a
j
-
sheaf
if it is a
j
-sheaf on every object
U
of
\mathcal{C}
. Let
\mathbf{Sh}(\mathcal{C},j)
denote the full subcategory of
\mathbf{Pre}(\mathcal{C})
whose objects are
j
-sheaves.

Definition 2.19
.

We say that a category
\mathcal{D}
is a
Grothendieck topos
or just
topos
for short, if there exists a site
(\mathcal{C},j)
and an equivalence
\mathcal{D}\simeq\mathbf{Sh}(\mathcal{C},j)
.

Lemma 2.20
.

Given a category
\mathcal{C}
, a presheaf
X
on
\mathcal{C}
, a refinement
f:r\to t
of families over an object
U\in\mathcal{C}
, and an
X
-matching family
\{x_{j}\}
over
t
, if
x
is an amalgamation for
\{x_{j}\}
, then
x
is an amalgamation for
f^{*}\{x_{j}\}
.

Proof.

Since
f:r\to t
is a refinement,
X(r_{i})(x)=X(t_{\alpha(i)}f_{i})(x)=X(f_{i})X(t_{\alpha}(i))(x)
. But
\{x_{j}\}
is an
X
-matching family over
t
, so
X(t_{\alpha(i)})(x)=x_{\alpha(i)}
for all
i
. Thus
X(r_{i})(x)=X(f_{i})(x_{\alpha(i)})
, so
x
is an amalgamation for
f^{*}\{x_{j}\}
.
∎

Lemma 2.21
.

Given a category
\mathcal{C}
and a presheaf
X
on
\mathcal{C}
, then
X
is a sheaf on the empty family
\varnothing
over an object
U\in\mathcal{C}
if and only if
X(U)\cong*
.

Proof.

Given a family
r
of morphisms over an object
U\in\mathcal{C}
,
X
is a sheaf on
r
if
X(U)\to\text{Match}(r,X)
is an isomorphism. When
r=\varnothing
, there exists only a single matching family, namely the empty one.
∎

Definition 2.22
.

We say that an object
U
in a site
(\mathcal{C},j)
is
singular
if the empty family
of morphisms
\varnothing
is a
j
-covering family on
U
. We say that a site
(\mathcal{C},j)
is
nonsingular
if it has no singular objects, and
singular
otherwise.

Example 2.23
.

Given a topological space
X
, the site
(\mathcal{O}(X),j_{X})
from Example
2.9
is singular, because the initial object
\varnothing
in
\mathcal{O}(X)
has exactly two covering families, the set
\{1_{\varnothing}:\varnothing\to\varnothing\}
and the empty family
\varnothing
. Thus in order for a presheaf
A
on
X
to be a sheaf,
A(\varnothing)\cong*
.

Lemma 2.24
.

Given an object
U
in a category
\mathcal{C}
and a nonempty family of morphisms
r
over
U
consisting of isomorphisms, every presheaf
X
on
\mathcal{C}
is a sheaf on
r
.

Proof.

Let
r=\{r_{i}:U_{i}\to U\}_{i\in I}
be a family of isomorphisms over
U
,
X
a presheaf on
\mathcal{C}
and suppose that
\{x_{i}\}
is an
X
-matching family over
r
. We want to show that
\{x_{i}\}
has a unique amalgamation.

First we note that
X(r_{i}^{-1})(x_{i})=X(r_{j}^{-1})(x_{j})
for all
i,j\in I
, because
\{x_{i}\}
is an
X
-matching family over
r
and the following commutative diagram is an intersection square

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces U}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{U_{j}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{U_{i}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces U}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces r_{j}^{-1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces r_{i}^{-1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces r_{j}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces r_{i}}

Letting
x=X(r_{i}^{-1})(x_{i})
, we see that
x
is an amalgamation of
\{x_{i}\}
.

Now suppose that
y\in X(U)
is also an amalgamation of
\{x_{i}\}
. Then
X(r_{i})(y)=x_{i}
for all
i\in I
, so
X(r_{i}^{-1})X(r_{i})(y)=X(r_{i}^{-1})(x_{i})=x
. Therefore
y=x
. Thus
x
is a unique amalgamation, and so
X
is a sheaf on
r
.
∎

Example 2.25
.

If
\mathcal{C}
is a category, then we can consider the empty, identity and isomorphism coverages of Example
2.11
on
\mathcal{C}
. Lemma
2.24
proves that every presheaf on
(\mathcal{C},j_{\text{iso}})
and
(\mathcal{C},j_{\text{id}})
is a sheaf. It is similarly easy to see that every presheaf on
(\mathcal{C},j_{\text{max}})
is a sheaf. Therefore we have

(3)

\mathbf{Pre}(\mathcal{C})=\mathbf{Sh}(\mathcal{C},j_{\text{iso}})=\mathbf{Sh}(\mathcal{C},j_{\text{id}})=\mathbf{Sh}(\mathcal{C},j_{\text{max}}).

Notation 2.26
.

Since every category of presheaves
\mathbf{Pre}(\mathcal{C})
is in particular a category of sheaves
\mathbf{Sh}(\mathcal{C},j_{\text{id}})
, every presheaf category is a Grothendieck topos. We will refer to these kinds of toposes as
presheaf toposes
, and if
(\mathcal{C},j)
is a site, then we will sometimes call
\mathbf{Sh}(\mathcal{C},j)
its
sheaf topos
.

Lemma 2.27
.

Suppose that
j
is a coverage on a category
\mathcal{C}
such that pullbacks along covering maps exist. If
r=\{r_{i}:U_{i}\to U\}_{i\in I}
is a covering family of
U
, then a collection
\{s_{i}\in X(U_{i})\}
of sections of a presheaf
X
on
\mathcal{C}
is a matching family for
r
if and only if for every pullback square of the form

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{U_{i}\times_{U}U_{j}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{U_{j}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{U_{i}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces U}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces r_{i}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces r_{j}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{i}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{j}}

it follows that
X(\pi_{i})(s_{i})=X(\pi_{j})(s_{j})
.

Proof.

(\Rightarrow)
This is clear.

(\Leftarrow)
Suppose we have maps
g:V\to U_{i}
and
h:V\to U_{j}
, then we have the following commutative diagram:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces V}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{U_{i}\times_{U}U_{j}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{U_{j}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{U_{i}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces U}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{i}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{j}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces r_{i}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces r_{j}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces h}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces k}

where
k
is the unique map to the pullback. The following holds:

X(g)(s_{i})=X(\pi_{i}k)(s_{i})=X(k)X(\pi_{i})(s_{i})=X(k)X(\pi_{j})(s_{j})=X(\pi_{j}k)(s_{j})=X(h)(s_{j}).

∎

Example 2.28
.

If
X
is a topological space, then
\mathcal{O}(X)
has pullbacks where if
U_{i}\subseteq U
and
U_{j}\subseteq U
are morphisms, then their pullback is given by their intersection
U_{i}\cap U_{j}
. Hence by Lemma
2.27
, to check that a family of sections is a matching family on
\mathcal{O}(X)
it is enough to check that they match on double intersections.

It is useful to compare different coverages on the same underlying category.

Definition 2.29
.

Given a small category
\mathcal{C}
and coverages
j,j^{\prime}
on
\mathcal{C}
, we write

•

j\subseteq j^{\prime}
, if for every
U\in\mathcal{C}
it follows that
j(U)\subseteq j^{\prime}(U)
,

•

j\leq j^{\prime}
, if for every
r^{\prime}\in j^{\prime}
there exists a
r\in j
and a refinement
r\leq r^{\prime}
,

We say that
j^{\prime}

contains

j
if
j\subseteq j^{\prime}
, and that
j

refines

j^{\prime}
if
j\leq j^{\prime}
. We say two coverages
j,j^{\prime}
on the same underlying small category
\mathcal{C}
are
equivalent
if
j\leq j^{\prime}
and
j^{\prime}\leq j
.

Lemma 2.30
.

Given coverages
j,j^{\prime}
on a small category
\mathcal{C}
,

(1)

if
j\leq j^{\prime}
, then
\mathbf{Sh}(\mathcal{C},j)\subseteq\mathbf{Sh}(\mathcal{C},j^{\prime})
.

(2)

if
j\subseteq j^{\prime}
, then
\mathbf{Sh}(\mathcal{C},j^{\prime})\subseteq\mathbf{Sh}(\mathcal{C},j)
,

Proof.

(1) follows from Lemma
4.17
, and (2) follows because if
j\subseteq j^{\prime}
, then
j^{\prime}\leq j
by just taking the identity refinement for every covering family.
∎

Example 2.31
.

Suppose that
(X,\tau)
is a topological space with topology
\tau
and
\mathscr{B}
is a basis for
\tau
. We can consider the two coverages
j_{X}
(Example
2.9
) and
j_{\mathscr{B}}
(Example
2.10
) on
\mathcal{O}(X)
. Clearly
j_{\mathscr{B}}\subseteq j_{X}
, and thus
\mathbf{Sh}(\mathcal{O}(X),j_{X})\subseteq\mathbf{Sh}(\mathcal{O}(X),j_{\mathscr{B}})
. Now let us show that
j_{\mathscr{B}}\leq j_{X}
. Suppose that
U
is an open subset of
X
and
\mathcal{U}=\{U_{i}\subseteq U\}
is an open cover. Then every point
x\in U
belongs to some
U_{i}
, and therefore there exists a basis element
B_{x}\in\mathscr{B}
such that
x\in B_{x}
and
B_{x}\subseteq U_{i}
. Thus
B=\{B_{x}\}_{x\in U}
forms an open cover of
U
and
B\leq\mathcal{U}
. Thus
j_{X}
and
j_{\mathscr{B}}
are equivalent, and hence
\mathbf{Sh}(\mathcal{O}(X),j_{X})=\mathbf{Sh}(\mathcal{O}(X),j_{\mathscr{B}})
.

3.
Sieves

Definition 3.1
.

Given a category
\mathcal{C}
with
U\in\mathcal{C}
, a
sieve

R
over
U
is a family of morphisms over
U
that is closed under precomposition, namely if
r_{i}:U_{i}\to U\in R
, then for any morphism
g:V\to U_{i}
, the composite
r_{i}g:V\to U
is also in
R
. Let
\text{Sieve}(U)
denote the full subcategory of
\text{Fam}(U)
on the sieves over
U
. Note that if
R
and
T
are sieves, then
R\leq T
if and only if
R\subseteq T
. Let
\text{sieve}(U)
denote the poset truncation of
\text{Sieve}(U)
.

Lemma 3.2
.

Given a category
\mathcal{C}
with
U\in\mathcal{C}
, there is an isomorphism of posets

(4)

\text{sieve}(U)\cong\text{Sub}(y(U)),

where
\text{Sub}(y(U))
denotes the poset of subobjects
R\hookrightarrow y(U)
in
\mathbf{Pre}(\mathcal{C})
.

Proof.

If
R\hookrightarrow y(U)
is a subobject, then consider
\phi(R)=\bigcupop\displaylimits_{V\in\mathscr{C}}R(V)
. We want to show that this is a sieve on
U
, namely that it is closed under precomposition. So suppose that
f:V\to U\in R(V)
and
g:W\to V
is a map in
\mathcal{C}
. Since
R
is a subfunctor, that means that the following diagram commutes:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{R(V)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{R(W)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{C}(V,U)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{C}(W,U)}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g^{*}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces R(g)}

Thus
\phi(R)
is a closed under precomposition. Furthermore if
R\hookrightarrow T\hookrightarrow y(U)
, then
\phi(R)\subseteq\phi(T)
. So
\phi
defines a map
\phi:\text{Sub}(y(U))\to\text{sieve}(U)
of posets.

Conversely, if
R=\{f:V\to U\}
is a sieve on
U
, then let
\psi(R)
be the presheaf defined by setting
\psi(R)(V)
to be the set of maps in
R
with domain
V
. It is easy to check that this is a subfunctor
\psi(R)\hookrightarrow y(U)
and that if
R\subseteq T
where
T
is a sieve, then
\psi(R)\hookrightarrow\psi(T)\hookrightarrow y(U)
.

It is not hard to show that these constructions are inverse to each other, and thus define an isomorphism of posets.
∎

Notation 3.3
.

Thanks to the above result, when we refer to a sieve, we leave ambiguous whether we are thinking of it as a family of morphisms closed under precomposition or as a subfunctor.

Definition 3.4
.

Given a category
\mathcal{C}
, with
U\in\mathcal{C}
, and a family of morphisms
r=\{r_{i}:U_{i}\to U\}
over
U
, let
\overline{r}
denote the set of morphisms
f:V\to U
such that
f
factors through some
r_{i}\in r
:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces V}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces U}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{U_{i}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces s_{f}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces r_{i}}

We say that
R
is the sieve
generated
by
r
if
R=\overline{r}
, and that
r
is a
generating family
for
R
. It is easy to see that
R
is then the smallest sieve such that
r\subseteq R
.

Lemma 3.5
.

Given two families of morphisms
r,r^{\prime}
on an object
U
in a small category
\mathcal{C}
, then
r\leq r^{\prime}
if and only if
\overline{r}\subseteq\overline{r^{\prime}}
.

Proof.

Let
r=\{r_{i}:U_{i}\to U\}_{i\in I}
and
r^{\prime}=\{r^{\prime}_{j}:U^{\prime}_{j}\to U\}_{j\in J}
.

(\Rightarrow)
If
r\leq r^{\prime}
, then for every
i\in I
, there is a commutative diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{U_{i}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{U_{j}^{\prime}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces U}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces r_{i}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces r^{\prime}_{j}}

for some
j\in J
. Hence
r\subseteq\overline{r}^{\prime}
. But this implies that
\overline{r}\subseteq\overline{r}^{\prime}
.

(\Leftarrow)
Suppose that
\overline{r}\subseteq\overline{r^{\prime}}
. Then
r\subseteq\overline{r^{\prime}}
, which is basically the definition of
r\leq r^{\prime}
.
∎

Proposition 3.6

(
[
nLa24a
, Lemma 3.5]
)
.

Given a category
\mathcal{C}
with
X\in\mathcal{C}
and a sieve
R
over
X
, where
r=\{r_{i}:U_{i}\to X\}
is a generating family for
R
, let
U=\sumop\displaylimits_{i\in I}y(U_{i})
. Then there is an isomorphism of presheaves:

(5)

R\cong\text{coeq}\left(U\times_{y(X)}U\rightrightarrows U\right)

where the right hand side denotes the coequalizer of the two projection maps from the pullback
U\times_{y(X)}U
.

Proof.

Given
V\in\mathcal{C}
, let
C\coloneqq\text{coeq}\left(U\times_{y(X)}U\rightrightarrows U\right)(V)
. Then we have

\displaystyle C

\displaystyle\cong\text{coeq}\left(\mathbf{Pre}(\mathcal{C})(y(V),U\times_{y(X)}U)\rightrightarrows\mathbf{Pre}(\mathcal{C})(y(V),U)\right)

\displaystyle\cong\text{coeq}\left(\mathbf{Pre}(\mathcal{C})(y(V),U)\times_{\mathbf{Pre}(\mathcal{C})(y(V),y(X))}\mathbf{Pre}(\mathcal{C})(y(V),U)\rightrightarrows\mathbf{Pre}(\mathcal{C})(y(V),U)\right)

\displaystyle\cong\text{coeq}\left(\mathbf{Pre}(\mathcal{C})(y(V),U)\times_{\mathcal{C}(V,X)}\mathbf{Pre}(\mathcal{C})(y(V),U)\rightrightarrows\mathbf{Pre}(\mathcal{C})(y(V),U)\right)

\displaystyle\cong\text{coeq}\left(\left(\sumop\displaylimits_{i}\mathcal{C}(V,U_{i})\right)\times_{\mathcal{C}(V,X)}\left(\sumop\displaylimits_{j}\mathcal{C}(V,U_{j})\right)\rightrightarrows\sumop\displaylimits_{i}\mathcal{C}(V,U_{i})\right),

where the third isomorphism holds by the Yoneda lemma and that fact that colimits are computed objectwise.

Explicitly,
C
is the set of morphisms
V\to U_{i}
for some
i\in I
, modulo the following equivalence relation: for morphisms
f:V\to U_{i}
,
f^{\prime}:V\to U_{j}
, then
f\sim f^{\prime}
if and only if the following diagram commutes:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces V}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{U_{j}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{U_{i}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces r_{i}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces r_{j}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{f^{\prime}}}

Define the map
\phi:C\to R(V)
as follows: given
f:V\to U_{i}
, post-compose with
r_{i}:U_{i}\to X
. This is clearly well defined and injective by definition. If
f^{\prime}\in R(V)
, then
f^{\prime}
can be written as a composite
f^{\prime}=r_{i}f^{\prime\prime}
where
f^{\prime\prime}:V\to U_{i}
is some morphism in
\mathcal{C}
. Thus
\phi(f^{\prime\prime})=f^{\prime}
, so
\phi
is surjective, and therefore a bijection. Naturality is easy to show.
∎

Remark 3.7
.

Since any sieve is a generating family for itself, this means that we can always write any sieve
R\hookrightarrow y(U)
as

(6)

R\cong\text{coeq}\left(\sumop\displaylimits_{g:W\to U,f:V\to U\in R}y(W)\times_{y(U)}y(V)\rightrightarrows\sumop\displaylimits_{f\in R}y(V)\right).

Another convenient characterization of a sieve as a colimit is given by the following result.

Lemma 3.8
.

Given a small category
\mathcal{C}
and a sieve
R\hookrightarrow y(U)
we have

R\cong\text{coeq}\left(\sumop\displaylimits_{W\xrightarrow{g}V\xrightarrow{f}U\in R}y(W)\rightrightarrows\sumop\displaylimits_{V\xrightarrow{f}U\in R}y(V)\right)

where the left hand sum is indexed by all pairs of maps
(g,f)
such that
fg\in R
, and the two maps are given by projection
(g,f)\mapsto g
and composition
(g,f)\mapsto fg
.

Proof.

This follows from the coYoneda Lemma
A.75
, and the conventional way of writing any colimit as a coequalizer of coproducts.
∎

Lemma 3.9

(
[
Joh02
, C2.1 Lemma 2.1.3]
)
.

Given a category
\mathcal{C}
, a presheaf
X
on
\mathcal{C}
is a sheaf on a family of morphisms
r=\{U_{i}\to U\}
if and only if it is a sheaf on the sieve
R=\overline{r}
it generates.

Proof.

(\Rightarrow)
First we note that there is a refinement
i:r\to\overline{r}=R
given componentwise by the identity map. Now if
\{x_{f}\}_{f\in R}
is a matching family for
R
, then we can restrict this family
\{x_{f}\}|_{r}\coloneqq i^{*}\{x_{f}\}
to a matching family on
r
by Lemma
2.15
. Since
X
is a sheaf on
r
, there is an amalgamation
x\in X(U)
. We want to show that this is an amalgamation for
\{x_{f}\}
. Note that since
x
is an amalgamation for
\{x_{f}\}|_{r}
, we know that
X(r_{i})(x)=x_{r_{i}}
, so the above is equivalent to asking that
X(s_{f})(x_{r_{i}})=x_{f}
whenever
f
factors as
r_{i}s_{f}
for some
s_{f}:V\to U_{i}
. Consider the commutative diagram:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces V}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{U_{i}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces V}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces U}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces r_{i}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces s_{f}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}

since both
f
and
r_{i}
belong to
R
and
\{x_{f}\}
is a matching family for
R
, this implies that
X(1_{V})(x_{f})=x_{f}=X(s_{f})(x_{r_{i}})
, which proves that
x
is an amalgamation for
\{x_{f}\}
.

(\Leftarrow)
Suppose
X
is a sheaf on
R
, and
\{x_{i}\}
is a matching family for
r
. By definition every morphism in
R
factors through some
r_{i}\in r
. Using the axiom of choice, we can construct a refinement
\pi:R\to r
, where we choose
\pi(r_{i})=r_{i}
for all
r_{i}\in r
. Then
\pi^{*}\{x_{i}\}
is a matching family for
R
such that
i^{*}\pi^{*}\{x_{i}\}=\{x_{i}\}
. Since
X
is a sheaf on
R
,
\{y_{f}\}
amalgamates uniquely to some
y
on
X(U)
. Thus
y
is also an amalgamation of
\{x_{i}\}
. Let us show that
y
is a unique amalgamation of
\{x_{i}\}
. If
z
were another amalgamation of
\{x_{i}\}
, then
X(r_{i})(z)=x_{i}
, so for every morphism
f\in R
, which can be written as a composite
f=r_{i}s_{f}
, then

X(r_{i}s_{f})(z)=X(s_{f})X(r_{i})(z)=X(s_{f})(x_{i})=y_{f}.

Thus
z
is also an amalgamation of
\{y_{f}\}
, so
y=z
. Thus
X
is a sheaf on
r
.
∎

Lemma 3.10
.

Given a morphism
g:V\to U
, recall the definition of pullback of families by
g
(Definition
2.3
). If
R
is a sieve over
U
, then as a presheaf
g^{*}(R)
is a pullback

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{g^{*}(R)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces R}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{y(V)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{y(U)}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lrcorner}

Similarly if
S
is a sieve over
V
, then as a presheaf
g_{*}(S)
is the image (Definition
A.41
) of the composite map

S\hookrightarrow y(V)\xrightarrow{g}y(U).

Lemma 3.11
.

Let
\mathcal{C}
be a category and
g:V\to U
be a morphism. Then we have an adjunction

(7)

g_{*}:\text{sieve}(V)\rightleftarrows\text{sieve}(U):g^{*}.

where
g_{*}
is the pushforward map on families (Definition
2.3
).

Lemma 3.12
.

Let
\mathcal{C}
be a category and
R\hookrightarrow y(U)
a sieve. A map
g:V\to U
in
\mathcal{C}
belongs to
R
if and only if
g^{*}(R)=y(V)
.

Proof.

(\Rightarrow)
If
R
is a sieve on
U
, then
g^{*}(R)=\{f:W\to V\,:\,gf\in R\}
. But
R
is a sieve and
g\in R
, so every map
f:W\to V
has this property, since
R
is closed under precomposition.

(\Leftarrow)
Suppose that
g^{*}(R)=y(V)
. Then
g_{*}(1_{V})\in R
, thus
g\in R
.
∎

Definition 3.13
.

We say that a coverage
j
is
sifted
if every
r\in j(U)
is a sieve
5
5

5

Strictly speaking, there do not exist any sifted coverages, because Definition
2.6
requires that
(1_{U})
be a covering family. As soon as a sieve
R
contains the identity map, then
R=y(U)
. Thus for
j
to be a sifted coverage, we instead require that
y(U)
be a covering sieve for every object
U
.
. We call covering families of sifted coverages
covering sieves
.

Remark 3.14
.

We will see later that being sifted is a very useful property for a coverage to have when one wishes to prove certain theorems. One immediate convenience is the following result.

Lemma 3.15
.

Let
R
be a sieve on an object
U
in a category
\mathcal{C}
and
X
a presheaf on
\mathcal{C}
. A collection
\{s_{f}\in X(V)\}_{f\in R}
of sections for every
f:V\to U
in
R
is a matching family if and only if

X(g)(s_{f})=s_{fg}

for every morphism
g:W\to V
in
\mathcal{C}
.

Proof.

(\Rightarrow)
Suppose
\{s_{f}\}
is a matching family, then consider the commutative diagram:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces W}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces W}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces V}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces U}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces gf}

this implies that
X(g)(s_{f})=s_{fg}
.

(\Leftarrow)
Suppose we have a commutative diagram:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{V^{\prime}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces V}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces U}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces h}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f^{\prime}}

where
f,f^{\prime}\in R
. Then
X(g)(s_{f})=s_{fg}=s_{f^{\prime}h}=X(h)(s_{f^{\prime}})
, thus
\{s_{f}\}
is a matching family.
∎

Lemma 3.16
.

Given a category
\mathcal{C}
, a presheaf
X
on
\mathcal{C}
, and a sieve
R\hookrightarrow y(U)
there is a canonical map

\mathbf{Pre}(\mathcal{C})(R,X)\to\text{Match}(R,X)

and furthermore this map is a bijection.

Proof.

The canonical map
\mathbf{Pre}(\mathcal{C})(R,X)\to\text{Match}(R,X)
can be described as follows. Given a map of presheaves
h:R\to X
and a morphism
f:V\to U
in
R
we obtain a commutative diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{R(U)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{R(V)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{X(U)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{X(V)}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces R(f)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces h_{U}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces h_{V}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X(f)}

and
f\in R(V)
so we obtain an element
h_{V}(f)\in X(V)
. We claim that the set of sections
\{h_{V}(f)\}_{f\in R}
is a matching family of
X
over
R
. By Lemma
3.15
, it is enough to show that if
g:W\to V
is an arbitrary morphism in
\mathcal{C}
, then
X(g)(h_{V}(f))=X(h_{V}(gf))
. However this follows by naturality of
h
.

Now to see that the map
\mathbf{Pre}(\mathcal{C})(R,X)\to\text{Match}(R,X)
is a bijection, note that any matching family
\{s_{f}\}
for
X
over
R
gives rise to a map of presheaves
h:R\to X
defined componentwise by
h_{V}(f)=s_{f}
. Being a matching family guarantees that this is a natural transformation. It is easy to see this defines an inverse to the canonical map and thus establishes the bijection.
∎

Corollary 3.17
.

Given a presheaf
X
on a category
\mathcal{C}
, and a sieve
R
over an object
U\in\mathcal{C}
,
X
is a sheaf on
R
if and only if the canonical map

(8)

X(U)\cong\mathbf{Pre}(\mathcal{C})(y(U),X)\to\mathbf{Pre}(\mathcal{C})(R,X)

induced by precomposing with the inclusion
R\hookrightarrow y(U)
is a bijection.

Proof.

This follows from Lemma
3.15
.
∎

From the above results, we obtain a convenient description of the sheaf condition for sieves. If
X
is a presheaf and
R
a sieve on
U
, then an
X
-matching family on
R
is a map
m:R\to X
, and an amalgamation for
m
is a map
x:y(U)\to X
making the following diagram commute

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces R}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{y(U)}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces m}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces x}

So
X
is a sheaf on
R
if such amalgamations always exist and are unique.

Remark 3.18
.

The following description of the sheaf condition is what is typically written in textbooks, which only consider the sifted case. We see it as a special case of our formalism here.

Corollary 3.19
.

Given a small category
\mathcal{C}
and
R\hookrightarrow y(U)
a sieve on
\mathcal{C}
generated by a family of morphisms
r=\{r_{i}:U_{i}\to U\}
, then a presheaf
X
is a sheaf on
R
if and only if the following diagram is an equalizer:

X(U)\to\prodop\displaylimits_{i\in I}X(U_{i})\rightrightarrows\prodop\displaylimits_{i,j\in I}X(U_{i})\times_{X(U)}X(U_{j}).

Proof.

This follows from combining Corollary
3.17
and Proposition
3.6
.
∎

Another convenient form for sheaves on sieves is given by the following result.

Lemma 3.20
.

Given a small category
\mathcal{C}
and a sieve
R\hookrightarrow y(U)
, a presheaf
X
is a sheaf on
R
if and only if

X(U)\cong\lim_{V\xrightarrow{f}U\in R}X(V).

Proof.

By Corollary
3.17
,
X
is a sheaf on
R
if and only if

X(U)\to\mathbf{Pre}(\mathcal{C})(R,X)

is a bijection. By the coYoneda Lemma
A.75
, we have

\displaystyle\mathbf{Pre}(\mathcal{C})(R,X)

\displaystyle\cong\mathbf{Pre}(\mathcal{C})\left(\underset{y(V)\to R}{\text{colim}}\,y(U),X\right)

\displaystyle\cong\lim_{y(V)\to R}\mathbf{Pre}(\mathcal{C})(y(V),X)

\displaystyle\cong\lim_{f:V\to U\in R}X(V).

∎

Definition 3.21
.

If
j
is a coverage, then let
\overline{j}
denote the collection of families where
R\in\overline{j}(U)
if
R=\overline{r}
for some
r\in j(U)
. We call
\overline{j}
the
sifted closure
of
j
.

Lemma 3.22
.

Given a coverage
j
on a category
\mathcal{C}
, then
\overline{j}
is a sifted coverage of
\mathcal{C}
.

Proof.

Clearly
\overline{j}
is sifted. We wish to show it is a coverage. Suppose we have a covering family
R\in\overline{j}(U)
, and a map
g:V\to U
. We wish to show that there is a covering family
R^{\prime}\in\overline{j}(V)
such that for every map
k\in R^{\prime}
,
gk
factors through some
l\in R
. Since
R=\overline{r}
, we know that since
j
is a coverage, there exists some covering family
t\in j(V)
with the corresponding property. Let
R^{\prime}=\overline{t}
. Then for every
k\in R^{\prime}
, there exists a
k_{j}
such that
k=t_{j}k_{j}
, and a map
s_{j}
making the following diagram commute:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces W}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{V_{j}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{U_{i}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces V}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces U}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces k_{j}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces t_{j}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces r_{i}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces s_{j}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces k}

but then
gk
factors through some
r_{i}
, so
gk\in R
. Thus
g_{*}(R^{\prime})\subseteq R
, and
\overline{j}
is a coverage.
∎

Corollary 3.23
.

A presheaf
X
on a category
\mathcal{C}
is a sheaf for a coverage
j
if and only if it is a sheaf for
\overline{j}
. In other words,
\mathbf{Sh}(\mathcal{C},j)=\mathbf{Sh}(\mathcal{C},\overline{j})
.

Proof.

This follows from Lemma
3.9
.
∎

4.
Coverage Closures

In this section, we detail two closure operations corresponding to different properties that are desirable for a coverage to have.

4.1.
Composition Closure

In this section we describe composition closure. The usual reference for this material would probably be
[
Joh02
, Section C.2]
, but we note that the statement of
[
Joh02
, Lemma C.2.1.7]
as written is incorrect, see Zhen Lin Low’s MathOverflow answer
[
Low
]
. The proof of Lemma
4.7
is inspired by the proof given in the MathOverflow answer above.

Definition 4.1
.

If
(\mathcal{C},j)
is a site, then we say that
j
is
composition closed
if the following condition holds: if
r=\{r_{i}:U_{i}\to U\}\in j(U)
, and for each
i
there is a
t^{i}=\{t^{i}_{j}:V_{ij}\to U_{i}\}\in j(U_{i})
, then
(r\circ t)\coloneqq\bigcupop\displaylimits_{i}(r_{i})_{*}(t^{i})\in j(U)
.

Remark 4.2
.

It will be very useful in what follows to visualize covering families and their composites as infinitely wide, but finitely high trees. For example a covering family
r=\{r_{i}:U_{i}\to U\}
over
U
can be viewed as an infinitely wide tree of height
1
:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{U_{i}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\dots}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{U_{i^{\prime}}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces U}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces r_{i}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces r_{i^{\prime}}}

Now suppose that
t^{i}=\{t^{i}_{j}:V^{i}_{j}\to U_{i}\}
is a covering family on
U_{i}
. Then since
(1_{U_{j}})
is a covering family for every
U_{j}
, if
(\mathcal{C},j)
is composition closed, the composite family

(r_{i})_{*}(t^{i})\cup\left(\bigcupop\displaylimits_{j\in(I\setminus i)}(r_{j})_{*}(1_{U_{j}})\right)=(r_{i})_{*}(t^{i})\cup(r\setminus r_{i})

is a covering family over
U
, and can be visualized as follows:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{V^{i}_{j}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\dots}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{V^{i}_{j^{\prime}}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{U_{i}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\dots}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{U_{i^{\prime}}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces U}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces r_{i}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces r_{i^{\prime}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces t^{i}_{j}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces t^{i}_{j^{\prime}}}

We can thus compose covering families in an operadic fashion. We will formalize this intuition now.

Definition 4.3

(
[
Low16
, Appendix A.2]
)
.

Let
\mathcal{C}
be a category with
U\in\mathcal{C}
. A
path
on
U
is a (possibly empty) finite ordered tuple
(f_{m},\dots,f_{1})
of morphisms such that
\text{dom}(f_{i})=\text{cod}(f_{i+1})
for
0<i<m
and
\text{cod}(f_{1})=U
, and we say that
m
is the
length
of the path. Given a path
(f_{m},\dots,f_{1})
, we call
(f_{n},\dots,f_{1})
a
prefix
if
n\leq m
. The
leaf node
of a path
(f_{m},\dots,f_{1})
is the domain of
f_{m}
.

Given a set
T
of paths on
U
, we say that a path
(f_{m},\dots,f_{1})\in T
is
maximal
if for every path
(g_{n},\dots,g_{1})\in T
with
m\leq n
and
(f_{m},\dots,f_{1})=(g_{m},\dots,g_{1})
, then
m=n
. A
tree

T
on
U
is a small set of paths on
U
satisfying the following conditions:

(1)

The empty path is an element of
T
,

(2)

if
(f_{m+1},f_{m},\dots,f_{1})\in T
then
(f_{m},\dots,f_{1})\in T
,

(3)

every path is a prefix of a maximal path,

(4)

there exists a finite maximal path length
6
6

6

We have added this condition from Low’s original definition to ease our induction proofs.
, which we call the
height
of the tree
T
.

Given a tree
T
on an object
U
, let
T^{\circ}
denote the family of morphisms

T^{\circ}=\{U_{m}\xrightarrow{f_{m}}U_{m-1}\to\dots\to U_{1}\xrightarrow{f_{1}}U\,:\,(f_{1},\dots,f_{m})\text{ is a maximal path of }T\},

we call this the
composite
of the tree
T
. If
T
contains only the empty branch, then let
T^{\circ}=\{1_{U}\}
.

We say a subset
T^{\prime}\subseteq T
is a
sub-tree
if it is also a tree on
U
. Given a tree
T
and
n\geq 0
, let
T_{\leq n}
denote the sub-tree of
T
of paths of length less than or equal to
n
.

Definition 4.4

(
[
Low16
, Appendix A.2]
)
.

Let
(\mathcal{C},j)
be a site, with
U\in\mathcal{C}
. We say that a tree
T
on
U
is a
j
-tree
if:

(1)

The set of morphisms
f:V\to U
such that
(f)\in T
, is either empty or a covering family in
j(U)
, and

(2)

for every path
f=(f_{m},\dots,f_{1})\in T
, the set of morphisms
g:V\to U_{m}
such that
(g,f_{m},\dots,f_{1})\in T
, is either empty or a covering family of
U_{m}=\text{dom}(f_{m})
.

Remark 4.5
.

A
j
-tree can be more easily described as the result of an iterative procedure as follows. Start with a covering family
r
on
U
. Then suppose that for some subset of domains
U_{i}
of the covering maps there are covering families
t^{i}
. Attach those families, extending the tree. Now keep doing this at every stage, attaching new covering famliies and extending the tree, but eventually stop at some finite stage
n
. This gives a
j
-tree, and conversely every
j
-tree can be obtained in this way. This immediately gives the following result, which is the key feature of
j
-trees.

Lemma 4.6
.

If
T
is a
j
-tree and
\{U_{i}\}_{i\in I}
is its set of leaf nodes, and for every
i\in I
,
T_{i}
is a
j
-tree on
U_{i}
, then the composite tree is a
j
-tree.

Lemma 4.7
.

Let
(\mathcal{C},j)
be a site and
X
a
j
-sheaf. If
X
is a sheaf on a family of morphisms
r=\{r_{i}:U_{i}\to U\}_{i\in I}
, and for every
i\in I
, there is a covering family
t^{i}=\{t^{i}_{j}:V^{i}_{j}\to U_{i}\}_{j\in J_{i}}
, then
X
is a sheaf on
(r\circ t)=\bigcupop\displaylimits_{i}(r_{i})_{*}(t^{i})
, where
(r_{i})_{*}(t^{i})
is the pushforward family from Definition
2.3
.

Proof.

Let
\{x^{i}_{j}\in X(V^{i}_{j})\}_{i\in I,j\in J_{i}}
be a matching family of
X
over
(r\circ t)
. We wish to show that there is a unique amalgamation.

Now for each
i\in I
, consider the set
\{x^{i}_{j}\in X(V^{i}_{j})\}_{j\in J_{i}}
. This is a matching family for
X
over
t^{i}
. Since
X
is a sheaf and
t^{i}
is a covering family, there exists a unique amalgamation
x_{i}\in X(U_{i})
such that
X(t^{i}_{j})(x_{i})=x^{i}_{j}
. We wish to prove that
\{x_{i}\in X(U_{i})\}_{i\in I}
is a matching family for
r
. Doing this will take some work. So suppose that we have a commutative diagram of the form

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{U_{i^{\prime}}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{U_{i}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces U}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces r_{i}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces r_{i^{\prime}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces h}

we wish to show that
X(g)(x_{i})=X(h)(x_{i^{\prime}})
. Now since
t^{i}
is a covering family, and
g:B\to U_{i}
is an arbitrary map, there exists a covering family
s=\{s_{k}:W_{k}\to B\}_{k\in K}
over
B
and a refinement
f:g_{*}(s)\to t^{i}
, with index map
\alpha:K\to J_{i}
. Similarly since
t^{i^{\prime}}
is a covering family, there exists a covering family
s^{\prime}=\{s^{\prime}_{k^{\prime}}:W^{\prime}_{k^{\prime}}\to B\}_{k^{\prime}\in K^{\prime}}
over
B
and a refinement
f^{\prime}:h_{*}(s^{\prime})\to t^{i^{\prime}}
, with index map
\beta:K^{\prime}\to J_{i^{\prime}}
. Thus for every
k\in K
and
k^{\prime}\in K^{\prime}
, we obtain the following commutative diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{W^{\prime}_{k^{\prime}}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{V^{i^{\prime}}_{\beta(k^{\prime})}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{W_{k}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{U_{i^{\prime}}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{V^{i}_{\alpha(k)}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{U_{i}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces U}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces r_{i}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces r_{i^{\prime}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces h}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces t^{i^{\prime}}_{\beta(k^{\prime})}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces s^{\prime}_{k^{\prime}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f^{\prime}_{k^{\prime}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces t^{i}_{\alpha(k)}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f_{k}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces s_{k}}

Now since
s^{\prime}
is a covering family of
W^{\prime}_{k^{\prime}}
, and
s_{k}:W_{k}\to B
is an arbitrary map, there exists a covering family
q=\{q_{\ell}:Q_{\ell}\to W_{k}\}_{\ell\in L}
, and a refinement
\sigma:(b_{k})_{*}(q)\to s^{\prime}
, with index map
\gamma:L\to K^{\prime}
. Thus for every
\ell
, we obtain the following commutative diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{Q_{\ell}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{W^{\prime}_{\gamma(\ell)}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{V^{i^{\prime}}_{\beta\gamma(\ell)}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{W_{k}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{U_{i^{\prime}}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{V^{i}_{\alpha(k)}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{U_{i}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces U}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces r_{i}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces r_{i^{\prime}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces h}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces t^{i^{\prime}}_{\beta\gamma(\ell)}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces s^{\prime}_{\gamma(\ell)}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f^{\prime}_{\gamma(\ell)}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces t^{i}_{\alpha(k)}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f_{k}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces s_{k}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces q_{\ell}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sigma_{\ell}}

Now notice that
X(f_{k}q_{\ell})(x^{i}_{\alpha(k)})=X(f^{\prime}_{\gamma(\ell)}\sigma_{\ell})(x^{i^{\prime}}_{\gamma(\ell)})
, because
\{x^{i}_{j}\}
is a matching family for
(r\circ t)
. Now

X(f^{\prime}_{\gamma(\ell)}\sigma_{\ell})(x^{i^{\prime}}_{\gamma(\ell)})=X(t^{i^{\prime}}_{\beta\gamma(\ell)}f^{\prime}_{\gamma(\ell)}\sigma_{\ell})(x_{i^{\prime}})=X(hs_{k}q_{\ell})(x_{i^{\prime}}),

and similarly

X(f_{k}q_{\ell})(x^{i}_{\alpha}(k))=X(t^{i}_{\alpha}(k)f_{k}q_{\ell})(x_{i})=X(gs_{k}q_{\ell})(x_{i}).

Thus for every
\ell
we have

(9)

X(gs_{k}q_{\ell})(x_{i})=X(hs_{k}q_{\ell})(x_{i^{\prime}}).

Let
z=X(gs_{k})(x_{i})
and
z^{\prime}=X(hs_{k})(x_{i^{\prime}})
. Then consider the sets
\{X(q_{\ell})(z)\}_{\ell\in L}
and
\{X(q_{\ell})(z^{\prime})\}_{\ell\in L}
. These are both matching families for
X
over
q
, which is a covering family. Since
X
is a sheaf,
z
is a unique amalgamation of
\{X(q_{\ell})(z)\}
and
z^{\prime}
is a unique amalgamation of
\{X(q_{\ell})(z^{\prime})\}
. But by (
9
),
z
is also an amalgamation for
\{X(q_{\ell})(z^{\prime})\}
. Thus
z=z^{\prime}
. Therefore

(10)

X(s_{k})X(g)(x_{i})=X(s_{k})X(h)(x_{i^{\prime}}).

Now let us repeat this trick. Namely let
y=X(g)(x_{i})
and
y^{\prime}=X(h)(x_{i^{\prime}})
, and consider the sets
\{X(s_{k})(y)\}
and
\{X(s_{k})(y^{\prime})\}
. These are a matching family for
X
over
s
. Since
X
is a sheaf,
y
is a unique amalgamation of
\{X(s_{k})(y)\}
and
y^{\prime}
is a unique amalgamation of
\{X(s_{k})(y^{\prime})\}
. But by (
10
),
y
is also an amalgamation for
\{X(s_{k})(y^{\prime})\}
. Thus
y=X(g)(x_{i})=X(h)(x_{i^{\prime}})=y^{\prime}
, as we wished to show. Thus
\{x_{i}\}
is a matching family for
r
.

Now since
X
is a sheaf on
r
, there exists a unique amalgamation
x\in X(U)
such that
X(r_{i})(x)=x_{i}
for all
i\in I
. This is clearly an amalgamation for
\{x^{i}_{j}\}
as
X(t^{i}_{j}r_{i})(x)=X(t^{i}_{j})(x_{i})=x^{i}_{j}
for every
i\in I,j\in J_{i}
. Thus
X
is a sheaf on
(r\circ t)
.
∎

Lemma 4.8
.

Suppose that
(\mathcal{C},j)
is a site,
U\in\mathcal{C}
, and
X
is a
j
-sheaf. If
T
is a
j
-tree, then
X
is a sheaf on
T^{\circ}
.

Proof.

We will prove this by induction on the height of
T
. For the base case, if the height of
T
is
0
, then by Lemma
2.24
,
X
is a sheaf on
T^{\circ}=(1_{U})
. For the inductive step, suppose that
X
is a sheaf on the composite of every
j
-tree of height
n
, and suppose that
T
is a
j
-tree of height
n+1
. Let us show that
X
is a sheaf on
T^{\circ}
. By assumption
X
is a sheaf on
T_{\leq n}^{\circ}
. But by Lemma
4.7
, this implies that
X
is a sheaf on
T^{\circ}
, as
T
can be constructed from
T_{\leq n}
by adding on covering families to its leaf nodes.
∎

Definition 4.9
.

Given a site
(\mathcal{C},j)
, we say that it is
j
-tree closed
if for every object
U
and every
j
-tree
T
on
U
, its composite
T^{\circ}
is a covering family on
U
.

Lemma 4.10

(
[
Low16
, Lemma A.2.10]
)
.

Let
(\mathcal{C},j)
be a site. Then
j
is a composition closed coverage if and only if it is
j
-tree closed.

Proof.

(\Leftarrow)
If
j
is closed under
j
-trees, then clearly it is composition closed.
(\Rightarrow)
Conversely, we can build any height
n

j
-tree by taking
n
-many composites. Thus
j
is closed under
j
-trees.
∎

Definition 4.11
.

Given a site
(\mathcal{C},j)
, let
\text{comp}(j)
denote the collection of families on
\mathcal{C}
where
\text{comp}(j)(U)
consists of those families of morphisms of the form
T^{\circ}
for some
j
-tree
T
on
U
.

Lemma 4.12
.

Given a site
(\mathcal{C},j)
, a
j
-tree
T
on an object
U\in\mathcal{C}
and a morphism
f:V\to U
, there exists a
j
-tree
S
on
V
such that
f_{*}(S^{\circ})\leq T^{\circ}
.

Proof.

Let us prove this by induction on the height of
T
. When the height of
T
is
1
, then
T^{\circ}
is a covering family, and then the result holds by the fact that
j
is a coverage.

Now suppose that the hypothesis holds for every height
n

j
-tree on
U
, and let
T
be a height
n+1

j
-tree on
U
. Then
T_{\leq n}
is a height
n

j
-tree on
U
, and so by the hypothesis there exists a
j
-tree
S
on
V
such that
f_{*}(S)\leq T_{\leq n}^{\circ}
.

So for each
s_{a}:V_{a}\to V
in
S^{\circ}
, there exists a map
s^{i}_{a}:V_{a}\to U_{i}
such that the following diagram commutes

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{V_{j}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{U_{i}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces V}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces U}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces s^{i}_{j}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces s_{j}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces r_{i}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}

where
r_{i}\in T^{\circ}_{\leq n}
. Now if
r_{i}\in T^{\circ}
, then we are done. If not, then there must be a path
(f_{n},\dots,f_{1})
that is maximal in
T_{\leq n}
but is not maximal in
T
such that
f_{n}\circ\dots\circ f_{1}=r_{i}
. Since it is not maximal, that means there is a covering family
t_{i}=\{t^{i}_{k}:U^{i}_{k}\to U_{i}\}
such that each
(t^{i}_{k},f_{n},\dots,f_{1})
is a maximal path in
T
. Since
j
is a coverage, there exists a covering family
q_{a}=\{q^{a}_{\ell}:V^{a}_{\ell}\to V_{j}\}
such that
(s^{i}_{a})_{*}(q_{a})\leq t_{i}
. By composing the
j
-tree
S
with the covering family
q_{a}
for each
s_{j}\in S^{\circ}
, we obtain a new
j
-tree
Q
such that
f_{*}(Q^{\circ})\leq T^{\circ}
.
∎

Lemma 4.13
.

If
(\mathcal{C},j)
is a site, then
\text{comp}(j)
is a coverage on
\mathcal{C}
, and it is the smallest composition closed coverage on
\mathcal{C}
containing
j
, in the sense that
j\subseteq\text{comp}(j)
(Definition
2.29
) and if
j^{\prime}
is a composition closed coverage such that
j\subseteq j^{\prime}
, then
\text{comp}(j)\subseteq j^{\prime}
.

Proof.

Let us show that
\text{comp}(j)
is a coverage. Suppose that
r\in\text{comp}(j)(U)
and
f:V\to U
is a morphism. Since
r
is a covering family, there exists a
j
-tree
T
such that
T^{\circ}=r
. Now by Lemma
4.12
, there exists a
j
-tree
S
on
V
such that
f_{*}(S^{\circ})\leq T^{\circ}
. Thus
\text{comp}(j)
is a coverage, and by Lemma
4.6
, it is composition closed. The fact that it is the smallest composition closed coverage containing
j
is not hard to prove using Lemma
4.10
.
∎

Lemma 4.14
.

Given a site
(\mathcal{C},j)
and an object
U\in\mathcal{C}
, if
T
and
S
are
j
-trees on
U
, then there exists a
j
-tree
R
such that
R^{\circ}\leq T^{\circ}
and
R^{\circ}\leq S^{\circ}
.

Proof.

Suppose that we fix a map
s_{i}:V_{i}\to U
in
S^{\circ}
, then by Lemma
4.12
, there is a
j
-tree
Q^{i}
on
V_{i}
such that
(s_{i})_{*}((Q^{i})^{\circ})\leq T^{\circ}
. Thus we can compose to obtain a new
j
-tree
R=\cup_{i}(s_{i}\circ Q^{i})
on
U
. By its construction we have
R^{\circ}\leq T^{\circ}
. But we also have
R^{\circ}\leq S^{\circ}
, by taking any map
r\in R^{\circ}
and factoring it as
r=s_{i}q^{i}
, with
s_{i}\in S^{\circ}
and
q^{i}\in(Q^{i})^{\circ}
. Thus
R
is a
j
-tree that refines both
T
and
S
.
∎

Proposition 4.15
.

Given a site
(\mathcal{C},j)
, a presheaf
X
on
\mathcal{C}
is a
j
-sheaf if and only if it is a
\text{comp}(j)
-sheaf. In other words

\mathbf{Sh}(\mathcal{C},j)=\mathbf{Sh}(\mathcal{C},\text{comp}(j)).

Proof.

This follows from Lemma
4.7
.
∎

4.2.
Refinement Closure

In this section we describe the second closure condition on coverages, refinement closure.

Definition 4.16
.

Given a site
(\mathcal{C},j)
we say that the coverage
j
is
refinement closed
if for every
U\in\mathcal{C}
,
j(U)
satisfies the following condition: if
r\in j(U)
and
f:r\to t
is a refinement, then
t\in j(U)
.

Lemma 4.17

(
[
Joh02
, Lemma 2.1.6]
)
.

Suppose
j
is a coverage on a category
\mathcal{C}
. If
X
is a sheaf on
\mathcal{C}
,
r\in j(U)
is a covering family,
t=\{t_{j}:V_{j}\to U\}
is a family of morphisms and
f:r\to t
is a refinement, then
X
is a sheaf on
t
.

Proof.

Suppose that
X
is a sheaf, and
r=\{r_{i}:U_{i}\to U\}
is a covering family. Suppose that
f:r\to t
is a refinement, so that there exists a function
\alpha:I\to J
such that for every
i
, there is a map
f_{i}:U_{i}\to V_{\alpha(i)}
such that
r_{i}=t_{\alpha(i)}f_{i}
.

Let
\{a_{j}\}
be a matching family for
t
, and let
\{b_{i}\}=f^{*}\{a_{j}\}
be the pullback matching family on
r
. Since
X
is a sheaf, and
r
is a covering family, there exists a unique amalgamation
b\in X(U)
such that
X(r_{i})(b)=b_{i}
.

Now since
r
is a covering family, for every
t_{j}:V_{j}\to U
, there exists a covering family
p^{j}=\{p^{j}_{k}:W^{j}_{k}\to V_{j}\}_{k\in K}
and maps
l_{k}:W_{k}^{j}\to U_{i}
for some
i
making the following diagram commute:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{W^{j}_{k}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{U_{i}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{V_{\alpha(i)}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{V_{j}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces U}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces U}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p^{j}_{k}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces t_{j}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces r_{i}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f_{i}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces t_{\alpha(i)}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces l_{k}}

Now we wish to show that
b
is an amalgamation for
\{a_{j}\}
, so that
X(t_{j})(b)=a_{j}
for every
j
. We will do this by exploiting the fact that
p^{j}
is a covering family.

First note that since
\{a_{j}\}
is a matching family, we have

X(p^{j}_{k})(a_{j})=X(f_{i}l_{k})(a_{\alpha(i)}).

This implies that

X(p^{j}_{k})(a_{j})=X(l_{k})X(f_{i})(a_{\alpha(i)})=X(l_{k})(b_{i})=X(l_{k})X(r_{i})(b)=X(p^{j}_{k})X(t_{j})(b).

But it is clear that
\{X(p^{j}_{k})(a_{j})\}_{k\in K}
is an
X
-matching family on
p^{j}
by functoriality. Since
X
is a sheaf, and
p^{j}
is a covering family, there exists a unique amalgamation on
V_{j}
. But both
a_{j}
and
X(t_{j})(b)
are such amalgamations. Thus
X(t_{j})(b)=a_{j}
. Thus
b
is an amalgamation for
\{a_{j}\}
.
∎

Definition 4.18
.

Let
\text{ref}(j)
denote the collection of families where
\text{ref}(j)(U)
consists of those families
t
over
U
such that there exists a covering family
r\in j(U)
and a refinement
f:r\to t
.

Lemma 4.19
.

If
(\mathcal{C},j)
is a site, then
\text{ref}(j)
is a coverage on
\mathcal{C}
, and it is the smallest refinement closed coverage on
\mathcal{C}
containing
j
.

Proof.

Let us show that
\text{ref}(j)
is a coverage. Suppose that
f:W\to V
is a morphism in
\mathcal{C}
and
t=\{t_{j}:V_{j}\to V\}
is a covering family of
\text{ref}(j)(V)
. Then there exists a refinement
g:r\to t
where
r=\{r_{i}:U_{i}\to U\}\in j(V)
. Thus there exists a family
s=\{s_{j}:W_{j}\to W\}\in j(W)
that factors through some
r_{i}:U_{i}\to U
. Therefore it factors through
t_{\alpha(i)}:V_{\alpha(i)}\to V
where
\alpha
is determined by the refinement
g
. Since
s\in j(W)
, and
j\subseteq\text{ref}(j)
, we have
s\in\text{ref}(j)(W)
. Thus
\text{ref}(j)
is a coverage. Now if
j^{\prime}
is a refinement closed coverage such that
j\subseteq j^{\prime}
, then for every
r\in j
and every refinement
r\leq t
, it follows that
t\in j^{\prime}
. Thus
\text{ref}(j)\subseteq j^{\prime}
.
∎

Proposition 4.20
.

If
(\mathcal{C},j)
is a site, then a presheaf
X
on
\mathcal{C}
is a
j
-sheaf if and only if it is a
\text{ref}(j)
-sheaf. In other words

\mathbf{Sh}(\mathcal{C},j)=\mathbf{Sh}(\mathcal{C},\text{ref}(j)).

Proof.

This follows from Lemma
4.17
.
∎

Now in the case that
j
is a sifted coverage, we notice that there exists a refinement
R\leq T
of sieves if and only if
R\subseteq T
. This observation and Lemma
4.17
proves the following result.

Corollary 4.21
.

If
j
is a sifted coverage on
\mathcal{C}
,
X
is a sheaf on
\mathcal{C}
,
R\in j(U)
is a covering sieve, and
T
is a sieve on
U
, then
X
is a sheaf on
T
if
R\subseteq T
.

Lemma 4.22
.

If
j
is a composition closed coverage on a category
\mathcal{C}
, then so is
\text{ref}(j)
.

Proof.

Suppose that
r=\{r_{i}:U_{i}\to U\}_{i\in I}
is a
\text{ref}(j)
-covering family on
U
, so that there exists a
j
-covering family
r^{\prime}
and a refinement
r^{\prime}\leq r
. Furthermore suppose that for every
i
, there is a
\text{ref}(j)
-covering family
t^{i}=\{t^{i}_{j}:V^{i}_{j}\to U_{i}\}
on
U_{i}
. Then there exists a
j
-covering family
(t^{\prime})^{i}
and a refinement
(t^{\prime})^{i}\leq t^{i}
. We wish to show that
(r\circ t)
is a
\text{ref}(j)
covering family. For each
i\in I
, we have the following commutative diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{W^{i^{\prime}}_{k}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{(V^{\prime})^{i}_{j^{\prime}}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{V^{i}_{j}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{U^{\prime}_{i^{\prime}}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{U_{i}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces U}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces r_{i}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces t^{i}_{j}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces r^{\prime}_{i^{\prime}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(t^{\prime})^{i}_{j^{\prime}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces s^{i^{\prime}}_{k}}

where
s^{i^{\prime}}=\{s^{i^{\prime}}_{k}:W_{k}\to U^{\prime}_{i^{\prime}}\}
is a
j
-covering family on
U^{\prime}_{i^{\prime}}
which exists since there is a map
U^{\prime}_{i^{\prime}}\to U_{i}
,
(t^{\prime})^{i}
is a covering family and
j
is a coverage. We can thus take the composite
(r^{\prime}\circ s)
, since
j
is composition closed, and obtain a
j
-covering family on
U
that refines
(r\circ t)
. Thus
(r\circ t)
is a
\text{ref}(j)
covering family, and thus
\text{ref}(j)
is composition closed.
∎

5.
Local Epimorphisms

Definition 5.1
.

Given a site
(\mathcal{C},j)
, a morphism
f:X\to Y
of presheaves over
\mathcal{C}
is a
j
-local epimorphism
, if for every section
s:y(U)\to Y
there exists a
j
-tree
T
on
U
such that for every
r_{i}:U_{i}\to U
in
T^{\circ}
, there exists a section
s_{i}:y(U_{i})\to X
making the following diagram commute

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{y(U_{i})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{y(U)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces s_{i}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces r_{i}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces s}

In other words,
s_{*}(T^{\circ})\leq f
.

Lemma 5.2
.

If
(\mathcal{C},j)
is a composition closed coverage, then a map
f:X\to Y
of presheaves is a
j
-local epimorphism if and only if for every section
s:y(U)\to Y
there exists a covering family
r=\{r_{i}:U_{i}\to U\}
and local sections
s_{i}:y(U_{i})\to X
making the following diagram commute for every
i

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{y(U_{i})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{y(U)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces s}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces r_{i}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces s_{i}}

i.e.
s_{*}(r)\leq f
.

Remark 5.3
.

Note that any epimorphism of presheaves is a local epimorphism. Let us call a morphism
f:X\to Y
that satisfies the condition of Lemma
5.2
a
strong
j
-local epimorphism
. Clearly strong
j
-local epimorphisms are
j
-local epimorphisms, but the converse is not true, see Remark
7.39
.

Lemma 5.4
.

Given a site
(\mathcal{C},j)
, if
f:X\to Y
and
g:Y\to Z
are
j
-local epimorphisms of presheaves on
\mathcal{C}
, then
gf:X\to Z
is a
j
-local epimorphism.

Proof.

Given a section
s:y(U)\to Z
, since
g
is a
j
-local epimorphism, there exists a
j
-tree
T
on
U
, such that every
t_{i}\in T^{\circ}
factors through
g
, providing the following commutative diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{y(U_{i})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{y(U)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Z}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces s}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces t_{i}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces s_{i}}

But then
s_{i}
provides a section of
Y
, and since
f
is a
j
-local epimorphism, there exists a
j
-tree
T_{i}
on
U_{i}
that factors through
f
. By Lemma
4.6
, the composition of
T
and
T_{i}
for every
i
is itself a
j
-tree, and thus
gf
is a
j
-local epimorphism.
∎

Lemma 5.5
.

Let
f:X\to Y
and
g:Y\to Z
be maps of presheaves. If
gf
is a
j
-local epimorphism, then so is
g
.

Proof.

Given a section
s:y(U)\to Z
, we can find a
j
-tree
T
on
U
such that
s_{*}(T^{\circ})\leq gf
. But this means that
s_{*}(T^{\circ})\leq g
, so
g
is a
j
-local epimorphism.
∎

Lemma 5.6
.

Given a site
(\mathcal{C},j)
a map
f:X\to Y
of presheaves on
\mathcal{C}
is a
j
-local epimorphism if and only if for every section
s:y(U)\to Y
, the pullback
s^{*}(f):y(U)\times_{Y}X\to y(U)
is a
j
-local epimorphism.

Proof.

(\Rightarrow)
Suppose that
f
is a
j
-local epimorphism,
s:y(U)\to Y
is a section and
t:y(V)\to y(U)
is a section. Then there exists a
j
-tree
T
on
V
such that
(st)_{*}(T^{\circ})\leq f
. But this implies that
t_{*}(T^{\circ})\leq s^{*}(f)
.

(\Leftarrow)
Given a section
s:y(U)\to Y
, if the pullback
s^{*}(f)
is a
j
-local epimorphism, then taking
1_{y(U)}:y(U)\to y(U)
, there exists a
j
-tree
T
on
U
such that
T^{\circ}\leq s^{*}(f)
, which is equivalent to
s_{*}(T^{\circ})\leq f
. Thus
f
is a
j
-local epimorphism.
∎

Lemma 5.7
.

Given a site
(\mathcal{C},j)
, a map
f:X\to Y
is a
j
-local epimorphism if and only if for every map
g:Z\to Y
the pullback
g^{*}(f):Z\times_{Y}X\to Z
is a
j
-local epimorphism.

Proof.

(\Rightarrow)
By Lemma
5.6
the map
g^{*}(f)
is a
j
-local epimorphism if and only if
s^{*}(g^{*}(f))
is a
j
-local epimorphism for every section
s:y(U)\to Z
. But in the following commutative diagram, the outer rectangle is a pullback

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{y(U)\times_{Y}X}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{Z\times_{Y}X}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{y(U)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Z}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces s^{*}(g^{*}(f))}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lrcorner}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g^{*}(f)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lrcorner}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces s}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g}

and
gs:y(U)\to Y
is a section. Thus again by Lemma
5.6
, this implies that
f
is a
j
-local epimorphism.

(\Leftarrow)
Taking
g=1_{Y}
shows that
f
is a
j
-local epimorphism.
∎

Notation 5.8
.

Given a map
f:X\to Y
in a category
\mathcal{C}
with pullbacks, let
\Delta_{f}:X\to X\times_{Y}X
denote the
slice diagonal map
, which is the unique map obtained by the universal property of pullbacks

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{X\times_{Y}X}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{0}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 1_{X}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 1_{X}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Delta_{f}}

Note that this map is a monomorphism.

Lemma 5.9
.

A map
f:X\to Y
in a category
\mathcal{C}
with pullbacks is a monomorphism if and only if the slice diagonal
\Delta_{f}:X\to X\times_{Y}X
is an isomorphism.

Proof.

First let us show that
f
is a monomorphism if and only if every map
