h:Z\to X\times_{Y}X
factors through the slice diagonal map
\Delta_{f}
. Suppose that
f
is a mono. Let
h_{0}=\pi_{0}h
and
h_{1}=\pi_{1}h
. Then we have
fh_{0}=fh_{1}
, and since
f
is a mono, this implies that
h_{0}=h_{1}
. Now
h
factors through
\Delta_{f}
because
\Delta_{f}h_{0}=\Delta_{f}pi_{0}h=h
. Conversely, if every map to
X\times_{Y}X
factors through
\Delta_{f}
, then any pair of maps
h_{0},h_{1}:Z\to X
such that
fh_{0}=fh_{1}
determines a map
h:Z\to X\times_{Y}X
that factors through
\Delta_{f}
which implies that
h_{0}=h_{1}
, so
f
is a mono.

Now let us show that every map
h:Z\to X\times_{Y}X
factors through the diagonal
\Delta_{f}
if and only if
\Delta_{f}
is an isomorphism. Suppose every map to
X\times_{Y}X
factors through
\Delta_{f}
. Then
\mathcal{C}(Z,\Delta_{f}):\mathcal{C}(Z,X)\to\mathcal{C}(Z,X\times_{Y}X)
is injective because
\Delta_{f}
is a mono, and it is surjective by assumption. Thus by the Yoneda Lemma
\Delta_{f}
is an isomorphism. Conversely, if
\Delta_{f}
is an isomorphism, then obviously every map to
X\times_{Y}X
factors through
\Delta_{f}
by its inverse.
∎

Definition 5.10
.

Given a site
(\mathcal{C},j)
, we say a map
f:X\to Y
of presheaves on
\mathcal{C}
is a
j
-local monomorphism
if the slice diagonal map
\Delta_{f}:X\to X\times_{Y}X
is a
j
-local epimorphism. We say that
f
is a
j
-
local isomorphism
if it is both a
j
-local monomorphism and a
j
-local epimorphism.

Remark 5.11
.

Similarly to Remark
5.3
all isomorphisms and monomorphisms of presheaves are local isomorphisms and local monomorphisms, respectively.

Lemma 5.12
.

Given a site
(\mathcal{C},j)
, a map
f:X\to Y
of presheaves on
\mathcal{C}
is a
j
-local monomorphism if and only if for every pair of sections
s,s^{\prime}:y(U)\to X
such that
f(s)=f(s^{\prime})
, there exists a
j
-tree
T
on
U
such that for every
r_{i}:U_{i}\to U
in
T^{\circ}
, the restrictions of
s,s^{\prime}
are equal, i.e.
X(r_{i})(s)=X(r_{i})(s^{\prime})
.

Lemma 5.13
.

Given a site
(\mathcal{C},j)
, if
f:X\to Y
and
g:Y\to Z
are maps of presheaves that are additionally
j
-local monomorphisms, then
gf
is a
j
-local monomorphism.

Proof.

Suppose that
s,s^{\prime}:y(U)\to X
are sections such that
gf(s)=gf(s^{\prime})
. Then since
g
is a
j
-local monomorphism and
f(s),f(s^{\prime}):y(U)\to Y
are sections such that
g(f(s))=g(f(s^{\prime}))
, there exists a
j
-tree
T
on
U
such that for every
r_{i}:U_{i}\to U
in
T^{\circ}
,
X(r_{i})(f(s))=X(r_{i})(f(s^{\prime}))
. Thus
f(X(r_{i})(s))=f(X(r_{i})(s^{\prime}))
. Since
f
is a
j
-local monomorphism, this implies that there exists a
j
-tree
T^{i}
such that for every
t^{i}_{j}:V^{i}_{j}\to U_{i}
,
X(t^{i}_{j})X(r_{i})(s)=X(t^{i}_{j})X(r_{i})(s^{\prime})
. Composing
T
with all of the
T^{i}
, we obtain a
j
-tree
T^{\prime}
such that for every
r_{i}t^{i}_{j}\in(T^{\prime})^{\circ}
,
X(r_{i}t^{i}_{j})(s)=X(r_{i}t^{i}_{j})(s^{\prime})
. Thus
gf
is a
j
-local monomorphism.
∎

Corollary 5.14
.

Given a site
(\mathcal{C},j)
, the class of
j
-local isomorphisms is closed under composition.

Lemma 5.15

(
[
KS05
, Lemma 16.2.4]
)
.

Given a site
(\mathcal{C},j)
and maps
f:X\to Y
,
g:Y\to Z
of presheaves on
\mathcal{C}
:

(1)

if
f
is a
j
-local monomorphism, then for every morphism
h:W\to Y
, the pullback
h^{*}(f):W\times_{Y}X\to W
is a
j
-local monomorphism,

(2)

if
f
is a
j
-local isomorphism, then for every morphism
h:W\to Y
, the pullback
h^{*}(f):W\times_{Y}X\to W
is a
j
-local isomorphism,

(3)

if
gf
is a
j
-local epimorphism, and
g
is a
j
-local monomorphism, then
f
is a
j
-local epimorphism,

(4)

if
gf
is a
j
-local monomorphism, and
f
is a
j
-local epimorphism, then
g
is a
j
-local monomorphism,

(5)

if
gf
is a
j
-local monomorphism, then
f
is a
j
-local monomorphism,

(6)

if
f:X\to Y
and
g:Y\to Z
are maps of presheaves on
\mathcal{C}
, then if any two morphisms in
\{f,g,gf\}
are
j
-local isomorphisms, then all of them are
j
-local isomorphisms.

Proof.

(1) We have the following pasting diagram of pullback squares

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{W\times_{Y}X}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{W\times_{Y}(X\times_{Y}X)\cong(W\times_{Y}X)\times_{W}(W\times_{Y}X)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{X\times_{Y}X}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces W}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Delta_{f}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces h}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lrcorner}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lrcorner}

and since the upper left map is
\Delta_{h^{*}(f)}
, and
\Delta_{f}
is a
j
-local epi by assumption, this implies
\Delta_{h^{*}(f)}
is a
j
-local epi by Lemma
5.7
. Thus
h^{*}(f)
is a
j
-local monomorphism.

(2) This follows from (1) and Lemma
5.7
.

(3) We have the following pasting diagram of pullback squares

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{X\times_{Z}Y}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{Y\times_{Z}Y}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Z}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces q}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lrcorner}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Delta_{g}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces k}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lrcorner}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\ell}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lrcorner}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g}

Now
\ell k
is a
j
-local epi since
gf
is, and similarly
q
is a
j
-local epi since
\Delta_{g}
is. Thus
f
is a
j
-local epi.

(4) We have the following pasting diagram of pullback squares

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{X\times_{Z}X}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{Y\times_{Z}X}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{X\times_{Z}Y}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{Y\times_{Z}Y}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Z}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\ell^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces k^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lrcorner}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces k}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lrcorner}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\ell}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lrcorner}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lrcorner}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g}

and
\ell,\ell^{\prime},k,k^{\prime}
are all
j
-local epimorphisms since
f
is. Thus
q=\ell k^{\prime}=\ell^{\prime}k
is a
j
-local epi. Note that the following square commutes

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{X\times_{Z}X}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{Y\times_{Z}Y}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Delta_{gf}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces q}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Delta_{g}}

But
q
and
\Delta_{gf}
are
j
-local epimorphisms, therefore by Lemma
5.5
, this implies that
\Delta_{g}
is a
j
-local epi, so
g
is a
j
-local mono.

(5) Note that the following commutative square is a pullback

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{X\times_{Y}X}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{X\times_{Z}X}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Delta_{f}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lrcorner}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Delta_{gf}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces k}

where
k
is the canonical map induced by the universal property of the pullback. Thus
\Delta_{f}
is a
j
-local epi since
\Delta_{gf}
is.

(6) By Corollary
5.14
, if
f
and
g
are
j
-local isos, then so is
gf
.

Now suppose that
gf
and
g
are
j
-local isos. Then by (3),
f
is a
j
-local epi, and by (4),
f
is a
j
-local mono and therefore a
j
-local iso.

Finally suppose that
gf
and
f
are
j
-local isos. By Lemma
5.5
, this implies that
g
is a
j
-local epi, and by (5), this implies that
g
is a
j
-local mono.
∎

6.
Saturated and Grothendieck Coverages

6.1.
Saturated Coverages

In Section
4
, we considered refinement and composition closed coverages and their corresponding closure operations. In this section we will consider coverages closed under both conditions and compare such coverages with Grothendieck coverages/topologies.

Definition 6.1

(
[
Low16
, Definition A.2.8(d)]
)
.

We say that a coverage
j
on a category
\mathcal{C}
is
saturated
if it is refinement and composition closed.

Remark 6.2
.

Our saturated coverages are precisely Shulman’s weakly
\kappa
-ary topologies for
\kappa
the cardinality of the Grothendieck universe
\mathbb{U}
(see Definition
A.15
), though we require the underlying categories to be small, see
[
Shu12
, Definition 3.1]
.

Example 6.3
.

For any topological space
X
, the open cover coverage
j_{X}
on
\mathcal{O}(X)
is saturated. This is because if
\mathcal{U}=\{U_{i}\subseteq U\}
is an open cover of an open subset
U\subseteq X
, and
\mathcal{V}=\{V^{i}_{j}\subseteq U_{i}\}
is an open cover of
U_{i}
, then
(\mathcal{U}\setminus\{U_{i}\})\cup\mathcal{V}
is an open cover of
U
, so
j_{X}
is composition closed. Now if
\mathcal{U}
and
\mathcal{V}
are collections of open subsets of
U\subseteq X
such that
\mathcal{V}
is an open cover and there is a refinement
\mathcal{U}\leq\mathcal{V}
, then
\mathcal{U}
must be an open cover of
U
as well. Thus
j_{X}
is refinement closed, and therefore it is saturated.

Example 6.4
.

The jointly epimorphic coverage
(\mathbf{FinSet},j_{\text{epi}})
from Example
2.8
is saturated.

Definition 6.5

(
[
Low16
, Definition A.2.15(d)]
)
.

Let
(\mathcal{C},j)
be a site. Say that a family of morphisms
r=\{r_{i}:U_{i}\to U\}
on
U
is
j
-
saturating
if the map

(11)

\overline{r}\hookrightarrow y(U)

given by the inclusion of the sieve generated by
r
to the maximal sieve on
U
is a
j
-local epimorphism (Definition
5.1
).

Lemma 6.6
.

Let
(\mathcal{C},j)
be a site. A family of morphisms
r=\{r_{i}:U_{i}\to U\}
is saturating if and only if for any map
g:V\to U
, there exists a
j
-tree
T
on
V
and a refinement
g_{*}(T^{\circ})\leq r
.

Proof.

Follows immediately from the definitions.
∎

Lemma 6.7
.

Let
(\mathcal{C},j)
be a site. A family of morphisms
r=\{r_{i}:U_{i}\to U\}
is saturating if and only if there exists a
j
-tree
T
on
U
and a refinement
T^{\circ}\leq r
.

Proof.

(\Rightarrow)
Suppose that
r
is saturating. Then if we let
g=1_{U}
, then Lemma
6.6
implies that there exists a
j
-tree
T
on
U
and a refinement
f:T^{\circ}\to r
.

(\Leftarrow)
Suppose that
r
is refined by a
j
-tree
T
, and suppose that
g:V\to U
is a morphism in
\mathcal{C}
, then by Lemma
4.12
, there exists a
j
-tree
S
on
V
and a refinement
g_{*}(S^{\circ})\leq T^{\circ}
, which implies that
g_{*}(S^{\circ})\leq r
. Thus by Lemma
6.6
,
r
is saturating.
∎

Corollary 6.8
.

If
(\mathcal{C},j)
is composition closed, then a family of morphisms
t
on
U
is saturating if and only if there exists a covering family
r
on
U
and a refinement
r\leq t
.

Definition 6.9
.

Given a site
(\mathcal{C},j)
, let
\text{sat}(j)
denote the collection of families on
\mathcal{C}
where
\text{sat}(j)(U)
is the set of saturating families of morphisms on
U
. We call this the
saturation closure
of
j
.

Lemma 6.10
.

Given a site
(\mathcal{C},j)
the saturation closure
\text{sat}(j)
is precisely the refinement closure of the composition closure of
j
:

\text{sat}(j)=\text{ref}(\text{comp}(j)).

Proof.

This follows from Lemma
6.7
.
∎

Proposition 6.11

(
[
Low16
, Proposition A.2.15]
)
.

Given a site
(\mathcal{C},j)
, the collection of families
\text{sat}(j)
is the smallest saturated coverage on
\mathcal{C}
containing
j
.

Proof.

By Lemma
6.10
,
\text{sat}(j)
is clearly refinement closed, and by Lemma
4.22
,
\text{sat}(j)
is also composition closed. Thus
\text{sat}(j)
is saturated. If
j^{\prime}
is a saturated coverage containing
j
, then by Lemma
4.13
,
\text{comp}(j)\subseteq j^{\prime}
. But by Lemma
4.19
, it follows that
\text{sat}(j)\subseteq j^{\prime}
.
∎

Corollary 6.12
.

A coverage
j
on a category
\mathcal{C}
is saturated if and only if
\text{sat}(j)=j
. Thus if
j
is a saturated coverage, then a family
r
on
U
is covering if and only if it is saturating.

Proposition 6.13
.

Given a site
(\mathcal{C},j)
, a presheaf
X
is a
j
-sheaf if and only if it is a
\text{sat}(j)
-sheaf. In other words

\mathbf{Sh}(\mathcal{C},j)=\mathbf{Sh}(\mathcal{C},\text{sat}(j)).

Proof.

This follows from Proposition
4.15
, Proposition
4.20
and Lemma
6.10
.
∎

Lemma 6.14
.

Let
j
be a coverage on
\mathcal{C}
. Then a morphism
f:X\to Y
of presheaves on
\mathcal{C}
is a
j
-local epimorphism (mono/iso) if and only if it is a
\text{sat}(j)
-local epimorphism (mono/iso).

Proof.

(\Rightarrow)
Suppose that
f:X\to Y
is a
j
-local epimorphism. Then for every
s:y(U)\to Y
, there exists a
j
-tree
T
on
U
such that
s_{*}(T^{\circ})\leq f
. But then
T^{\circ}
is a covering family in
\text{sat}(j)
. Thus by Lemma
5.2
,
f
is a
\text{sat}(j)
-local epimorphism.

(\Leftarrow)
If
f:X\to Y
is a
\text{sat}(j)
-local epimorphism, then for every
s:y(U)\to Y
, there exists a covering family
r\in\text{sat}(j)(U)
such that
s_{*}(r)\leq\{f\}
. But by Lemma
6.7
,
r
is a
\text{sat}(j)
-covering family if and only if it is
j
-saturating. This implies that there exists a
j
-tree
T
on
U
such that
T^{\circ}\leq r
. Thus
s_{*}(T^{\circ})\leq\{f\}
, so
f
is a
j
-local epimorphism.
∎

Lemma 6.15
.

Let
(\mathcal{C},j)
be a site. If
r
is a family on
U
, then
r
is saturating if and only if
\overline{r}
is saturating. Furthermore every
j
-covering family is saturating. Therefore
j\subseteq\text{sat}(j)
and
\overline{j}\subseteq\text{sat}(j)
.

Proof.

(\Rightarrow)
This follows just from the definition of coverage (Definition
2.6
) and
j
-local epimorphism (Definition
5.1
).

(\Leftarrow)
If
\overline{r}
is saturating, then since
\overline{\overline{r}}=\overline{r}
, this implies that
\overline{r}\hookrightarrow y(U)
is a
j
-local epimorphism, and thus
r
is saturating. Now if
r\in j
is a covering family, then it is saturating by taking
r
to refine itself by the identiy in Lemma
6.7
.
∎

Corollary 6.16
.

If
j
is a saturated coverage, then
\overline{j}\subseteq j
.

Lemma 6.17
.

If
j
is a saturated coverage on a category
\mathcal{C}
,
U\in\mathcal{C}
, and
r,r^{\prime}\in j(U)
, then there exists a covering family
r^{\prime\prime}\in j(U)
and refinements
r^{\prime\prime}\leq r
and
r^{\prime\prime}\leq r^{\prime}
. In other words,
j(U)
is a finitely cofiltered (Definition
C.8
) poset.

Proof.

Since covering families are
j
-trees, by Corollary
4.14
, there exists a
j
-tree
R
on
U
and refinements
R^{\circ}\leq r
and
R^{\circ}\leq r^{\prime}
. But
j
is saturated, so by Lemma
4.10
,
r^{\prime\prime}=R^{\circ}
is a covering family that refines both
r
and
r^{\prime}
.
∎

Lemma 6.18
.

Given a family of morphisms
r=\{r_{i}:U_{i}\to U\}_{i\in I}
in a site
(\mathcal{C},j)
, the corresponding map
\sumop\displaylimits_{i}r_{i}:\sumop\displaylimits_{i\in I}y(U_{i})\to y(U)
is a
j
-local epimorphism if and only if the corresponding family
r
is
j
-saturating.

Proof.

By Proposition
3.6
we have the following commutative diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\sumop\displaylimits_{i,j}y(U_{i})\times_{y(U)}y(U_{j})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\sumop\displaylimits_{i}y(U_{i})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\overline{r}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{y(U)}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sumop\displaylimits_{i}r_{i}}

where
\pi
is a coequalizer, and hence an epimorphism. Thus if
r
is saturating, then
\overline{r}\hookrightarrow y(U)
is a
j
-local epi, which by Lemma
5.4
implies that
\sumop\displaylimits_{i}r_{i}
is a
j
-local epi. Conversely, if
\sumop\displaylimits_{i}r_{i}
is a
j
-local epi, then by Lemma
5.5
,
\overline{r}\hookrightarrow y(U)
is a
j
-local epi, and thus
r
is saturating.
∎

Definition 6.19
.

Let
J
be a sifted coverage on a category
\mathcal{C}
. We say that
J
is a
sifted-saturated coverage
if it is composition closed and it satisfies the following weaker version of refinement closure which we call
sifted refinement closure
: If
R
is a covering sieve on
U
, and
T
is a sieve on
U
such that
R\subseteq T
, then
T
is a covering sieve on
U
.

Lemma 6.20
.

Let
(\mathcal{C},j)
be a site. If
j
is a saturated coverage, then
\overline{j}
is a sifted-saturated coverage.

Proof.

Suppose that
R\in\overline{j}(U)
is a covering sieve,
T
is a sieve on
U
and
R\subseteq T
. By definition of
\overline{j}(U)
, there exists a covering family
r=\{r_{i}:U_{i}\to U\}\in j(U)
such that
R=\overline{r}
. Thus there exists a refinement
r\leq R
. Since
j
is refinement closed, this implies that
R\in j(U)
, and therefore
T\in j(U)
. Since
T=\overline{T}
, this implies that
T\in\overline{j}(U)
. Thus
\overline{j}
is refinement closed. Now suppose that for every
f:V\to U\in R
, there exists a
\overline{j}
-covering sieve
T_{f}\in J(V)
with
j
-covering family
t^{f}=\{t^{f}_{j}:V_{j}\to V\}
such that
T_{f}=\overline{t^{f}}
. We want to show that
(R\circ T)=\bigcupop\displaylimits_{f\in R}f_{*}(T_{f})
is a
\overline{j}
-covering sieve on
U
. Now it is easy to see that
R=\bigcupop\displaylimits_{i}(r_{i})_{*}(y(U_{i}))
and
T_{f}=\bigcupop\displaylimits_{j}(t^{f}_{j})_{*}(y(V_{j}))
. Since
j
is saturated, and
(1_{W})\in j(W)
for every
W\in\mathcal{C}
, then by Lemma
6.15
, this implies that
y(W)\in j(W)
. Thus
(R\circ T)=(r\circ y_{r}\circ t\circ y_{t})
, where we are letting
y_{r}
and
y_{t}
denote the collection of maximal sieves
y(W)
for every
W
in the domains of
r
and
t
respectively. But
j
is composition closed so
(r\circ y_{r}\circ t\circ y_{t})=(R\circ T)
is a covering sieve. Thus
\overline{j}
is sifted-saturated.
∎

6.2.
Grothendieck Coverages

We will now consider the more traditional notion of a Grothendieck coverage. The work of the previous section will be used to show how a coverage generates a Grothendieck coverage, and this is useful in applications. However the main usage of Grothendieck coverages is in proving theorems about Grothendieck toposes.

Definition 6.21
.

A
Grothendieck coverage
7
7

7

More commonly known as a
Grothendieck topology
on an (essentially small) category
\mathcal{C}
is a sifted collection of families
J
on
\mathcal{C}
such that

(G1)

y(U)\in J(U)
for every
U\in\mathcal{C}
,

(G2)

for any sieve
R\in J(U)
and any morphism
g:V\to U
,
g^{*}(R)\in J(V)
, and

(G3)

if
R\in J(U)
,
R^{\prime}
is a sieve on
U
, and
g^{*}(R^{\prime})\in J(V)
for every
g\in R(V)
, then
R^{\prime}\in J(U)
.

We refer to a category
\mathcal{C}
equipped with a Grothendieck coverage a
Grothendieck site
. Note that (G1) and (G2) imply that a Grothendieck coverage is a sifted coverage.

Lemma 6.22

(
[
Jar15
, Lemma 3.12]
)
.

Let
(\mathcal{C},J)
be a Grothendieck site. Then the following hold:

(1)

if
R,R^{\prime}
are sieves on
U
,
R\subseteq R^{\prime}
and
R
is a covering sieve, then
R^{\prime}
is a covering sieve,

(2)

if
R,R^{\prime}
are covering sieves, then
R\cap R^{\prime}
is a covering sieve.

Proof.

(1) Let
g:V\to U\in R
, then by Lemma
3.12
, we know that
g^{*}R=g^{*}R^{\prime}=y(V)
, which is a covering sieve of
V
by (G1). Since this is true for all
g\in R
, then
R^{\prime}
is a covering sieve by (G3).

(2) Let
g\in R
, then
g^{*}(R\cap R^{\prime})=g^{*}R\cap g^{*}R^{\prime}=y(V)\cap g^{*}R^{\prime}=g^{*}R^{\prime}
by Lemma
3.12
. By
(G2)
,
g^{*}R^{\prime}\in J(V)
, thus by
(G3)
,
R\cap R^{\prime}
is a covering sieve.
∎

Lemma 6.23
.

Given a small category
\mathcal{C}
and a set
\{J_{i}\}_{i\in I}
of Grothendieck coverages on
\mathcal{C}
, let
J=\bigcapop\displaylimits_{i\in I}J_{i}
denote the sifted collection of families where
R\in J(U)
if and only if
R\in J_{i}(U)
for all
i\in I
. Then
J
is a Grothendieck coverage.

Proof.

Since
y(U)\in J_{i}(U)
for all
i\in I
,
J
satisfies (G1). If
R\in J(U)
, then
R\in J_{i}(U)
for all
i
, and since each
J_{i}
is a Grothendieck coverage
g^{*}(R)\in J_{i}(V)
for every map
g:V\to U
, so
g^{*}(R)\in J(V)
, so
J
satisfies (G2). Similar reasoning shows that
J
satisfies (G3).
∎

Remark 6.24
.

Thanks to Lemma
6.23
, we can generate a Grothendieck coverage given any collection of families. Indeed, suppose that
\mathcal{C}
is a small category and
j
is a collection of families on
\mathcal{C}
. Then let
\text{Gro}(j)
denote the intersection of all the Grothendieck coverages
J
such that
j\subseteq J
.

Lemma 6.25

(
[
Jar15
, Lemma 3.12.3]
)
.

If
(\mathcal{C},J)
is a Grothendieck site, and
R\in J(U)
is a covering sieve, such that for every
f:V\to U
with
f\in R
, there is a covering sieve
T_{f}\in J(V)
. Then
(R\circ T)=\bigcupop\displaylimits_{f\in R}f_{*}(T_{f})
is a covering sieve. In other words,
J
is composition closed.

Proof.

First consider
f^{*}(R\circ T)=\bigcupop\displaylimits_{g\in R}f^{*}g_{*}(T_{g})
. Now if
g=f
, then
T_{f}\subseteq f^{*}f_{*}(T_{f})
Thus
T_{f}\subseteq f^{*}(R\circ T)
, and since
T_{f}
is a covering sieve, by Lemma
6.22
,
f^{*}(R\circ T)
is a covering sieve. Since
f^{*}(R\circ T)
is a covering sieve for every
f\in R
, by
(G3)
, therefore
(R\circ T)
is a covering sieve.
∎

Remark 6.26
.

Note that while Grothendieck coverages are composition closed, they are
not
refinement closed. Indeed, Grothendieck coverages must be sifted, but if
R
is a covering sieve of a Grothendieck coverage
J
over
U
, then there exists a refinement
R\leq(1_{U})
, but
(1_{U})
is not a sieve, so it cannot belong to
J(U)
. However, they are sifted refinement closed, which is easy to see. This means that Grothendieck coverages and saturated coverages are two different kinds of mathematical objects, but we will show they are equivalent in a certain sense.

Proposition 6.27
.

Given a category
\mathcal{C}
, the Grothendieck coverages on
\mathcal{C}
are precisely the sifted-saturated coverages on
\mathcal{C}
.

Proof.

Suppose that
J
is a Grothendieck coverage, then by Lemma
6.22
and Lemma
6.25
,
J
is a sifted-saturated coverage. Conversely suppose that
J
is a sifted-saturated coverage. We wish to show that
J
is a Grothendieck coverage. Suppose that
R\in J(U)
and
g:V\to U
is a morphism. Since
J
is a coverage, there exists a covering sieve
T\in J(V)
such that
g_{*}(T)\subseteq R
. But by Lemma
3.11
, this is equivalent to
T\subseteq g^{*}(R)
. Since
J
is sifted-refinement closed, this implies that
g^{*}(R)\in J(V)
. Now let us show (G3). Suppose that
R\in J(U)
,
R^{\prime}\subseteq y(U)
and for every
g\in R
,
g^{*}(R^{\prime})\in J(V)
. Then
(R\circ R^{*}(R^{\prime}))=\bigcupop\displaylimits_{g\in R}g_{*}g^{*}(R^{\prime})
is a covering sieve since
J
is composition closed, and for every
g\in R
, it follows that
g_{*}g^{*}(R^{\prime})\subseteq R^{\prime}
, thus
(R\circ R^{*}(R^{\prime}))\subseteq R^{\prime}
. But
J
is sifted-refinement closed, so
R^{\prime}\in J(V)
.
∎

Lemma 6.28
.

Suppose that
(\mathcal{C},J)
is a Grothendieck site. Then a sieve
R
on an object
U
is a covering sieve for
J
if and only if the inclusion
R\hookrightarrow y(U)
is a
J
-local isomorphism if and only if it is a
J
-local epimorphism.

Proof.

(\Rightarrow)
Suppose that
R
is a
J
-covering sieve on
U
. Let us show that
R\hookrightarrow y(U)
is a
J
-local epimorphism. If
x:y(V)\to y(U)
is a section, then since
R\in J(U)
is a covering family and
J
is a coverage, then there exists a covering family
S\in J(V)
such that for every
s_{i}:V_{i}\to V
in
S
, it factors through some map in
R
. Thus
R\hookrightarrow y(U)
is a
J
-local epimorphism. Since it is a monomorphism, this implies that it is also a
J
-local isomorphism.

(\Leftarrow)
Suppose that
R\hookrightarrow y(U)
is a
J
-local epimorphism. Then it is a
J
-local isomorphism. We want to show that
R
is a
J
-covering sieve on
U
. Given any map
f:V\to U
, since
R\hookrightarrow y(U)
is a
J
-local epimorphism, there is a
J
-covering sieve
S_{f}
on
V
such that
S_{f}\subseteq f^{*}(R)
. Thus
f^{*}(R)
is a
J
-covering sieve for every
f
in the maximal sieve
y(U)
. Thus by (G3),
R\in J(U)
.
∎

Recall that if
f:X\to Y
is a map of presheaves, then
f
factors through its image presheaf
\text{im}(f)
(Definition
A.77
).

Lemma 6.29
.

Given a Grothendieck site
(\mathcal{C},J)
, a map
f:X\to y(U)
of presheaves is a
J
-local epimorphism if and only if
\text{im}(f)\hookrightarrow y(U)
is a
J
-covering sieve.

Proof.

(\Rightarrow)
Suppose that
f:X\to y(U)
is a
J
-local epimorphism, and we factor it as

X\xrightarrow{e_{f}}\text{im}(f)\xhookrightarrow{i_{f}}y(U).

Then by Lemma
5.5
,
i_{f}
is a
J
-local epimorphism. Thus by Lemma
6.28
,
\text{im}(f)
is a
J
-covering sieve.

(\Leftarrow)
Conversely if
\text{im}(f)\hookrightarrow y(U)
is a
J
-covering sieve, then by Lemma
6.28
,
i_{f}
is a
J
-local epimorphism, and
e_{f}
is an epimorphism, hence a
J
-local epimorphism, and thus by Lemma
5.4
,
f
is a
J
-local epimorphism.
∎

Lemma 6.30
.

Given a Grothendieck site
(\mathcal{C},J)
, a map
f:X\to Y
of presheaves is a
J
-local epimorphism if and only if for every section
s:y(U)\to Y
, the pullback
s^{*}(f):y(U)\times_{Y}X\to y(U)
is a
J
-local epimorphism.

Proof.

This is a special case of Lemma
5.6
.
∎

6.3.
Comparison of Saturated and Grothendieck Coverages

In this section we compare saturated coverages and Grothendieck coverages, showing that for any category
\mathcal{C}
, there is an isomorphism between the corresponding posets of saturated and Grothendieck coverages. This leads to Corollary
6.38
showing that to every coverage
j
there exists a unique smallest Grothendieck coverage containing
\overline{j}
and with the same sheaves as
j
.

Lemma 6.31
.

Let
j
be a saturated coverage on a category
\mathcal{C}
. Then
\overline{j}
is a Grothendieck coverage.

Proof.

This follows from Lemma
6.20
.
∎

Definition 6.32
.

Suppose that
J
is a sifted coverage on
\mathcal{C}
, let
J^{\circ}
denote the collection of families on
\mathcal{C}
where a family
r
on
U
belongs to
J^{\circ}(U)
if
\overline{r}\in J(U)
. We call this the
interior coverage
of
J
.

Lemma 6.33
.

If
J
is a Grothendieck coverage on a category
\mathcal{C}
, then
J^{\circ}
is a saturated coverage.

Proof.

Suppose that
r\in J^{\circ}(U)
and
r\leq t
. Then
\overline{r}\in J(U)
and
\overline{r}\subseteq\overline{t}
. Thus
\overline{t}\in J(U)
, so
t\in J^{\circ}(U)
. Thus
J^{\circ}
is refinement closed. Now given a
J^{\circ}
-covering family
r=\{r_{i}:U_{i}\to U\}_{i\in I}
on
U
and for each
i
a
J^{\circ}
-covering family
t^{i}
on
U_{i}
, then we want to show that
\overline{(r\circ t)}\in J(U)
. Now note that
\{t^{i}\}_{i\in I}
is an
I
-indexed family of
J^{\circ}
-covering families. Let
\overline{I}
denote the indexing set for
\overline{r}
. We wish to extend
\{\overline{t^{i}}\}
to a new set
\{S^{j}\}
of
J^{\circ}
-covering sieves indexed by
j\in\overline{I}
. For
i\in I\subseteq\overline{I}
let
S^{i}=\overline{t^{i}}
. Now if
g\in\overline{r}
, then
g
factors as
g=r_{k}g_{j}
for some morphism
g_{j}
and some covering map
r_{k}
. Then set
S^{j}=g_{j}^{*}(\overline{t^{k}})
. This is a covering sieve since
J
is a Grothendieck coverage. Thus
(\overline{r}\circ S)
is a covering sieve on
U
and furthermore
(\overline{r}\circ S)\subseteq\overline{(r\circ t)}
. Thus by Lemma
6.22
,
\overline{(r\circ t)}
is a covering sieve. Thus
J^{\circ}
is a saturated coverage.
∎

Definition 6.34
.

Given a category
\mathcal{C}
, let
\mathbf{SatCvg}(\mathcal{C})
denote the (large) poset of saturated coverages equipped with the
\subseteq
relation. Similarly let
\mathbf{GroCvg}(\mathcal{C})
denote the (large) poset of Grothendieck coverages. The constructions
\overline{(-)}
and
(-)^{\circ}
defined above can easily be seen to define maps of posets.

Proposition 6.35
.

The maps of (large) posets

(12)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathbf{SatCvg}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathbf{GroCvg}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\overline{(-)}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{(-)^{\circ}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\dashv}

form an isomorphism of posets.

Proof.

Let us show that
\overline{J^{\circ}}=J
and
(\overline{j})^{\circ}=j
. Clearly
j\subseteq(\overline{j})^{\circ}
, so let us prove the converse. If
r\in(\overline{j})^{\circ}
, then
\overline{r}\in\overline{j}
, so by Collorary
6.16
,
\overline{r}\in j
. But
j
is saturated, so by Lemma
6.15
, this implies that
r\in j
. Now for
J
a Grothendieck coverage, it is clear that
\overline{J^{\circ}}\subseteq J
, so let us prove the converse. Suppose that
R\in J
and
r
is a family such that
\overline{r}=R
. Then
r\in J^{\circ}
, so
\overline{r}=R\in\overline{J^{\circ}}
. Thus
J\subseteq\overline{J^{\circ}}
.
∎

Definition 6.36
.

Given a coverage
j
on a category
\mathcal{C}
, let

\text{Gro}(j)\coloneqq\overline{\text{sat}(j)}.

We call this the
Grothendieck closure
of
j
.

Lemma 6.37
.

Given a coverage
j
on a category
\mathcal{C}
, we have

(13)

\text{Gro}(j)=\text{Gro}(\overline{j}).

Furthermore,
\text{Gro}(j)
is the smallest Grothendieck coverage containing
\overline{j}
.

Proof.

By Lemma
6.15
, we have that
\overline{j}\subseteq\text{sat}(j)
, and therefore
\text{sat}(\overline{j})\subseteq\text{sat}(j)
. Thus
\text{Gro}(\overline{j})=\overline{\text{sat}(\overline{j})}\subseteq\overline{\text{sat}(j)}=\text{Gro}(j)
. Conversely, it is easy to see that
j\subseteq\left(\overline{\text{sat}(\overline{j})}\right)^{\circ}=\text{sat}(\overline{j})
, since if
r\in j
, then
\overline{r}\in\overline{j}
and therefore
r\in\text{sat}(\overline{j})
. Thus
\text{sat}(j)\subseteq\text{sat}(\overline{j})
and thus
\text{Gro}(j)\subseteq\text{Gro}(\overline{j})
.

Now suppose that
J
is a Grothendieck coverage such that
\overline{j}\subseteq J
. Then
j\subseteq(\overline{j})^{\circ}\subseteq J^{\circ}
, but
J^{\circ}
is saturated by Lemma
6.33
, so
\text{sat}(j)\subseteq J^{\circ}
. Thus
\text{Gro}(j)\subseteq J
.
∎

Corollary 6.38
.

Given a coverage
j
on a category
\mathcal{C}
, there exists a Grothendieck coverage
\text{Gro}(j)
containing
\overline{j}
and such that
\mathbf{Sh}(\mathcal{C},j)=\mathbf{Sh}(\mathcal{C},\text{Gro}(j))
.

Lemma 6.39
.

Given a site
(\mathcal{C},j)
a map
f:X\to Y
of presheaves on
\mathcal{C}
is a
j
-local epimorphism (mono/iso) if and only if it is a
\text{Gro}(j)
-local epimorphism (mono/iso).

Proof.

By Lemma
6.14
, we can assume that
j
is a saturated coverage. Thus it is sufficient to show that
f
is a
j
-local epimorphism (mono/iso) if and only if it is a
\overline{j}
-local epimorphism (mono/iso).

(\Rightarrow)
If
f
is a
j
-local epimorphism, then by Corollary
6.16
,
\overline{j}\subseteq j
, so
f
is a
\overline{j}
-local epimorphism. This similarly implies that if
f
is a
j
-local monomorphism or isomorphism, then it is a
\overline{j}
-local monomorphism or isomorphism.

(\Leftarrow)
Since every
j
-covering family is contained within a
\overline{j}
-covering family, if
f
lifts against all the
\overline{j}
-covering families, then it lifts against all the
j
-covering families. Thus
f
is a
j
-local epimorphism (mono/iso).
∎

Lemma 6.40
.

Let
j
be a saturated coverage on a small category
\mathcal{C}
, let
U\in\mathcal{C}
and let
r,r^{\prime}\in j(U)
. If we consider
j(U)
as a preorder, ordered by refinement, then the meet of
r
and
r^{\prime}
exists and is given by

r\wedge r^{\prime}=\overline{r}\cap\overline{r}^{\prime}.

In particular, this implies that
\text{sat}(j)(U)
is a finitely cofiltered category (Definition
C.8
).

Proof.

First we note that
\overline{r}\cap\overline{r}^{\prime}
is
j
-saturating because the following diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\overline{r}\cap\overline{r}^{\prime}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\overline{r}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\overline{r}^{\prime}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{y(U)}}

is a pullback, and both
\overline{r}\hookrightarrow y(U)
and
\overline{r}^{\prime}\hookrightarrow y(U)
are
j
-local epimorphisms since
r,r^{\prime}\in j(U)
and thus are
j
-saturating by Corollary
6.12
. Hence
\overline{r}\cap\overline{r}^{\prime}\hookrightarrow\overline{r}^{\prime}
is a
j
-local epimorphism, and thus the composite
\overline{r}\cap\overline{r}^{\prime}\hookrightarrow y(U)
is a
j
-local epimorphism by Lemma
7.3
.

Now
\overline{\overline{r}\cap\overline{r}^{\prime}}=\overline{r}\cap\overline{r}^{\prime}\subseteq\overline{r}
. Thus by Lemma
3.5
we have
\overline{r}\cap\overline{r}^{\prime}\leq r
, and similarly for
r^{\prime}
. Now if
t\leq r
and
t\leq r^{\prime}
, then
\overline{t}\subseteq\overline{r}\cap\overline{r}^{\prime}=\overline{\overline{r}\cap\overline{r}^{\prime}}
. Thus again by Lemma
3.5
, we have
t\leq\overline{r}\cap\overline{r}^{\prime}
.
∎

Remark 6.41
.

Given a saturated coverage
j
, for every
U\in\mathcal{C}
,
j(U)
is a preorder under refinement, and we can consider
\overline{j}(U)
as a poset under inclusion. If we let
j(U)_{\sim}
denote the quotient of
j(U)
by
r\sim r^{\prime}
if
r\leq r^{\prime}
and
r^{\prime}\leq r
, then we get an isomorphism of posets

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{j(U)_{\sim}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\overline{j}(U)}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\overline{(-)}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota}

where
\iota
is just inclusion. Basically this is just the fact that
r\leq\overline{r}
and
\overline{r}\leq r
.

The following result is very important for Section
7
. It says in the language of Appendix
B
, that
j
-sheaves are
W
-local, where
W
is the class of
j
-local isomorphisms.

Proposition 6.42
.

Let
(\mathcal{C},J)
be a Grothendieck coverage,
Z
a
J
-sheaf, and let
f:X\to Y
be a
J
-local isomorphism. If
g:X\to Z
is a map of presheaves, then there exists a unique map
h:Y\to Z
such that
hf=g
.

Proof.

Suppose that
s:y(U)\to Y
is a section. Then since
J
is composition closed, there exists a
J
-covering sieve
R\hookrightarrow y(U)
such that for every
r_{i}:U_{i}\to U
in
R
there is a map
s_{i}:y(U_{i})\to X
making the following diagram commute

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{y(U_{i})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Z}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{y(U)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces s_{i}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces r_{i}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces s}

Now let us show that
\{g(s_{i})\in Z(U_{i})\}
is a
Z
-matching family. First note that if we have a commutative diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces U_{ij}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{U_{j}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{U_{i}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces U}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{j}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{i}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces r_{j}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces r_{i}}

then we obtain sections
X(\pi_{i})(s_{i}),X(\pi_{j})(s_{j})\in X(U_{ij})
such that

f(X(\pi_{i})(s_{i}))=Y(\pi_{i})(f(s_{i}))=Y(\pi_{i})Y(r_{i})(s)=Y(\pi_{j})Y(r_{j})(s)=Y(\pi_{j})(f(s_{j}))=f(X(\pi_{j})(s_{j})).

Now since
f
is a local monomorphism, there exists a
J
-covering sieve
T^{ij}
on
U_{ij}
such that if
t_{k}:V_{k}\to U_{ij}\in T^{ij}
, then
X(t_{k})X(\pi_{i})(s_{i})=X(t_{k})X(\pi_{j})(s_{j})
. Thus
\{g(X(t_{k})X(\pi_{i})(s_{i}))=g(X(t_{k})X(\pi_{j})(s_{j})\}_{k}
is a
Z
-matching family on
T^{ij}
. But both
g(X(\pi_{i})(s_{i}))=Z(\pi_{i})(g(s_{i}))
and
g(X(\pi_{j})(s_{j}))=Z(\pi_{j})(g(s_{j}))
are amalgamations on
Z(U_{ij})
. But
Z
is a
J
-sheaf, and hence has unique amalgamations, so
Z(\pi_{i})(g(s_{i}))=Z(\pi_{j})(g(s_{j}))
. Thus
\{g(s_{i})\}
is a
Z
-matching family for
R
, so there exists a unique amalgamation
h(s)\in Z(U)
such that
Z(r_{i})(h(s))=g(s_{i})
. Let
h:Y(U)\to Z(U)
be defined objectwise by
s\mapsto h(s)
.

Now let us show that the above construction didn’t depend on the choice of covering families. Suppose that there is another covering sieve
R^{\prime}=\{r^{\prime}_{i^{\prime}}:U_{i^{\prime}}\to U\}_{i^{\prime}\in I^{\prime}}
on
U
with corresponding sections
s^{\prime}_{i^{\prime}}\in X(U_{i^{\prime}})
. Then as above, we obtain two
Z
-matching families
\{g(s_{i})\}_{i\in I}
and
\{g(s^{\prime}_{i^{\prime}})\}_{i^{\prime}\in I^{\prime}}
on
R
and
R^{\prime}
respectively. Now since
J
is Grothendieck coverage, by Lemma
6.22
, the sieve
R\cap R^{\prime}
is a
J
-covering sieve.

Thus we have two
Z
-matching families
\{g(s_{\ell})\}_{\ell\in I\cap I^{\prime}}
,
\{g(s^{\prime}_{\ell})\}_{\ell\in I\cap I^{\prime}}
on
R\cap R^{\prime}
. Furthermore
f(s_{\ell})=Y(r_{\ell})(s)=f(s^{\prime}_{\ell})
for every
r_{\ell}\in R\cap R^{\prime}
. Thus since
f
is a local mono, there exists a
J
-covering sieve
T^{\ell}
on
U_{\ell}
such that for every
t^{\ell}_{k}:V^{\ell}_{k}\to U_{\ell}\in T^{\ell}
, we have

X(t^{\ell}_{k})(s_{\ell})=X(t^{\ell}_{k})(s^{\prime}_{\ell}).
Thus
Z(t^{\ell}_{k})(g(s_{\ell}))=Z(t^{\ell}_{k})(g(s^{\prime}_{\ell}))
. Therefore
\{Z(t^{\ell}_{k})(g(s_{\ell}))=Z(t^{\ell}_{k})(g(s^{\prime}_{\ell}))\}_{k}
is a
Z
-matching family for
T^{\ell}
, with amalgamations
g(s_{\ell})
and
g(s^{\prime}_{\ell})
. Since
Z
is a
J
-sheaf, amalgamations on
T^{\ell}
are unique, so this implies that
g(s_{\ell})=g(s^{\prime}_{\ell})
for every
\ell\in I\cap I^{\prime}
.

Now if
h(s)
is an amalgamation for
\{g(s_{i})\}_{i\in I}
and
h^{\prime}(s)
is an amalgamation for
\{g(s^{\prime}_{i^{\prime}})\}_{i^{\prime}\in I^{\prime}}
, then both
h(s)
and
h^{\prime}(s)
are amalgamations for
\{g(s_{\ell})=g(s^{\prime}_{\ell})\}_{\ell\in I\cap I^{\prime}}
. But
Z
is a
J
-sheaf, and therefore amalgamations for
R\cap R^{\prime}
are unique, therefore
h(s)=h^{\prime}(s)
. Thus the assignment
s\mapsto h(s)
doesn’t depend on the chosen covering families.

Let us show that the assignment
h_{U}:Y(U)\to Z(U)
extends to a natural transformation
h:Y\to Z
. If
a:V\to U
is a morphism in
\mathcal{C}
, then since
J
is a coverage, there exists a covering sieve
T=\{t_{j}:V_{j}\to V\}_{j\in J}
an index map
\alpha:J\to I
and maps
\ell_{j}:V_{j}\to U_{\alpha}(j)
. Taking
\{Z(\ell_{j})(g(s_{\alpha(j)}))\}=\{g(s_{\alpha(j)}\ell_{j})\}
, we get a
Z
-matching family on
T
, and hence get the unique amalgamation
h(sa)
. But
Z(t_{j})Z(a)(h(s))=Z(at_{j})(h(s))=Z(\ell_{j})Z(r_{\alpha(j)})(h(s))=Z(\ell_{j})g(s_{\alpha(j)})
. Thus
Z(a)(h(s))
is also an amalgamation, and hence
h(Y(a)(s))=h(sa)=Z(a)(h(s))
.

Now let us show that
hf=g
. If
s\in Y(U)
is a section such that
s=f(x)
for some
x\in X(U)
, then
\{X(a)(x)\}_{a\in y_{U}}
is an
X
-matching family for the maximal covering sieve
y_{U}=\{a:V\to U\}
, such that
f(X(a)(x))=Y(a)(f(x))=Y(a)(s)
. Thus
\{g(X(a)(x))=Z(a)(g(x))\}
is a
Z
-matching family for
y_{U}
, with unique amalgamation
g(x)
and thus
h(s)=h(f(x))=g(x)
.

Uniqueness of
h
follows from uniqueness of amalgamations.
∎

Corollary 6.43
.

The hypothesis of Proposition
6.42
holds for an arbitrary site
(\mathcal{C},j)
, not just Grothendieck sites.

Proof.

This follows from Lemma
6.39
.
∎

7.
Sheafification and Lex Localizations

In this section, we use the theory of localizations as in Appendix
B
to study Grothendieck toposes. The main goal for this section is Theorem
7.18
, also called the “Little Giraud Theorem”, which characterizes Grothendieck toposes by lex localizations of presheaf topoi. This is well-trodden ground, see
[
Joh02
, Corollary 2.1.11]
. However, we diverge from the usual way of proving this using Lawvere-Tierney topologies and instead use systems of local epimorphisms and isomorphisms.

7.1.
Systems of Local Epimorphisms

Definition 7.1

(
[
KS05
, Section 16.1]
)
.

Given a presheaf topos
\mathbf{Pre}(\mathcal{C})
a
system of local epimorphisms
consists of a class
E=\{f:X\to Y\}
of morphisms of presheaves such that:

(1)

if
f:X\to Y
is an epi, then
f\in E
,

(2)

if
f:X\to Y
is in
E
and
g:Y\to Z
is in
E
, then
gf\in E
,

(3)

if
gf
as above is in
E
, then
g\in E
,

(4)

an arbitrary morphism
f:X\to Y
is in
E
if and only if for every section
s:y(U)\to Y
the pullback
s^{*}(f):y(U)\times_{Y}X\to X
is in
E
.

If
E
is a system of local epimorphisms on
\mathbf{Pre}(\mathcal{C})
and
f\in E
we call
f
an
E
-local epimorphism or
E
-local epi for short. We will use the statements
f\in E
and
f
is an
E
-local epi interchangeably.

Lemma 7.2
.

The axiom (4) of Definition
7.1
is equivalent to the following (4’): a map
f:X\to Y
of presheaves is an
E
-local epi if and only if for every arbitrary map
g:Z\to Y
, the pullback map
g^{*}(f):Z\times_{Y}X\to Z
is an
E
-local epi.

Proof.

(4^{\prime}\Rightarrow 4)
If
f:X\to Y
is an
E
-local epi, then for every section
s:y(U)\to Y
, the pullback morphism
s^{*}(f)
is an
E
-local epi. Now conversely suppose that every
s^{*}(f)
is in
E
. We want to show that
f
is in
E
. Let
g:Z\to Y
be a map of presheaves, and suppose that
s:y(U)\to Z
is a section. Consider the following diagram of pullback squares

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{y(U)\times_{Z}(Z\times_{Y}X)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{Z\times_{Y}X}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{y(U)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Z}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces s^{*}(g^{*}(f))}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lrcorner}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g^{*}(f)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lrcorner}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces s}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g}

since both squares are pullbacks, the outer square is a pullback. Thus by assumption
s^{*}(g^{*}(f))
is an
E
-local epi. But since
s
was arbitrary, this proves that
g^{*}(f)
is in
E
. But since
g
was arbitrary, this proves that
f
is in
E
.

(4\Rightarrow 4^{\prime})
If
f
is in
E
, then by a similar argument as above, for every map
g:Z\to Y
,
g^{*}(f)
is in
E
. Conversely if every
g^{*}(f)
is in
E
, then taking
g
to be the identity shows that
f
is in
E
.
∎

Lemma 7.3
.

Given a coverage
j
on
\mathcal{C}
, the class of
j
-local epimorphims forms a system of local epimorphisms.

Proof.

(1) is clear, (2) follows from Lemma
5.4
, and (3) follows from Lemma
5.5
. Now suppose that
f:X\to Y
is a map of presheaves and for all sections
s:y(U)\to Y
the pullback
s^{*}(f):y(U)\times_{Y}X\to y(U)
is a
j
-local epimorphism. We want to show that
f
is a
j
-local epimorphism. So suppose that
s:y(U)\to Y
is a section. Then
s^{*}(f)
is a
j
-local epimorphism, so if we consider the identity map
1_{y(U)}:y(U)\to y(U)
, then there exists a
j
-tree
T
on
U
such
T^{\circ}\leq s^{*}(f)
, which implies that
s_{*}(T^{\circ})\leq f
. Since this holds for any section
s
, this shows that
f
is a
j
-local epimorphism. Thus (4).
(\Leftarrow)
holds.

Conversely, suppose that
f
is a local epi. We want to show that for every
s:y(U)\to Y
, the map
s^{*}(f)
is a local epi. Suppose that
s^{\prime}:y(V)\to y(U)
is a section, then since
f
is a local epi, there exists a
j
-tree
T
on
V
and a refinement
g:(ss^{\prime})_{*}(T^{\circ})\to f
as in the following commutative diagram

(14)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{y(V_{i})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{y(U)\times_{Y}X}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{y(V)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{y(U)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces s}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces s^{*}(f)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces s^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces t_{i}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g_{i}}

By the universal property of the pullback, there is a unique dotted map as above making the diagram commute, and therefore defining a refinement
(s^{\prime})_{*}(T^{\circ})\leq s^{*}(f)
as we wanted to show, thus proving (4).
(\Rightarrow)
.
∎

Definition 7.4
.

Given a system
E
of local epimorphisms on
\mathbf{Pre}(\mathcal{C})
, let
j(E)
denote the collection of families on
\mathcal{C}
where for every
U\in\mathcal{C}
, a family
r\in j(E)(U)
if and only if
\overline{r}\hookrightarrow y(U)
is an
E
-local epimorphism.

Lemma 7.5
.

Given a system
E
of local epimorphims on
\mathbf{Pre}(\mathcal{C})
, the collection
j(E)
of families on
\mathcal{C}
is a saturated coverage.

Proof.

Let us show
j(E)
is a coverage. Suppose that
r\in j(E)(U)
for some
U\in\mathcal{C}
, and
g:V\to U
is a morphism in
\mathcal{C}
. Then
g^{*}(\overline{r})\in j(E)(V)
, since
E
is a system of local epimorphisms. But
g^{*}(\overline{r})=\overline{g^{*}(r)}
, so
g^{*}(r)\in j(E)(V)
and
g_{*}g^{*}(r)\subseteq r
. Thus
j(E)
is a coverage.

Clearly
j(E)
contains all identity families, so let us show that
j(E)
is refinement closed. Suppose that
r\in j(E)(U)
, so that
\overline{r}\hookrightarrow y(U)
is an
E
-local epimorphism, and there exists a refinement
r\leq t
. We want to show that
\overline{t}\hookrightarrow y(U)
is an
E
-local epi. Now
\overline{r}\subseteq\overline{t}
, so we have the following commmutative diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\overline{t}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\overline{r}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{y(U)}}

and thus
\overline{t}\hookrightarrow y(U)
is an
E
-local epi by Definition
7.1
.(3).

Now let us show that
j(E)
is composition closed. Let
r=\{r_{i}:U_{i}\to U\}_{i\in I}\in j(E)(U)
and for each
i\in I
, suppose that
t^{i}\in j(E)(U_{i})
. We want to show that
(r\circ t)=\bigcupop\displaylimits_{i}(r_{i})_{*}(t^{i})\in j(E)(U)
. First let us prove that
r:\sumop\displaylimits_{i}y(U_{i})\to y(U)
is an
E
-local epimorphism. By Proposition
3.6
we have the following commutative diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\sumop\displaylimits_{i,j}y(U_{i})\times_{y(U)}y(U_{j})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\sumop\displaylimits_{i}y(U_{i})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\overline{r}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{y(U)}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces r}

where
\pi
is a coequalizer, and hence an epimorphism. Thus by Defintion
7.1
.(1),
\pi
is an
E
-local epi, and
\overline{r}\hookrightarrow y(U)
is an
E
-local epi by assumption. Thus by Definition
7.1
.(2),
r
is an
E
-local epi. Now note that the following commutative diagram is a pullback

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\overline{t^{i}}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\bigcupop\displaylimits_{i}\overline{t^{i}}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{y(U_{i})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\sumop\displaylimits_{i}y(U_{i})}}

Thus if
s:y(V)\to\sumop\displaylimits_{i}y(U_{i})
is a section, then by the Yoneda lemma,
s
factors through some
y(U_{i})
and taking the pullback we obtain the following pair of pullback diagrams

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{s^{*}(\overline{t^{i}})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\overline{t^{i}}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\bigcupop\displaylimits_{i}\overline{t^{i}}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{y(V)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{y(U_{i})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\sumop\displaylimits_{i}y(U_{i})}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lrcorner}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lrcorner}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces s}

and since
\overline{t^{i}}\hookrightarrow y(U_{i})
is an
E
-local epi by assumption,
s^{*}(\overline{t^{i}})\hookrightarrow y(V)
is an
E
-local epi. But the outer rectangle is also a pullback, and since
s
was arbitrary, this implies that
\bigcupop\displaylimits_{i}\overline{t^{i}}\hookrightarrow\sumop\displaylimits_{i}y(U_{i})
is an
E
-local epi.

Now
\overline{(r\circ t)}=\bigcupop\displaylimits_{i}(r_{i})_{*}(\overline{t^{i}})
is precisely the image of the map
\bigcupop\displaylimits_{i}\overline{t^{i}}\to\sumop\displaylimits_{i}y(U_{i})\to y(U)
, so we have the following commutative diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\bigcupop\displaylimits_{i}\overline{t^{i}}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\sumop\displaylimits_{i}y(U_{i})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\overline{(r\circ t)}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{y(U)}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces r}

But then the composite map
\bigcupop\displaylimits_{i}\overline{t^{i}}\to y(U)
is an
E
-local epi, and thus by (3),
\overline{(r\circ t)}\hookrightarrow y(U)
is an
E
-local epi. Thus
j(E)
is composition closed, and therefore saturated.
∎

Proposition 7.6
.

Given an (essentially small) category
\mathcal{C}
, there is a bijection

\{\text{saturated coverages on }\mathcal{C}\}\cong\{\text{systems of local epis on }\mathbf{Pre}(\mathcal{C})\}.

Proof.

In Lemma
7.3
, we gave a map

\varphi:\{\text{coverages on }\mathcal{C}\}\to\{\text{systems of local epis on }\mathbf{Pre}(\mathcal{C})\},

where
\varphi(j)
is the system of local epimorphisms given by the class of
j
-local epimorphisms.
Conversely, in Lemma
7.5
, we gave a map

\psi:\{\text{systems of local epis on }\mathbf{Pre}(\mathcal{C})\}\to\{\text{saturated coverages on }\mathcal{C}\},

where
\psi(E)
is the coverage
j(E)
. It is easy to see that if
j
is a coverage, then
\psi\varphi(j)
is precisely
\text{sat}(j)
. We claim that if
E
is a system of local epimorphisms, then
\varphi\psi(E)=E
. So suppose that
f:X\to Y
belongs to
\varphi\psi(E)
. Thus
f
is a
j(E)
-local epimorphism. Since
j(E)
is saturated, by Lemma
5.2
, this implies that for every section
s:y(U)\to Y
there exists a
j(E)
-covering family
r
on
U
such that
s_{*}(r)\leq f
, and furthermore
s_{*}(\overline{r})\leq f
. Now
r
being a
j(E)
-covering family means that
\overline{r}\hookrightarrow y(U)
belongs to
E
. Now
s_{*}(\overline{r})\leq f
implies that
\overline{r}\leq s^{*}(f)
. But this means that
\overline{r}\hookrightarrow y(U)
factors through
s^{*}(f)
, and hence by Definition
7.1
.(3),
s^{*}(f)\in E
. Since this is true for every section
s
, this implies that
f\in E
. Conversely if
f:X\to Y
is in
E
, then we want to show that for every
s:y(U)\to Y
, there exists a
j(E)
-covering family
r
such that
s_{*}(f)\leq f
. But for every such
s:y(U)\to Y
, we know that
s^{*}(f)
belongs to
E
. Taking the image of
s^{*}(f)
we obtain a monomorphism
\text{im}(s^{*}(f))\hookrightarrow y(U)
, and by Definition
7.1
.(3), this belongs to
E
. Thus
r=\bigcupop\displaylimits_{V\in\mathcal{C}}\text{im}(s^{*}(f))(V)
is a
j(E)
-covering family. If
r_{i}:y(U_{i})\to y(U)
belongs to
\text{im}(s^{*}(f))(U_{i})
, then by definition it factors through
s^{*}(f)
, and therefore
s_{*}(r_{i})\leq f
. Therefore
s_{*}(r)\leq f
, so
f
is a
j(E)
-local epimorphism. Thus
\varphi\psi(E)=E
. Therefore if we restrict
\varphi
to saturated coverages, then we obtain a bijection.
∎

7.2.
Systems of Local Isomorphisms

Definition 7.7
.

Let
W
be a class of morphisms in a category
\mathcal{C}
. We say that
W
satisfies the
2-of-3 property
if given any pair of composeable morphisms
f:X\to Y
and
g:Y\to Z
if any two of the morphisms in the set
\{f,g,gf\}
belongs to
W
, then all three of them belong to
W
.

Definition 7.8
.

Let
\mathbf{Pre}(\mathcal{C})
be a presheaf topos, and let
W
be a class of morphisms of presheaves. We say that
W
is a
system of local isomorphisms
if

(1)

every isomorphism belongs to
W
,

(2)

W
satisfies the 2-of-3 property,

(3)

a map
f:X\to Y
belongs to
W
if and only if for every section
s:y(U)\to Y
, the pullback morphism
s^{*}(f):y(U)\times_{Y}X\to y(U)
is in
W
.

If
W
is a system of local isomorphisms,
f:X\to Y
is in
W
we say that
f\in W
or
f
is a
W
-local isomorphism or
f
is a
W
-local iso, interchangeably.

Lemma 7.9
.

The axiom (3) of Definition
7.8
is equivalent to the following (3’): a map
f:X\to Y
of presheaves is in
W
if and only if for every arbitrary map
g:Z\to Y
, the pullback map
g^{*}(f):Z\times_{Y}X\to Z
is in
W
.

Proof.

(3^{\prime}\Rightarrow 3)
If
f:X\to Y
is in
W
, then for every section
s:y(U)\to Y
, the pullback morphism
s^{*}(f)
is in
W
. Now conversely suppose that every
s^{*}(f)
is in
W
. We want to show that
f
is in
W
. Let
g:Z\to Y
be a map of presheaves, and suppose that
s:y(U)\to Z
is a section. Consider the following diagram of pullback squares

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{y(U)\times_{Z}(Z\times_{Y}X)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{Z\times_{Y}X}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{y(U)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Z}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces s^{*}(g^{*}(f))}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lrcorner}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g^{*}(f)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lrcorner}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces s}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g}

since both squares are pullbacks, the outer square is a pullback. Thus by assumption
s^{*}(g^{*}(f))
is in
W
. But since
s
was arbitrary, this proves that
g^{*}(f)
is in
W
. But since
g
was arbitrary, this proves that
f
is in
W
.

(3\Rightarrow 3^{\prime})
If
f
is in
W
, then by a similar argument as above, for every map
g:Z\to Y
,
g^{*}(f)
is in
W
. Conversely if every
g^{*}(f)
is in
W
, then taking
g
to be the identity shows that
f
is in
W
.
∎

Definition 7.10
.

Suppose that
W
is a system of local isomorphisms. We say that a map
f:X\to Y
is a
W
-local epimorphism if its image
\text{im}(f)\hookrightarrow Y
is a
W
-local isomorphism. We say that
f
is a
W
-local mono if
\Delta_{f}:X\to X\times_{Y}X
is a
W
-local epi. Note that a sieve
R\hookrightarrow y(U)
is a
W
-local isomorphism if and only if it is a
W
-local epimorphism.

Lemma 7.11
.

Suppose that
W
is a system of local isomorphisms on a presheaf topos
\mathbf{Pre}(\mathcal{C})
,
R\hookrightarrow y(U)
is a
W
-local iso, and
S\hookrightarrow y(U)
is a sieve, then

(I)

if
R\subseteq S
, then
S\hookrightarrow y(U)
is a
W
-local iso, and

(II)

if for every
f:V\to U
in
R
, the map
f^{*}(S)\hookrightarrow y(V)
is a
W
-local iso, then
S\hookrightarrow y(U)
is a
W
-local iso.

Proof.

(I) Suppose that
R\hookrightarrow y(U)
is a
W
-local isomorphism,
S\hookrightarrow y(U)
is a sieve, and
R\subseteq S
. This implies that
R\cap S=R
, and therefore using Lemma
A.38
, the following commutative diagram is a pullback square

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces R}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces R}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces S}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{y(U)}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lrcorner}

Thus by Definition
7.8
.(3’),
R\hookrightarrow S
is a
W
-local isomorphism, and by Definition
7.8
.(2), this implies
S\hookrightarrow y(U)
is a
W
-local isomorphism.

(II) Suppose that
R\in J(W)(U)
,
S\hookrightarrow y(U)
, and for every
f:V\to U
in
R
, the pullback
f^{*}(S)\hookrightarrow y(V)
is a
W
-local iso, then we want to show that
S\hookrightarrow y(U)
is a
W
-local iso. Suppose that
f^{*}(S)\hookrightarrow y(V)
is a
W
-local iso for every
f:V\to U
in
R
. Then for every
g:y(V)\to R
, we have a commutative diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{g^{*}(S)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{R\cap S}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces S}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{y(V)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces R}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{y(U)}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lrcorner}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g}

where the right hand square is a pullback and the outer rectangle is a pullback. Thus the left hand square is a pullback. By assumption
g^{*}(S)\hookrightarrow y(V)
is a
W
-local isomorphism, and since
g:y(V)\to R
was arbitrary, this implies that
R\cap S\hookrightarrow R
is a
W
-local isomorphism. Thus
R\cap S\hookrightarrow y(U)
is a
W
-local isomorphism, which implies that
S\hookrightarrow y(U)
is a
W
-local isomorphism by (I).
∎

Remark 7.12
.

Lemma
6.22
implies that if
W
is a system of local isomorphisms, then the collection of sieve inclusions
\{R\hookrightarrow y(U)\}
that are
W
-local isomorphisms forms a Grothendieck coverage.

Lemma 7.13

(
[
KS05
, Exercise 16.5]
)
.

If
W
is a system of local isomorphisms and
E
is the class of
W
-local epis, then
E
is a system of local epimorphisms.

Proof.

Suppose that
f:X\to Y
is an epimorphism. Then
\text{im}(f)\hookrightarrow Y
is an isomorphism and therefore a
W
-local isomorphism.

Suppose that
f:X\to Y
and
g:X\to Y
are morphisms such that
gf
is a
W
-local epimorphism. We want to show that
g
is a
W
-local epi. First note that the following diagram is a pullback

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\text{im}(gf)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\text{im}(gf)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\text{im}(g)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Z}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces i}

where
i
is the obvious inclusion map.
Now let us show that
i
is a
W
-local isomorphism. Suppose that
s:y(U)\to\text{im}(g)
is a section. Then we obtain the following pair of pullback diagrams

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{y(U)\times_{\text{im}(g)}\text{im}(gf)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\text{im}(gf)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\text{im}(gf)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{y(U)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\text{im}(g)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Z}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces k}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lrcorner}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces i}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lrcorner}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces s}

so the outer rectangle is a pullback. Since
\text{im}(gf)\hookrightarrow Z
is a local isomorphism by assumption, this implies that
k
is a local isomorphism. Since
s
was arbitrary, this implies that
g_{*}
is a
W
-local isomorphism. Thus by the 2-of-3 property of
W
-local isomorphisms,
\text{im}(g)\hookrightarrow Z
is a
W
-local isomorphism, and therefore
g
is a
W
-local epi.

Now let
f:X\to Y
be a
W
-local epimorphism and
s:y(U)\to Y
. Then since images of presheaf maps are stable under pullback we have the following pair of pullback diagrams

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{y(U)\times_{Y}X}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\text{im}(s^{*}(f))}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\text{im}(f)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{y(U)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lrcorner}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lrcorner}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces s}

Thus
\text{im}(s^{*}(f))\hookrightarrow y(U)
is a
W
-local isomorphism, so
s^{*}(f)
is a
W
-local epimorphism.

Conversely suppose that for every
s:y(U)\to Y
, the pullback map
s^{*}(f)
is a
W
-local epimorphism. We want to show that
f
is a
W
-local epimorphism. But if we pull back
f
by
s
, then the image of
s^{*}(f)
is a
W
-local isomorphism by assumption. Thus
\text{im}(f)\hookrightarrow y(U)
is a
W
-local isomorphism. Therefore
f
is a
W
-local epimorphism.

Then taking
g
to be the identity shows that
f
is a
W
-local epi. Conversely if
f
is a
W
-local epi, we want to show every pullback map is. This follows from the fact that
\mathbf{Pre}(\mathcal{C})
has pullback stable image factorizations (Lemma
A.79
). Namely taking the pullback of
\text{im}(f)\hookrightarrow Y
along any map
g:Z\to Y
gives
\text{im}(g^{*}(f))\hookrightarrow Z
, which is a
W
-local isomorphism, since
W
-local isomorphisms are stable under pullback, thus
g^{*}(f)
is a
W
-local epi.

If
f:X\to Y
and
g:Y\to Z
are
W
-local epis, then we want to show that
gf
is a
W
-local epi
8
8

8

This part of the proof is due to
[
Dou
]
.
. Let us first suppose that
g
is of the form
g:Y\to y(U)
. Suppose that
s:y(V)\to\text{im}(g)
is a section, then
s
factors through
g
, giving a map
\widetilde{s}:y(V)\to Y
such that
g\widetilde{s}=s
. Now taking the pullback
\widetilde{s}^{*}(f)
, we have the commutative diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{y(V)\times_{Y}X}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{y(V)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{y(V)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\text{im}(g)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{y(U)}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\widetilde{s}^{*}(f)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lrcorner}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\widetilde{s}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces s}

Now
\widetilde{s}^{*}(f)
is a
W
-local epimorphism because
f
is. Thus
\text{im}(\widetilde{s}^{*}(f))\hookrightarrow y(V)
is a
W
-local isomorphism. But
\text{im}(\widetilde{s}^{*}(f))\subseteq s^{*}(\text{im}(gf))
. Indeed, if
h:W\to V
is in the image of
\widetilde{s}^{*}(f)
, then there exists an
x\in X(W)
such that
\widetilde{s}(h)=f(x)
. But then
s(h)=g\widetilde{s}(h)=gf(x)
. Thus by Lemma
7.11
.(I), this implies that
s^{*}(\text{im}(gf))\hookrightarrow y(V)
is a
W
-local isomorphism. Since this is true for every
s\in\text{im}(g)
, by Lemma
7.11
.(II), this implies that
\text{im}(gf)\hookrightarrow y(U)
is a
W
-local isomorphism.

Now suppose that
f:X\to Y
and
g:Y\to Z
are arbitrary
W
-local epimorphisms. Then for any section
s:y(U)\to Z
, we have the following pair of pullback diagrams

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{y(U)\times_{Z}X}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{y(U)\times_{Z}Y}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{y(U)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Z}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lrcorner}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lrcorner}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces s}

and thus as proven above, since
W
-local epimorphisms are stable under pullback, this implies that the two left vertical maps are
W
-local epimorphisms. By the argument of the preceding section, this implies their composite is a
W
-local epimorphism. Since
s:y(U)\to Z
was an arbitrary section, and the outer rectangle is a pullback, this implies that
gf
is a
W
-local epimorphism.
∎

Lemma 7.14
.

Given a system
E
of local epimorphisms, let
W
denote the class of
E
-local isomorphisms. Then
W
is a system of local isomorphisms.

Proof.

(1) This clearly holds because if
f:X\to Y
is an isomorphism, then it is an
E
-local isomorphism.

(2) Since
E
is the class of
j
-local epimorphisms for some saturated coverage
j
, this follows from Lemma
5.15
.(6).

(3’)
(\Rightarrow)
holds by Lemma
5.15
.(2) and (3’)
(\Leftarrow)
is obvious.
∎

Proposition 7.15
.

Given a small category
\mathcal{C}
, there is a bijection

\{\text{systems of local epimorphisms on }\mathbf{Pre}(\mathcal{C})\}\cong\{\text{systems of local isomorphisms on }\mathbf{Pre}(\mathcal{C})\}.

Proof.

By Lemma
7.14
, we have a function (between large sets)

\sigma:\{\text{systems of local epis on }\mathbf{Pre}(\mathcal{C})\}\to\{\text{systems of local isos on }\mathbf{Pre}(\mathcal{C})\}

where if
E
is a system of local epis, then
\sigma(E)
is the class of
E
-local isomorphisms. Namely
\sigma(E)
is the class of morphisms that are both
E
-local epis and
E
-local monos. Conversely, by Lemma
7.13
, we have a function

\tau:\{\text{systems of local isos on }\mathbf{Pre}(\mathcal{C})\}\to\{\text{systems of local epis on }\mathbf{Pre}(\mathcal{C})\},

where if
W
is a system of local isos, then
\tau(W)
is the class of
W
-local epis. Namely
\tau(W)
is the class of morphisms
f:X\to Y
such that
\text{im}(f)\hookrightarrow Y
is a
W
-local iso. Let us show that these functions are inverse to each other.

First let us show that if
W
is a system of local isos, and we define
E=\tau(W)
to be the class of
W
-local epis, then if
f:X\to Y
is in
W
, then
f
is in
E
. First note that the following is a pullback

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\text{im}(f)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lrcorner}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}

so that if
s:y(U)\to\text{im}(f)
is a section, then taking the pullback, we obtain pasting diagram of pullbacks

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{y(U)\times_{Y}X}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{y(U)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\text{im}(f)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces k}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lrcorner}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lrcorner}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces s}

but the outer rectangle is a pullback, so
k
is in
W
. Since this is true for arbitrary
s:y(U)\to\text{im}(f)
, this implies that
X\twoheadrightarrow\text{im}(f)
is in
W
. Thus by 2-of-3,
\text{im}(f)\hookrightarrow y(U)
is in
W
. Therefore
f\in E
.

Now we want to show that if
W
is a system of local isos,
E=\tau(W)
is the class of
W
-local epis, and we let
W^{\prime}=\sigma(E)
be the class of
E
-local isos, then we want to show that
W=\sigma\tau(W)=W^{\prime}
.

First let us show that
W\subseteq W^{\prime}
. We know that if
f\in W
, then
f\in E
. Thus we need only to show that
\Delta_{f}\in E
. We have the following commutative diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{X\times_{Y}X}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Delta_{f}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 1_{X}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 1_{X}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p_{0}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lrcorner}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}

since
f
is a
W
-local iso, then so are
p_{0}
and
p_{1}
. But then by
2
-of-
3
, since
1_{X}
is also a
W
-local iso, this implies that
\Delta_{f}
is a
W
-local iso. Therefore by the above argument since
W\subseteq E
, this implies that
\Delta_{f}\in E
. Therefore
W\subseteq W^{\prime}
.

Conversely let us show that
W^{\prime}\subseteq W
. If
f:X\to Y
in
W^{\prime}
, then it is a
W
-local epi and a
W
-local mono. Thus
\text{im}(f)\hookrightarrow Y
is in
W
. Now note that the following diagram is a pullback

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{X\times_{Y}X}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\text{im}(f)}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p_{0}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lrcorner}

Now note that
\Delta_{f}:X\to X\times_{Y}X
is a monomorphism, which implies that it is its own image, and since
\Delta_{f}
is a
W
-local epi, this implies that
\Delta_{f}\in W
. Now since
p_{0}\Delta_{f}=1_{X}
, by 2-of-3, this implies that
p_{0}
(and
p_{1}
) are in
W
. Now if
s:y(U)\to\text{im}(f)
was an arbitrary section, then it factors through
X
, and taking the pullback we obtain a pasting diagram of pullbacks

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{y(U)\times_{\text{im}(f)}X}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{X\times_{Y}X}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{y(U)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\text{im}(f)}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces k}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lrcorner}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p_{0}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lrcorner}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces s}

thus
k
is in
W
. But since this was true for arbitrary
s:y(U)\to\text{im}(f)
, this implies that
X\twoheadrightarrow\text{im}(f)
is in
W
. Since
\text{im}(f)\hookrightarrow Y
is also in
W
since
f
is a
W
-local epi, this implies that
f\in W
. Thus
W^{\prime}\subseteq W
. Therefore we have shown that
W=\sigma\tau(W)=W^{\prime}
.

Now suppose that
E
is a system of local epis, let
W=\sigma(E)
and
E^{\prime}=\tau(W)
. We want to show that
E=\tau\sigma(E)=E^{\prime}
. Let us show that
E\subseteq E^{\prime}
. Morphisms in
E^{\prime}
are precisely the maps
f:X\to Y
such that
\text{im}(f)\hookrightarrow Y
belong to
W
, i.e. are in
E
and are
E
-local monos. Thus if
f:X\to Y
is in
E
, so is
\text{im}(f)\hookrightarrow Y
. Thus we need only show it is an
E
-local mono. But it is a monomorphism, thus an
E
-local mono. Therefore
f\in E^{\prime}
.

Conversely let us show that
E^{\prime}\subseteq E
. If
\text{im}(f)\hookrightarrow Y
belongs to
W
, then it belongs to
E
, but so does
X\twoheadrightarrow\text{im}(f)
, since it is an epi, therefore
f\in E
. Thus
E^{\prime}=\sigma\tau(E)=E
. Thus
\sigma
and
\tau
are inverses to each other and define a bijection.
∎

Thus we have obtained the following bijections

(15)

\{\text{system of local isos}\}\underset{\sigma}{\overset{\tau}{\rightleftarrows}}\{\text{system of local epis}\}\underset{\varphi}{\overset{\psi}{\rightleftarrows}}\{\text{saturated coverages}\}

The compositions are simple to interpret as well. If
j
is a saturated coverage, then
\sigma\varphi(j)
is the class of
j
-local isomorphisms. If
W
is a system of local isomorphisms, then
\psi\tau(W)
is the coverage where
r\in(\psi\tau(W))(U)
if and only if
\overline{r}\hookrightarrow y(U)
is in
W
.

7.3.
Lex Reflective Localizations of Presheaf Topoi

In this section we introduce left exact (lex) reflective localizations of presheaf topoi. These will turn out to correspond precisely to Grothendieck topoi. The theory of localization is a vital tool with which to study Grothendieck topoi. We collect the basics of this theory in Appendix
B
, and encourage the reader to head there first before returning to this section.

Definition 7.16
.

Let
\mathbf{Pre}(\mathcal{C})
be a presheaf topos. A
lex localization
of
\mathbf{Pre}(\mathcal{C})
consists of a reflective subcategory (Definition
B.13
)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{E}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathbf{Pre}(\mathcal{C})}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces i}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces L}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\dashv}

such that the reflector
L
preserves finite limits. We call
\mathcal{E}
a
lex reflective subcategory
of
\mathbf{Pre}(\mathcal{C})
.

Lemma 7.17
.

Let
\mathcal{E}\hookrightarrow\mathbf{Pre}(\mathcal{C})
be a lex reflective subcategory with reflector
L
and let
W=L^{-1}(\text{iso})
. Then
W
is a system of local isomorphisms.

Proof.

Clearly (1) and (2) of Definition
7.8
hold. Let us prove (3’). Suppose that
f:X\hookrightarrow Y
is in
W
. We want to show that
g^{*}(f)
is in
W
for every
g:Z\to Y
. Since
L
is lex,
L(g^{*}(f))=L(g)^{*}(L(f))
. Since isomorphisms are stable under pullback, this implies that
L(g)^{*}(L(f))
is an isomorphism, so
g^{*}(f)
is in
W
. Conversely suppose that
g^{*}(f)
is in
W
for every
g:Z\to Y
. Then taking
g
to be the identity shows that
f
is in
W
.
∎

Thus by (
15
), to every lex reflective subcategory
\mathcal{E}\hookrightarrow\mathbf{Pre}(\mathcal{C})
with reflector
L
, we can associate the saturated coverage
j(L)\coloneqq\psi\tau(L^{-1}(\text{iso}))
. Call this the
coverage induced by
L
.

Theorem 7.18

(The Little Giraud Theorem
9
9

9

We got this name from
[
Joh02
, Corollary C.2.1.11]
. For the actual Giraud Theorem, see Section
11
.
)
.

Given a lex reflective subcategory
\mathcal{E}\hookrightarrow\mathbf{Pre}(\mathcal{C})
, with reflector
L
, there is an equivalence of categories

(16)

\mathcal{E}\simeq\mathbf{Sh}(\mathcal{C},j(L)).

Proof.

By Proposition
B.15
, we know that
\mathcal{E}
is equivalent to the full subcategory of
L^{-1}(\text{iso})
-local presheaves. Let
W=L^{-1}(\text{iso})
, and suppose that
X
is a
W
-local presheaf. Then if
r\in j(L)(U)
is a covering family, then
\overline{r}\hookrightarrow y(U)
is in
W
. Thus the function

\mathbf{Pre}(\mathcal{C})(y(U),X)\to\mathbf{Pre}(\mathcal{C})(\overline{r},X)

is a bijection. But this implies that
X
is sheaf on
\overline{j(L)}
, equivalently a sheaf on
j(L)
.

Now suppose that
Z
is a
j(L)
-sheaf. Then by Proposition
6.42
and Corollary
6.43
,
Z
is local with respect to the
j(L)
-local isomorphisms. But the class of
j(L)
-local isomorphisms is precisely the class
W
. Thus
Z
is
W
-local. Therefore the full subcategory of
W
-local presheaves is precisely the full subcategory of
j(L)
-sheaves.
∎

To summarize, we have obtained the following commutative diagram

(17)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\{\text{saturated coverages on }\mathcal{C}\}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\{\text{systems of local epis on }\mathbf{Pre}(\mathcal{C})\}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\{\text{systems of local isos on }\mathbf{Pre}(\mathcal{C})\}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\{\text{Grothendieck coverages on }\mathcal{C}\}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\{\text{lex reflective subcategories of }\mathbf{Pre}(\mathcal{C})\}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varphi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\overline{(-)}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\psi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sigma}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\tau}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(-)^{\circ}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces W}

where
W
is the map that takes a lex reflective subcategory
\mathcal{E}\hookrightarrow\mathbf{Pre}(\mathcal{C})
with reflector
L
and gives the class
W=L^{-1}(\text{iso})
. We wish to fill in this diagram with the dashed arrow. This will be the content of the next section.

7.4.
Sheafification

Given a site
(\mathcal{C},j)
and
U\in\mathcal{C}
, let
\text{sat}(j)(U)
denote the category whose objects are
j
-saturating families and whose morphisms are refinements.

Definition 7.19
.

Given a site
(\mathcal{C},j)
and a presheaf
X
on
\mathcal{C}
, let us define a new presheaf
X^{+}
on
\mathcal{C}
objectwise as follows. If
U\in\mathcal{C}
, then let

(18)

X^{+}(U)=\underset{r\in\text{sat}(j)(U)^{\text{op}}}{\text{colim}}\text{Match}(r,X),

This construction defines a functor
(-)^{+}:\mathbf{Pre}(\mathcal{C})\to\mathbf{Pre}(\mathcal{C})
, which we call the
plus construction
.

The colimit in (
18
) is over a filtered category by Lemma
6.40
, but it can often be more convenient to work with a filtered poset, i.e. a directed set. Let
\text{Gro}(j)(U)
denote the poset of
j
-covering families ordered by the subset relation. There is a map

\phi:\underset{R\in\text{Gro}(j)(U)^{\text{op}}}{\text{colim}}\,\mathbf{Pre}(\mathcal{C})(R,X)\to\underset{r\in\text{sat}(j)(U)^{\text{op}}}{\text{colim}}\,\text{Match}(r,X)

which considers an equivalence class of a matching family
m:R\to X
as an equivalence class of matching families by refinement. This map is well-defined, since two matching families
m:R\to X
and
n:T\to X
are identified in the left-hand set (by Lemma
C.13
) if there exists a covering sieve
S\subseteq R\cap T
such that
m|_{R\cap T}=n|_{R\cap T}
, and this implies that there is a common refinement, so
\phi([m])=\phi([n])
. Now it is surjective, as a
X
-matching family
\{x_{i}\}
on a
j
-saturating family
r
can be extended (non-uniquely) to a matching family
x:\overline{r}\to X
by choosing for each
f\in\overline{r}
a factorization

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces V}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{U_{i}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces U}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces s}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces r_{i}}

and setting
x_{f}=X(s)(x_{i})
. Then we have a refinement
\overline{r}\leq r
given by the factorizations given above which pulls
\{x_{i}\}
back to
x
and hence identifies them in the quotient. It is also injective. For assume that we have matching families
m:R\to X
and
n:S\to X
, and a common refinement
f:t\to R
and
g:t\to S
such that
f^{*}(m)=g^{*}(n)
. Then
\overline{t}\subseteq R
and
\overline{t}\subseteq S
, so
m
and
n
are identified in the domain. Thus we proven the following result.

Corollary 7.20
.

Given a site
(\mathcal{C},j)
and a presheaf
X
, then for every
U\in\mathcal{C}
, we have

X^{+}(U)\cong\underset{R\in\text{Gro}(j)(U)^{\text{op}}}{\text{colim}}\mathbf{Pre}(\mathcal{C})(R,X).

Remark 7.21
.

We also note that the above result holds just as well if we took the colimit over just
j
-trees rather than
j
-saturating families.

We can thus describe
X^{+}(U)
explicitly as follows. It is the set of all
X
-matching families over some
R
, where
R
is a covering sieve on
U
, modulo the following equivalence relation: we identify two matching families
R\xrightarrow{m}X\xleftarrow{n}R^{\prime}
if there exists a covering sieve
R^{\prime\prime}\subseteq R\cap R^{\prime}
such that the following diagram commutes:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces R}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{R^{\prime\prime}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{R^{\prime}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces m}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces n}

We denote the image of a matching family
m:R\to X
in
X^{+}(U)
by
[m]
.

If
f:V\to U
is a morphism in
\mathcal{C}
, then
X^{+}(f):X^{+}(U)\to X^{+}(V)
is defined by

X^{+}(f)([R\xrightarrow{m}X])=[f^{*}R\to R\xrightarrow{m}X].

To fully define
(-)^{+}
as a functor we must also define how it acts on presheaf maps
g:X\to Y
. This is simply defined in components as
g^{+}([R\xrightarrow{m}X])=[R\xrightarrow{m}X\xrightarrow{g}Y]
.

Remark 7.22
.

We will use the form of the plus construction given in Corollary
7.20
for the rest of this section, as it will significantly reduce the complications of the proofs. In fact, we will assume we are working over a Grothendieck site
(\mathcal{C},J)
for the rest of this section, in which case we have

X^{+}(U)\cong\underset{R\in J(U)^{\text{op}}}{\text{colim}}\mathbf{Pre}(\mathcal{C})(R,X).

Lemma 7.23
.

Suppose that
X
is a presheaf, and suppose that we have two
X
-matching families
m:R\to X
and
n:R^{\prime}\to X
on sieves that fit into the following commutative diagram:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{R^{\prime}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces R}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces n}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces m}

in which case we call
n
a
sub-matching family
of
m
. If a section
x:y(U)\to X
is an amalgamation for
m
, then it is also an amalgamation for
n
.

Proof.

If
x
is an amalgamation for
m
, then the following diagram commutes

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{R^{\prime}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces R}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{y(U)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces n}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces m}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces x}

thus
x
is an amalgamation for
n
.
∎

Definition 7.24
.

Given a Grothendieck site
(\mathcal{C},J)
, define the natural transformation
\eta:1_{\mathbf{Pre}(C)}\Rightarrow(-)^{+}
componentwise as follows. If
x:y(U)\to X
is a section, then set
\eta_{X}(x)=[y(U)\xrightarrow{x}X]
. Call
\eta
the
unit map
of the plus construction.

Lemma 7.25
.

If
X
is a sheaf on a Grothendieck site
(\mathcal{C},J)
, then
\eta_{X}:X\to X^{+}
is an isomorphism.

Proof.

Let us construct an inverse to
\eta
in components. If
U\in\mathcal{C}
, then we want to define

\eta_{U}^{-1}:X^{+}(U)\to X(U)
. If
[R\xrightarrow{m}X]\in X^{+}(U)
, then since
X
is a sheaf, there is a unique amalgamation
y(U)\xrightarrow{x}X
of
m
, so define
\eta_{U}^{-1}([R\to X])=x
to be that unique amalgamation. Let us show this is well defined. Suppose that
[R\xrightarrow{m}X]=[R^{\prime}\xrightarrow{n}X]
, so that we have a commutative diagram:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{R^{\prime\prime}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{R^{\prime}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces R}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces m}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces n}

then since
X
is a sheaf,
m
and
n
amalgamate to a unique
x
and
y
respectively. By Lemma
7.23
, this implies that
x
and
y
are also amalgamations for
R^{\prime\prime}
. But
R^{\prime\prime}
is a covering sieve and
X
is a sheaf, so
x=y
. Thus
\eta_{U}^{-1}
is well defined and it is easy to see that it defines an inverse map to
\eta
.
∎

Lemma 7.26
.

Given a Grothendieck site
(\mathcal{C},J)
, and a map
f:X\to Y
of presheaves on
\mathcal{C}
, if
Y
is a sheaf, then
f
factors uniquely:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{X^{+}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\eta_{X}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\exists!\widetilde{f}}

Proof.

Since
Y
is a sheaf, the map
\eta_{Y}
in the naturality square

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{X^{+}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{Y^{+}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\eta_{X}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f^{+}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\eta_{Y}}

is an isomorphism by Lemma
7.25
. So let us define
\widetilde{f}=\eta_{Y}^{-1}f^{+}
. Now let us show that
\widetilde{f}
is unique. Suppose that
\widehat{f}:X^{+}\to Y
is a map of presheaves such that
\widehat{f}\eta_{X}=f
. Then given a map
g:U\to V
in
\mathcal{C}
, we have the following commutative diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{X^{+}(U)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{X^{+}(V)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{Y(U)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{Y(V)}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y(g)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\widehat{f}_{V}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{X^{+}(g)}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\widehat{f}_{U}}

So if
[R\xrightarrow{m}X]\in X^{+}(U)
for some covering sieve
R\in J(U)
, and
g:V\to U
is any morphism, then

\left(Y(g)\circ\widehat{f}_{U}\right)\left([R\xrightarrow{m}X]\right)=\left(\widehat{f}_{V}\circ X^{+}(g)\right)\left([R\xrightarrow{m}X]\right)=\widehat{f}_{V}\left([g^{*}R\to R\xrightarrow{m}X]\right).

Now if
g\in R
, then by Lemma
3.12
,
g^{*}R=y(V)
. Thus for
g\in R
,
