\left(Y(g)\circ\widehat{f}_{U}\right)\left([R\xrightarrow{m}X]\right)=\widehat{f}_{V}\left([y(V)\xrightarrow{g}R\xrightarrow{m}X]\right).

Thus the composite
y(V)\xrightarrow{g}R\xrightarrow{m}X
is precisely the element
m_{g}\in X(V)
of the matching family
m:R\to X
.

Therefore we have

\widehat{f}_{V}\left([m_{g}]\right)=\widehat{f}_{V}\eta_{X}(m_{g})=f(m_{g}).

Now let
\widehat{f}_{U}([R\xrightarrow{m}X])=y
, so that
Y(g)(y)=f(m_{g})
. In other words, we have shown that
y
is an amalgamation for the
Y
-matching family
\{f(m_{g})\}
. But

\widetilde{f}_{U}([R\xrightarrow{m}X])=\eta^{-1}_{Y}f^{+}_{U}([R\xrightarrow{m}X])=\eta^{-1}_{Y}([R\xrightarrow{m}X\xrightarrow{f}Y]).

Thus
\widetilde{f}_{U}([R\xrightarrow{m}X])
is the unique amalgamation of the
Y
-matching family
\{f(m_{g})\}
. But as shown above,
y
is such an amalgamation. Thus
\widehat{f}_{U}([R\xrightarrow{m}X])=\widetilde{f}_{U}([R\xrightarrow{m}X])
.
∎

Proposition 7.27
.

Given a presheaf
X
on a Grothendieck site
(\mathcal{C},J)
, the presheaf
X^{+}
is separated (Definition
2.18
).

Proof.

We wish to show that for every
R\in J(U)
the restriction map

\text{res}_{R,X^{+}}:X^{+}(U)\to\mathbf{Pre}(\mathcal{C})(R,X^{+})

is injective. The map
\text{res}_{R,X^{+}}
takes an element
[R\xrightarrow{m}X]
to the collection of matching families
\{[g^{*}(R)\to R\xrightarrow{m}X]\}_{g\in R}
, where
g:V\to U
is a morphism in
R
and
[g^{*}(R)\to R\xrightarrow{m}X]\in X^{+}(V)
. So suppose that
m,n\in X^{+}(U)
such that
\text{res}_{R,X^{+}}(m)=\text{res}_{R,X^{+}}(n)
. This means that for every
g:V\to U
in
R
, there is a covering sieve
S_{g}\hookrightarrow y(V)
such that the following diagram commutes

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{g^{*}(R)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces R}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces R}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces S_{g}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{g^{*}(R)}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces m}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces n}

But then
S=\bigcupop\displaylimits_{g\in R}g_{*}(S_{g})
is a covering sieve over
U
by Lemma
6.25
, and
m|_{S}=n|_{S}
. Thus
[R\xrightarrow{m}X]=[R\xrightarrow{n}X]
.
∎

Theorem 7.28
.

Given a Grothendieck site
(\mathcal{C},J)
, if
X
is a
J
-separated presheaf on
\mathcal{C}
, then
X^{+}
is a
J
-sheaf.

Proof.

We wish to show that given a matching family
R\xrightarrow{m}X^{+}
, there exists a unique amalgamation
y(U)\xrightarrow{x}X^{+}
. By Proposition
7.27
, we need only to show that it exists, since
X^{+}
being separated guarantees the amalgamation is unique.

So given
m:R\to X^{+}
, for each
g:V\to U\in R
, we have an element
[R_{g}\xrightarrow{m_{g}}X]\in X^{+}(V)
, where
R_{g}\in J(V)
. Now consider
\widetilde{R}=\bigcupop\displaylimits_{g\in R}g_{*}(R_{g})
. We wish to construct a natural transformation
\widetilde{R}\xrightarrow{\sigma}X
that will serve as the amalgamation
x=[\widetilde{R}\xrightarrow{\sigma}X]
of
\{[m_{g}]\in X^{+}(V)\}_{g:V\to U\in R}
.

We will construct this natural transformation in terms of its components and thus will have to show it is well defined and natural. If
h:W\to U\in\widetilde{R}
, then
h=gt
for some
g\in R
and some
t:W\to V\in R_{g}
. Define
\sigma_{W}:\widetilde{R}(W)\to X(W)
by
\sigma_{W}(h)=m_{g}(t)
.

Let us show that this is well defined. Suppose that
h=gt=g^{\prime}t^{\prime}
for
g:V\to U
and
g^{\prime}:V^{\prime}\to U
in
R
, and
t:W_{g}\to V\in R_{g}
,
t^{\prime}:W_{g^{\prime}}\to V^{\prime}\in R_{g^{\prime}}
. We want to show that
m_{g}(t)=m_{g^{\prime}}(t^{\prime})
. Since
\{[m_{g}]\}
is an
X^{+}
-matching family, we have

X^{+}(t)[m_{g}]=[m_{gt}]=[m_{g^{\prime}t^{\prime}}]=X^{+}(t^{\prime})[m_{g^{\prime}}].

So there exists a covering sieve
S\subseteq t^{*}R_{g}\cap(t^{\prime})^{*}(R_{g^{\prime}})
such that
m_{g}|_{S}=m_{g^{\prime}}|_{S}
, or equivalently for every
s\in S
,

X(s)(m_{g}(t))=X(s)(m_{g^{\prime}}(t^{\prime})).

But
\{X(s)(m_{g}(t))=X(s)(m_{g^{\prime}}(t^{\prime}))\}_{s\in S}
is an
X
-matching family over
S
. But
S
is a covering sieve,
X
is separated, and both
m_{g}(t)
and
m_{g^{\prime}}(t^{\prime})
are amalgamations. Thus
m_{g}(t)=m_{g^{\prime}}(t^{\prime})
, which implies that
\sigma_{W}
is well-defined.

We must now show that the
\sigma_{W}
assemble into a natural transformation, namely we wish to show that if
k:W^{\prime}\to W
is an arbitrary morphism in
\mathcal{C}
, then the following diagram commutes

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\widetilde{R}(W)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{X(W)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\widetilde{R}(W^{\prime})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{X(W^{\prime})}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sigma_{W}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\widetilde{R}(k)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X(k)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sigma_{W^{\prime}}}

So suppose that
h\in\widetilde{R}(W)
. Then
X(k)\sigma_{W}(h)=X(k)(m_{g}(t))
for some
g\in R
and some
t\in R_{g}(W)
, and
\sigma_{W^{\prime}}\widetilde{R}(k)(h)=\sigma_{W^{\prime}}(hk)=m_{g}(tk)
. But we know that
m_{g}(tk)=m_{g}(R_{g}(k)(t))=X(k)(m_{g}(t))
since
m_{g}:R_{g}\to X
is a natural transformation. Thus
\sigma:\widetilde{R}\to X
is a well defined natural transformation.

All that is left to show is that
[\widetilde{R}\xrightarrow{\sigma}X]
is an amalgamation of
m
. In other words we need to show that
X^{+}(g)[\sigma]=[g^{*}\widetilde{R}\to\widetilde{R}\xrightarrow{\sigma}X]=[R_{g}\xrightarrow{m_{g}}X].

Now
R_{g}\subseteq g^{*}\widetilde{R}=g^{*}\bigcupop\displaylimits_{h\in R}h_{*}R_{h}
since
R_{g}\subseteq g^{*}g_{*}R_{g}
, and if
t\in R_{g}
, then
gt\in\widetilde{R}
and
\sigma(gt)=m_{g}(t)
. Thus the following diagram commutes

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{g^{*}\widetilde{R}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\widetilde{R}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{R_{g}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sigma}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces m_{g}}

which is what we wanted to show. Thus
[\sigma]
is an amalgamation of
\{[m_{g}]\}
.
∎

Corollary 7.29
.

Given a Grothendieck site
(\mathcal{C},J)
and a presheaf
X
, the presheaf
(X^{+})^{+}
is a
J
-sheaf.

Definition 7.30
.

Given a Grothendieck site
(\mathcal{C},J)
, let
a:\mathbf{Pre}(\mathcal{C})\to\mathbf{Sh}(\mathcal{C},J)
denote the composite functor
a=((-)^{+})^{+}
. We call this the
sheafification functor
.

Proposition 7.31
.

Given a Grothendieck site
(\mathcal{C},J)
, the functor
a:\mathbf{Pre}(\mathcal{C})\to\mathbf{Sh}(\mathcal{C},J)

(1)

preserves finite limits,

(2)

is left adjoint to the inclusion
i:\mathbf{Sh}(\mathcal{C})\hookrightarrow\mathbf{Pre}(\mathcal{C})
, and

(3)

if
X
is a sheaf, then the unit
\eta_{X}:X\to aiX
of the adjunction
a\dashv i
, is an isomorphism.

Proof.

(1) First note that for every
U\in\mathcal{C}
, the poset
J(U)
is cofiltered. This follows from Lemma
6.22
. Therefore if
X:K\to\mathbf{Pre}(\mathcal{C})
is a finite diagram of presheaves, then

(\lim_{k\in K}X(k))^{+}(U)=\underset{R\in J(U)^{\text{op}}}{\text{colim}}\mathbf{Pre}(\mathcal{C})(R,\lim_{k\in K}X(k))\cong\underset{R\in J(U)^{\text{op}}}{\text{colim}}\lim_{k\in K}\mathbf{Pre}(\mathcal{C})(R,X(k)),

and since filtered colimits commute with finite limits in
\mathbf{Set}
(Proposition
C.15
), we have

\underset{R\in J(U)^{\text{op}}}{\text{colim}}\lim_{k\in K}\mathbf{Pre}(\mathcal{C})(R,X(k))\cong\lim_{k\in K}\underset{R\in J(U)^{\text{op}}}{\text{colim}}\mathbf{Pre}(\mathcal{C})(R,X(k))\cong(\lim_{k\in K}X^{+}(k))(U).

(2) Let
X
be a presheaf, and consider the composition:

X\xrightarrow{\eta_{X}}X^{+}\xrightarrow{\eta_{X^{+}}}X^{++}=aX

Now if we have a map
X\to iY
, where
Y
is a sheaf, then two applications of Lemma
7.26
gives a unique map
aX\to Y
. It is easy to check this defines an adjunction
a\dashv i
and the composite map
\eta_{X^{+}}\circ\eta_{X}
is the unit of the adjunction.

(3) follows from Lemma
7.25
.
∎

Remark 7.32
.

Given a site
(\mathcal{C},j)
and a presheaf
X
, then
aX=(X^{+})^{+}
is a
j
-sheaf, because
aX
is a
\text{Gro}(j)
-sheaf, and by Corollary
6.38
,
a
is a functor
a:\mathbf{Pre}(\mathcal{C})\to\mathbf{Sh}(\mathcal{C},j)
and has all the same properties as in Proposition
7.31
.

Corollary 7.33
.

Given a site
(\mathcal{C},j)
, the full subcategory
\mathbf{Sh}(\mathcal{C},j)\hookrightarrow\mathbf{Pre}(\mathcal{C})
is a lex reflective subcategory of
\mathbf{Pre}(\mathcal{C})
.

Proposition 7.34
.

Given a site
(\mathcal{C},j)
, the class of morphisms
W=a^{-1}(\text{iso})
that are inverted by sheafification is precisely the class
L
of
j
-local isomorphisms.

Proof.

By Proposition
6.42
, sheaves are all
L
-local. Clearly if a presheaf
X
is
L
-local, then it is a sheaf, since all inclusions
\overline{r}\hookrightarrow y(U)
, where
r
is a
j
-covering family are
j
-local isomorphisms, by Lemma
6.15
. So sheaves are precisely the
L
-local objects. But by Lemma
B.11
and Corollary
7.33
, they are also precisely the
W
-local objects. But again by Lemma
B.11
,
W
is precisely the class of
W
-local equivalences. But this is precisely the class of
L
-local equivalences, since a presheaf is
W
-local if and only if it is
L
-local if and only if it is a sheaf. Hence
W=L
.
∎

Now we can fill in the final piece of the commutative diagram (
17
). Note that we don’t need to include the bijection between saturated coverages and Grothendieck coverages, as sheafification is defined for any general site.

(19)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\{\text{systems of local epis on }\mathbf{Pre}(\mathcal{C})\}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\{\text{systems of local isos on }\mathbf{Pre}(\mathcal{C})\}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\{\text{saturated coverages on }\mathcal{C}\}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\{\text{lex reflective subcategories of }\mathbf{Pre}(\mathcal{C})\}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sigma}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\psi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\tau}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces W^{-1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varphi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces a}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces W}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces a^{-1}}

where
W^{-1}
is the map
\alpha\psi\tau
and
a^{-1}
is the map
\psi\tau W
. It is easy to see that each morphism above is a bijection with inverse given parallel to it, and furthermore the entire diagram commutes. The function
W\circ a
is precisely the map that sends a saturated coverage
j
to its class of
j
-local isomorphisms.

Corollary 7.35
.

Given a site
(\mathcal{C},j)
, the category of sheaves
\mathbf{Sh}(\mathcal{C},j)
has all small limits and colimits. Limits are computed pointwise, and agree with those in
\mathbf{Pre}(\mathcal{C})
. Colimits are computed by taking the colimit in
\mathbf{Pre}(\mathcal{C})
and then sheafifying the result.

Proof.

This follows from Proposition
B.17
.
∎

Corollary 7.36
.

Saturated and Grothendieck coverages are rigid in the sense that if
j
and
j^{\prime}
are coverages on a small category
\mathcal{C}
such that
\mathbf{Sh}(\mathcal{C},j)\simeq\mathbf{Sh}(\mathcal{C},j^{\prime})
, then
\text{sat}(j)=\text{sat}(j^{\prime})
and
\text{Gro}(j)=\text{Gro}(j^{\prime})
.

Corollary 7.37
.

A map
f:X\to Y
of presheaves is a
j
-local iso/epi/mono-morphism if and only if
af
is an iso/epi/mono-morphism of sheaves.

Proof.

The map
f
is a
j
-local isomorphism if and only if
af
is an isomorphism by Proposition
7.34
. If
f:X\to Y
is a
j
-local epimorphism, then
\text{im}(f)\hookrightarrow Y
is a
j
-local isomorphism. Now
a
preserves colimits and finite limits by Proposition
7.31
and since the image of
f
coincides with the regular coimage by Lemma
A.78
,
\text{im}(af)\cong a(\text{im}(f))
. Thus
\text{im}(af)\hookrightarrow aY
is an isomorphism, and hence an epimorphism. Furthermore
a
preserves epimorphisms, so
aX\twoheadrightarrow\text{im}(af)
is an epimorphism in
\mathbf{Sh}(\mathcal{C},j)
. Therefore
af
is an epimorphism.

Conversely, if
af:aX\to aY
is an epimorphism in
\mathbf{Sh}(\mathcal{C},j)
, then
\text{im}(af)\hookrightarrow aY
is an isomorphism, so
\text{im}(f)\hookrightarrow Y
is a
j
-local isomorphism, and therefore
f
is a
j
-local epimorphism. Similarly,
af
is a monomorphism if and only if it is a
j
-local monomorphism.
∎

Corollary 7.38
.

Let
X
and
Y
be sheaves on a site
(\mathcal{C},j)
. Then a map
f:X\to Y
is a
j
-local monomorphism if and only if it is a monomorphism of sheaves if and only if it a monomorphism of presheaves.

Proof.

This follows from Corollary
7.37
, Proposition
B.17
and the fact that monomorphisms can be characterized by pullback diagrams.
∎

Remark 7.39
.

Here we will consider an extended example to show that we cannot replace
j
-local epimorphisms in Corollary
7.37
with strong
j
-local epimorphisms (Remark
5.3
). We will show that there exists a
j
-local epimorphism
f:X\to Y
that is not a strong
j
-local epimorphism and such that
af:aX\to aY
, where
a
here is the sheafification functor of Definition
7.30
, is an epimorphism of sheaves.

Consider the category
\mathcal{C}
, given as follows

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces W}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces V}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces U}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces q}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p}

equipped with the collection of families
j
, defined by

j(U)=\{1_{U},p\},\qquad j(V)=\{1_{V},q\},\qquad j(W)=\{1_{W}\}.

It is easy to see that this collection of families is a coverage. It is refinement closed, but not composition closed. Consider the presheaves
X
and
Y
on
\mathcal{C}
defined by

X(U)=\{a\},\;X(V)=\{b\},\;X(W)=\{c\},\;X(p)(a)=b,\;X(q)(b)=c,

Y(U)=\{\alpha,\alpha^{\prime}\},\;Y(V)=\{\beta,\beta^{\prime}\},\;Y(W)=\{\gamma\},\;Y(p)(\alpha)=\beta,\;Y(p)(\alpha^{\prime})=\beta^{\prime},\;Y(q)(\beta)=Y(q)(\beta^{\prime})=\gamma.

Now let
f:X\to Y
be the morphism of presheaves defined by

f_{U}(a)=\alpha,\qquad f_{V}(b)=\beta,\qquad f_{W}(c)=\gamma.

Now this map is not a strong
j
-local epimorphism. For instance, consider the section
\alpha^{\prime}:y(U)\to Y
. There are two covering families on
U
, the identity and
p
. There is no section
s:y(U)\to X
such that
f_{U}s=\alpha^{\prime}
, so
f
does not lift against the identity. Similarly, consider the covering family
p
. The composite map

y(V)\xrightarrow{p}y(U)\xrightarrow{\alpha^{\prime}}Y

is the section
\beta^{\prime}:y(V)\to Y
. There is no section
s:y(V)\to X
such that
f_{V}s=\beta^{\prime}
. However, note that
f
does lift against
q
using the identity covering family on
W
.

Now for every section
s:y(U)\to Y
,
f
lifts against the
j
-tree
T_{pq}
given by
T_{pq}^{\circ}=\{pq\}
. In other words, for
s=\alpha,\alpha^{\prime}
, the following diagram commutes

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{y(W)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{y(V)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{y(U)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces c}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces q}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces s}

because
spq=\gamma=f_{W}(c)
. Thus
f
is a
j
-local epimorphism.

Now let us consider
f^{+}:X^{+}\to Y^{+}
. Clearly
X
is a sheaf, so
X^{+}\cong X
by
7.25
. Now
Y
is not a sheaf, because the map

Y(V)\to\underset{r\in\text{sat}(j)(V)^{\text{op}}}{\text{colim}}\,\text{Match}(r,Y)

is not a bijection. Indeed, there are only two
j
-saturating families on
V
, given by the identity and
q
, and there is a unique refinement
q\to 1_{V}
, making
q
terminal in
\text{sat}(j)(V)^{\text{op}}
. Thus

\underset{r\in\text{sat}(j)(V)^{\text{op}}}{\text{colim}}\,\text{Match}(r,Y)\cong\text{Match}(q,Y)\cong Y(W).

The map
Y(V)\to Y(W)
is thus
Y(q)
, which is not a bijection. Now let us compute
Y^{+}
. The category of
j
-saturating families on
U
looks like

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces W}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces V}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces U}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces V}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces U}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces U}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces U}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces q}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces q}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 1_{U}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 1_{U}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 1_{U}}

so
T_{pq}
is a terminal object in
\text{sat}(j)(U)^{\text{op}}
. Hence

\underset{r\in\text{sat}(j)(U)^{\text{op}}}{\text{colim}}\,\text{Match}(r,Y)\cong\text{Match}(T_{pq}^{\circ},Y)\cong\{(y_{V},y_{W})\in Y(V)\times Y(W)\,|\,Y(q)(y_{V})=y_{W}\}\cong\{(\beta,\gamma),(\beta^{\prime},\gamma)\}.

So we have

Y^{+}(U)=\{(\beta,\gamma),(\beta^{\prime},\gamma)\},\;Y^{+}(V)=\{\gamma\},\;Y^{+}(W)=\{\gamma\},\;Y^{+}(p)(\beta,\gamma)=Y^{+}(p)(\beta^{\prime},\gamma)=\gamma,\;Y^{+}(q)(\gamma)=\gamma.

This is still not a sheaf. Doing the plus construction again, and using similar arguments to the above, we find

aY(U)=aY(V)=aY(W)=\{\gamma\},\;aY(p)=aY(q)=1_{\{\gamma\}}.

Now both
X
and
Y
are terminal sheaves on
(\mathcal{C},j)
, and the map
af:aX\cong X\to aY
is an isomorphism, and therefore an epimorphism of sheaves.

7.5.
Variant of the Plus Construction

In this short section, we describe a different, but equivalent version of the plus construction. This variant is useful in the context of higher category theory and appears for example in
[
Lur18b
]
and
[
nLa21
]
. Since this section is not terribly central to our tale, we’ll be a little bit more terse in our descriptions than usual.

Given a Grothendieck site
(\mathcal{C},J)
, let
\text{Cov}_{J}:\mathcal{C}^{\text{op}}\to\mathbf{Poset}
denote the pseudofunctor that sends an object
U
to
J(U)
, thought of as a poset, and sends a morphism
f:U\to V
to the functor
f^{*}:J(V)\to J(U)
of posets defined by
(R\hookrightarrow y(V))\mapsto(f^{*}R\hookrightarrow y(U))
. Taking the Grothendieck construction of this pseudofunctor provides us with a category
J(\mathcal{C})\coloneqq\intop\nolimits\text{Cov}_{J}
, whose objects are pairs
(U\in\mathcal{C},R\in J(U))
and morphisms
(U,R)\to(V,S)
are maps
f:U\to V
such that
R\subseteq f^{*}S
. This has a canonical functor
r:J(\mathcal{C})\to\mathcal{C}
defined by
(U,R)\mapsto U
. This functor
r
has a right adjoint
s:\mathcal{C}\to J(\mathcal{C})
, defined by
U\mapsto(U,y(U))
.

The functors
r
and
s
induce functors on the presheaf categories by Lemma
A.69
, and since adjoints are unique up to isomorphism, we end up with the following quadruple adjunction:

(20)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathbf{Pre}(\mathcal{C})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathbf{Pre}(J(\mathcal{C}))}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Delta_{r}\cong\Sigma_{s}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Sigma_{r}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Pi_{r}\cong\Delta_{s}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Pi_{s}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\dashv}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\dashv}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\dashv}

Using the limit definition of right Kan extension, we see that if
X\in\mathbf{Pre}(\mathcal{C})
, then

(\Pi_{s}X)(U,R)\cong\lim\left[\left((U,R)\downarrow s^{\text{op}}\right)\to\mathcal{C}^{\text{op}}\xrightarrow{X}\mathbf{Set}\right].

Lemma 7.40
.

If
X
is a presheaf, then

(\Pi_{s}X)(U,R)\cong\lim_{V\to U\in R}X(V)\cong\mathbf{Pre}(\mathcal{C})(R,X)

Proof.

Let us examine the comma category
((U,R)\downarrow s^{\text{op}})
. This has objects
((U,R),V,f)
, where
V\in\mathcal{C}
and
f
is a map in
J(\mathcal{C})^{\text{op}}
from
(U,R)
to
s^{\text{op}}(V)
, which is equivalently a map
(V,yV)\to(U,R)
. Such a map is equivalent to a map
f:V\to U
such that
y(V)\hookrightarrow f^{*}R
, which is only possible if
f\in R
. Some diagram chasing then proves the lemma.
∎

Similarly the formula for left Kan extension gives that if
G\in\mathbf{Pre}(J(\mathcal{C}))
, then

(\Sigma_{r}G)(U)\cong\text{colim}\left[(r^{\text{op}}\downarrow U)\to J(\mathcal{C})^{\text{op}}\xrightarrow{G}\mathbf{Set}\right]

Lemma 7.41
.

If
G
is a presheaf on
J(\mathcal{C})
, then

(\Sigma_{r}G)(U)\cong\underset{R\in J(U)^{\text{op}}}{\text{colim}}G(U,R)

Proof.

First note that
(r^{\text{op}}\downarrow U)\cong(U\downarrow r)^{\text{op}}
. Consider the functor
X:J(U)\to(U\downarrow r)
that takes an inclusion
R\hookrightarrow R^{\prime}
to:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces U}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{r(U,R)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{r(U,R^{\prime})}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces r(1_{U})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 1_{U}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 1_{U}}

We want to show that this functor is initial (Definition
C.10
), thus proving the lemma. So we need to show that if
d\in(U\downarrow r)
, then we want to show that
(X\downarrow d)
is connected. An object in
(X\downarrow d)
is a triangle:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces U}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{r(U,R)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{r(V,S)}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces r(d)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 1_{U}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces d}

So if we have two objects, we want to show they are connected by a zig-zag of morphisms. The following commutative diagram proves this:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces U}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{r(U,R^{\prime})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{r(U,R\cap R^{\prime})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{r(U,R)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{r(V,S)}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces r(d)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 1_{U}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces d}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces r(d)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 1_{U}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces r(1_{U})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces r(1_{U})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 1_{U}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces r(d)}

because if
R\hookrightarrow d^{*}S
and
R^{\prime}\hookrightarrow d^{*}S
, then
R\cap R^{\prime}\hookrightarrow d^{*}S
.
∎

Thus if
U\in\mathcal{C}
, we have

(21)

X^{+}(U)\cong(\Sigma_{r}\Pi_{s}X)(U)\cong\underset{R\in J(U)^{\text{op}}}{\text{colim}}\,\mathbf{Pre}(\mathcal{C})(R,X).

7.6.
Sheafification in one go

While we can obtain a variety of useful properties of the sheafification functor
a:\mathbf{Pre}(\mathcal{C})\to\mathbf{Sh}(\mathcal{C},j)
by its description as the plus construction applied twice
a=(-)^{++}
, it is very difficult to work with in practice. In this section, we describe a more useful description of the sheafification of a presheaf. We follow the wonderful blog post
[
Kim20
]
, and also mention the similar descriptions given in
[
Car19
, Page 34]
and
[
Yuh07
]
.

Definition 7.42
.

Let
(\mathcal{C},j)
be a site and
X
a presheaf on
\mathcal{C}
. We say that two sections
s,t\in X(U)

are
locally equal
if there exists a
j
-covering family
r=\{r_{i}:U_{i}\to U\}_{i\in I}
such that
X(r_{i})(s)=X(r_{i})(t)
for all
i\in I
.

Given a family of morphisms
r=\{r_{i}:U_{i}\to U\}_{i\in I}
we say that a collection of sections
\{x_{i}\in X(U_{i})\}_{i\in I}
is
X
-
locally matching
if for every intersection square

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces U_{ij}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{U_{j}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{U_{i}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces U}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces r_{i}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces r_{j}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces u_{i}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces u_{j}}

the sections
X(u_{i})(x_{i})
and
X(u_{j})(x_{j})
are locally equal.

Lemma 7.43
.

Suppose that
\{x_{i}\}
is an
X
-locally matching family on
t=\{t_{j}:V_{j}\to U\}_{j\in J}
, and there is a refinement
f:r\to t
where
r=\{r_{i}:U_{i}\to U\}_{i\in I}
and with index map
\alpha:I\to J
. Then
f^{*}\{x_{j}\}=\{X(f_{i})(x_{\alpha(i)})\}_{i\in I}
is locally matching on
r
.

Proof.

This follows from the same argument as in Lemma
2.15
.
∎

Thus we obtain a presheaf
\text{LocMatch}(-,X):\text{Fam}(U)^{\text{op}}\to\mathbf{Set}
.

Definition 7.44
.

Given a site
(\mathcal{C},j)
, a presheaf
X
and
U\in\mathcal{C}
, let
X^{\dagger}
denote the presheaf defined objectwise by

(22)

X^{\dagger}(U)=\underset{r\in\text{sat}(j)(U)^{\text{op}}}{\text{colim}}\,\text{LocMatch}(r,X).

The action of
X^{\dagger}
on morphisms is described as follows. Suppose that
f:U\to V
is a morphism,
t=\{t_{j}:V_{j}\to V\}
is a
j
-saturating family and
\{x_{j}\}
is an
X
-locally matching family on
t
, then since
\text{sat}(j)
is a coverage, there exists a
j
-saturating family
r=\{U_{i}\to U\}
and maps
s_{i}:U_{i}\to V_{\alpha(i)}
such that the following diagram commutes

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{U_{i}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{V_{\alpha(i)}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces U}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces V}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces s_{i}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces r_{i}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces t_{\alpha}(i)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}

So define
X^{\dagger}(f)[\{x_{j}\}]=[\{X(s_{i})(x_{\alpha(i)})\}]
. We will show in Lemma
7.49
that this is well-defined.

Definition 7.45
.

Given two families of morphisms
r=\{r_{i}:U_{i}\to U\}_{i\in I}
and
t=\{t_{j}:V_{j}\to U\}_{j\in J}
with families of local sections
\{x_{i}\in X(U_{i})\}_{i\in I}
and
\{y_{j}\in X(V_{j})\}_{j\in J}
, we say that
\{x_{i}\}
and
\{y_{j}\}
are
X
-
locally equivalent
if for each commutative square of the form

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{W_{ij}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{V_{j}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{U_{i}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces U}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces v_{j}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces u_{i}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces t_{j}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces r_{i}}

for every
i
and
j
, the sections
X(u_{i})(x_{i})
and
X(v_{j})(y_{j})
are locally equal. We write
\{x_{i}\}\approx_{X}\{y_{j}\}
to denote that they are locally equivalent.

Remark 7.46
.

We note that a family
\{x_{i}\in X(U_{i})\}
of local sections is
X
-locally equivalent to itself
\{x_{i}\}\approx_{X}\{x_{i}\}
if and only if
\{x_{i}\}
is
X
-locally matching. Thus we see that on
X
-locally matching families,
\approx_{X}
is an equivalence relation.

Lemma 7.47
.

Given a site
(\mathcal{C},j)
, families of morphisms

r=\{r_{i}:U_{i}\to U\}_{i\in I}
,
t=\{t_{j}:V_{j}\to U\}_{j\in J}
, and an
X
-locally matching family
\{x_{j}\in X(V_{j})\}_{j\in J}
on
t
, suppose that
f:r\to t
is a refinement. Then
f^{*}\{x_{j}\}\approx_{X}\{x_{j}\}
.

Proof.

Suppose we have a commutative diagram of the form

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{W_{ij}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{V_{j}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{U_{i}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces U}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces v_{j}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces u_{i}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces t_{j}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces r_{i}}

Then since
f
is a refinement with index map
\alpha:I\to J
, we can extend this to the commutative diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{W_{ij}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{V_{j}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{V_{\alpha(i)}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{U_{i}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces U}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{v_{j}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces v_{\alpha(i)}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{u_{i}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{t_{j}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces t_{\alpha}(i)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f_{i}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{r_{i}}}

Since
\{x_{j}\}
is
X
-locally matching, this implies that
X(v_{\alpha(i)})(x_{\alpha(i)})
and
X(v_{j})(x_{j})
are locally equal. But
X(v_{\alpha(i)})(x_{\alpha(i)})=X(u_{i})X(f_{i})(x_{\alpha(i)})
. Hence
f^{*}\{x_{j}\}=\{X(f_{i})(x_{\alpha(i)})\}_{i\in I}
and
\{x_{j}\}_{j\in J}
are
X
-locally equivalent.
∎

Lemma 7.48
.

Given a site
(\mathcal{C},j)
, a presheaf
X
, two
X
-locally matching families
\{x_{i}\}
and
\{y_{j}\}
are identified in
X^{\dagger}(U)
if and only if they are
X
-locally equivalent.

Proof.

Suppose that
r=\{r_{i}:U_{i}\to U\}_{i\in I}
and
t=\{t_{j}:V_{j}\to U\}_{j\in J}
are
j
-saturating families on
U
and
\{x_{i}\}
,
\{y_{j}\}
are
X
-locally matching families on
r
and
t
respectively.

(\Rightarrow)
Suppose that
x=[\{x_{i}\}]
and
y=[\{y_{j}\}]
are identified as elements of

X^{\dagger}(U)=\underset{s\in\text{sat}(j)(U)^{\text{op}}}{\text{colim}}\,\text{LocMatch}(s,X).

Since
\text{sat}(j)(U)
is finitely cofiltered by Lemma
6.40
, then by Lemma
C.13
, this means that there exists a
j
-saturating family
s
on
U
with refinements
f:s\to r
and
g:s\to t
, such that
f^{*}\{x_{i}\}=g^{*}\{y_{j}\}
. But
\{x_{i}\}\approx_{X}f^{*}\{x_{i}\}=g^{*}\{y_{j}\}\approx_{X}\{y_{j}\}
by Lemma
7.47
, hence
\{x_{i}\}
and
\{y_{j}\}
are locally equivalent.

(\Leftarrow)
Suppose that
\{x_{i}\}
and
\{y_{j}\}
are
X
-locally equivalent matching families. Then again, since
\text{sat}(j)(U)
is cofiltered, there exists a common refinement
f:s\to r
and
g:s\to t
, where
s=\{s_{k}:W_{k}\to U\}_{k\in K}
. But then for every
k\in K
we have the commutative diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{W_{k}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{V_{\beta(k)}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{U_{\alpha(k)}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces U}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g_{k}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f_{k}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces s_{k}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces t_{\beta(k)}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces r_{\alpha(k)}}

But then
r_{\alpha}=\{r_{\alpha(k)}:U_{\alpha(k)}\to U\}_{k\in K}
and
t_{\beta}=\{t_{\beta(k)}:V_{\beta(k)}\to U\}_{k\in K}
are
j
-saturating since they are both refined by
s
. Thus there exists a
j
-covering family
h^{k}=\{h^{k}_{\ell}:A^{k}_{\ell}\to W_{k}\}_{\ell\in L^{k}}
such that
X(f_{k}h^{k}_{\ell})(r_{\alpha(k)})=X(g_{k}h^{k}_{\ell})(t_{\beta(k)})
. Now there are obvious refinements
r_{\alpha}\to r
and
t_{\beta}\to t
given by identity maps. The composite family
h\circ s
on
U
is
j
-saturating, refines both
r_{\alpha}
and
t_{\beta}
, hence refining both
r
and
t
, and when
\{x_{i}\}
and
\{y_{j}\}
are pulled back along the refinements are equal. Hence they are identified in
X^{\dagger}(U)
.
∎

Lemma 7.49
.

The action of
X^{\dagger}
on morphisms as described after Definition
7.44
is well-defined.

Proof.

Suppose that
f:U\to V
is a morphism,
t=\{t_{j}:V_{j}\to V\}
is a
j
-saturating family and
\{x_{j}\}
is an
X
-locally matching family on
t
, then since
\text{sat}(j)
is a coverage, there exists a
j
-saturating family
r=\{r_{i}:U_{i}\to U\}
and maps
a_{i}:U_{i}\to V_{\alpha(i)}
such that
t_{\alpha(i)}a_{i}=fr_{i}
. Suppose we chose a different
j
-saturating family
s=\{s_{i^{\prime}}:U_{i^{\prime}}\to U\}
with maps
b_{i^{\prime}}:U_{i^{\prime}}\to V_{\beta(i^{\prime})}
. Then for every intersection square for
s
and
r
we get a larger intersection square for
t

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{U_{ii^{\prime}}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{U_{i^{\prime}}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces U}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{U_{i}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{V_{\beta(i^{\prime})}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces V}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{V_{\alpha(i)}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces c_{i^{\prime}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces d_{i}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces s_{i^{\prime}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces b_{i^{\prime}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces r_{i}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces a_{i}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces t_{\beta(i^{\prime})}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces t_{\alpha(i)}}

Since
\{x_{j}\}
is
X
-locally matching, this implies that
\{X(a_{i})(x_{\alpha(i)}\}
and
\{X(b_{i^{\prime}})(x_{\beta(i^{\prime})})\}
are locally equivalent. Hence by Lemma
7.48
, they are identified in
X^{\dagger}(U)
and thus its action on morphisms is well-defined. Choosing different representatives for
[\{x_{i}\}]
is proved similarly.
∎

Proposition 7.50
.

Given a site
(\mathcal{C},j)
and a presheaf
X
on
\mathcal{C}
, the presheaf
X^{\dagger}
is a
j
-sheaf.

Proof.

Given
U\in\mathcal{C}
, let
r=\{r_{i}:U_{i}\to U\}
be a
j
-covering family on
U
and let
\{x_{i}\in X^{\dagger}(U_{i})\}
be a
X^{\dagger}
-matching family on
r
. We want to show that there is a unique amalgamation. Each
x_{i}
is an equivalence class
\{x^{i}_{a}\}
of an
X
-locally matching family on a
j
-saturating family
r^{i}=\{r^{i}_{a}:U^{i}_{a}\to U_{i}\}_{a\in A^{i}}
, which are all
X
-locally equivalent when pulled back along intersection squares. Now by composing all of the
j
-saturating families we obtain a
j
-saturating family
\{U^{i}_{a}\to U\}
and it is not hard to see that
\cup_{i}\{x^{i}_{a}\}
is now an
X
-locally matching family, and furthermore
[\cup_{i}\{x^{i}_{a}\}]
is an amalgamation for the
x_{i}
.

Now suppose that
x
and
y
are amalgamations for
\{x_{i}\}
. So there are
j
-saturating families
c=\{c_{p}:C_{p}\to U\}
and
d=\{d_{q}:D_{q}\to U\}
and
X
-locally matching families
\{x_{p}\}
,
\{y_{q}\}
that amalgamate the
\{x^{i}_{a}\}
. So we have a commutative diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{U^{i}_{a}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{U_{i}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{U_{b}^{i^{\prime}}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{C_{p}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces U}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{D_{q}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces r^{i}_{a}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces r_{i}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces r^{i^{\prime}}_{b}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces c_{p}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces d_{q}}

Such that when
\{x_{p}\}
and
\{y_{q}\}
are pulled back, they are locally equivalent to
x_{i}
. Hence they are locally equivalent to each other, so
x
and
y
are identified in
X^{\dagger}(U)
.
∎

For any presheaf
X
, there is a map

\eta:X\to X^{\dagger}

that sends a section
x\in X(U)
to the equivalence class of the
X
-locally matching family
\{x\}
on the
j
-saturating family
\{1_{U}:U\to U\}
.

Lemma 7.51
.

Given a site
(\mathcal{C},j)
if
X
is a
j
-sheaf then the map
\eta
is an isomorphism.

Proof.

Since
X
is a
j
-sheaf, if two sections
x
and
y
are
X
-locally equal, then they are equal. Thus a family of sections is
X
-locally matching if and only if they are
X
-matching. In other words,
X^{\dagger}
is precisely
X^{+}
when
X
is a sheaf, and hence by Lemma
7.25
, the map
\eta
is an isomorphism.
∎

Lemma 7.52
.

Given a site
(\mathcal{C},j)
, any map
f:X\to Y
of presheaves where
Y
is a sheaf factors uniquely through a map
\widetilde{f}:X^{\dagger}\to Y
.

Proof.

The proof is similar to Lemma
7.26
, the map
\widetilde{f}
sends an equivalence class of an
X
-locally matching family to the unique amalgamation of its image under
f
in
Y
.
∎

Since
X^{\dagger}
is a sheaf, this implies that
(-)^{\dagger}
is left adjoint to the inclusion
i:\mathbf{Sh}(\mathcal{C},j)\hookrightarrow\mathbf{Pre}(\mathcal{C})
. Since adjoints are unique up to isomorphism, Proposition
7.31
implies the following result.

Corollary 7.53
.

Given a site
(\mathcal{C},j)
and a presheaf
X
, there is an isomorphism

X^{\dagger}\cong aX,

where
a
denotes sheafification by two applications of the plus construction (Definition
7.30
).

8.
Examples of Sites

In this section, we finally introduce a wide variety of examples of sites from across the literature. Doing this requires delving into some set-theoretic difficulties, as we do in Section
8.4
. First, we discuss some particular properties of sites that are often satisfied by examples in the literature, such as being (sub)canonical or pullback-stable.

8.1.
More Kinds of Coverages

8.1.1.
(Sub)canonical coverages

Definition 8.1
.

We say that a site
(\mathcal{C},j)
is
subcanonical
if for every
U\in\mathcal{C}
, the representable presheaf
y(U)
is a
j
-sheaf.

Example 8.2
.

The sites of Example
2.11
, the site
(\mathcal{O}(X),j_{X})
of a topological space
X
from Example
2.9
, the basis coverage from Example
2.10
, and the site
(\mathbf{FinSet},j_{\text{epi}})
from Example
2.8
are all subcanonical.

Definition 8.3
.

We say that a coverage
j
on a small category
\mathcal{C}
is
canonical
if
\text{Gro}(j)
is the largest subcanonical Grothendieck coverage on
\mathcal{C}
. Hence there is a unique canonical Grothendieck coverage on any small category.

Definition 8.4
.

Given a small category
\mathcal{C}
, we say that a sieve
R\hookrightarrow y(U)
is a
colimit sieve
if
\{f:V\to U\}_{f\in R}
forms a colimit cocone
10
10

10

specifically it is a colimit over the diagram
\pi:(R\downarrow U)\to\mathcal{C}
, where
(R\downarrow U)
is the full subcategory of
\mathcal{C}/_{U}
on the morphisms in
R
, and
\pi
is defined by
\pi(f:V\to U)=V
.
. We say that
R
is a
universal colimit sieve
if it is a colimit sieve and furthermore for every morphism
f:V\to U
, the sieve
f^{*}(R)
is a colimit sieve.

Given a small category
\mathcal{C}
, let
J_{\text{uni}}
denote the sifted collection of families given by the universal colimit sieves.

Lemma 8.5
.

Given a small category
\mathcal{C}
a sieve
R\hookrightarrow y(U)
is a colimit sieve if and only if every representable
y(V)
is a sheaf on
R
.

Proof.

By Lemma
3.20
, a representable
y(V)
is a sheaf on
R
if and only if

y(V)(U)\cong\lim_{f:W\to U\in R}y(V)(W).

But this means that

\mathcal{C}(U,V)\cong\lim_{f\in R}\mathcal{C}(W,V)\cong\mathcal{C}\left(\underset{f:W\to U\in R}{\text{colim}}\,W,V\right).

Since this is true for every
V\in\mathcal{C}
, by the Yoneda lemma this implies that
U\cong\underset{f:W\to U\in R}{\text{colim}}\,W
. In other words,
R
is a colimit sieve.
∎

Hence if
(\mathcal{C},J)
is a subcanonical Grothendieck coverage, then every
J
-covering sieve
R
is a colimit sieve. The following result gives a nice characterization of the canonical Grothendieck coverage. It is surprisingly nontrivial to prove.

Proposition 8.6

(
[
Les19
, Theorem 3.1.1 and Theorem 3.2.1]
)
.

Given a small category
\mathcal{C}
, the collection of families
J_{\text{uni}}
is a Grothendieck coverage, and it is the canonical Grothendieck coverage.

Proposition 8.7

(
[
Les19
, Theorem 3.2.4]
)
.

Let
\mathcal{C}
be a cocomplete category with all pullbacks, and furthermore suppose that its coproducts are stable under pullback and disjoint
11
11

11

see Section
11
for the definition of disjoint coproducts.
. Let
j_{\text{uniepi}}
denote the collection of families where
\{r_{i}:U_{i}\to U\}\in j_{\text{uniepi}}(U)
if and only if
\sumop\displaylimits_{i}r_{i}:\sumop\displaylimits_{i}U_{i}\to U
is a universal effective epimorphism
12
12

12

i.e. it is an effective epimorphism that is stable under pullbacks
. Then
j_{\text{uniepi}}
is a Grothendieck pretopology, and it is canonical.

8.1.2.
Stability

Definition 8.8
.

Given a small category
\mathcal{C}
, we say that a coverage
j
on
\mathcal{C}
is
pullback-stable
if for any
j
-covering family
r=\{r_{i}:U_{i}\to U\}_{i\in I}
and for any morphism
g:V\to U
in
\mathcal{C}
, the pullback

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{V\times_{U}U_{i}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{U_{i}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces V}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces U}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g^{*}(r_{i})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lrcorner}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces r_{i}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g}

exists in
\mathcal{C}
, and the family
\{g^{*}(r_{i}):V\times_{U}U_{i}\to V\}_{i\in I}
is a
j
-covering family of
V
.

One advantage of working with pullback-stable coverages is Lemma
2.27
. There are other technical conveniences as well.

Definition 8.9

(
[
Rob12
, Definition 3.3]
)
.

Let
(\mathcal{C},j)
be a pullback-stable site. We say that a covering family
r=\{r_{i}:U_{i}\to U\}_{i\in I}
is
effective
, if
r
is a colimit cocone of the diagram consisting of all spans
U_{i}\leftarrow U_{i}\times_{U}U_{j}\rightarrow U_{j}
. Note that if
r
is a singleton, then
r
is effective if and only if it is a regular epimorphism.

Lemma 8.10
.

If
(\mathcal{C},j)
is a pullback-stable site, then it is subcanonical (Definition
8.1
) if and only if all of its covering families are effective.

Proof.

(\Rightarrow)
Suppose that
(\mathcal{C},j)
is pullback-stable and subcanonical. Then for every
U\in\mathcal{C}
, the representable presheaf
y(U)
is a
j
-sheaf. Thus by Corollary
3.19
, if
r=\{r_{i}:U_{i}\to U\}
is a
j
-covering family and
V\in\mathcal{C}
, then

\displaystyle\mathcal{C}(U,V)\cong y(V)(U)

\displaystyle\cong\text{eq}\left(\prodop\displaylimits_{i}y(V)(U_{i})\rightrightarrows\prodop\displaylimits_{i,j}y(V)(U_{i}\times_{U}U_{j})\right)

\displaystyle\cong\text{eq}\left(\prodop\displaylimits_{i}\mathcal{C}(U_{i},V)\rightrightarrows\prodop\displaylimits_{i,j}\mathcal{C}(U_{i}\times_{U}U_{j},V)\right)

\displaystyle\cong\mathcal{C}\left(\text{coeq}\left(\coprodop\displaylimits_{i}U_{i}\leftleftarrows\coprodop\displaylimits_{i,j}U_{i}\times_{U}U_{j}\right),V\right).

But since
V
was arbitrary, by the Yoneda lemma, we have that
U\cong\text{coeq}\left(\coprodop\displaylimits_{i}U_{i}\leftleftarrows\coprodop\displaylimits_{i,j}U_{i}\times_{U}U_{j}\right)
. Thus
r
is effective.

(\Leftarrow)
If all of the covering families are effective, then the reverse of the above argument shows that
\mathcal{C}
is subcanonical.
∎

One of the most common kinds of coverages that appear in the literature are Grothendieck pretopologies. These are especially common in differential and algebraic geometry.

Definition 8.11
.

Given a small category
\mathcal{C}
, we say that a coverage
j
is a
Grothendieck pretopology
if
\{f:V\to U\}\in j(U)
for every isomorphism
f
,
j
is composition closed, and
j
is pullback-stable.

A majority of the sites we will see in the next section are pullback-stable, but there are several important exceptions.

8.1.3.
Dense and atomic coverages

Definition 8.12
.

Given a small category
\mathcal{C}
, let
J_{\text{dense}}
denote the sifted collection of families where a sieve
R\hookrightarrow y(U)
belongs to
J_{\text{dense}}
if and only if for every map
f:V\to U
there exists a map
g:W\to V
such that
fg\in R
. In other words,
R\in J_{\text{dense}}(U)
if and only if
f^{*}(R)
is nonempty for every morphism
f
. It is easy to see that
J_{\text{dense}}
is a Grothendieck coverage, which we call the
dense Grothendieck coverage
on
\mathcal{C}
. We say a coverage
j
is dense if
\text{Gro}(j)
is the dense Grothendieck coverage.

An important application of the dense Grothendieck coverage is the following result.

Proposition 8.13

(
[
MM12
, Corollary VI.1.5]
)
.

Given a small category
\mathcal{C}
, the category of sheaves on the dense Grothendieck site
(\mathcal{C},J_{\text{dense}})
is equivalent to the sheaves on
\mathbf{Pre}(\mathcal{C})
with respect to the double negation Lawvere-Tierney topology
13
13

13

We do not cover Lavwere-Tierney topologies in these notes, see
[
MM12
, Section V.1]
for more.
j_{\neg\neg}
.

Definition 8.14

(
[
Car12
, Page 3]
)
.

Given a small category
\mathcal{C}
, let
J_{\text{at}}
denote the smallest Grothendieck coverage on
\mathcal{C}
such that every nonempty sieve is
J_{\text{at}}
-covering. We call this the
atomic Grothendieck coverage
. We say a coverage
j
is atomic if
\text{Gro}(j)=J_{\text{at}}
.

Remark 8.15
.

We note that since Grothendieck coverages are closed under (small) intersections by Lemma
6.23
, we know that
J_{\text{at}}
exists by taking the intersection of the set of Grothendieck coverages that have nonempty covering sieves.

For any small category it is clear that
J_{\text{dense}}\subseteq J_{\text{at}}
. We will now characterize when they are equal.

Definition 8.16
.

We say that a small category
\mathcal{C}
satisfies the (right)
Ore condition
if for every cospan of morphisms
A\to B\leftarrow C
there exists a span
A\leftarrow D\to B
such that the square commutes

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces D}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces C}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B}

Given a small category
\mathcal{C}
let
J_{\text{Ore}}
denote the sifted collection families where
R\in J_{\text{Ore}}(U)
is covering if and only if
R
is nonempty.

Remark 8.17
.

Clearly any category with pullbacks satisfies the Ore condition.

Lemma 8.18
.

If
\mathcal{C}
is a small category that satisfies the Ore condition, then
J_{\text{Ore}}
is a Grothendieck coverage. Furthermore it is the atomic Grothendieck coverage and the dense Grothendieck coverage.

Proof.

Clearly
J_{\text{Ore}}
satisfies (G1). Now suppose that
R\hookrightarrow y(U)
is nonempty and
f:V\to U
is a morphism. Since
R
is nonempty, there exists a map
g:V^{\prime}\to U
in
R
. Hence we have a cospan

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces W}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{V^{\prime}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces V}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces U}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces k}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces h}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}

Since
\mathcal{C}
satisfies the Ore condition, there exists a map
h:W\to V
such that
fh\in R
. Hence
h\in f^{*}(R)
. Thus
J_{\text{Ore}}
satisfies (G2).

Now suppose that
R\hookrightarrow y(U)
is nonempty,
R^{\prime}\hookrightarrow y(U)
is a sieve and for every morphism
f:V\to U
the sieve
f^{*}(R^{\prime})
is nonempty. Then choosing
f=1_{U}
, we see that
R^{\prime}
is nonempty and hence
J_{\text{Ore}}
-covering. Thus
J_{\text{Ore}}
satisfies (G3) and is therefore a Grothendieck coverage.

It is clear that
J_{\text{Ore}}=J_{\text{at}}
, and it is also clear that
J_{\text{Ore}}=J_{\text{dense}}
.
∎

Corollary 8.19
.

If
\mathcal{C}
is a small category and
J_{\text{dense}}=J_{\text{at}}
on
\mathcal{C}
, then
\mathcal{C}
satisfies the Ore condition.

When
\mathcal{C}
satisfies the Ore condition, we will refer to
J_{\text{Ore}}=J_{\text{at}}=J_{\text{dense}}
as the atomic Grothendieck coverage. We say that a site
(\mathcal{C},j)
is Ore-atomic if
\mathcal{C}
satisfies the Ore condition and
j
is atomic. Checking the sheaf condition is a little bit easier on Ore-atomic sites.

Lemma 8.20

(
[
MM12
, Lemma III.4.2]
)
.

Let
(\mathcal{C},j)
be an Ore-atomic site. Then a presheaf
X
on
\mathcal{C}
is a
j
-sheaf if and only if for every morphism
f:V\to U
, every section
x\in X(V)
, and every fork
14
14

14

I.e.
fg=fh
.

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces W}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces V}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces U}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces h}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}

such that
X(g)(x)=X(h)(x)
, there exists a unique
y\in X(U)
such that
x=X(f)(y)
.

8.2.
Examples

Originally the domain of algebraic topology, sheaf theory has now extended itself across mathematics. We recommend the book
[
Ros22
]
for more on how sheaf theory is extending outwards. Here we try and give a wide array of examples of sites and their properties.

Example 8.21
.

Given a topological space
X
, recall the open cover coverage
(\mathcal{O}(X),j_{X})
from Example
2.9
. As we saw in Example
6.3
, this is a saturated site. Furthermore it is pullback-stable.

Example 8.22
.

Recall the jointly epimorphic coverage
(\mathbf{FinSet},j_{\text{epi}})
from Example
2.8
. From Example
6.4
, we know that this coverage is saturated. It is easy to check that it is also pullback-stable, and hence also a Grothendieck pretopology. By Proposition
8.7
, it is also canonical.

Let us also consider the singleton coverage
j_{\text{sepi}}
15
15

15

sepi stands for singleton epi.
where a covering family consists of a single surjective function
r=\{f:S^{\prime}\to S\}
. If
\{r_{i}:S_{i}\to S\}
is a
j_{\text{epi}}
-covering family of a set
S
, then it can be refined by the map
\pi:\sumop\displaylimits_{i}S_{i}\to S
. Hence
j_{\text{sepi}}\leq j_{\text{epi}}
and clearly
j_{\text{epi}}\leq j_{\text{sepi}}
. Thus the two coverages are equivalent.

Note that every
j_{\text{epi}}
-sheaf
X
is completely determined by its value on a singleton
X(*)=A
. This is because every set has a finest covering family given by the inclusion of all of its elements
\{x:*\hookrightarrow S\}_{x\in S}
, and all of its intersections are empty. So
X(S)=X(\sumop\displaylimits_{x\in S}*)\cong\prodop\displaylimits_{x\in S}X(*)\cong S^{A}
. From this it is not hard to prove that
\mathbf{Sh}(\mathbf{FinSet},j_{\text{epi}})\cong\mathbf{Set}
.

If
(\mathcal{C},j)
is a site and
U\in\mathcal{C}
, then
j(U)
always has a terminal object given by the family
(1_{U})
. However, it is far more rare for
j(U)
to have an initial object. The next example demonstrates when each
j(U)
has an initial object. Having such an initial object (which we might also just call think of as the “most refined cover of
U
”), can be very useful, especially when considering sheafification, see Section
7.4
.

Example 8.23
.

We say that a topology
\tau
on a set
X
is
Alexandrov
if arbitrary intersections of open sets are open. We say a topological space equipped with an Alexandrov topology is an Alexandrov space. Given a preorder
(P,\leq)
, we can equip
P
with a canonical topology
\tau_{\text{Alex}}
whose open sets are the up-sets of
P
, i.e. those subsets
U\subseteq P
such that if
x\in U
and
x\leq y
, then
y\in U
. This makes
(P,\tau_{\text{Alex}})
into an Alexandrov space. In fact, every Alexandrov space can be constructed in this way, see
[
Asn18
]
. This means that in particular for every
x\in P
, the set
(\uparrow x)=\{y\in P\,:\,x\leq y\}
is open. That means that there is a “most refined cover”
\text{min}(P)=\{(\uparrow x)\subseteq P\}_{x\in P}
of
P
. In other words, given any object
U\in\mathcal{O}(P)
,
j_{P}(U)
has an initial object given by
\text{min}(U)=\{(\uparrow x)\subseteq U\}_{x\in U}
.

Example 8.24
.

Let
P
be a preorder equipped with the Alexandrov topology (Example
8.23
), and consider its corresponding site
(\mathcal{O}(P),j_{P})
. Let us define a functor
\varphi:\mathbf{Sh}(\mathcal{O}(P),j_{P})\to\text{Fun}(P,\mathbf{Set})
by setting
\varphi(X)(x)=X((\uparrow x))
. This makes sense because if
x\leq y
, then
(\uparrow y)\subseteq(\uparrow x)
. It turns out (
[
Cur14
, Theorem 4.2.10]
) that this functor
\varphi
is an equivalence, with quasi-inverse
\psi:\text{Fun}(P,\mathbf{Set})\to\mathbf{Sh}(\mathcal{O}(P),j_{P})
given by

\psi(F)(U)\coloneqq\lim_{x\in U}F(x).

This can be equivalently described as follows, if
F:P\to\mathbf{Set}
is a functor, then
\psi(F)
is the right Kan extension of
F
along the functor
\uparrow:P\to\mathcal{O}(P)^{\text{op}}
given by
x\mapsto(\uparrow x)
. In other words, for preorders
P
we have

\text{Fun}(P,\mathbf{Set})\cong\mathbf{Sh}(\mathcal{O}(P),j_{P}).

This observation is the starting point for the theory of
cellular sheaves
, see
[
Cur14
]
for a detailed introduction to this subject.

Example 8.25
.

A
graph
16
16

16

Often, these are called simple graphs.

G
consists of a finite set
V(G)
and a set
E(G)
of subsets of
V(G)
of cardinality
2
. A morphism of graphs
f:G\to H
is a function
V(f):V(G)\to V(H)
such that if
\{x,y\}\in E(G)
, then
\{f(x),f(y)\}\in E(H)
. Let
\mathbf{Gr}
denote the small category
17
17

17

In other words, let
\mathbf{Gr}
denote a small skeleton of the category of graphs.
of graphs. Let us define a collection of families
j_{\mathbf{Gr}}
on
\mathbf{Gr}
as follows. If
G
is a graph, then we say that a family
\{r_{i}:G_{i}\to G\}_{i\in I}
is covering if each
r_{i}
is a monomorphism (it is injective on vertices), and furthermore
\cup_{i\in I}r_{i}(G_{i})\cong G
. It is not hard to see that this coverage is saturated and pullback-stable. This site has been shown to be useful in algorithmics, see
[
Alt+23
]
. We note that for each graph
G
,
j_{\mathbf{Gr}}(G)
has an initial object given by the cover consisting of all the vertices and edges of
G
. Hence a
j_{\mathbf{Gr}}
-sheaf is completely determined by its values on a vertex
K^{1}
and an edge
K^{2}
18
18

18

K^{n}
is the complete graph on
n
vertices, i.e.
V(K^{n})=\{1,\dots,n\}
and every pair of distinct vertices is connected by an edge.
.

Example 8.26
.

Let
\mathbf{Man}
denote the small category
19
19

19

See Example
A.27
whose objects are finite dimensional smooth manifolds and whose morphisms are smooth functions. Define a collection of families
j_{\text{open}}
on
\mathbf{Man}
as follows: For
M\in\mathbf{Man}
, let
j_{\text{open}}(M)
denote the collection of open covers of
M
. In other words a family
\{r_{i}:U_{i}\to M\}
is a
j_{\text{open}}
-covering family if and only if
U_{i}
is an open subset of
M
,
r_{i}
is the inclusion map, and
\cup_{i}\,U_{i}=M
. Then
j_{\text{open}}
is a coverage. Indeed if
\{U_{i}\subseteq M\}
is an open cover and
f:N\to M
is a smooth map, then
f^{-1}(U_{i})
is a smooth open submanifold of
N
(in fact it is a pullback of
f
and the inclusion, even though not all pullbacks exist in
\mathbf{Man}
) for each
i
, and
\{f^{-1}(U_{i})\subseteq N\}
is an open cover of
N
. It is easy to see that
(\mathbf{Man},j_{\text{open}})
is a Grothendieck pretopology. However it is not refinement closed.

We can similarly define another coverage
j_{\text{emb}}
on
\mathbf{Man}
as follows. Say a family
\{r_{i}:U_{i}\to M\}
of morphisms is a
j_{\text{emb}}
-covering family if and only if each
r_{i}:U_{i}\to M
is an open embedding
20
20

20

This means that it is an immersion, and the underlying map of topological spaces is a homeomorphism onto its image.
, and
\cup_{i}\,r_{i}(U_{i})=M
. It is easy to see that
j_{\text{emb}}\leq j_{\text{open}}
and
j_{\text{open}}\leq j_{\text{emb}}
. Hence they define the same sheaves.

Example 8.27
.

Now consider the following full subcategories

\mathbf{Cart}\hookrightarrow\mathbf{Open}\hookrightarrow\mathbf{Man},

where
\mathbf{Cart}
is the full subcategory of
\mathbf{Man}
whose objects are cartesian spaces, i.e. manifolds diffeomorphic to
\mathbb{R}^{n}
for some
n\geq 0
, and
\mathbf{Open}
is the full subcategory whose objects are diffeomorphic to open subsets of a cartesian space. We obtain induced coverages (Example
2.13
)
j_{\text{emb}}|_{\mathbf{Open}},j_{\text{open}}|_{\mathbf{Open}}.

Notice however that if we restrict
j_{\text{emb}},j_{\text{open}}
to
\mathbf{Cart}
, then covers must consist of cartesian spaces. However if
\{U_{i}\subseteq U\}
is a cartesian open cover and
f:V\to U
is a smooth map, there is no reason that
\{f^{-1}(U_{i})\subseteq V\}
will be a cartesian open cover. However as we will see in Example
8.29
, every open cover can be refined by a cartesian open cover, and thus we obtain induced coverages
j_{\text{emb}}|_{\mathbf{Cart}},j_{\text{open}}|_{\mathbf{Cart}}
on
\mathbf{Cart}
. However, because
f^{-1}(U_{i})
does not necessarily exist in
\mathbf{Cart}
, the sites
(\mathbf{Cart},j_{\text{emb}}|_{\mathbf{Cart}})
and
(\mathbf{Cart},j_{\text{open}}|_{\mathbf{Cart}})
are not Grothendieck pretopologies. Each site
(\mathcal{C},j)
with
\mathcal{C}\in\{\mathbf{Cart},\mathbf{Open},\mathbf{Man}\}
and
j\in\{j_{\text{emb}},j_{\text{open}}\}
is composition closed, but not necessarily refinement closed, simply because arbitrary smooth maps refined by injective maps are not necessarily injective.

Example 8.28
.

Let
j_{\text{sub}}
denote the collection of families on
\mathbf{Man}
where for
M\in\mathbf{Man}
, a family
\{f:N\to M\}\in j_{\text{sub}}(M)
consists of a single surjective submersion
21
21

21

This is a smooth map of manifolds that is surjective, and whose map on every tangent space is surjective.
. We note that
j_{\text{sub}}
is not equivalent to
j_{\text{open}}
. Indeed, consider the constant presheaf
\underline{\mathbb{Z}}
on
\mathbf{Man}
that assigns to every manifold the set of integers and the to every morphism the identity map. We note that the empty family
\varnothing
is a cover of each empty manifold
\varnothing_{n}
22
22

22

There is one empty manifold of every dimension, with empty atlas.
in
j_{\text{open}}
, but it is not a cover in
j_{\text{sub}}
. By Lemma
2.21
,
\underline{\mathbb{Z}}
is not a sheaf on
(\mathbf{Man},j_{\text{open}})
, but it is not hard to check that it is a sheaf on
(\mathbf{Man},j_{\text{sub}})
. We learned of this counterexample from
[
Wal24
, Warning 5.1.4]
23
23

23

We should also mention that this paper of Waldorf’s is an excellent complement to these notes, but that it uses a different notion of equivalence of coverages, and even a different notion of sheaves! However, with these different notions, Waldorf proves that
j_{\text{sub}}
and
j_{\text{open}}
are equivalent.
. We also note that pullbacks of surjective submersions along arbitrary maps exist in
\mathbf{Man}
, and it is thus not hard to show that
(\mathbf{Man},j_{\text{sub}})
is a Grothendieck pretopology.

Example 8.29
.

Given a finite dimensional smooth manifold
M
, we say that an open cover
\mathcal{U}=\{U_{i}\subseteq M\}_{i\in I}
is
good
if every nonempty finite intersection
U_{i_{1}}\cap\dots U_{i_{n}}
of the open subsets is diffeomorphic to some
\mathbb{R}^{n}
. Let
j_{\text{good}}
denote the collection of families on
\mathbf{Man}
given by the good open covers. Let us show that the good covers form a coverage. If
\{U_{i}\subseteq M\}
is a good cover and
g:N\to M
a smooth map, then
\{g^{-1}(U_{i})\subseteq N\}
is an open cover, but not necessarily good. By
[
Bot82
, Corollary 5.2]
, this open cover can be refined by a good open cover
\{W_{k}\subseteq N\}
so that for every
W_{k}
in the good open cover, there exists a
U_{i}
such that
W_{k}\subseteq g^{-1}(U_{i})
, and thus the following diagram commutes:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{W_{k}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{g^{-1}(U_{i})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{U_{i}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces N}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g|_{g^{-1}(U_{i})}}

Thus
j_{\text{good}}
is a coverage on
\mathbf{Man}
. We can similarly define such induced coverages on
\mathbf{Cart}
and
\mathbf{Open}
. We note that none of these coverages are composition closed. This was proven by David Roberts in
[
Rob24
]
.

By taking
g
to be the identity in the diagram above, we see that
j_{\text{good}}\leq j_{\text{open}}
on each category
\mathcal{C}\in\{\mathbf{Cart},\mathbf{Open},\mathbf{Man}\}
, and clearly
j_{\text{open}}\leq j_{\text{good}}
. Hence the three coverages
j_{\text{good}},j_{\text{emb}},j_{\text{open}}
are all equivalent on each category
\mathcal{C}\in\{\mathbf{Cart},\mathbf{Open},\mathbf{Man}\}
.

Example 8.30

(
[
MM12
, Section III.9]
)
.

Let
G
be a topological group. A (right)
G
-action on a set
S
consists of a discrete group action such that the map
\rho:S\times G\to S
is continuous when
S
is given the discrete topology. Let
\text{Sub}_{G}
denote the category whose objects are
G
-sets of the form
G/U
, where
U
is an open subgroup of
G
. In other words, the objects are right cosets of the form
Ug
, and morphisms are
G
-equivariant maps
f:G/U\to G/V
. The existence of such a map turns out to be equivalent to the existence of an element
h\in G
such that
U\subseteq hVh^{-1}
. So we can identify
\text{Sub}_{G}
with the category of open subgroups
U\subseteq G
and morphisms
h:U\to V
are elements
h\in G
such that
U\subseteq hVh^{-1}
, and composition is given by multiplication. Since open subgroups are closed under intersection, it is not hard to check that
\text{Sub}_{G}
satisfies the Ore condition (Definition
8.16
), and hence we can consider the atomic Grothendieck coverage
J_{\text{at}}
on
\text{Sub}_{G}
. By
[
MM12
, Theorem III.9.1]
, the category
\mathbf{Sh}(\text{Sub}_{G},J_{\text{at}})
of sheaves on the atomic site of
\text{Sub}_{G}
is equivalent to the category all
G
-sets. Hence the category of
G
-sets is a Grothendieck topos.

Example 8.31
.

We present a collection of families
j_{\text{Pav}}
on the category
\mathbf{Cart}
that we call the
Pavlov collection
. It was introduced by Dmitri Pavlov in
[
Pav22
, Theorem 4.11, Remark 4.12]
with the motivation of finding simpler conditions to check when a simplicial presheaf on
\mathbf{Cart}
is an
\infty
-stack. The only non-identity family in
j_{\text{Pav}}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
is the family of subset inclusions

\{(4i,4i+3)\times\mathbb{R}^{n-1}\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{n},(4i+2,4i+5)\times\mathbb{R}^{n-1}\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{n}\}_{i\in\mathbb{Z}}.

Pavlov proves that this collection generates (in the sense of Remark
6.24
the same Grothendieck topology as
(\mathbf{Cart},j_{\text{open}})
. While this collection might seem odd, its characterization leads to the remarkable result
[
Pav22
, Proposition 4.13]
.

Example 8.32
.

Let
\mathbb{C}\mathbf{Man}
denote the category of finite dimensional complex manifolds and holomorphic maps. This is an essentially small category, using a similar argument
24
24

24

Now one just needs to bound the size of all
as in Example
A.28
. Let
j_{\text{open}}
be the collection of families given by open covers. It is easy to see that
j_{\text{open}}
is a coverage, and in fact a Grothendieck pretopology.

There is a similar chain of full subcategory inclusions as in
8.27
. First we need some definitions. We say that a complex manifold
M
is a Stein manifold if there exists an injective, proper, holomorphic, immersion
f:M\to\mathbb{C}^{n}
for some
n\geq 0
. Equivalently,
M
is Stein if and only if it is biholomorphically equivalent to a closed complex submanifold of
\mathbb{C}^{n}
. There are many other equivalent ways to define Stein manifolds, see
[
For11
, Chapter 2]
. A key property of Stein manifolds is that they have trivial Dolbeault cohomology
[
For11
, Theorem 2.4.6]
. In complex geometry, Stein manifolds are analogous to Cartesian spaces (Example
8.27
).

A polydisk is a complex manifold of the form

D_{k_{1}\dots k_{n}}=\{(z_{1},\dots,z_{n})\in\mathbb{C}^{n}\,:\,|z_{i}|<k_{i}\}

with each
k_{i}>0
. Polydisks are Stein manifolds.

There are full subcategory inclusions

\mathbb{C}\mathbf{Disk}\hookrightarrow\mathbf{Stein}\hookrightarrow\mathbb{C}\mathbf{Man},

where
\mathbf{Stein}
is the full subcategory of
\mathbb{C}\mathbf{Man}
on the Stein manifolds, and
\mathbb{C}\mathbf{Disk}
is the full subcategory on the polydisks.

If
f:M\to N
is a map of complex manifolds and
U\hookrightarrow N
is an open subset that is a Stein manifold, then by
[
Lár03
, Lemma 4.1]
,
f^{-1}(U)
is a Stein open subset of
M
25
25

25

This lemma also proves that finite intersections of Stein open subsets is Stein, hence making this coverage analogous to
j_{\text{good}}
from Example
8.29
. Hence restricting
j_{\text{open}}
to
\mathbf{Stein}
, we obtain an induced coverage
j_{\text{open}}|_{\mathbf{Stein}}
.

Now suppose that
f:U\to V
is a map of polydisks of complex dimension
n
and
m
respectively and
\mathcal{V}=\{V_{i}\subseteq V\}
is a cover by polydisks. The preimages
U_{i}=f^{-1}(V_{i})
, which assemble into an open cover
\mathcal{U}=\{U_{i}\subseteq U\}
will not necessarily be polydisks, but they will be Stein by
[
Lár03
, Lemma 4.1]
. Furthermore each
U_{i}
will be an open subset of
\mathbb{C}^{n}
. Hence by
[
For11
, Section 2.2, Theorem 2.1.3]
, since
U_{i}
is Stein and an open subset of
\mathbb{C}^{n}
, it is a domain of holomorphy (
[
For11
, Page 45]
), hence it is a domain in
\mathbb{C}^{n}
. Thus by the proof of
[
FS77
, Lemma II.1]
, there exists a refinement
\mathcal{U}^{\prime}\leq\mathcal{U}
where
\mathcal{U}^{\prime}
is an open cover by polydisks. Hence we obtain an induced coverage
j_{\text{open}}|_{\mathbb{C}\mathbf{Disk}}
. Clearly all three of these sites are composition closed, but not refinement closed.

Example 8.33
.

Several coverages were introduced in
[
BW13
]
to study Manifold calculus, which involves Taylor series-like approximations of
\infty
-stacks. The set up is a bit different than in Example
8.27
. Given a fixed
d\geq 0
, let
\mathbf{Man}^{d}
denote the category of
d
-dimensional smooth manifolds and open embeddings. Given a fixed
k\geq 1
, let
j^{k}_{\text{open}}
denote the collection of families on
\mathbf{Man}^{d}
where for
M\in\mathbf{Man}^{d}
, a family
r
on
M
belongs to
j^{k}(M)
if it is an open cover
\mathcal{U}=\{U_{i}\subseteq M\}
such that for every collection of
\ell\leq k
distinct points
p_{1},\dots,p_{\ell}
in
M
, there exists a
U_{i}
such that
p_{1},\dots,p_{\ell}\in U_{i}
. We call this a
k
-cover
of
M
. Note that
j^{1}_{\text{open}}=j_{\text{open}}
, and
j^{n}_{\text{open}}\subseteq j^{m}_{\text{open}}
for
m\leq n
. The coverage
j^{k}_{\text{open}}
is called the
k
th
Weiss coverage
. For each
k\geq 1
, these coverages are composition closed. Indeed, suppose that
M
is a
d
-manifold, and suppose that
\mathcal{U}=\{U_{i}\subseteq M\}_{i\in I}
is a
k
-cover of
M
and for each
U_{i}
there is a
k
-cover
\mathcal{V}^{i}=\{V^{i}_{\alpha}\subseteq U_{i}\}
. If
p_{1},\dots,p_{\ell}
are a collection of distinct points with
\ell\leq k
, then there exists some
U_{i}
such that
p_{1},\dots,p_{\ell}\in U_{i}
. But since
\mathcal{V}^{i}
is a
k
-cover, this means that there exists a
V^{i}_{\alpha}
such that
p_{1},\dots,p_{\ell}\in V^{i}_{\alpha}
. Hence the composite cover is also a
k
-cover. From here it is easy to check that
j^{k}_{\text{open}}
is a Grothendieck pretopology on
\mathbf{Man}
for every
k\geq 1
.

There is an analogous coverage
j^{k}_{\text{good}}
, which is not composition closed, defined in
[
BW13
, Definition 2.9]
. There is also a coverage
j^{k}_{h}
, defined in
[
BW13
, Definition 2.5]
given by
k
-coverings of the form
\{M\setminus A_{i}\hookrightarrow M\}_{i\in 0,\dots,k}
, where
A_{0},\dots,A_{k}\subseteq M
are disjoint, closed subsets of
M
. It is shown in
[
BW13
, Remark 2.13, Section 5,7]
that while
j^{k}_{\text{good}}
and
j^{k}_{h}
are not equivalent to
j^{k}_{\text{open}}
, they do have the same
\infty
-stacks, and are therefore in a sense
\infty
-equivalent.

Example 8.34
.

Given a meet-semilattice
P
, let
j_{\text{meet}}
denote the collection of families where a family
r=\{U_{i}\leq U\}_{i\in I}\in j_{\text{pos}}(U)
if and only if for every
U^{\prime}\leq U
,
U^{\prime}
there exists a subset
S\subseteq P
such that for every
V\in S
,
V\leq U^{\prime}
and there is some
i\in I
such that
V\leq U_{i}
.

Let us show that this collection of families is a coverage. Suppose that
\{U_{i}\leq U\}
is a covering family and
U^{\prime}\leq U
. Then there exists a
S\subseteq P
such that for all
V\in S
,
V\leq U^{\prime}
and
V\leq U_{i}
for some
i
. Therefore we need only show that
S\in j_{\text{pos}}(U^{\prime})
. So suppose that
U^{\prime\prime}\leq U^{\prime}
. Then the family
\{U^{\prime\prime}\wedge V\subseteq U^{\prime\prime}\}_{V\in S}
satisfies the desired property. Thus
j_{\text{meet}}
is a coverage, which we call the
meet coverage
.

If
P
is a frame
26
26

26

i.e. has small joins, finite meets and small joins distribute over finite meets.
, then the collection of families
j_{\text{frm}}
on
P
where
r=\{U_{i}\leq U\}_{i\in I}\in j_{\text{frm}}(U)
if and only if
U
is a join of
r
, is a coverage. Indeed, if
U^{\prime}\leq U
, then
U^{\prime}
is a join of
\{U^{\prime}\wedge U_{i}\leq U^{\prime}\}
since
\bigveeop\displaylimits U^{\prime}\wedge U_{i}=U^{\prime}\wedge\bigveeop\displaylimits U_{i}=U^{\prime}\wedge U=U^{\prime}
. We call this the
frame coverage
.

Example 8.35
.

We say that a category
\mathcal{C}
is (infinitary)
lextensive
if it has finite limits and Van Kampen (small) finite coproducts (Definition
11.22
). Let
j_{\text{lex}}
denote the collection of families of the form
r=\{r_{i}:U_{i}\hookrightarrow U\}_{i\in I}
, where
I
is a (small) finite set, and such that the
r_{i}
define a cocone, i.e. each
r_{i}
is the inclusion into a coproduct
U\cong\sumop\displaylimits_{i}U_{i}
. Since coproducts are Van Kampen, this implies that the families in
j_{\text{lex}}
are stable under pullback, and hence defines a coverage, called the
lextensive coverage
.

Example 8.36

(
[
Rob12
, Definition A.4]
)
.

Let
\mathcal{C}
be a lextensive category. We say that a Grothendieck pretopology
j
on
\mathcal{C}
is
superextensive
if
j_{\text{lex}}\subseteq j
. We note that the (large) site
(\mathbf{Top},j_{\text{emb}})
from Example
8.58
is superextensive. Given a superextensive site
(\mathcal{C},j)
, let
\sumop\displaylimits j
denote the collection of singleton families on
\mathcal{C}
of the form
\{\sumop\displaylimits_{i\in I}r_{i}:\sumop\displaylimits_{i\in I}U_{i}\to U\}
where
\{r_{i}:U_{i}\to U\}_{i\in I}\in j(U)
. In
[
Rob12
, Proposition A.8]
it is proved that this is a singleton pretopology on
\mathcal{C}
and is subcanonical if and only if
j
is.

Example 8.37
.

We say that a category
\mathcal{C}
is
regular
if

(1)

it is finitely complete,

(2)

it has all kernel pairs (Definition
A.59
), and

(3)

regular epimorphisms (Definition
A.58
) are stable under pullbacks.

For a small, regular category
\mathcal{C}
, let
j_{\text{reg}}
denote the collection of families consisting of regular epimorphisms
r=\{f:U\to V\}
. Since these are stable under pullback, they form a coverage. We call this the
regular coverage
on
\mathcal{C}
. Since all epimorphisms are regular on
\mathbf{FinSet}
(Lemma
A.64
),
(\mathbf{FinSet},j_{\text{reg}})=(\mathbf{FinSet},j_{\text{sepi}})
.

Note that a wide class of categories is regular. These include algebraic categories (like
\mathbf{Grp}
,
\mathbf{Ring}
, etc.), abelian categories and quasitoposes
27
27

27

Though of course with the usual caveat that these sites are usually essentially large, see Section
8.4
.
.

The regular coverage on a regular category is a Grothendieck pretopology, as regular epimorphisms are closed under composition in regular categories.

Example 8.38
.

A
coherent category

\mathcal{C}
is a regular category (Example
8.37
) in which the subobject poset
\text{Sub}(U)
for all
U\in\mathcal{C}
has finite joins (which in this case are called unions), and for which the pullback functor
\text{Sub}(f):\text{Sub}(V)\to\text{Sub}(U)
for
f:U\to V
preserves finite joins. We say that
\mathcal{C}
is a
geometric category
if
\text{Sub}(U)
has all small joins and the pullback functors preserve small joins. Every quasitopos and topos is a coherent category.

Given a coherent category
\mathcal{C}
define the (potentially large) collection of families
j_{\text{coh}}
so that a family is
j_{\text{coh}}
-covering if it is of the form
r=\{r_{i}:U_{i}\to U\}_{i\in I}
where
I
is finite and
\bigcupop\displaylimits_{i}\,\text{im}(r_{i})=U
. In regular categories, images are pullback-stable, and in coherent categories unions of images are pullback-stable, hence
j_{\text{coh}}
is a pullback-stable coverage. It is not hard to see that it is also composition-closed and is furthermore a Grothendieck pretopology. We call this the
coherent coverage
, and if the families are allowed to be small, then we call it the
geometric coverage
.

Johnstone shows in
[
Joh02
, Example C.2.1.12.(d)]
that the coherent and geometric coverages are subcanonical.

Example 8.39
.

Let us briefly recall the idea of a first-order theory
\mathbb{T}
. First, a language
\Sigma{T}
consist of sets of sorts, function symbols and relation symbols. For example, the language for the theory of groups consists of a single sort
G
, two function symbols given by multiplication
m:(G,G)\to G
and inversion
i:G\to G
, and no relation symbols.

Given a language, one can then define the set of terms one can build. An example of a term for the language of groups is
x,y:G\,|\,m(x,m(y,i(x))):G
. The lefthand symbols form the context of the term, they are the variables being used. Formulas are then built from relation symbols in
\Sigma_{\mathbb{T}}
and terms. For example, let
\Sigma_{\mathbb{T}}
be the language of posets. This has one sort
P
, no function symbols, and one relation symbol
\leq
. Then an example of a formula for this language is
x,y:P\,|\,(x\leq y)\Rightarrow(\forall z.(z\leq x)\Rightarrow(z\leq y))
.

By restricting the usage of certain first-order logical symbols like
\wedge,\vee,\Rightarrow,\neg,\exists,\forall
, we obtain particular types of theories (such as cartesian, regular, coherent, geometric, etc.), see
[
Joh02
, Section D1.1]
for a list of different kinds of theories.

A sequent
\varphi\vdash\psi
consists of a pair of formulas in the same context. This represents that
\varphi
“implies”
\psi
. A theory
\mathbb{T}
then consists of a language
\Sigma_{\mathbb{T}}
and a set
S_{\mathbb{T}}
of sequents of formulas over the language. Using first-order logic, we obtain deduction rules that say when one sequent implies another, see
[
Joh02
, Section D1.3]
.

From a theory
\mathbb{T}
, one can construct a category
\mathbf{Syn}(\mathbb{T})
, called the
syntactic category
of
\mathbb{T}
. Its objects are certain equivalence classes of formulas, and morphisms are certain kinds of formulas which act intuitively as functions between the “sets” defined by the formulas. Based on the type of theory
\mathbb{T}
, the syntactic category
\mathbf{Syn}(\mathbb{T})
will have the corresponding kind of structure (cartesian, regular, coherent, geometric), see
[
Joh02
, Lemma D1.4.10]
. Each such structure has its own corresponding canonical site structure (cartesian categories are equipped with a trivial coverage) such as in Examples
8.37
and
8.38
, which are called the
syntactic sites
. The category of sheaves on a syntactic site
\mathbf{Sh}(\mathbf{Syn}(\mathbb{T}),j)
is called the
classifying topos
for the theory
\mathbb{T}
. Geometric morphisms
\mathbf{Sh}(\mathbf{Syn}(\mathbb{T}),j)\to\mathcal{E}
of toposes are equivalently models of
\mathbb{T}
in
\mathcal{E}
, see
[
MM12
, Section VIII]
for more on classifying toposes.

Example 8.40
.

We mention that the above idea of a syntactic site extends naturally to type theories, and to syntactic categories of type theories. Sheaves on such syntactic categories have been used to solve important problems in computer science, such as in
[
Alt+01
]
, where a normalization result is proved for a typed lambda calculus with coproducts. The study of sheaves for computer science in this way is sometimes referred to as
sheaf semantics
.

Example 8.41

(
[
MM12
, Exercise III.9.13]
)
.

Let
\mathbf{FinSet}_{m}
denote the small category of finite sets and monomorphisms. Then the category
\mathbf{FinSet}_{m}^{\text{op}}
satisfies the Ore condition (Definition
8.16
), since
\mathbf{FinSet}_{m}
has pushouts. Let
(\mathbf{FinSet}_{m}^{\text{op}},J_{\text{at}})
denote the atomic site (Definition
8.14
), then
\mathbf{Sh}(\mathbf{FinSet}_{m}^{\text{op}},J_{\text{at}})
is known as the
Shanuel topos
. Its objects are called nominal sets, which give a formal framework for bound variables and alpha equivalence in programming languages, see
[
Pit13
]
for more.

Example 8.42
.

Let
\tau
denote the full subcategory of
\mathbf{Top}
on the topological spaces
*
, the terminal object, and
\mathbb{N}_{\infty}
, the one-point compactification of the discrete natural numbers (equivalently it is homeomorphic to the
\{1,1/2,1/3,\dots,1/n,\dots,0\}\subseteq\mathbb{R}
with the subspace topology). Consider the canonical site
(\tau,J_{\text{can}})
, see Definition
8.3
. Sheaves on this site
(\tau,J_{\text{can}})
form what is called the
topological topos
, introduced by Johnstone in
[
Joh79
]
. The motivation for this topos, similarly to the other convenient categories mentioned in Example
8.58
, is to provide a convenient category in which to do topology. The category
\mathbf{Sh}(\tau,J_{\text{can}})
contains all sequential topological spaces
28
28

28

A topological space
X
is sequential if for every topological space
Y
a map
f:X\to Y
is continuous if and only if it is sequentially continuous, i.e. if
x_{n}\to x
then
f(x_{n})\to f(x)
.
as a reflective subcategory. For more about the topological topos, we recommend the series of blog posts
[
Gro24
]
.

Example 8.43
.

A
bornology
on a set
X
is a cover of
X
that is closed under subsets and finite unions. A set with a bornology is called a bornological space, and the elements of its bornology are called bounded subsets. Their invention is attributed to Mackey
[
Mac42
]
, and their history is reviewed in the french paper
[
Hog70
]
. Bornological spaces are a convenient framework for functional analysis, where all kinds of subtle problems arise from considering linear operators between locally convex topological vector spaces, with an emphasis on bounded subsets being more central to the theory than open subsets. Lawvere first described the bornological topos during unpublished talks he gave at Bogotá in 1983. Let
\mathbf{Cnt}
denote the small category of countable sets and functions. This category is lextensive, and the category of sheaves
\mathbf{Sh}(\mathbf{Cnt},j_{\text{lex}})
with the lextensive coverage (Example
8.35
) is the bornological topos. There is a full subcategory of bornological spaces given as a reflective subcategory of the bornological topos
[
EL02
]
.

Example 8.44
.

The recursive topos, or the topos of recursive sets, was defined by Mulry in
[
Mul82
]
with the goal of doing recursion theory in a cartesian closed category. Let
R
denote the monoid of all total recursive functions
f:\mathbb{N}\to\mathbb{N}
. Viewing this as a one-object category, equip it with the coherent coverage
(Example
8.38
)
(R,j_{\text{coh}})
. This turns out to be a canonical coverage as well. Sheaves on this site
\mathbf{Sh}(R,j_{\text{coh}})
form the recursive topos.

8.3.
Concrete Sheaves

Now let us consider several important examples in the literature that are not quite sheaf toposes. Instead they are quasitoposes of concrete sheaves.

Definition 8.45
.

A site
(\mathcal{C},j)
is
concrete
if

(1)

it has a terminal object
*
,

(2)

the functor
\mathcal{C}(*,-):\mathcal{C}\to\mathbf{Set}
is faithful, and

(3)

for every
j
-covering family
r=\{r_{i}:U_{i}\to U\}
, the function

\sumop\displaylimits_{i}\mathcal{C}(*,r_{i}):\sumop\displaylimits_{i}\mathcal{C}(*,U_{i})\to\mathcal{C}(*,U)

is surjective.

A sheaf
X
on a concrete site
(\mathcal{C},j)
is said to be
concrete
if the canonical function

X(U)\to\mathbf{Set}(\mathcal{C}(*,U),X(*))

obtained by applying
\mathcal{C}(*,-)\cong\mathbf{Pre}(\mathcal{C})(y(*),-)
to the map
y(U)\to X
is injective.

Definition 8.46
.

A
Grothendieck quasitopos

\mathcal{E}
is a category equivalent to one of the form
\mathbf{Sep}(\mathbf{Sh}(\mathcal{C},j),k)
. Namely it is a category equivalent to the category of
j
-sheaves on a site
(\mathcal{C},j)
which are also
k
-separated (Definition
2.18
) with respect to another coverage
k
on
\mathcal{C}
such that
\text{Gro}(j)\subseteq\text{Gro}(k)
.

Remark 8.47
.

Grothendieck quasitoposes, introduced in
[
BP91
]
, are very nice categories, close to being Grothendieck toposes in their properties. For example, Grothendieck quasitoposes are (small) complete, cocomplete, locally cartesian closed and locally presentable (Definiiton
C.31
). In fact, if a Grothendieck quasitopos
\mathcal{E}
is balanced
29
29

29

A category is balanced if every morphism
f:U\to V
which is a monomorphism and an epimorphism is an isomorphism.
then it is a Grothendieck topos. We recommend the following references
[
BH11
,
Wyl91
,
GL12
]
for more on Grothendieck quasitoposes.

Lemma 8.48
.

Given a concrete site
(\mathcal{C},j)
, let
k
denote the collection of families of the form
\{x:*\to U\}_{x\in\mathcal{C}(*,U)}
. A sheaf
X
on
(\mathcal{C},j)
is then a concrete sheaf if and only if it is separated with respect to
k
.

Proof.

Let us note that
\text{Gro}(j)\subseteq\text{Gro}(k)
by Definition
8.45
.(3). Then being
k
-separated is precisely what it means for a sheaf to be concrete.
∎

Corollary 8.49
.

Given a concrete site
(\mathcal{C},j)
, the full subcategory
\mathbf{ConSh}(\mathcal{C},j)\hookrightarrow\mathbf{Sh}(\mathcal{C},j)
of concrete sheave is a Grothendieck quasitopos.

Example 8.50
.

Diffeological spaces were introduced by Souriau in
[
Sou80
]
, and developed by his student Iglesias-Zemmour, leading to the textbook
[
Igl13
]
. The motivation here is to have a convenient category for doing differential geometry. It is arguably one of the most successful such frameworks (with competition from synthetic differential geometry, Example
8.61
), being easy to work with and understand, but also powerful enough to extend classical constructions from differential geometry. Originally the definition was given as a set with a collection of plots satisfing three simple conditions. Baez and Hoffnung proved in
[
BH11
]
that the category of diffeological spaces is equivalent to a category of concrete sheaves. More precisely, consider the site
(\mathbf{Open},j_{\text{open}})
from Example
8.26
. Then
[
BH11
, Proposition 24]
shows that the category of diffeological spaces is equivalent to the category of concrete sheaves on
(\mathbf{Open},j_{\text{open}})
. They prove a similar statement for so-called Chen spaces
[
Che77
]
. The author’s PhD thesis consists of two papers
[
Min24
,
Min24a
]
which study the cohomology of diffeological spaces from an
\infty
-stack perspective, which benefits from the sheaf-theoretic approach to differential geometry.

Example 8.51
.

A simplicial complex
K
consists of a set
V(K)
along with a set
S(K)
of nonempty finite subsets
