\sigma
of
V(K)
whose elements are called
simplices
, containing all of the singletons, and such that if
\sigma\in S(K)
and
\tau\subseteq\sigma
, then
\tau\in S(K)
. A map
f:K\to L
of simplicial complexes is a function
V(f):V(K)\to V(L)
such that if
\sigma\in S(K)
, then
f(\sigma)\in S(L)
. Let
\mathbf{Cpx}
denote the category of simplicial complexes. Now consider the small category
\mathbf{FinSet}_{\neq\varnothing}
of nonempty finite sets and functions. The collection of families
j_{\text{inc}}
given by singleton families
\{S\hookrightarrow S^{\prime}\}
of subset inclusions is pullback-stable and hence defines a coverage. However, this coverage is uninteresting, every presheaf on
\mathbf{FinSet}_{\neq\varnothing}
is a
j_{\text{inc}}
-sheaf. It is not hard to check that
(\mathbf{FinSet}_{\neq\varnothing},j_{\text{inc}})
is a concrete site. By
[
BH11
, Proposition 27]
, there is an equivalence of categories
\mathbf{Cpx}\simeq\mathbf{ConSh}(\mathbf{FinSet}_{\neq\varnothing},j_{\text{inc}})
. Hence the category of simplicial complexes is a Grothendieck quasitopos.

Example 8.52
.

One can similarly prove that the categories
\mathbf{Gr}_{\ell}
of loop graphs and
\mathbf{Gr}_{r}
of reflexive graphs are quasitoposes by showing they are categories of concrete presheaves. See
[
Bum+25
, Appendix A]
for more details.

Example 8.53
.

Recall from Example
8.42
the site
(\tau,J_{\text{can}})
. This site is concrete, and the concrete sheaves on this site are precisely the subsequential spaces, see
[
Har80
, Chapter IV]
.

Example 8.54
.

Quasi-topological spaces were introduced by Spanier in
[
Spa63
]
as a convenient category of topological spaces. Let
\mathbf{CH}
denote the full subcategory of
\mathbf{Top}
on the compact Hausdorff spaces. This category is very well-behaved, being a pretopos
[
MR20
]
. In particular it is a coherent category. Consider the large site
(\mathbf{CH},j_{\text{coh}})
with the coherent coverage (Example
8.38
). We note that while
\mathbf{Sh}(\mathbf{CH},j_{\text{coh}})
has large hom-sets, as we will discuss later in this section, the full subcategory
\mathbf{ConSh}(\mathbf{CH},j_{\text{coh}})
has small hom-sets, which is easy to see by the definition of concrete sheaves, and this category is equivalent to the category of quasi-topological spaces. We also recommend the paper
[
Dub06
]
for more on this example.

Example 8.55
.

Let
\mathbf{Borel}
denote the small category of standard Borel spaces and measurable functions between them, see
[
Heu+17
, Section II.A]
. We say that a family
\{b_{i}:B_{i}\to B\}
in this category is a covering family if and only if it is a countable coproduct. This gives a pullback-stable collection of families, and hence it is a coverage. It is an instance of an infinitary lextensive coverage (Example
8.35
). The concrete sheaves on the site
(\mathbf{Borel},j_{\mathbf{lex}})
are called Quasi-Borel spaces, and are a convenient category to work in for higher-order probability theory, see
[
Heu+17
]
.

8.4.
Large Sites

8.4.1.
Examples of Large Sites

The astute reader may have noticed that we did not include any examples involving the category
\mathbf{Top}
of topological spaces. The reason for this is simple,
\mathbf{Top}
is not an essentially small category. Therefore according to Definition
2.6
, it cannot be a site. This is a very restrictive and unfortunate state of affairs, and in this section we will discuss some ways of working around the smallness condition. We recommend the reader look at Section
A.1
before reading this section.

First, let us consider what can go wrong by augmenting Definition
2.6
to allow for collections of families to consist of large sets of morphisms. Let us say a category
\mathcal{C}
is
essentially large
if it is large and not essentially small.

Definition 8.56
.

A
large collection of families

j
on a large category
\mathcal{C}
consists of a large set
j(U)
whose elements are large sets of morphisms
r=\{r_{i}:U_{i}\to U\}_{i\in I}
. The definitions of coverage, refinement closure, composition closure, sifted closure, Grothendieck pretopology, Grothendieck coverage, matching family, amalgmation and sheaf are defined in the same way using large collections of families. We call
(\mathcal{C},j)
a
large site
if
j
is a large coverage and
\mathcal{C}
is essentially large.

There is no real “problem” with Definition
8.56
, in the sense that we can define everything as we have before. It makes perfect sense to talk about a single sheaf on a large site. However the category
\mathbf{Sh}(\mathcal{C},j)
of sheaves on a large site is less well-behaved than on small sites. Before discussing these disadvantages, let us see some examples of large sites.

Example 8.57
.

The category
\mathbf{Set}
of small sets is an essentially large category. Let
j_{\text{epi}}
denote the large collection of families where for a set
S
,
j(S)
denotes the large set of (small) families
r=\{r_{i}:S_{i}\to S\}_{i\in I}
that are jointly epimorphic, i.e.
\sumop\displaylimits_{i\in I}S_{i}\to S
is surjective. This defines a saturated, pullback-stable coverage on
\mathbf{Set}
.

Example 8.58
.

Let
\mathbf{Top}
denote the category of topological spaces. Since every set can be considered as a discrete topological space,
\mathbf{Top}
is essentially large. Similarly to Example
8.26
, we can define the coverages
(\mathbf{Top},j_{\text{open}})
of open covers and
(\mathbf{Top},j_{\text{emb}})
of open covers given by topological embeddings. Both of these are Grothendieck pretopologies.

We note that the category
\mathbf{Top}
is not cartesian closed. There are other “convenient” categories of topological spaces that are more often considered in algebraic topology, which are cartesian closed. These include the category
k\mathbf{Top}
of
k
-spaces, the category
\mathbf{CGWH}
of compactly generated weak Hausdorff spaces
30
30

30

Interestingly, the canonical Grothendieck coverages on
\mathbf{CGWH}
and
\mathbf{Top}
differ, see
[
Les19
, Example 5.2.7]
.
, and the category
\Delta\mathbf{Top}
of
\Delta
-generated topological spaces. See
[
Rez17
]
and
[
nLa25
]
. The open cover coverage restricts to all of these full subcategories of
\mathbf{Top}
.

Example 8.59
.

Let
G
be a group, and let
G\mathbf{Set}
denote the category of
G
-sets, i.e. those sets
S
equipped with a left action
\rho:G\times S\to S
, and where a morphism
f:S\to S^{\prime}
is an equivariant function
f(\rho(g,s))=\rho^{\prime}(g,f(s))
. We note that
G\mathbf{Set}
is equivalent to the category
\mathbf{Set}^{G}
, where we think of
G
as a category with one object. Let
j_{\text{G,epi}}
denote the collection of families on
G\mathbf{Set}
where a family
r=\{r_{i}:S_{i}\to S\}
is
j_{\text{G,epi}}
-covering if they are jointly epimorphic, i.e. if the underlying function
\sumop\displaylimits_{i}r_{i}:\sumop\displaylimits_{i}S_{i}\to S
is surjective. In other words, if we let
\pi:G\mathbf{Set}\to\mathbf{Set}
denote the functor that forgets the
G
-action, then
r\in j_{\text{G,epi}}(S)
if and only if
\pi(r)\in j_{\text{epi}}(\pi(S))
. This is a canonical coverage, and it turns out that
\mathbf{Sh}(G\mathbf{Set},j_{\text{G,epi}})
is equivalent to
G\mathbf{Set}
. We will see this as a consequence of Example
8.62
. In fact, the same constructions work if
G
is just a monoid. For an in-depth study of toposes of monoid actions see
[
Rog+21
]
.

Example 8.60
.

The notion of
condensed sets
has recently become a powerful tool in algebraic geometry
[
FS24
,
Man22
]
. The basic idea here is another instance of “convenience.” Here the idea is that the category of topological abelian groups is not an abelian category. Scholze and Clausin develop a category of condensed sets, which form a kind of convenient category of topological spaces, and abelian group objects in this category do form an abelian category, see the lecture notes
[
Sch19
]
. It is interesting to note that setting up this category comes with set-theoretic difficulties, for exactly the reasons we will discuss later in this section. Let
\mathbf{Pro}(\mathbf{FinSet})
denote the category of pro-objects in the category of finite sets. This is the full subcategory of
(\mathbf{Set}^{\mathbf{FinSet}})^{\text{op}}
on those functors
P:\mathbf{FinSet}\to\mathbf{Set}
that are cofiltered limits of representables. Via Stone duality, see
[
Joh82
]
, this category is equivalent to the full subcategory
\mathbf{St}
of
\mathbf{Top}
on the
Stone spaces
, those topological spaces that are compact, Hausdorff and totally disconnected
31
31

31

A nonempty topological space is totally disconnected if its only connected components are singletons.
.

Now the category
\mathbf{St}
of Stone spaces is essentially large. So, to avoid the set-theoretic issues that we will discuss later in this section, Scholze
[
Sch19
, Remark 1.3]
fixes an uncountable strong limit cardinal
32
32

32

These are precisely the same as strongly inaccessible cardinals (Definition
A.14
) without the requirement that
\kappa
be regular.

\kappa
, and lets
\mathbf{St}_{\kappa}
denote the full subcategory of Stone spaces whose underlying sets have cardinality less than
\kappa
. Thus
\mathbf{St}_{\kappa}
is a small category, and by
[
Lur18
, Proposition 17]
, it is a coherent category (Definition
8.38
). Equipping it with coherent coverage
j_{\text{coh}}
, we obtain the site
(\mathbf{St}_{\kappa},J_{\text{coh}})
. The category
C_{\kappa}=\mathbf{Sh}(\mathbf{St}_{\kappa},j_{\text{coh}})
is the category of
\kappa
-condensed sets. For every pair
\kappa<\kappa^{\prime}
of uncountable strong limit cardinals there is a fully faithful functor
C_{\kappa}\hookrightarrow C_{\kappa^{\prime}}
. The category of condensed sets is then defined to be the colimit
C=\text{colim}_{\kappa}C_{\kappa}
over the large poset of uncountable strong limit cardinals. This category is not a Grothendieck topos.

Independently, Barwick and Haine in
[
BH19
]
defined the closely related notion of
pyknotic set
. They deal with set-theoretic difficulties by the method of Section
8.4.4
, i.e. they fix (at least) three Grothendieck universes
\mathbbm{u}\in\mathbbm{U}\in\mathbbm{V}
, and call the sets in
\mathbbm{u}
“tiny,” and the sets in
\mathbbm{U}
small. They define
\mathbf{St}_{\mathbbm{u}}
to be the category of Stone spaces whose underlying sets are tiny, see
[
BH19
, Section 2.2]
. This is a small category, and it is coherent. The category of sheaves
P=\mathbf{Sh}(\mathbf{St}_{\mathbbm{u}},j_{\text{coh}})
is called the category of pyknotic sets. The differences between
C
and
P
might at first seem artificial, but they do have real consequences for their resulting theory. For example the set
\{0,1\}
with the indiscrete topology is pyknotic but not condensed
[
BH19
, Section 0.3]
.

Example 8.61
.

In synthetic differential geometry, the goal is to craft a framework in which to do differential geometry such that infinitesimals exist. This simplifies and clarifies many computations. For instance, it makes the tangent bundle of a manifold an exponential object. There are many different toposes that one can “do” synthetic differential geometry in. These are called “well-adapted models” of synthetic differential geometry. The book
[
MR13
]
by Moerdijk and Reyes is the standard reference for these well-adapted models. Other useful references are
[
Koc06
,
Lav13
]
.

Recall the category
\mathbf{Cart}
from Example
8.27
. This category has finite products, and thus can be considered as an algebraic theory. An algebra for this theory, i.e. a finite product-preserving functor
A:\mathbf{Cart}\to\mathbf{Set}
is called a
C^{\infty}
-ring
. Note that if
M
is a finite-dimensional smooth manifold, then
C^{\infty}(M,\mathbb{R})
, the set of smooth functions to the real numbers is a
C^{\infty}
-ring by
\mathbb{R}^{n}\mapsto C^{\infty}(M,\mathbb{R})^{n}
. In fact, this produces a fully faithful functor
\mathbf{Man}\hookrightarrow C^{\infty}\mathbf{Ring}^{\text{op}}
,
[
MR13
, Theorem 2.8]
.

Every
C^{\infty}
ring is a commutative
\mathbb{R}
-algebra, by just considering the usual multiplication map
\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{R}\to\mathbb{R}
in
\mathbf{Cart}
. Hence we can define ideals of
C^{\infty}
-rings as ideals of their underlying
\mathbb{R}
-algebra. There is then a canonical way of making quotients of
C^{\infty}
-rings
C
by ideals
I
into
C^{\infty}
-rings
C/I
, see
[
Joy13
]
.

A Weil algebra
W
is a finite dimensional (as an
\mathbb{R}
-vector space) commutative
\mathbb{R}
-algebra with maximal ideal
I
such that
W/I\cong\mathbb{R}
and
I^{n}=0
for some
n\geq 1
. There is a unique way of making a Weil algebra into a
C^{\infty}
-ring that respects the underlying
\mathbb{R}
-algebra structure, see the references at
[
Joy13
, Example 2.12]
. These Weil algebras are the objects that act as infinitesimal neighborhoods. Examples include
C^{\infty}(\mathbb{R})/(x^{2})
which by Hadamard’s Lemma
[
MR13
, Lemma 2.4.1]
is isomorphic to the ring of dual numbers
\mathbb{R}[\varepsilon]/(\varepsilon^{2})
.

Let
\mathbf{ThCart}
(infinitesimally thickened cartesian spaces) denote the full subcategory of
C^{\infty}\mathbf{Ring}^{\text{op}}
on objects of the form
C^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{n})\otimes_{\infty}W
, where
\otimes_{\infty}
denotes the coproduct in
C^{\infty}\mathbf{Ring}
and
W
. is a Weil algebra. We give this small category the coverage
j_{\text{Cah}}
whose covers are of the form
\{C^{\infty}(U_{i})\otimes_{\infty}W\xrightarrow{r_{i}\otimes_{\infty}1_{W}}C^{\infty}(U)\otimes_{\infty}W\}_{i\in I}
where
r=\{r_{i}:U_{i}\to U\}
is a covering family in
(\mathbf{Cart},j_{\text{open}})
, see Example
8.27
. Sheaves on this site
\mathbf{Sh}(\mathbf{ThCart},j_{\text{Cah}})
is called the
Cahiers topos
. It was originally defined by Dubuc in
[
Dub79
]
.

Now consider the category
C^{\infty}\mathbf{Ring}_{\text{Germ}}^{\text{op}}
which is the opposite of the full subcategory of germ-determined finitely presented
C^{\infty}
-rings. These are
C^{\infty}
-rings of the form
C^{\infty}(M)/I
where
M
is a finite dimensional smooth manifold and
I
is a germ-determined ideal, which means that for every
f\in C^{\infty}(M)
if the germ of
f
at a point
x
in the zero locus
33
33

33

the set of all points
x\in M
such that if
g\in I
, then
g(x)=0
.

of
I
is equal to a germ of some
g\in I
at
x
, then
f\in I
. This small category has a coverage given by covering families of the form
\{A_{i}\to B\}
where the corresponding maps
B\to A_{i}
in
C^{\infty}\mathbf{Ring}_{\text{Germ}}
are localizations at some
b\in B
, in the
C^{\infty}
-ring sense. See
[
MR13
, Appendix 2]
for more details and also for a large amount of other sites and toposes that give models for synthetic differential geometry.

Example 8.62
.

Given a small site
(\mathcal{C},j)
we can consider the category
\mathbf{Sh}(\mathcal{C},j)
of
j
-sheaves on
\mathcal{C}
. This is a large category, but it is also locally presentable (see Section
C
), hence there exists a small dense subcategory
\mathcal{D}\hookrightarrow\mathbf{Sh}(\mathcal{C},j)
. Then equipping
\mathcal{D}
with the canonical topology,
[
Joh02
]
proves that
\mathbf{Sh}(\mathcal{C},j)\simeq\mathbf{Sh}(\mathcal{D},J_{\text{can}})\simeq\mathbf{Sh}(\mathbf{Sh}(\mathcal{C},j),J_{\text{can}})
.

If
(\mathcal{C},j)
is a large site, then the sheafification functor
a:\mathbf{Pre}(\mathcal{C})\to\mathbf{Sh}(\mathcal{C},j)
, which is discussed in Section
7.4
, may not exist. Indeed, if
X
is a presheaf on
\mathcal{C}
, then the diagram whose colimit is being taken over in Definition
7.19
may be large. Hence the colimit may not actually exist in
\mathbf{Pre}(\mathcal{C})
. When
\mathcal{C}
is small,
\mathbf{Pre}(\mathcal{C})
is a large category, and it has all small limits and colimits. But if
\mathcal{C}
is an essentially large category, then by Lemma
A.30
,
\mathbf{Pre}(\mathcal{C})
is large, but that does not mean that it has all large colimits. In fact, by the same argument as in Freyd’s Theorem, Proposition
A.33
, if it had all large colimits, it would necessarily be a preorder. For an example of a presheaf on a large site whose sheafification does not exist see
[
Wat75
]
.

Another problem is with the categorical structure of
\mathbf{Pre}(\mathcal{C})
. If
\mathcal{C}
is an essentially large category, then
\mathbf{Pre}(\mathcal{C})
may not be cartesian closed. Indeed, each hom-set of
\mathbf{Pre}(\mathcal{C})
is essentially large, by Lemma
A.30
.(4). Hence the usual internal hom in
\mathbf{Pre}(\mathcal{C})
as given in Lemma
A.76
does not define a presheaf. This also implies that (pre)sheaf categories on large sites may not have subobject classifiers.

Let us now consider some workarounds to this.

8.4.2.
WISC

When
(\mathcal{C},j)
is a large site, it is sometimes still possible to guarantee that sheafification will exist.

Definition 8.63

(
[
Rob12
, Definition 3.19]
)
.

We say that a large site
(\mathcal{C},j)
satisfies WISC (weakly initial set of covers), if for every
U\in\mathcal{C}
, there exists a small subset
j^{\prime}(U)\subseteq j(U)
such that for every covering family
r\in j(U)
there exists a
t\in j^{\prime}(U)
and a refinement
t\to r
.

Lemma 8.64
.

If
(\mathcal{C},j)
is a large site that satisfies WISC, then sheafification exists.

Proof.

Given
U\in\mathcal{C}
, let
\text{Gro}(j^{\prime})(U)
denote the subposet of
\text{Gro}(j)(U)
on those sieves that are generated by covering families in
j^{\prime}(U)
. So while each
R\in\text{Gro}(j^{\prime})(U)
might be a large set,
\text{Gro}(j^{\prime})(U)
itself is small. We have an obvious inclusion functor
i:\text{Gro}(j^{\prime})(U)\to\text{Gro}(j)(U)
and we wish to show that this functor is initial (Definition
C.10
). So we want to show that for every
R\in\text{Gro}(j)(U)
the category
(i\downarrow R)
is nonempty and connected. But since
R\in\text{Gro}(j)(U)
, there exists a
r\in j(U)
such that
R=\overline{r}
. Hence there exists a
t\in j^{\prime}(U)
and a refinement
t\to r
, so
\overline{t}\subseteq R
. Thus
(i\downarrow R)
is nonemepty, and it is connected simply because intersections of covering sieves are covering sieves by Lemma
6.22
. Now the result follows from Corollary
7.20
, as now
X^{+}(U)
is isomorphic to a colimit of a small diagram, which therefore exists.
∎

Lemma 8.65
.

The large site
(\mathbf{Set},j_{\text{epi}})
of Example
8.57
satisfies WISC.

Proof.

For every set
S
, there is an initial covering family, given by inclusions of singletons
\{x:*\to S\}_{x\in S}
.
∎

The next result is due to Shulman as referenced in
[
Rob12
, Example 3.21]
.

Lemma 8.66
.

The large sites
(\mathbf{Top},j_{\text{open}}),(\mathbf{Top},j_{\text{emb}})
of Example
8.58
satisfy WISC.

Proof.

We note that for every topological space
X
,
j_{\text{open}}(X)
is actually a small set, since a covering family has to be equal to the collection of inclusions
\{U\subseteq X\}_{U\in\mathcal{U}}
of an open cover
\mathcal{U}
. There are only a sets worth of open covers on
X
. Now let us show that
(\mathbf{Top},j_{\text{emb}})
satisfies WISC. Suppose that
r\in j_{\text{emb}}(X)
is a large covering family. For each
x\in X
, we can choose (using the axiom of choice) an open subset
U_{x}
containing
x
such that
U_{x}
is the image of a map
r_{i}:U_{i}\to X
in
r
. Then
r_{x}=\{U_{x}\subseteq X\}_{x\in X}
is a
j_{\text{open}}
-covering family of
X
, and also a
j_{\text{emb}}
-covering family. Since
r_{i}
is a homeomorphism onto its image, the
r_{i}^{-1}
defines a refinement
r_{x}\to r
. Thus
j_{\text{open}}(X)\subseteq j_{\text{emb}}(X)
is a small subset that refines every covering family.
∎

Remark 8.67
.

We note that there are nontrivial large sites that do not satisfy WISC, see
[
Rob12
, Proposition 3.23]
.

8.4.3.
Small presheaves

Another alternative that is considered at times is to consider a full subcategory of all presheaves on a large category
\mathcal{C}
.

Definition 8.68
.

Given a large category
\mathcal{C}
, a presheaf
X
on
\mathcal{C}
is
small
if there exists a small diagram
d:I\to\mathcal{C}
and an isomorphism

X\cong\text{colim}_{i\in I}y(d(i)).

Let
P(\mathcal{C})
denote the full subcategory of small presheaves.

Clearly if
\mathcal{C}
is small, then every presheaf is small, by the coYoneda Lemma
A.75
.

There are some nice categorical advantages to considering
P(\mathcal{C})
. For instance, the Yoneda embedding factors through
P(\mathcal{C})
since we are assuming that
\mathcal{C}
is locally small. The large category
P(\mathcal{C})
is locally small, in contrast to
\mathbf{Pre}(\mathcal{C})
as maps between small presheaves are determined by a small set of maps in
\mathcal{C}
. It is also the case that
P(\mathcal{C})
has all small colimits and is the free small cocompletion of
\mathcal{C}
.

A major disadvantage to
P(\mathcal{C})
is that it might not have limits. Indeed, let
X
be an essentially large set, thought of as a discrete category. Then there is an isomorphism
P(X)\cong\mathbf{Set}_{/X}
, given on a small presheaf
F
by the map
\sumop\displaylimits_{x\in X}F(x)\to X
. But
\mathbf{Set}_{/X}
does not have a terminal object, since every object in
\mathbf{Set}
is a small set. We learned of this example from
[
Tri15
]
.

However, we do know precisely when
P(\mathcal{C})
has all small limits.

Proposition 8.69

(
[
DL07
]
)
.

The category
P(\mathcal{C})
has all small limits if and only if for every small diagram
d:I\to\mathcal{C}
and every cone
\sigma
over
d
there exists a small set
K
of cones over
d
and a map
\sigma\to\kappa
for
\kappa\in K
. This implies that if
\mathcal{C}
has all small limits then so does
P(\mathcal{C})
.

8.4.4.
Very Small Grothendieck Universe

This last method is quite simple: rather than using two nested Grothendieck universes
\mathbb{U}\in\mathbb{V}
for our foundations (see Section
A.1.2
), we allow for another nested Grothendieck universe
\mathbbm{u}\in\mathbb{U}\in\mathbb{V}
. We still call those sets in
\mathbb{U}
small, and now refer to those in
\mathbbm{u}
as
very small
.

Now this forces the category
\mathbf{Set}_{\mathbbm{u}}
of
\mathbbm{u}
-small sets to be
\mathbb{U}
-small. From here, one can then construct whatever essentially large category (as long as its objects are built from sets) one wishes to consider using very small sets. For example, we can consider the category
\mathbf{Top}_{\mathbbm{u}}
of topological spaces whose underlying sets are
\mathbbm{u}
-small, which is a small category. This method is used by Jardine in
[
Jar07
, Page 2]
and by Barwick and Haine in
[
BH19
]
, see Example
8.60
. We of course do not need the full power of a very small Grothendieck universe here, but merely a consistent cardinality bound on the underlying sets being used to construct the objects in the site.

The disadvantage here is that now one must always keep track of which universe one is working with when going between the now small site and other categories. Also now the site whose objects are constructed from very small sets can no longer have all small limits or colimits unless it is a preorder by Freyd’s Theorem
A.33
.

9.
Morphisms of Sites

The notion of a morphism between sites is not as straightforward as one might wish. We need to put conditions on a functor
F:(\mathcal{C},j)\to(\mathcal{D},j^{\prime})
between sites, such that we obtain a geometric morphism (Definition
9.1
) between toposes
\Delta_{F}:\mathbf{Sh}(\mathcal{D},j^{\prime})\to\mathbf{Sh}(\mathcal{C},j)
. However, geometric morphisms require a finite limit preserving condition, which makes the conditions we are looking for on
F
more technical. This is mainly due to sites not necessarily having all limits or colimits. This technical condition on
F
is called “flatness”, and we will introduce this notion in stages.

First, let us illustrate the main ideas of morphisms of sites using sites of topological spaces.

9.1.
The case of topological spaces

If
T
is a topological space, recall (Example
2.9
) its corresponding site
(\mathcal{O}(T),j_{T})
. If
f:T\to T^{\prime}
is a map of topological spaces, then we obtain a functor
f^{-1}:\mathcal{O}(T^{\prime})\to\mathcal{O}(T)
by
U\mapsto f^{-1}(U)
. Thus we get a functor
\Delta_{f^{-1}}:\mathbf{Pre}(\mathcal{O}(T))\to\mathbf{Pre}(\mathcal{O}(T^{\prime}))
by precomposition. For this brief section, let us denote this functor by
f_{*}
. So for a presheaf
X
on
\mathcal{O}(T)
and
U
an open subset of
T^{\prime}
, we have
f_{*}(X)(U)=X(f^{-1}(U))
.

If
X
is a sheaf on
\mathcal{O}(T)
(we’ll just say
X
is a sheaf on
T
), then
f_{*}(X)
is a sheaf on
T^{\prime}
. Indeed, if
U
is an open subset of
T^{\prime}
, and
\mathcal{U}=\{U_{i}\subseteq U\}_{i\in I}
is an open cover of
U
, then

f_{*}(X)(U)\to\text{eq}\left(\prodop\displaylimits_{i\in I}f_{*}(X)(U_{i})\rightrightarrows\prodop\displaylimits_{i,j\in I}f_{*}(X)(U_{i}\cap U_{j})\right)

is an isomorphism, because
f^{-1}(\mathcal{U})
is an open cover of
f^{-1}(U)
, and
X
is a sheaf on
T
.

Thus if we restrict
f_{*}
to
\mathbf{Sh}(\mathcal{O}(T),j_{T})
, which we’ll denote by
\mathbf{Sh}(T)
, then we obtain a functor
f_{*}:\mathbf{Sh}(T)\to\mathbf{Sh}(T^{\prime})
. This functor has a left adjoint
f^{*}:\mathbf{Sh}(T^{\prime})\to\mathbf{Sh}(T)
, which is most easily described using the equivalence between sheaves on
T
and local homeomorphisms (also called étale spaces) over
T
,
[
MM12
, Section II.9]
. However
f^{*}
is isomorphic to the composite functor

\mathbf{Sh}(T^{\prime})\xhookrightarrow{i}\mathbf{Pre}(T^{\prime})\xrightarrow{\Sigma_{f^{-1}}}\mathbf{Pre}(T)\xrightarrow{a}\mathbf{Sh}(T),

where the right hand functor denotes sheafification (Definition
7.30
). It turns out that every continuous map
f:X\to Y
induces a functor
\Sigma_{f^{-1}}
that preserves finite limits. Thus
f^{*}
is a left adjoint functor that preserves finite limits.

Abstracting this common scenario leads us to the following notion, which for our purposes can be considered the default notion of a morphism between toposes.

Definition 9.1
.

Given Grothendieck toposes
\mathcal{E}
and
\mathcal{F}
, a
geometric morphism

f_{*}:\mathcal{E}\to\mathcal{F}
is an adjunction

f^{*}:\mathcal{F}\rightleftarrows\mathcal{E}:f_{*},

such that the left adjoint
f^{*}
preserves finite limits.

Remark 9.2
.

Notice that the direction of the geometric morphism matches the direction of the right adjoint. The fact that
f^{*}
preserves finite limits is vitally important. One might think of this as requiring that
f^{*}
be compatible with the internal logical structure of the toposes, see Remark
A.40
.

9.2.
Flatness

9.2.1.
\mathbf{Set}
-valued Flatness

Definition 9.3
.

Let
\mathcal{C}
be a small category, and suppose we have a functor
A:\mathcal{C}\to\mathbf{Set}
. We can take the left Kan extension of
A
with respect to the Yoneda embedding:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{C}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathbf{Set}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathbf{Pre}(\mathcal{C})}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces y}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\text{Lan}_{y}A}

and let
A^{*}\coloneqq\text{Lan}_{y}A
. We call
A^{*}
the
Yoneda extension
of the presheaf
A
.

The idea of the Yoneda extension of
A
is quite straightforward. By the coYoneda Lemma
A.75
, a presheaf
X
is isomorphic to the colimit

X\cong\underset{y(U)\to X}{\text{colim}}\,y(U).

So we simply extend
A
in the “obvious” way:

A^{*}(X)=\underset{y(U)\to X}{\text{colim}}A(U).

Using coends we can write

A^{*}(X)=(\text{Lan}_{y}A)(X)\cong\intop\nolimits^{U\in\mathcal{C}}\mathbf{Pre}(\mathcal{C})(y(U),X)\times A(U)\cong\intop\nolimits^{U\in\mathcal{C}}X(U)\times A(U).

The righthand coend is often denoted
X\otimes_{\mathcal{C}}A
and called the
functor tensor product
. We will also adopt this notation for the Yoneda extension of
A
in what follows.
Note that this coend can equivalently be written as the coequalizer

(23)

X\otimes_{\mathcal{C}}A\cong\text{coeq}\left(\sumop\displaylimits_{U\xrightarrow{f}V}X(V)\times A(U)\rightrightarrows\sumop\displaylimits_{U\in\mathcal{C}}X(U)\times A(U)\right)

which is isomorphic to the set

(X\otimes_{\mathcal{C}}A)\cong\{(x,a)\in X(U)\times A(U)\,|\,U\in\mathcal{C}\}/\sim

where
\sim
denotes the smallest equivalence relation such that if
f:U\to V
,
x^{\prime}\in X(V)
, then
(X(f)(x^{\prime}),a)\sim(x^{\prime},A(f)(a))
. We denote the equivalence class of the pair
(x,a)
in
X\otimes_{\mathcal{C}}A
by
(x\otimes a)
.

Definition 9.4
.

We say that a functor
A:\mathcal{C}\to\mathbf{Set}
is a
\mathbf{Set}
-
flat functor
if the Yoneda extension of
A

A^{*}:\mathbf{Pre}(\mathcal{C})\to\mathbf{Set}

preserves finite limits.

Remark 9.5
.

The terminology comes from homological algebra, where an
R
-module
M
is said to be flat if
(-)\otimes_{R}M
is left exact, i.e. preserves finite limits. We refer to functors
A:\mathcal{C}\to\mathbf{Set}
as in Definition
9.4
as
\mathbf{Set}
-flat functors because we will soon introduce various other notions of flat functors whose codomain may not be
\mathbf{Set}
and even if it is, these other notions might not agree with
\mathbf{Set}
-flatness.

Lemma 9.6

(
[
Bor94
, Proposition 3.8.1]
)
.

Let
y:\mathcal{C}\to\mathbf{Pre}(\mathcal{C})
denote the usual Yoneda embedding and
y^{\text{co}}:\mathcal{C}^{\text{op}}\to\mathbf{Set}^{\mathcal{C}}
the covariant Yoneda embedding
y^{\text{co}}(c)=\mathcal{C}(c,-)
. Then if
X:\mathcal{C}^{\text{op}}\to\mathbf{Set}
is a presheaf, and
A:\mathcal{C}\to\mathbf{Set}
a copresheaf, then there is a natural isomorphism of sets

(\text{Lan}_{y}A)(X)\cong(\text{Lan}_{y^{\text{co}}}X)(A).

or equivalently

X\otimes_{\mathcal{C}}A\cong A\otimes_{\mathcal{C}^{\text{op}}}X.

Let us record the following result, which is a covariant version of the coYoneda Lemma
A.75
.

Lemma 9.7

(
[
Rie17
, Corollary 6.3.9]
)
.

Given a functor
A:\mathcal{C}\to\mathbf{Set}
, then for
U\in\mathcal{C}
,

y(U)\otimes_{\mathcal{C}}A\cong A(U).

Furthermore the isomorphisms are natural in
U
.

The following result is the key to understanding flatness.

Proposition 9.8

(
[
MM12
, Theorem VII.6.3]
)
.

Given a category
\mathcal{C}
, a functor
A:\mathcal{C}\to\mathbf{Set}
is
\mathbf{Set}
-flat if and only if its category of elements
\smallint A
is finitely cofiltered, i.e. if its opposite category is a finitely filtered category (Definition
C.8
).

Proof.

(\Leftarrow)
Note that if
X\in\mathbf{Pre}(\mathcal{C})
, then by Lemma
9.6
we can also write
A^{*}(X)
as the colimit:

(24)

\displaystyle A^{*}(X)

\displaystyle=(\text{Lan}_{y}A)(X)

\displaystyle\cong(\text{Lan}_{y^{\text{co}}}X)(A)

\displaystyle\cong\text{colim}\left((y^{\text{co}}\downarrow A)\to\mathcal{C}^{\text{op}}\xrightarrow{X}\mathbf{Set}\right)

\displaystyle\cong\text{colim}\left(\intop\nolimits A^{\text{op}}\xrightarrow{\pi_{A^{\text{op}}}}\mathcal{C}^{\text{op}}\xrightarrow{X}\mathbf{Set}\right)

where
\intop\nolimits A^{\text{op}}\coloneqq(\intop\nolimits A)^{\text{op}}
is the opposite of the category of elements of
A
.

By Proposition
C.15
, if
\intop\nolimits A^{\text{op}}
is a finitely filtered category then for every finite diagram
X:I\to\mathbf{Pre}(\mathcal{C})
the
\intop\nolimits A^{\text{op}}
-colimit and
I
-limit of the following functor

\intop\nolimits A^{\text{op}}\times I\xrightarrow{\pi_{A^{\text{op}}}\times X}\mathcal{C}^{\text{op}}\times\mathbf{Pre}(\mathcal{C})\xrightarrow{\text{ev}}\mathbf{Set}.

commute, equivalently,
A^{*}
preserves finite limits.

(\Rightarrow)
Suppose that
A^{*}
preserves finite limits. We will prove that
\intop\nolimits A^{\text{op}}
is finitely filtered using Lemma
C.9
. If we let
1
denote the terminal object in
\mathbf{Pre}(\mathcal{C})
, then
1\otimes_{\mathcal{C}}A
is a terminal object in
\mathbf{Set}
. This means that
\intop\nolimits A^{\text{op}}
is nonempty, satisfying Lemma
C.9
.(1)

Now suppose that
(U,x)
and
(V,y)
are objects in
\intop\nolimits A^{\text{op}}
. We want to show that there exists
W\in\mathcal{C}
,
z\in A(W)
and morphisms
f:W\to U
and
g:W\to V
in
\mathcal{C}
such that
A(f)(z)=x
and
A(g)(z)=y
. Since
(-)\otimes_{\mathcal{C}}A
preserves finite limits, the map

(y(U)\times y(V))\otimes_{\mathcal{C}}A\to A(U)\times A(V)

which sends an element
(f\in y(U)(W),g\in y(V)(W))\otimes(a\in A(W))
to
(A(f)(a),A(g)(a))
is an isomorphism. Thus
\intop\nolimits A^{\text{op}}
satisfies Lemma
C.9
.(2).

Now suppose we are given morphisms
f,g:(V,y)\to(U,x)
in
\intop\nolimits A
. We want to show that there exists a map
h:(W,z)\to(V,y)
such that
fh=gh
. Consider the equalizer

P\to y(V)\underset{g}{\overset{f}{\rightrightarrows}}y(U)

in
\mathbf{Pre}(\mathcal{C})
. Thus for every object
W\in\mathcal{C}
,
P(W)
is the set of maps
h:W\to V
such that
fh=gh
. Since
A^{*}
preserves finite limits, we obtain an equalizer

P\otimes_{\mathcal{C}}A\xrightarrow{k}A(V)\underset{A(g)}{\overset{A(f)}{\rightrightarrows}}A(U)

in
\mathbf{Set}
, and where
k(p\otimes a)=A(p)(a)
. Thus if
y\in A(V)
such that
A(f)(y)=A(g)(y)
, then there must exist a
(W,z)\in\intop\nolimits A
and a
a\in A(W)
such that
k(z\otimes a)=y
. Thus
\intop\nolimits A^{\text{op}}
satisfies Lemma
C.9
.(3). Thus
\intop\nolimits A^{\text{op}}
is a finitely filtered category.
∎

Corollary 9.9
.

A functor
A:\mathcal{C}\to\mathbf{Set}
is flat if and only if it can be written as a filtered limit of corepresentables.

Proof.

By Lemma
9.7
and Lemma
9.6
, we have

A\cong A\otimes_{\mathcal{C}^{\text{op}}}y^{\text{co}}\cong\intop\nolimits^{U\in\mathcal{C}^{\text{op}}}A(U)\times y^{\text{co}}(U)\cong\underset{(x,U)\in\intop\nolimits A}{\text{colim}}\,y^{\text{co}}(U)\cong\lim_{(x,U)\in\intop\nolimits A^{\text{op}}}y^{\text{co}}(U).

Hence the result follows from Proposition
9.8
.
∎

Remark 9.10
.

The general idea of “flatness” that we will pursue throughout this section is that a functor
f:\mathcal{C}\to\mathcal{D}
is “flat” if it “would preserve all finite limits if they existed”. This is not completely true for all notions of flatness we will introduce, but it is a good intuition to keep in mind.

Corollary 9.11
.

Let
\mathcal{C}
be a finitely complete category, then a functor
A:\mathcal{C}\to\mathbf{Set}
is
\mathbf{Set}
-flat if and only if
A
preserves finite limits.

Proof.

(\Rightarrow)
By Lemma
9.7
,
A
is isomorphic to
A^{*}\circ y
. The Yoneda embedding
y
preserves limits and
A^{*}
preserves finite limits, therefore
A
preserves finite limits.

(\Leftarrow)
If
A
preserves finite limits, we wish to show that
\intop\nolimits A
is cofiltered, which by Proposition
9.8
is equivalent to
A
being
\mathbf{Set}
-flat. Now
\intop\nolimits\,A
is nonempty, because
A
preserves the terminal object
A(*)=*
. It can be easily checked that the other two conditions for being cofiltered are equivalent to
A
preserving products and equalizers respectively.
∎

Corollary 9.12
.

Let
\mathcal{C}
be a small category. If
A:\mathcal{C}\to\mathbf{Set}
is
\mathbf{Set}
-flat, then it preserves all finite limits that exist in
\mathcal{C}
.

Proof.

The Yoneda embedding
y:\mathcal{C}\to\mathbf{Pre}(\mathcal{C})
preserves all limits that exist in
\mathcal{C}
, and
A
being
\mathbf{Set}
-flat implies that
A^{*}
preserves all finite limits. Thus
(A^{*}\circ y):\mathcal{C}\to\mathbf{Set}
preserves all finite limits that exist in
\mathcal{C}
, and by Lemma
9.7

(A^{*}\circ y)\cong A
, thus
A
preserves all finite limits that exist in
\mathcal{C}
.
∎

Remark 9.13
.

If
\mathcal{C}
is not finitely complete, then
A:\mathcal{C}\to\mathbf{Set}
preserving all finite limits that exist in
\mathcal{C}
does not imply that it is
\mathbf{Set}
-flat. Here is a simple counterexample, consider the category
\mathcal{C}
with two objects
0
and
1
and no non-identity morphisms. Thus the only finite limits that exist in
\mathcal{C}
are the identity cones. Thus the functor
F:\mathcal{C}\to\mathbf{Set}
that sends everything to the terminal set
*
preserves all finite limits that exist in
\mathcal{C}
, but
\intop\nolimits F\cong\mathcal{C}
is not cofiltered.

9.2.2.
Representable Flatness

Now we wish to generalize this notion of flat functor to the case when the codomain is not
\mathbf{Set}
.

Definition 9.14
.

We say a functor
F:\mathcal{C}\to\mathcal{D}
between essentially small categories is
representably flat
if for every
V\in\mathcal{D}
, the functor

\mathcal{D}(V,F(-)):\mathcal{C}\to\mathbf{Set}

is
\mathbf{Set}
-flat.

Remark 9.15
.

Note that in the case
\mathcal{D}=\mathbf{Set}
, it is
not
the case that
\mathbf{Set}
-flatness is equivalent to representable flatness. See
[
Lei11
]
for a counterexample.

Lemma 9.16
.

A functor
F:\mathcal{C}\to\mathcal{D}
is representably flat if and only if the comma category
(V\downarrow F)
is cofiltered for every
V\in\mathcal{D}
.

Proof.

This follows from the fact that
\intop\nolimits\mathcal{D}(V,F(-))\cong(V\downarrow F)
and Proposition
9.8
.
∎

Lemma 9.17
.

A functor
F:\mathcal{C}\to\mathcal{D}
is representably flat if and only if the left Kan extension
\Sigma_{F}=\text{Lan}_{F^{\text{op}}}:\mathbf{Pre}(\mathcal{C})\to\mathbf{Pre}(\mathcal{D})
preserves finite limits.

Proof.

(\Rightarrow)
For every
X\in\mathbf{Pre}(\mathcal{C})
and
V\in\mathcal{D}
, the left Kan extension is isomorphic to the colimit

(\Sigma_{F}X)(V)=(\text{Lan}_{F^{\text{op}}}X)(V)\cong\text{colim}\left((F^{\text{op}}\downarrow V)\xrightarrow{\pi}\mathcal{C}^{\text{op}}\xrightarrow{X}\mathbf{Set}\right)\cong\text{colim}\left((F\downarrow V)^{\text{op}}\xrightarrow{\pi}\mathcal{C}^{\text{op}}\xrightarrow{X}\mathbf{Set}\right).

So if
d:I\to\mathbf{Pre}(\mathcal{C})
is a finite diagram, we can consider the functor

(F\downarrow V)^{\text{op}}\times I\xrightarrow{\pi\times d}\mathcal{C}^{\text{op}}\times\mathbf{Pre}(\mathcal{C})\xrightarrow{\text{ev}}\mathbf{Set}.

So by Lemma
9.16
, if
F
is representably flat, then
(F\downarrow V)^{\text{op}}
is filtered, so by Proposition
C.15
,
\Sigma_{F}
preserves finite limits.

(\Leftarrow)
We note that
F
is representably flat if and only if for every
V\in\mathcal{D}
, the functor
\mathcal{D}(V,F(-)):\mathcal{C}\to\mathbf{Set}
is
\mathbf{Set}
-flat, which is the case if and only if its Yoneda extension
\mathcal{D}(V,F(-))^{*}:\mathbf{Pre}(\mathcal{C})\to\mathbf{Set}
preserves finite limits. But
\mathcal{D}(V,F(-))^{*}
is the functor that sends a presheaf
X
to
(\Sigma_{F}X)(V)
. Indeed we have

\displaystyle\mathcal{D}(V,F(-))^{*}(X)

\displaystyle=\intop\nolimits^{U\in\mathcal{C}}X(U)\times\mathcal{D}(V,F(U))

\displaystyle\cong\intop\nolimits^{U\in\mathcal{C}^{\text{op}}}\mathcal{D}^{\text{op}}(F^{\text{op}}(U),V)\times X(U)

\displaystyle\cong(\Sigma_{F}X)(V).

where the last isomorphism is from the proof of Lemma
A.70
.

So if
\mathcal{D}(V,F(-))^{*}
preserves finite limits for every
V\in\mathcal{D}
, then so does
\Sigma_{F}
, since limits in presheaf toposes are computed objectwise. So if
F
is representably flat, then
\Sigma_{F}
preserves finite limits.
∎

Lemma 9.18
.

Let
\mathcal{C}
and
\mathcal{D}
be categories such that
\mathcal{C}
is finitely complete. Then a functor
F:\mathcal{C}\to\mathcal{D}
is representably flat if and only if it preserves finite limits.

Proof.

(\Rightarrow)
If
F
is representably flat, then
\mathcal{D}(V,F(-))
is
\mathbf{Set}
-flat, thus by Corollary
9.11
,
\mathcal{D}(V,F(-))
preserves finite limits. So if
d:I\to\mathcal{D}
is a finite diagram, then

\mathcal{D}(V,F(\lim_{i\in I}d(i))\cong\lim_{i\in I}\mathcal{D}(V,F(d(i)))\cong\mathcal{D}(V,\lim_{i\in I}F(d_{i})).

Since this is true for all
V\in\mathcal{D}
, by the Yoneda lemma it implies that
F(\lim_{i}d(i))\cong\lim_{i}F(d(i))
. Thus
F
preserves finite limits.

(\Leftarrow)
If
F
preserves finite limits, then so does
\mathcal{D}(V,F(-))
for every
V\in\mathcal{D}
, thus by Corollary
9.11
,
\mathcal{D}(V,F(-))
is
\mathbf{Set}
-flat for every
V\in\mathcal{D}
, and therefore
F
is representably flat.
∎

Corollary 9.19
.

If
F:\mathcal{C}\to\mathcal{D}
is representably flat, then it preserves all finite limits that exist in
\mathcal{C}
.

Proof.

For every
V\in\mathcal{D}
, the functor
\mathcal{D}(V,F(-))
is
\mathbf{Set}
-flat, so by Corollary
9.12
, it preserves all limits that exist in
\mathcal{C}
. By the same argument in the proof of Lemma
9.18
, this shows that
F
preserves all finite limits that exist in
\mathcal{C}
.
∎

Corollary 9.20
.

If
\mathcal{C}
is finitely complete, then a functor
A:\mathcal{C}\to\mathbf{Set}
is
\mathbf{Set}
-flat if and only if it is representably flat if and only it preserves finite limits.

9.2.3.
Covering Flatness

We now introduce a more general notion of flatness called covering flatness. This notion of flatness is specific to the case of functors between sites. It is more general than representable flatness and
\mathbf{Set}
-flatness. Our inspiration for this section is the blog post
[
Shu11
]
.

Typically in the literature, morphisms of sites are defined to be those functors that are representably flat and preserve covering families. We will instead use the notion of covering flatness as then site dense functors (Definition
9.45
) will be examples of morphisms of sites.

This extra generality comes at quite a high price of added complexity and technicality. The reader should feel free to skip this section on first reading. However, less is written in the literature about covering flatness, (but see
[
Shu12
]
,
[
Car20
]
and
[
Kar04
]
) and so we include a section about it here. First we begin with some motivating observations.

Lemma 9.21
.

Given a functor
A:\mathcal{C}\to\mathbf{Set}
, the set
\lim A
is in bijection with the set of sections of the projection functor
\pi:\intop\nolimits A\to A
.

Proof.

This can be seen by noting that
\lim A\cong\mathbf{Set}(*,\lim A)\cong\mathbf{Set}^{\mathcal{C}}(\Delta(*),A)
. Natural transformations
\Delta(*)\to A
are precisely the same thing as sections of
\pi
. In more detail, if
\sigma\in\lim A
, and
\ell_{U}:\lim A\to A(U)
is the
U
-component of the limit cone, then
\ell_{U}(\sigma)\in A(U)
, and
U\mapsto(U,\ell_{U}(\sigma))
defines a section of
\pi:\intop\nolimits A\to A
. Conversely such a section defines a map
\Delta(*)\to A
, which is equivalently an element of
\lim A
.
∎

We now wish to characterize the cofilteredness of
\intop\nolimits A
using observations about
\lim A
by appealing to Lemma
9.21
. Given a functor
A:\mathcal{C}\to\mathbf{Set}
and a diagram
d:I\to\mathcal{C}
, we obtain a functor
d_{*}:\intop\nolimits Ad\to\intop\nolimits A
defined objectwise as follows. If
(i,a)\in\intop\nolimits Ad
, then let
d_{*}(i,a)=(d(i),a)
. Furthermore the following diagram commutes

(25)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\intop\nolimits Ad}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\intop\nolimits A}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces I}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{C}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{Ad}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces d}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{A}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces d_{*}}

Given a functor
A:\mathcal{C}\to\mathbf{Set}
and a finite diagram
d:I\to\mathcal{C}
, suppose that
\lambda:\Delta(U)\to d
is a cone in
\mathcal{C}
. Then
A(\lambda):\Delta(A(U))\to Ad
is a cone in
\mathbf{Set}
. Let
\ell:\Delta(\lim Ad)\to Ad
denote the limit cone in
\mathbf{Set}
. By the universal property of limits, there exists a unique map
h_{\lambda}:A(U)\to\lim Ad
such that the following diagram commutes

(26)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\Delta(A(U))}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{Ad}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\Delta(\lim Ad)}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Delta(h_{\lambda})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A(\lambda)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\ell}

Proposition 9.22
.

A functor
A:\mathcal{C}\to\mathbf{Set}
is
\mathbf{Set}
-flat if and only if for every finite diagram
d:I\to\mathcal{C}
, the set of maps
H(A)=\{h_{\lambda}:A(U)\to\lim Ad\}
is jointly epimorphic, i.e. the canonical map

H:\sumop\displaylimits_{\lambda:\Delta(U)\to d}A(U)\to\lim Ad

is an epimorphism.

Proof.

By Proposition
9.8
, it is equivalent to prove that
\intop\nolimits A
is cofiltered if and only if the above hypothesis holds.

(\Rightarrow)
An element
\sigma\in\lim Ad
is equivalent to a section
\sigma:I\to\intop\nolimits Ad
of
\pi_{Ad}
by Lemma
9.21
. Thus given
\sigma\in\lim Ad
, from (
25
), we obtain a finite diagram
d_{*}\sigma
in
\intop\nolimits A
. Since
\intop\nolimits A
is cofiltered, there exists a cone
\Lambda:\Delta((U,a))\to d_{*}\sigma
in
\intop\nolimits A
, with components
\Lambda_{i}:(U,a)\to(d(i),a_{i})
. Projecting this by
\pi_{A}
produces a cone
\lambda:\Delta(U)\to d
, which in turn produces a cone
A(\lambda):\Delta(A(U))\to Ad
in
\mathbf{Set}
. Thus there exists a unique
h_{\lambda}:A(U)\to\lim Ad
such that the following diagram commutes

(27)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\Delta(A(U))}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{Ad}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\Delta(\lim Ad)}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Delta(h_{\lambda})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A(\lambda)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\ell}

Thus if
f:i\to i^{\prime}
is a map in
I
, then we have the commutative diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{A(U)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\lim Ad}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{A(d(i))}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{A(d(i^{\prime}))}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces h_{\lambda}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A(\lambda_{i})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A(\lambda_{i^{\prime}})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\ell_{i}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\ell_{i^{\prime}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A(d(f))}

and furthermore
A(\lambda_{i})(a)=a_{i}
. Thus
a_{i}=\ell_{i}h_{\lambda}(a)
. But
\ell_{i}(\sigma)=a_{i}
by Lemma
9.21
. But
h_{\lambda}
is unique, so
h_{\lambda}(a)=\sigma
. Thus we have defined a section of
H
, which implies that it is an epimorphism.

(\Leftarrow)
Conversely suppose that
d:I\to\intop\nolimits A
is a finite diagram. We wish to show that it has a cone in
\intop\nolimits A
. The composite
\pi d
is a finite diagram
\pi d:I\to\mathcal{C}
. Thus by the hypothesis, the function
\Sigma_{\lambda:\Delta(U)\to\pi d}A(U)\to\lim A\pi d
is a surjection.

Now there is a unique map
\varphi:\lim A\to\lim A\pi d
by the universal property of limits, and
d:I\to\intop\nolimits A
is equivalently an element of
\lim A
by Lemma
9.21
. Thus
x=\varphi(d)
defines an element
x\in\lim A\pi d
. Furthermore
d
defines for each
i\in I
a pair
d(i)=(U_{i},a_{i})
with
U_{i}\in\mathcal{C}
and
a_{i}\in A(U_{i})
and if
\ell:\Delta(\lim A\pi d)\to A\pi d
is the limit cone, then
\ell_{i}(x)=a_{i}
for all
i\in I
. Since
\Sigma_{\lambda:\Delta(U)\to\pi d}A(U)\to\lim A\pi d
is surjective, there exists a cone
\lambda:\Delta(U)\to\pi d
and an element
z\in A(U)
such that
h_{\lambda}(z)=x
. Now let us consider the element
(z,U)
in
\intop\nolimits A
. Applying
A
to
\lambda
provides a cone
A(\lambda):A(U)\to A\pi d
, and furthermore
A(\lambda_{i})(z)=a_{i}
. Thus
(z,U)
is a cone over
d
, so
\intop\nolimits A
is cofiltered.
∎

Let us consider a slightly different way to think about the previous result. For a finite diagram
d:I\to\mathcal{C}
consider the presheaf
\text{Cone}(d)
on
\mathcal{C}
, that sends
U\in\mathcal{C}
to the set of cones
\lambda:\Delta(U)\to d
. In other words

\text{Cone}(d)(U)=\mathcal{C}^{I}(\Delta(U),d).

We note that

\text{Cone}(d)\cong\lim_{i\in I}y(d(i)).

Given a small category
\mathcal{C}
and a finite diagram
d:I\to\mathcal{C}
, consider the canonical map

q:\sumop\displaylimits_{\lambda:\Delta(U)\to d}y(U)\to\lim_{i\in I}y(d(i)),

which on each component
\lambda:\Delta(U)\to d
is defined to be the section
y(U)\to\lim_{i\in I}y(d(i))\cong\text{Cone}(d)
to be precisely the cone
\lambda
. This map is an epimorphism of presheaves, since if we have a cone
\sigma:\Delta(U)\to d
then we can lift it to the identity in
y(U)(U)\cong\mathcal{C}(U,U)
.

Now for any functor
A:\mathcal{C}\to\mathbf{Set}
, we get the following commutative diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{A^{*}\left(\sumop\displaylimits_{\lambda:\Delta(U)\to d}y(U)\right)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\lim_{i\in I}A^{*}(y(d(i)))}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{A^{*}\left(\lim_{i\in I}y(d(i))\right)}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A^{*}(q)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces K}

Since
A^{*}
is a left adjoint,
A^{*}(q)
is an epimorphism. Thus
H
is an epimorphism if and only if
K
is. That means that a functor
A:\mathcal{C}\to\mathbf{Set}
is
\mathbf{Set}
-flat if and only if the comparison map

K:A^{*}(\lim_{i\in I}y(d(i)))\to\lim_{i\in I}A^{*}(y(d(i)))=\lim_{i\in I}A(d(i))

is an epimorphism.

Proposition 9.23
.

A functor
F:\mathcal{C}\to\mathcal{D}
is representably flat if and only if for every finite diagram
d:I\to\mathcal{C}
, the map

(28)

H_{d}:\sumop\displaylimits_{\lambda:\Delta(U)\to d}y(F(U))\to\lim_{i\in I}y(F(d(i))),

is an epimorphism of presheaves on
\mathcal{D}
.

Proof.

The functor
F
is representably flat if and only if
\mathcal{D}(V,F(-)):\mathcal{C}\to\mathbf{Set}
is
\mathbf{Set}
-flat for every
V\in\mathcal{D}
. By Proposition
9.22
, this is equivalent to the function

(29)

\pi_{V}:\sumop\displaylimits_{\lambda:\Delta(U)\to d}\mathcal{D}(V,F(U))\to\lim_{i\in I}\mathcal{D}(V,F(d(i)))

being surjective for every
V\in\mathcal{D}
. This is equivalent to
H_{d}
being an epimorphism of presheaves.
∎

There is a canonical map

(30)

K_{d}^{\sharp}:\text{Cone}(d)\to\Delta_{F}\text{Cone}(Fd),

given by pushing forward a cone
\lambda:\Delta(U)\to d
to a cone
F(\lambda):\Delta(F(U))\to Fd
. By the adjunction from Lemma
A.69
, we obtain a map

(31)

K_{d}:\Sigma_{F}\text{Cone}(d)\to\text{Cone}(Fd)

of presheaves over
\mathcal{D}
.

Lemma 9.24
.

The map
H_{d}
of (
28
) is an epimorphism of presheaves if and only if
K_{d}
is an epimorphism of presheaves.

Proof.

First note that
\text{Cone}(d)\cong\lim_{i\in I}y(d(i))
and
\text{Cone}(Fd)\cong\lim_{i\in I}y(Fd(i))
. Now for every
V\in\mathcal{D}
, by Lemma
9.6
we have that

\displaystyle(\Sigma_{F}\text{Cone}(d))(V)

\displaystyle\cong\text{Cone}(d)\otimes_{\mathcal{C}}\mathcal{D}(V,F(-))

\displaystyle\cong\mathcal{D}(V,F(-))\otimes_{\mathcal{C}^{\text{op}}}\text{Cone}(d)

\displaystyle\cong\text{colim}\left(\intop\nolimits\text{Cone}(d)^{\text{op}}\to\mathcal{C}\xrightarrow{\mathcal{D}(V,F(-))}\mathbf{Set}\right)

\displaystyle\cong\underset{\lambda:\Delta(U)\to d}{\text{colim}}\mathcal{D}(V,F(U)).

Thus there is a map
q:\sumop\displaylimits_{\lambda:\Delta(U)\to d}y(F(U))\to\Sigma_{F}\text{Cone}(d)
and it is objectwise an epimorphism (the map
q
takes a pair
(\lambda:\Delta(U)\to d,f:V\to F(U))
to its equivalence class in
(\Sigma_{F}\text{Cone}(d))(V)
), and thus an epimorphism of presheaves. Furthermore the following diagram commutes

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\sumop\displaylimits_{\lambda:\Delta(U)\to d}y(F(U))}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\text{Cone}(Fd)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\Sigma_{F}\text{Cone}(d)}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H_{d}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces q}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces K_{d}}

So if
K_{d}
is an epimorphism, then so is
H_{d}
, since epimorphisms compose. However if
H_{d}
is an epimorphism, then
K_{d}
is as well. Thus
K_{d}
is an epimorphism if and only if
H_{d}
is.
∎

Now we have come to the definition of covering flatness, which will generalize the equivalent definition of representable flatness coming from Proposition
9.23
.

Definition 9.25
.

Let
F:\mathcal{C}\to(\mathcal{D},j)
be a functor to a site. We say that
F
is
covering flat
if for every finite diagram
d:I\to\mathcal{C}
, the canonical map

(32)

K_{d}:\Sigma_{F}\,\text{Cone}(d)\to\text{Cone}(Fd)

is a
j
-local epimorphism.

Remark 9.26
.

Note that
K_{d}
is a
j
-local epi if and only if
H_{d}
is a
j
-local epi by Lemma
5.4
and Lemma
5.5
.

Let us concretely spell out what
H_{d}
being a
j
-local epimorphism means: For any cone
\sigma:\Delta(V)\to Fd
, there exists a
j
-tree
T_{\sigma}
with
T^{\circ}_{\sigma}=\{r_{i}:V_{i}\to V\}_{i\in I}
, such that for each
i\in I
there is a cone
\lambda_{i}:\Delta(U_{i})\to d
and a map
f_{i}:V_{i}\to F(U_{i})
such that the following diagram commutes

(33)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\Delta(V_{i})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\Delta(F(U_{i}))}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\Delta(V)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Fd}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Delta(f_{i})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Delta(r_{i})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F\lambda_{i}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sigma}

We can paraphrase this by saying that
F
is covering flat if for every finite diagram
d:I\to\mathcal{C}
, every cone over
Fd
factors locally through the
F
-image of a cone over
d
.

We can also characterize covering flatness using a sort of “local version” of Lemma
9.16
, along with Lemma
C.9
.

Definition 9.27

(
[
Car20
, Definition 3.2]
)
.

Let
F:(\mathcal{C},j)\to(\mathcal{D},j^{\prime})
be a functor between sites, with
V\in\mathcal{D}
. We say that
(V\downarrow F)
is
locally cofiltered
if

(1)

there exists a
j^{\prime}
-tree
T
with
T^{\circ}=\{r_{i}:V_{i}\to V\}_{i\in I}
such that
(V_{i}\downarrow F)
is nonempty for every
i\in I
,

(2)

for every pair of morphisms
f:V\to F(U)
and
g:V\to F(U^{\prime})
, there exists a
j^{\prime}
-tree
T
with
T^{\circ}=\{r_{i}:V_{i}\to V\}_{i\in I}
, and for each
i\in I
maps
f_{i}:U_{i}\to U
,
g_{i}:U_{i}\to U^{\prime}
and
h_{i}:V_{i}\to F(U_{i})
such that the following diagram commutes

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{V_{i}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{F(U_{i})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces V}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{F(U^{\prime})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{F(U)}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces h_{i}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces r_{i}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g_{i}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f_{i}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}

(3)

for every pair of morphisms
f,g:U\to U^{\prime}
in
\mathcal{C}
and morphism
h:V\to F(U)
in
\mathcal{D}
such that
F(f)h=F(g)h
, there exists a
j^{\prime}
-tree
T
with
T^{\circ}=\{r_{i}:V_{i}\to V\}_{i\in I}
such that for every
i\in I
there are morphisms
k_{i}:U_{i}\to U
and
h_{i}:V_{i}\to F(U_{i})
such that
fk_{i}=gk_{i}
and the following diagram commutes

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{V_{i}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{F(U_{i})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces V}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{F(U)}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces h_{i}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces r_{i}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F(k_{i}),}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces h}

We leave the next lemma to the reader. It is basically just a reworking of Lemma
C.9
.

Lemma 9.28
.

A functor
F:(\mathcal{C},j)\to(\mathcal{D},j^{\prime})
is covering flat if and only if for every
V\in\mathcal{D}
, the category
(V\downarrow F)
is locally cofiltered.

Remark 9.29
.

Let us consider Definition
9.25
when
(\mathcal{D},j)=(\mathbf{Set},j_{\text{epi}})
is the category of sets with the jointly epimorphic coverage (Example
8.57
). So let
A:\mathcal{C}\to(\mathbf{Set},j_{\text{epi}})
be a covering flat functor. This coverage is clearly composition closed, and therefore
K_{d}
is a
j
-local epi if and only if for every cone
y(S)\to\text{Cone}(Ad)
, there is a jointly epimorphic family
r=\{r_{i}:S_{i}\to S\}
over
S
forming a commutative diagram

(34)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{y(S_{i})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\sumop\displaylimits_{\lambda:\Delta(U)\to d}y(A(U))}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{y(S)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\text{Cone}(Ad)}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H_{d}}

If we consider the jointly epimorphic family
(1_{*})
, i.e. the identity function on the singleton set, then
A
being covering-flat implies that we can lift every map
y(*)\to\text{Cone}(Ad)
to a map
y(*)\to\sumop\displaylimits_{\lambda:\Delta(U)\to d}y(A(U))
. In other words it means that

\sumop\displaylimits_{\lambda:\Delta(U)\to d}\mathbf{Set}(*,A(U))\to\text{Cone}(Ad)(*)

is an epimorphism of sets. But this map is isomorphic to

\sumop\displaylimits_{\lambda:\Delta(U)\to d}A(U)\to\lim Ad.

So if
A
is covering flat, then it is
\mathbf{Set}
-flat by Proposition
9.22
. Conversely if
A
is
\mathbf{Set}
-flat, then using the axiom of choice, we can obtain a section to the map above. Thus for any map
s:S\to\lim Ad
, we can lift it to a map
s^{\prime}:S\to\sumop\displaylimits_{\lambda:\Delta(U)\to d}A(U)
, which means that we can write
S\cong\sumop\displaylimits_{\lambda}S_{\lambda}
, where each
S_{\lambda}
is the preimage of
s^{\prime}
landing in the
\lambda
component of
\sumop\displaylimits_{\lambda:\Delta(U)\to d}A(U)
, and therefore obtain a commutative diagram of the form (
34
).

When
(\mathcal{D},j)=(\mathcal{D},j_{\text{triv}})
is the category
\mathcal{D}
equipped with the trivial coverage (Example
2.11
), then
j_{\text{triv}}
-local epimorphisms are precisely epimorphisms of presheaves. Thus by Proposition
9.23
, a functor
F:\mathcal{C}\to(\mathcal{D},j_{\text{triv}})
is covering flat if and only if it is representably flat.

Thus
\mathbf{Set}
-flatness and representable flatness are special cases of covering flatness.

Proposition 9.30

(
[
Shu12
, Proposition 4.16]
)
.

A functor
F:\mathcal{C}\to(\mathcal{D},j)
is covering flat if and only if the composite functor

(35)

\mathbf{Pre}(\mathcal{C})\xrightarrow{\Sigma_{F}}\mathbf{Pre}(\mathcal{D})\xrightarrow{a}\mathbf{Sh}(\mathcal{D},j)

preserves finite limits.

Proof.

(\Leftarrow)
Suppose that
a\Sigma_{F}
preserves finite limits, and let
d:I\to\mathcal{C}
be a finite diagram. Then consider the map

\sumop\displaylimits_{\lambda:\Delta(U)\to d}y(U)\to\lim_{i\in I}y(d(i))\cong\text{Cone}(d)

which in component
\lambda
is the section
y(U)\to\text{Cone}(d)
defined by
\lambda
. This map is clearly an epimorphism of presheaves. Then since
a\Sigma_{F}
preserves finite limits and is a composite of left adjoints, it preserves epimorphisms, so the map

a\Sigma_{F}\left(\sumop\displaylimits_{\lambda}y(U)\to\lim_{i}y(d(i))\right)\cong a\left(\sumop\displaylimits_{\lambda}y(F(U))\to\lim_{i}y(F(d(i)))\right)

is an epimorphism. Thus
F
is covering flat.

(\Rightarrow)
Now suppose that
F
is covering flat. Then the map

a\Sigma_{F}\lim_{i\in I}y(d(i))\to\lim_{i\in I}a\Sigma_{F}y(d(i))

is an epimorphism. But if we let
A^{*}_{V}
denote the functor
A^{*}_{V}:\mathbf{Pre}(\mathcal{C})\to\mathbf{Set}
defined by
X\mapsto(a\Sigma_{F}X)(V)
, then this is equivalent to the map

(36)

A_{V}^{*}(\lim_{i\in I}y(d(i)))\to\lim_{i\in I}A_{V}^{*}(y(d(i)))

being an epimorphism for every
V\in\mathcal{C}
.

Now let
A_{V}:\mathcal{C}\to\mathbf{Set}
denote the functor
A_{V}(U)=[ay(F(U))](V)
. Then
A^{*}_{V}
is the Yoneda extension (Definition
9.3
) of
A_{V}
. Therefore
A_{V}
is
\mathbf{Set}
-flat if and only if (
36
) an epimorphism. But by Proposition
9.22
this is the case if and only if
A^{*}_{V}
preserves finite limits. Thus if
F
is covering flat, then
A^{*}_{V}
preserves finite limits for every
V\in\mathcal{C}
, and since limits are computed pointwise in sheaf toposes, this implies that
a\Sigma_{F}
preserves finite limits.
∎

9.3.
Site Morphisms

Definition 9.31
.

Given sites
(\mathcal{C},j)
and
(\mathcal{D},j^{\prime})
, we say a functor
F:\mathcal{C}\to\mathcal{D}
is a
morphism of sites
if it is covering flat and it sends every
j
-saturating family
r
to a
j^{\prime}
-saturating family
F(r)
.

Remark 9.32
.

In other words
F:(\mathcal{C},j)\to(\mathcal{D},j^{\prime})
is a morphism of sites if and only if
F
is
j^{\prime}
-covering flat and
F
sends
\text{sat}(j)
-covering families to
\text{sat}(j^{\prime})
-covering families.

Note also that if
F
satisfies the stronger property of sending
j
-covering families to
j^{\prime}
-covering families, then it sends
j
-trees to
j^{\prime}
-trees and therefore by Lemma
6.7
sends
j
-saturating families to
j^{\prime}
-saturating families. So if
F
is covering flat and sends
j
-covering families to
j^{\prime}
-covering families then it is a morphism of sites.

Proposition 9.33
.

Suppose that
F:(\mathcal{C},j)\to(\mathcal{D},j^{\prime})
is a morphism of sites. Then the precomposition functor
\Delta_{F}:\mathbf{Pre}(\mathcal{D})\to\mathbf{Pre}(\mathcal{C})
takes
j^{\prime}
-sheaves to
j
-sheaves, namely it descends to a functor

(37)

\Delta_{F}:\mathbf{Sh}(\mathcal{D},j^{\prime})\to\mathbf{Sh}(\mathcal{C},j)

Further,
\Delta_{F}
is right adjoint to the functor (
35
) restricted to
\mathbf{Sh}(\mathcal{C},j)
.

Proof.

Suppose that
X
is a sheaf on
\mathcal{D}
. We wish to show that
\Delta_{F}(X)
is a sheaf on
(\mathcal{C},j)
. By Lemma
3.9
, it is enough to show that if
U\in\mathcal{C}
and
r\in j(U)
, then the canonical map

\mathbf{Pre}(\mathcal{C})(y(U),\Delta_{F}(X))\to\mathbf{Pre}(\mathcal{C})(\overline{r},\Delta_{F}(X))

is an isomorphism.

Now by the
\Sigma_{F}\dashv\Delta_{F}
adjunction we have the following commutative diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathbf{Pre}(\mathcal{C})(y(U),\Delta_{F}(X))}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathbf{Pre}(\mathcal{C})(\overline{r},\Delta_{F}(X))}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathbf{Pre}(\mathcal{D})(\Sigma_{F}(y(U)),X)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathbf{Pre}(\mathcal{D})(\Sigma_{F}(\overline{r}),X)}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cong}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cong}

Now since
X
is a
j^{\prime}
-sheaf, by the sheafification adjunction we have

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathbf{Pre}(\mathcal{C})(y(U),\Delta_{F}(X))}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathbf{Pre}(\mathcal{C})(\overline{r},\Delta_{F}(X))}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathbf{Sh}(\mathcal{D},j^{\prime})(a\Sigma_{F}(y(U)),X)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathbf{Sh}(\mathcal{D},j^{\prime})(a\Sigma_{F}(\overline{r}),X)}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cong}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cong}

Now by Lemma
A.70
, we have
a\Sigma_{F}(y(U))\cong ay(F(U))
, and furthermore by Lemma
3.6
and Proposition
9.30
we have

\displaystyle a\Sigma_{F}(\overline{r})

\displaystyle\cong a\Sigma_{F}\left(\text{coeq}\left(\sumop\displaylimits_{i,j\in I}y(U_{i})\times_{y(U)}y(U_{j})\rightrightarrows\sumop\displaylimits_{i\in I}y(U_{i})\right)\right)

\displaystyle\cong\text{coeq}\left(\sumop\displaylimits_{i,j\in I}ay(F(U_{i}))\times_{ay(F(U))}ay(F(U_{j}))\rightrightarrows\sumop\displaylimits_{i\in I}ay(F(U_{i}))\right)

\displaystyle\cong a\,\text{coeq}\left(\sumop\displaylimits_{i,j\in I}y(F(U_{i}))\times_{y(F(U))}y(F(U_{j}))\rightrightarrows\sumop\displaylimits_{i\in I}y(F(U_{i}))\right)

\displaystyle\cong a\overline{F(r)}.

Therefore we have

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathbf{Sh}(\mathcal{D},j^{\prime})(a\Sigma_{F}(y(U)),X)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathbf{Sh}(\mathcal{D},j^{\prime})(a\Sigma_{F}(\overline{r}),X)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathbf{Sh}(\mathcal{D},j^{\prime})(ay(F(U)),X)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathbf{Sh}(\mathcal{D},j^{\prime})(a\overline{F(r)},X)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathbf{Pre}(\mathcal{D})(y(F(U)),X)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathbf{Pre}(\mathcal{D})(\overline{F(r)},X)}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cong}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cong}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cong}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cong}

So by Proposition
6.42

\Delta_{F}(X)
is a sheaf on
r
if and only if
\overline{F(r)}\hookrightarrow y(F(U))
is a
j^{\prime}
-local epimorphism, which is the same thing as saying that
F(r)
is
j^{\prime}
-saturating. Since
F
is a morphism of sites,
\Delta_{F}(X)
is a sheaf on
r
.

We obtain the desired adjunction
a\Sigma_{F}\dashv\Delta_{F}
as follows. First note that if
X\in\mathbf{Pre}(\mathcal{C})
, then by the coYoneda Lemma
A.75
we have

a\Sigma_{F}(X)\cong a\Sigma_{F}\left(\underset{x:y(U)\to X}{\text{colim}}\,\,y(U)\right)\cong\underset{x:y(U)\to X}{\text{colim}}\,a\Sigma_{F}(y(U))

and by Lemma
A.70
, we have

a\Sigma_{F}(X)\cong\underset{x:y(U)\to X}{\text{colim}}\,ay(F(U)).

Thus if
X\in\mathbf{Sh}(\mathcal{C},j)
and
Y\in\mathbf{Sh}(\mathcal{D},j^{\prime})
, then

(38)

\displaystyle\mathbf{Sh}(\mathcal{D},j^{\prime})(a\Sigma_{F}(X),Y)

\displaystyle\cong\mathbf{Sh}(\mathcal{D},j^{\prime})\left(\underset{x:y(U)\to X}{\text{colim}}\,ay(F(U)),Y\right)

\displaystyle\cong\lim_{x:y(U)\to X}\mathbf{Sh}(\mathcal{D},j)(ay(F(U)),Y)

\displaystyle\cong\lim_{x:y(U)\to X}\mathbf{Pre}(\mathcal{D})(y(F(U)),iY)

\displaystyle\cong\lim_{x:y(U)\to X}Y(F(U))

\displaystyle\cong\mathbf{Sh}(\mathcal{C},j)\left(\underset{x:y(U)\to X}{\text{colim}}\,y(U),\Delta_{F}(Y)\right)

\displaystyle\cong\mathbf{Sh}(\mathcal{C},j)(X,\Delta_{F}(Y))

∎

Notation 9.34
.

In what follows, if
F:(\mathcal{C},j)\to(\mathcal{D},j^{\prime})
is a morphism of sites, then we let
F_{*}:\mathbf{Sh}(\mathcal{D},j^{\prime})\to\mathbf{Sh}(\mathcal{C},j)
denote the functor
\Delta_{F}
restricted to
\mathbf{Sh}(\mathcal{D},j^{\prime})
, and we let
F^{*}
denote its left adjoint. This is in accordance with the usual notation used for geometric morphisms.

Corollary 9.35
.

A morphism of sites
F:(\mathcal{C},j)\to(\mathcal{D},j^{\prime})
induces a geometric morphism of sheaf topoi:

(39)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathbf{Sh}(\mathcal{C},j)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathbf{Sh}(\mathcal{D},j^{\prime})}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F^{*}=a\Sigma_{F}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F_{*}=\Delta_{F}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\dashv}

Furthermore the following diagram commutes up to isomorphism

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{C}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{D}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathbf{Sh}(\mathcal{C},j)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathbf{Sh}(\mathcal{D},j^{\prime})}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces ay}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces ay}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F^{*}=a\Sigma_{F}}

Definition 9.36
.

We say that a morphism of sites
F:(\mathcal{C},j)\to(\mathcal{D},j^{\prime})
is a
Morita equivalence
if
F^{*}
(equivalently
F_{*}
) is an equivalence. We say that two sites are Morita equivalent, if there exists a zig-zag of Morita equivalences between them.

Lemma 9.37
.

A functor
F:(\mathcal{C},j)\to(\mathcal{D},j^{\prime})
is a morphism of sites if and only if
F:(\mathcal{C},\text{sat}(j))\to(\mathcal{D},\text{sat}(j^{\prime}))
is morphisms of sites.

Proof.

Firstly
F
is
j^{\prime}
-covering flat (Definition
9.25
) if for every finite diagram
d:I\to\mathcal{C}
the map
\Sigma_{F}\text{Cone}(d)\to\text{Cone}(F(d))
is a
j^{\prime}
-local epimorphism. But by Lemma
6.14
, the above map is a
j^{\prime}
-local epimorphism if and only if it is a
\text{sat}(j^{\prime})
-local epimorphism, and hence
F
is
j^{\prime}
-covering flat if and only if it is
\text{sat}(j^{\prime})
-covering flat. Thus we need only to show that
F
preserves
j
-saturating families if and only if it preserves
\text{sat}(j)
-saturating families. But a family is
j
-saturating if and only if it is
\text{sat}(j)
-covering if and only if it is
\text{sat}(j)
-saturating. Thus
F
is a morphism of sites if and only if it is a morphism of sites on the saturations.
∎

9.3.1.
Comorphisms of sites

Definition 9.38
.

Given sites
(\mathcal{C},j)
and
(\mathcal{D},j^{\prime})
, a
comorphism of sites
34
34

34

This is also called a cover-reflecting functor in
[
Joh02
, Section C.2.3]
. We took the name comorphism of sites from
[
Car20
, Section 3.3]
.

F:(\mathcal{C},j)\to(\mathcal{D},j^{\prime})
is a functor
F:\mathcal{C}\to\mathcal{D}
such that for every
U\in\mathcal{C}
and every
j^{\prime}
-saturating family
r
on
F(U)
, there exists a
j
-saturating family
t
on
U
and a refinement
F(t)\leq r
.

Lemma 9.39
.

A functor
F:(\mathcal{C},j)\to(\mathcal{D},j^{\prime})
is a comorphism of sites if and only if
F:(\mathcal{C},\text{sat}(j))\to(\mathcal{D},\text{sat}(j^{\prime}))
is a comorphism of sites.

Proof.

This follows from the simple observation that a family
r
is
j
-saturating if and only if it is
\text{sat}(j)
-covering if and only if it is
\text{sat}(j)
-saturating.
∎

Proposition 9.40
.

If
F:(\mathcal{C},j)\to(\mathcal{D},j^{\prime})
is a comorphism of sites, then it induces a geometric morphism

(40)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathbf{Sh}(\mathcal{C},j)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathbf{Sh}(\mathcal{D},j^{\prime})}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Pi_{F}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces a\Delta_{F}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\dashv}

Proof.

Since limits in
\mathbf{Sh}(\mathcal{D},j^{\prime})
are computed objectwise (Proposition
B.17
), and
\Delta_{F}:\mathbf{Pre}(\mathcal{D})\to\mathbf{Pre}(\mathcal{C})
has a left adjoint, it preserves limits, and
a
preserves finite limits by Proposition
7.31
. Thus
a\Delta_{F}
preserves finite limits.

Now we need to show that if
X
is a sheaf on
(\mathcal{C},j)
, then
\Pi_{F}(X)
is a sheaf on
(\mathcal{D},j^{\prime})
. Given
V\in\mathcal{D}
and
r=\{r_{k}:V_{k}\to V\}\in j(V)
, with
i:\overline{r}\hookrightarrow y(V)
then by Lemma
3.23
,
\Pi_{F}(X)
is a sheaf on
r
if the canonical map

\mathbf{Pre}(\mathcal{D})(i,\Pi_{F}(X)):\mathbf{Pre}(\mathcal{D})(y(V),\Pi_{F}(X))\to\mathbf{Pre}(\mathcal{D})(\overline{r},\Pi_{F}(X))

is an isomorphism.

Now by the adjunction
\Pi_{F}:\mathbf{Pre}(\mathcal{C})\rightleftarrows\mathbf{Pre}(\mathcal{D}):\Delta_{F}
, we have the following commutative diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathbf{Pre}(\mathcal{D})(y(V),\Pi_{F}(X))}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathbf{Pre}(\mathcal{D})(\overline{r},\Pi_{F}(X))}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathbf{Pre}(\mathcal{C})(\Delta_{F}(y(V)),X)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathbf{Pre}(\mathcal{C})(\Delta_{F}(\overline{r}),X)}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cong}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cong}

Thus by Proposition
6.42
, it is enough to show that
\Delta_{F}(\overline{r})\to\Delta_{F}(y(V))
is a
j
-local isomorphism, because
X
is a
j
-sheaf.

Now
\Delta_{F}
has a left adjoint and therefore preserves monomorphisms. Thus
\Delta_{F}(i)
is a monomorphism, and hence a
j
-local monomorphism.

Now let us show that
\Delta_{F}(i):\Delta_{F}(\overline{r})\to\Delta_{F}(y(V))
is a
j
-local epimorphism. Suppose that we have a section
x:y(U)\to\Delta_{F}(y(V))
. This is the same thing as a map
x:F(U)\to V
. We want to find a
j
-tree
T
on
U
such that
F(T^{\circ})
refines
r
.

Firstly, since
j
is a coverage, there exists a
j^{\prime}
-covering family
s=\{s_{\ell}:W_{\ell}\to F(U)\}
with
s\in j(F(U))
such that
x_{*}(s)\leq r
. But since
F
is a comorphism of sites, and
s
being
j^{\prime}
-covering implies that it is
j^{\prime}
-saturating, there exists a
j
-saturating family
t=\{t_{\ell}:B_{\ell}\to U\}
on
U
such that
F(t)\leq s
. But by Lemma
6.7
, since
t
is
j
-saturating, there exists a
j
-tree
T
on
U
and a refinement
T^{\circ}\leq t
. Thus if
T^{\circ}=\{g_{i}:U_{i}\to U\}_{i\in I}
, then we have the following commutative diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{F(U_{i})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{F(B_{\ell})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{W_{k}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{V_{j}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{F(U)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{F(U)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{F(U)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces V}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F(g_{i})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{F(t_{\ell})}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces s}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{r_{j}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces x}

so that
x_{*}(F(T^{\circ}))\leq r
.
Which implies that there are morphisms
y(U_{i})\to\Delta_{F}(\overline{r})
for every
i\in I
making the following diagram commute

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{y(U_{i})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\Delta_{F}(\overline{r})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{y(U)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\Delta_{F}(y(V))}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces t_{i}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Delta_{F}(i)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces x}

In other words
\Delta_{F}(i)
is a
j
-local epimorphism, and hence a
j
-local isomorphism. Thus
\Pi_{F}(X)
is a
j^{\prime}
-sheaf.

Now suppose that
X\in\mathbf{Sh}(\mathcal{D},j^{\prime})
and
Y\in\mathbf{Sh}(\mathcal{C},j)
, then we have

\displaystyle\mathbf{Sh}(\mathcal{C},j)(a\Delta_{F}(X),Y)

\displaystyle\cong\mathbf{Pre}(\mathcal{C})(\Delta_{F}(X),iY)

\displaystyle\cong\mathbf{Pre}(\mathcal{D})(X,\Pi_{F}(iY))

\displaystyle\cong\mathbf{Sh}(\mathcal{D},j^{\prime})(X,\Pi_{F}(Y)).

Where the last isomorphism holds because
\Pi_{F}
sends sheaves to sheaves,
X
is a sheaf, and
i:\mathbf{Pre}(\mathcal{D})\hookrightarrow\mathbf{Sh}(\mathcal{D},j^{\prime})
is fully faithful, though we abuse notation above.
∎

Lemma 9.41
.

Given a site
(\mathcal{C},j)
with
U\in\mathcal{C}
, the projection functor
\pi_{/U}:\mathcal{C}_{/U}\to\mathcal{C}
is a comorphism of sites
\pi_{/U}:(\mathcal{C}_{/U},j_{/U})\to(\mathcal{C},j)
, see Example
2.12
.

9.3.2.
Examples of Morphisms of Sites

Lemma 9.42
.

Given a site
(\mathcal{C},j)
, the identity functor
1_{\mathcal{C}}
induces a morphism of sites
1_{\mathcal{C}}:(\mathcal{C},j_{\text{triv}})\to(\mathcal{C},j)
, where
j_{\text{triv}}
is the trivial coverage on
\mathcal{C}
, Example
2.11
. The induced geometric morphism is precisely the inclusion-sheafification adjunction

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathbf{Sh}(\mathcal{C},j)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathbf{Pre}(\mathcal{C})}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces i}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces a}

Lemma 9.43
.

Given a site
(\mathcal{C},j)
, let
(*,j_{\text{triv}})
denote the terminal category equipped with the trivial coverage. Then the unique functor
\mathcal{C}\to*
is a morphism of sites, and the induced geometric morphism is

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathbf{Sh}(\mathcal{C},j)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathbf{Set}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Gamma}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces a(-)_{c}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\dashv}

where
a(-)_{c}
sends a set
S
to the sheafification of the constant presheaf
S_{c}
, and
\Gamma
is the functor of global sections, namely
\Gamma(X)=\mathbf{Sh}(\mathcal{C},j)(\mathbf{1},X)
, where
\mathbf{1}
is the terminal sheaf.

Remark 9.44
.

It is an unfortunate fact of life that if
(\mathcal{C},j)
is a site with
U\in\mathcal{C}
, then
\pi_{/U}:\mathcal{C}_{/U}\to\mathcal{C}
is not in general a morphism of sites. For example, if
\mathcal{C}
is the discrete category on two objects
0
and
1
, equipped with the trivial coverage, then
\pi_{/0}
is a morphism of sites if and only if it is representably flat. But
(1\downarrow\pi_{/0})
is empty, and therefore not cofiltered. However it is a comorphism of sites always by Lemma
9.41
. See the discussion after
[
Joh02
, Lemma C.2.3.3]
for more on this.

9.4.
Dense Morphisms of Sites

Definition 9.45

(
[
Shu12
, Definition 11.1]
)
.

Given sites
(\mathcal{C},j)
and
(\mathcal{D},j^{\prime})
, we say a functor
F:\mathcal{C}\to\mathcal{D}
is
site dense
if the following conditions hold:

(D1)

a family of morphisms
r
is a
j
-saturating family if and only if
F(r)
is a
j^{\prime}
-saturating family,

(D2)

for every
V\in\mathcal{D}
, there exists a
j^{\prime}
-saturating family of
V
of the form
\{F(U_{i})\to V\}
,

(D3)

for every pair
U,U^{\prime}\in\mathcal{C}
, and morphism
g:F(U)\to F(U^{\prime})
, there exists a
j
-saturating family
a=\{a_{i}:U_{i}\to U\}_{i\in I}
and a family
b=\{b_{i}:U_{i}\to U^{\prime}\}_{i\in I}
(notice that
a
and
b
have the same domains) over
U^{\prime}
such that
g\circ F(a)=F(b)
, and

(D4)

for every
U,U^{\prime}\in\mathcal{C}
and morphisms
f,g:U\to U^{\prime}
such that
F(f)=F(g)
, there exists a
j
-saturating family
r=\{r_{i}:U_{i}\to U\}_{i\in I}
such that
fr_{i}=gr_{i}
for all
i\in I
.

Remark 9.46
.

Usually
\mathcal{C}
is taken to be a subcategory of
\mathcal{D}
, and
F
is the inclusion functor. In this case condition (D4) holds trivially. Furthermore if
\mathcal{C}
is a full subcategory of
\mathcal{D}
, then condition (D3) holds trivially. By
[
Car20
, Remark 5.2]
, if
F
is a morphism of sites, then (D4) holds. Note that in general site dense functors are not representably flat, hence the necessity of using covering flatness.

Lemma 9.47
.

Given sites
(\mathcal{C},j)
,
(\mathcal{D},j^{\prime})
and a functor
F:\mathcal{C}\to\mathcal{D}
, if
F
satisfies the condition that a family of morphisms
r
is
j
-covering if and only if
F(r)
is
j^{\prime}
-covering, then
F
satisfies (D1).

Proof.

Suppose that
r
is
j
-saturating. Then there exists a
j
-tree
T
such that
T^{\circ}\leq r
. Thus
F(T^{\circ})\leq F(r)
. Since
F
takes
j
-covering families to
j^{\prime}
-covering families, it takes
j
-trees to
j^{\prime}
-trees. Hence
F(r)
is
j^{\prime}
-saturating.

Conversely suppose that
F(r)
is
j^{\prime}
-saturating. Then by
∎

Lemma 9.48
.

A functor
F:(\mathcal{C},j)\to(\mathcal{D},j^{\prime})
is a site dense functor if and only if
F:(\mathcal{C},\text{sat}(j))\to(\mathcal{D},\text{sat}(j^{\prime}))
is a site dense functor.

Proof.

This follows from the simple observation that a family
r
is
j
-saturating if and only if it is
\text{sat}(j)
-covering if and only if it is
\text{sat}(j)
-saturating.
∎

Lemma 9.49

(
[
Shu12
, Theorem 11.2]
)
.

If
(\mathcal{C},j)
and
(\mathcal{D},j^{\prime})
are sites and
F:(\mathcal{C},j)\to(\mathcal{D},j^{\prime})
is a site dense functor, then it is a morphism of sites.

Proof.

By Lemma
9.48
, we can assume that both
(\mathcal{C},j)
and
(\mathcal{D},j^{\prime})
are saturated sites. Then we can replace all occurrences of “saturating” in Definition
9.45
with “covering”.

Now (D1) implies that
F
sends
j
-covering families to
j^{\prime}
-covering families, so we need only to show that
F
is covering flat.

Suppose that
d:I\to\mathcal{C}
is a finite diagram and
\sigma:\Delta(V)\to Fd
is a cone. We want to show that
\sigma
locally factors through a cone over
d
. By (D2), we know there is a
j^{\prime}
-covering family
r=\{r_{k}:F(U_{k})\to V\}_{k\in K}
, and thus for every
k\in K
and
i\in I
, we have a map

F(U_{k})\xrightarrow{r_{k}}V\xrightarrow{\sigma_{i}}F(d(i)).

Thus by (D3), there exists a
j
-covering family
a^{k,i}=\{W^{k,i}_{\ell}\to U_{k}\}_{\ell\in L^{k,i}}
and a family
b^{k,i}=\{b^{k,i}_{\ell}:W^{k,i}_{\ell}\to d(i)\}_{\ell\in L^{k,i}}
such that
\sigma_{i}r_{k}F(a^{k,i})=F(b^{k,i})
.

In other words, for every
\ell
,
k
and
i
, we have the following commutative diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{F(U_{k})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{F(W^{k,i}_{\ell})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces V}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{F(d(i))}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces r_{k}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F(a^{k,i}_{\ell})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F(b^{k,i}_{\ell})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sigma_{i}}

Note that
r\circ F(a^{k,i})
is a
j^{\prime}
-covering family.

Now since
I
is finite, we can consider the meet
a^{k}=\bigwedgeop\displaylimits_{i\in I}a^{k,i}
by Lemma
6.40
. Let us write
a^{k}=\{a^{k}_{n}:W^{k}_{n}\to U_{k}\}_{n\in N^{k}}
. So for every
k\in K
and
i\in I
, there exists some
n\in N^{k}
and
\ell\in L^{k,i}
and a commutative diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{W^{k}_{n}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{W^{k,i}_{\ell}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{d(i)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{U_{k}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces s^{k,i}_{\ell}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces a^{k}_{n}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces b^{k,i}_{\ell}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces a^{k,i}_{\ell}}

Let us write
b^{k,i}=b^{k,i}_{\ell}s^{k,i}_{\ell}
. Then for every fixed
k\in K
, the family
\{b^{k,i}:W^{k}_{n}\to d(i)\}
forms a cone over the discrete diagram
\{d(i)\}_{i\in I}
. Now we wish to modify the maps above to form a cone over
d
.

So suppose that
f:i\to i^{\prime}
is a morphism in
I
. Then for each
k\in K
, applying (D4) to the maps
d(f)\circ b^{k,i}
and
b^{k,i^{\prime}}
, we obtain a
j
-covering family
g_{f}=\{g^{f}_{\alpha}:B^{f}_{\alpha}\to W^{k}_{n}\}_{\alpha\in A_{f}}
such that
d(f)b^{k,i}g^{f}_{\alpha}=b^{k,i^{\prime}}g^{f}_{\alpha}
for all
\alpha\in A^{k,n}
. In other words we have

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{d(i)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{B^{f}_{\alpha}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{W^{k}_{n}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{d(i^{\prime})}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces d(f)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g^{f}_{\alpha}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces b^{k,i}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces b^{k,i^{\prime}}}

Now again since
I
is finite, we can consider the meet
g=\bigwedgeop\displaylimits_{f\in\text{Mor}(I)}g^{f}
which is a
j
-covering family on
W^{k}_{n}
. Then if we let
g=\{g_{\beta}:B_{\beta}\to W^{k}_{n}\}
, then for each fixed
\beta
, the composite maps
\{b^{k,i}g_{\beta}\}_{i\in I}
form a cone over
d
. Composing
g_{\beta}
with
a^{k}_{n}
