gives us the following commutative diagram for every
k
,
i
and
\beta
.

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{F(B_{\beta})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{F(B_{\beta})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{F(W^{k}_{n})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{F(U_{k})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces V}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{F(d(i))}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F(g_{\beta})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F(b^{k,i}g_{\beta})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F(a^{k}_{n})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces r_{k}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sigma_{i}}

Furthermore the left hand families compose to give a
j
-covering family
(r\circ a^{k}\circ g)
over
V
. Thus
F
is covering flat.
∎

Remark 9.50
.

Thanks to Lemma
9.49
, we may also refer to a site dense functor simply as a
dense morphism of sites
.

Lemma 9.51
.

If
F:(\mathcal{C},j)\to(\mathcal{D},j^{\prime})
is a site dense functor, then
F
is a comorphism of sites.

Proof.

By Lemma
9.48
and Lemma
9.39
, we can assume that
(\mathcal{C},j)
and
(\mathcal{D},j^{\prime})
are saturated sites.
Suppose that
U\in\mathcal{C}
and
r=\{r_{k}:V_{k}\to F(U)\}_{k\in K}\in j^{\prime}(F(U))
. By (D2), there is a
j^{\prime}
-covering family
s^{k}=\{s^{k}_{i}:F(U^{k}_{i})\to V_{k}\}
for every
k\in K
. Thus for every
i
and
k
we obtain a map

F(U^{k}_{i})\xrightarrow{s^{k}_{i}}V_{k}\xrightarrow{r_{k}}F(U).

So by (D3), we have a
j
-covering family
a=\{a^{k,i}_{\ell}:W^{k,i}_{\ell}\to U^{k}_{i}\}
and a family
b=\{b^{k,i}_{\ell}:W^{k,i}_{\ell}\to U\}
such that the following diagram commutes

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{F(U^{k}_{i})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{F(W^{k,i}_{\ell})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{V_{k}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{F(U)}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces s^{k}_{i}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F(a^{k,i}_{\ell})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F(b^{k,i}_{\ell})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces r_{k}}

But note that since
F
sends
j
-covering families to
j^{\prime}
-covering families, the composite family
F(b)=(r\circ s^{k}\circ F(a))
is a
j^{\prime}
-covering family of
F(U)
. By (D1),
F
reflects covering families, which implies that
b
is a
j
-covering family of
U
. But we also have that
F(b)\leq r
, so
F
is a comorphism of sites.
∎

Theorem 9.52

(The Comparison Lemma,
[
Joh02
, Theorem C.2.2.3]
,
[
Shu12
, Theorem 11.8]
)
.

If
F:(\mathcal{C},j)\to(\mathcal{D},j^{\prime})
is a site dense functor, then the induced geometric morphism

\Delta_{F}=F_{*}:\mathbf{Sh}(\mathcal{D},j^{\prime})\to\mathbf{Sh}(\mathcal{C},j)

is an equivalence of categories, i.e.
F
is a Morita equivalence.

Proof.

By Proposition
6.13
and Lemma
9.48
, we can assume that
(\mathcal{C},j)
and
(\mathcal{D},j^{\prime})
are saturated sites. By Lemma
9.49
and Lemma
9.51
we know that
F
is both a morphism and comorphism of sites. Thus the functors
\Delta_{F}
and
\Pi_{F}
take sheaves to sheaves.

Now we want to show that if
X\in\mathbf{Sh}(\mathcal{C},j)
, then the counit map

\varepsilon_{X}:\Delta_{F}\Pi_{F}(X)\to X

is an isomorphism.

Suppose that
U\in\mathcal{C}
, then by Lemma
A.69
we have

\Delta_{F}\Pi_{F}(X)(U)\cong\Pi_{F}(X)(F(U))\cong\lim_{F(V)\to F(U)}X(V),

and the counit
\varepsilon_{X}:\Delta_{F}\Pi_{F}(X)(U)\to X(U)
sends an element
x\in\lim_{F(V)\to F(U)}X(V)
to the component
x_{1_{F(U)}}\in X(U)
given by the identity map
1_{F(U)}:F(U)\to F(U)
.

Let us define a map
\varphi_{U}:X(U)\to\Delta_{F}\Pi_{F}(X)(U)
as follows. Given
x\in X(U)
and a map
f:F(V)\to F(U)
in
\mathcal{D}
, since
F
is site dense, by (D3) there is a
j
-covering family
r=\{r_{i}:W_{i}\to V\}_{i\in I}
and a family
t=\{t_{i}:W_{i}\to U\}_{i\in I}
such that
fF(r)=F(t)
. So we obtain for every
i\in I
an element
X(t_{i})(x)\in X(W_{i})
. Now let us show that the family
\{X(t_{i})(x)\}
forms an
X
-matching family on
r
.

So suppose we have an intersection square for
r

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{W_{ij}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{W_{j}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces U}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{W_{i}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces V}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces U}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces b}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces a}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces t_{j}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces r_{j}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces r_{i}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces t_{i}}

We want to show that
X(b)X(t_{j})(x)=X(a)X(t_{i})(x)
. But note that applying
F
to the above diagram we obtain a commutative diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{F(W_{ij})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{F(W_{j})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{F(U)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{F(W_{i})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{F(V)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{F(U)}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F(b)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F(a)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F(t_{j})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F(r_{j})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F(r_{i})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F(t_{i})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}

So

F(t_{j})F(b)=fF(r_{j})F(b)=fF(r_{i})F(a)=F(t_{i})F(a).

Now by (D4), there exists a
j
-covering family
s=\{s_{k}:B_{k}\to W_{ij}\}_{k\in K_{ij}}
such that
t_{j}bs_{k}=t_{i}as_{k}
for every
k\in K_{ij}
. Thus we obtain an
X
-matching family
\{X(s_{k})X(t_{j}b)(x)=X(s_{k})X(t_{i}a)(x)\}
on
s
. Since
X
is a
j
-sheaf, this implies that there exists a unique amalgamation
w\in X(W_{ij})
. Thus

X(t_{j}b)(x)=w=X(t_{i}a)(x)

for every
i,j\in I
. Thus
\{X(t_{i})(x)\}_{i\in I}
is an
X
-matching family on
r
, and since
X
is a
j
-sheaf, this has a unique amalgamation
y\in X(V)
.

Thus having fixed a covering family
r
and family
t
, we have obtained for every map
f:F(V)\to F(U)
a map
X(U)\to X(V)
. By construction we know that this map forms a cone and thus we obtain a map
X(U)\to\lim_{F(V)\to F(U)}X(V)
. Now it is not too hard to show that this map is inverse to the
U
-component of the counit map
\varepsilon_{X}(U)
. Indeed, by considering the above construction for the identity
F(U)\to F(U)
, we can always use the trivial covering family by the identity map. Since inverses are unique, we see that the map above does not depend on
r
and
t
, and therefore is well-defined. Thus
\varepsilon_{X}
is a natural isomorphism.

Now we wish to show that if
X\in\mathbf{Sh}(\mathcal{D},j^{\prime})
, then the unit map

\eta_{X}:X(V)\to\Pi_{F}\Delta_{F}(X)(V)

is an isomorphism. This is equivalently the map

X(V)\to\lim_{F(U)\to V}X(F(U))

that sends an element
x\in X(V)
to the collection of elements
\{X(f)(x)\}_{f:F(U)\to V}
.

Now by (D2) there exists a
j^{\prime}
-saturating family
r=\{r_{i}:F(U_{i})\to V\}
and hence
\eta_{X}(x)
restricted to
r
is an
X
-matching family on
r

\{X(r_{i})(x)\}
. Now the collection of all morphisms
\{F(U)\to V\}
is refined by
r
, and since
j^{\prime}
is saturated, this implies that
\{F(U)\to V\}
is
j^{\prime}
-covering. Since
X
is a
j^{\prime}
-sheaf, this implies that
\eta_{X}
is an isomorphism.
∎

9.4.1.
Examples

Example 9.53
.

Consider the sequence of full subcategory inclusions from Example
8.27
, where each category is equipped with the open cover coverage

(\mathbf{Cart},j_{\text{open}})\hookrightarrow(\mathbf{Open},j_{\text{open}})\hookrightarrow(\mathbf{Man},j_{\text{open}})

Since each of these functors is a full subcategory inclusion, (D3) and (D4) hold trivially. Since each of these sites is composition closed, if
r
is a family of morphisms in say
(\mathbf{Open},j_{\text{open}})
, then it is a covering family if and only if it is a covering family when included into
(\mathbf{Man},j_{\text{open}})
. Now if
r
is a family in
\mathbf{Open}
such that its image in
\ \mathbf{Man}
is refined by a
j_{\text{open}}
-covering family, then by restricting/pulling back each open subset of a cartesian space along the refinement (which are then open subsets of a cartesian space), we obtain a cover by open subsets of cartesian spaces. Hence (D1) holds. A similar argument holds for
\mathbf{Cart}
. Now as discussed in Example
8.29
, we can refine any open cover of a manifold by a good open cover, and hence (D2) holds for both inclusions. Hence both inclusions are dense site morphisms, so all three sites are Morita equivalent.

Example 9.54
.

Consider the sequence of full subcategory inclusions from Example
8.32

(\mathbb{C}\mathbf{Disk},j_{\text{open}})\hookrightarrow(\mathbf{Stein},j_{\text{open}})\hookrightarrow(\mathbb{C}\mathbf{Man},j_{\text{open}})

where we are abusing notation and letting
j_{\text{open}}
also denote the induced coverages. Since each of these functors is a full subcategory inclusion, (D3) and (D4) hold trivially. Since each of these sites is composition closed, if
r
is a family of morphisms in say
(\mathbf{Stein},j_{\text{open}})
, then it is a covering family if and only if it is a covering family when included into
(\mathbb{C}\mathbf{Man},j_{\text{open}})
. Now if
r
is a family in
\mathbf{Stein}
such that its image in
\mathbb{C}\mathbf{Man}
is refined by a
j_{\text{open}}
-covering family, then by restricting/pulling back each Stein manifold along the refinement (which are then Stein manifolds by
[
Lár03
, Lemma 4.1]
), we obtain a cover by Stein open subsets. Hence (D1) holds. The argument for
(\mathbb{C}\mathbf{Disk},j_{\text{open}})
being a coverage in Example
8.32
also proves (D1) holds for the inclusion
(\mathbb{C}\mathbf{Disk},j_{\text{open}})\hookrightarrow(\mathbf{Stein},j_{\text{open}})
. Now if
M
is any complex manifold, let
\mathcal{U}
be an open cover of
M
by coordinate charts, i.e. domains of
\mathbb{C}^{n}
. Then by
[
FS77
, Lemma II.1]
, we can refine it by a covering by polydisks. Hence both of the above site inclusions satisfy (D2). Hence both inclusions are dense site morphisms, so all three sites are Morita equivalent.

One might wish that for every manifold the obvious map
i:\mathcal{O}(M)\to\mathbf{Man}_{/M}
to be site dense, but unfortunately this is not the case.

Example 9.55
.

From Example
2.12
, we know that given any site
(\mathcal{C},j)
, and any
U\in\mathcal{C}
, there is a canonical site structure on the slice category
(\mathcal{C}_{/U},j_{/U})
. Consider a manifold
M\in\mathbf{Man}
. We obtain two sites
(\mathcal{O}(M),j_{M})
and
(\mathbf{Man}_{/M},j_{/M})
.

There is an obvious functor
i:\mathcal{O}(M)\to\mathbf{Man}_{/M}
that sends an open subset to its inclusion
U\hookrightarrow M
. This functor clearly preserves covering families, and since both sites are saturated, it therefore preserves saturating families. Now
\mathcal{O}(M)
is finitely complete, with all of its finite limits equal to finite products, which are given by intersections. These are sent to finite products in
\mathbf{Man}_{/M}
, so by Lemma
9.18
,
i
is representably flat, and therefore it is a morphism of sites.

Unfortunately,
i
is not a dense morphism of sites in general. For example, take
M=\mathbb{R}^{0}=*
. Then
\mathbf{Sh}(\mathcal{O}(*),j_{*})\cong\mathbf{Set}
, and
\mathbf{Sh}(\mathbf{Man}_{/*},j_{/*})\cong\mathbf{Sh}(\mathbf{Man},j_{/*})\cong\mathbf{Pre}(\mathbf{Man})
.

We call
(\mathcal{O}(M),j_{M})
the
petit site
and
(\mathbf{Man}_{/M},j_{/M})
the
gros site
of
M
.

Remark 9.56
.

While the petit and gros sites do not have equivalent sheaf topoi, they are “homotopy equivalent” in a certain sense, see
[
MM12
, Page 416]
.

10.
Points of a Site

In this section we discuss points of sites. When a site has a set of enough points, this provides us with another way to test when a morphism of presheaves is a local isomorphism.

Definition 10.1
.

If
\mathcal{E}
is a Grothendieck topos, then a
point
of
\mathcal{E}
is a geometric morphism
p_{*}:\mathbf{Set}\to\mathcal{E}
.

Let us specialize first to the case of presheaf topoi. We will show that points of a presheaf topos are given precisely by
\mathbf{Set}
-flat functors. As we’ve seen in Section
9
, if
\mathcal{C}
is a small category, a functor
A:\mathcal{C}\to\mathbf{Set}
is
\mathbf{Set}
-flat (Definition
9.4
) if and only if
A^{*}=(-)\otimes_{\mathcal{C}}A:\mathbf{Pre}(\mathcal{C})\to\mathbf{Set}
preserves finite limits. Let us for the moment just consider arbitrary functors
A:\mathcal{C}\to\mathbf{Set}
. Then
(-)\otimes_{\mathcal{C}}A
clearly preserves colimits and has a right adjoint, which we call the
skyscraper construction
. Let
A_{*}:\mathbf{Set}\to\mathbf{Pre}(\mathcal{C})
be defined objectwise for any
S\in\mathbf{Set}
and
U\in\mathcal{C}
by

(A_{*}(S))(U)=\mathbf{Set}(A(U),S).

Now
A_{*}
is right adjoint to
(-)\otimes_{\mathcal{C}}A
by the following computation. For every
X\in\mathbf{Pre}(\mathcal{C})
and
S\in\mathbf{Set}
we have

\displaystyle\mathbf{Set}(X\otimes_{\mathcal{C}}A,S)

\displaystyle\cong\mathbf{Set}\left(\intop\nolimits^{U\in\mathcal{C}}X(U)\times A(U),S\right)

\displaystyle\cong\intop\nolimits_{U\in\mathcal{C}}\mathbf{Set}(X(U)\times A(U),S)

\displaystyle\cong\intop\nolimits_{U\in\mathcal{C}}\mathbf{Set}(X(U),\mathbf{Set}(A(U),S))

\displaystyle\cong\mathbf{Pre}(\mathcal{C})(X,\mathbf{Set}(A(-),S))

\displaystyle\cong\mathbf{Pre}(\mathcal{C})(X,A_{*}(S)).

Thus from any functor
A:\mathcal{C}\to\mathbf{Set}
, we obtain an adjunction
A^{*}=(-)\otimes_{\mathcal{C}}A\dashv A_{*}
. Now let us show the converse, i.e. that if we have an adjunction
L:\mathbf{Pre}(\mathcal{C})\rightleftarrows\mathbf{Set}:R
, we obtain a functor
A:\mathcal{C}\to\mathbf{Set}
.

Let
\mathbf{Adj}(\mathbf{Pre}(\mathcal{C}),\mathbf{Set})
denote the category whose objects are adjunctions
L:\mathbf{Pre}(\mathcal{C})\rightleftarrows\mathbf{Set}:R
and whose morphisms are natural transformations between the left adjoints (equivalently right adjoints) of the adjunctions.

Given a functor
A:\mathcal{C}\to\mathbf{Set}
, let
\phi(A)
denote the adjunction
A^{*}\dashv A_{*}
constructed above. This construction extends to a functor
\phi:\mathbf{Set}^{\mathcal{C}}\to\mathbf{Adj}(\mathbf{Pre}(\mathcal{C}),\mathbf{Set})
. Indeed, if
f:A\to B
is a natural transformation, then since
\text{Lan}_{y}:\mathbf{Set}^{\mathcal{C}}\to\mathbf{Set}^{\mathbf{Pre}(\mathcal{C})}
is a functor, one obtains a natural transformation
(-)\otimes_{\mathcal{C}}f:A^{*}=(-)\otimes_{\mathcal{C}}A\to(-)\otimes_{\mathcal{C}}B=B^{*}
.

Conversely, given an adjunction
L:\mathbf{Pre}(\mathcal{C})\rightleftarrows\mathbf{Set}:R
, let
\psi(L\dashv R)
denote the composite functor

\mathcal{C}\xrightarrow{y}\mathbf{Pre}(\mathcal{C})\xrightarrow{L}\mathbf{Set}.

This also clearly extends to a functor
\psi:\mathbf{Adj}(\mathbf{Pre}(\mathcal{C}),\mathbf{Set})\to\mathbf{Set}^{\mathcal{C}}
.

Lemma 10.2
.

The functors
\phi
and
\psi
defined above form an equivalence of categories

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathbf{Set}^{\mathcal{C}}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathbf{Adj}(\mathbf{Pre}(\mathcal{C}),\mathbf{Set})}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\phi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\psi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\simeq}

Proof.

Suppose that
A
is a functor
A:\mathcal{C}\to\mathbf{Set}
. By the coYoneda Lemma (Lemma
A.75
), every presheaf is a colimit of representables, so if
X\in\mathbf{Pre}(\mathcal{C})
, then

X\otimes_{\mathcal{C}}A\cong\left(\underset{y(U)\to X}{\text{colim}}\,y(U)\right)\otimes_{\mathcal{C}}A\cong\underset{y(U)\to X}{\text{colim}}\,A(U).

Thus
A^{*}=(-)\otimes_{\mathcal{C}}A
is completely determined by what it does to representables. So if we consider the functor
\psi(\phi(A))
, which is the composite

\mathcal{C}\xrightarrow{y}\mathbf{Pre}(\mathcal{C})\xrightarrow{A^{*}}\mathbf{Set},

then by Lemma
9.7
, we have an isomorphism
\psi(\phi(A))=(A^{*}\circ y)\cong A
, natural in
A
.

Conversely, given an adjunction
L:\mathbf{Pre}(\mathcal{C})\rightleftarrows\mathbf{Set}:R
, then
\psi(L\dashv R)=(L\circ y)
, and we have
\phi(\psi(L\dashv R))=\text{Lan}_{y}(L\circ y)
. But by Lemma
9.7
,
\text{Lan}_{y}(L\circ y)
and
L
are isomorphic on representables, and thus by the coYoneda Lemma are naturally isomorphic functors. Thus
\phi(\psi(L\dashv R))\cong L\dashv R
.
∎

Now if
\mathcal{C}
is a small category, let
\mathbf{Geo}(\mathbf{Set},\mathbf{Pre}(\mathcal{C}))
denote the full subcategory of
\mathbf{Adj}(\mathbf{Pre}(\mathcal{C}),\mathbf{Set})
whose objects are the geometric morphisms. Let
\mathbf{Flat}(\mathcal{C})
denote the category whose objects are the
\mathbf{Set}
-flat functors
A:\mathcal{C}\to\mathbf{Set}
and whose morphisms are natural transformations. The functor
\psi
from Lemma
10.2
restricts to a functor
\psi^{\prime}:\mathbf{Flat}(\mathcal{C})\to\mathbf{Geo}(\mathbf{Set},\mathbf{Pre}(\mathcal{C}))
. Similarly the functor
\phi
from Lemma
10.2
also restricts to a functor
\phi^{\prime}:\mathbf{Geo}(\mathbf{Set},\mathbf{Pre}(\mathcal{C}))\to\mathbf{Flat}(\mathcal{C})
because if
A_{*}:\mathbf{Set}\to\mathbf{Pre}(\mathcal{C})
is a geometric morphism, then
\phi(A^{*}\vdash A_{*})=(A^{*}\circ y)
is
\mathbf{Set}
-flat, since
\text{Lan}_{y}(A^{*}\circ y)\cong A^{*}
and
A^{*}
preserves finite limits.

Proposition 10.3

(
[
MM12
, Theorem VII.5.2]
)
.

Given a small category
\mathcal{C}
, the functors
\psi^{\prime},\phi^{\prime}
defined above form an equivalence of categories

(41)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathbf{Flat}(\mathcal{C})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathbf{Geo}(\mathbf{Set},\mathbf{Pre}(\mathcal{C}))}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\phi^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\psi^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\simeq}

So we have now shown that points of presheaf toposes
\mathbf{Set}\to\mathbf{Pre}(\mathcal{C})
are equivalent to
\mathbf{Set}
-flat functors
\mathcal{C}\to\mathbf{Set}
. We would like to generalize this result to Grothendieck toposes. For that, we need to put more conditions on the functor
\mathcal{C}\to\mathbf{Set}
.

Definition 10.4
.

Given a site
(\mathcal{C},j)
, we say a functor
A:\mathcal{C}\to\mathbf{Set}
is
j
-continuous
if for every
U\in\mathcal{C}
and every covering family
r=\{r_{i}:U_{i}\to U\}_{i\in I}\in j(U)
, the corresponding map

\sumop\displaylimits_{i\in I}A(U_{i})\xrightarrow{\sumop\displaylimits_{i\in I}A(r_{i})}A(U).

is an epimorphism of sets.

Remark 10.5
.

Note that this is equivalent to
A^{*}
sending the map
\sumop\displaylimits_{i\in I}r_{i}:\sumop\displaylimits_{i\in I}y(U_{i})\to y(U)
to an epimorphism. By the proof of Lemma
6.18
, this is equivalent to
A^{*}
sending the map
i:\overline{r}\hookrightarrow y(U)
of presheaves to an epimorphism. Note that if
A
is furthermore
\mathbf{Set}
-flat, then
A^{*}
preserves monomorphisms and epimorphisms, and therefore sends
i
to an isomorphism.

Lemma 10.6

(
[
MM12
, Lemma VII.5.3]
)
.

Given a site
(\mathcal{C},j)
and a point
A:\mathcal{C}\to\mathbf{Set}
, the functor
A_{*}:\mathbf{Set}\to\mathbf{Pre}(\mathcal{C})
factors through
\mathbf{Sh}(\mathcal{C},j)
if and only if
A
is
j
-continuous.

Proof.

We want to show that if
S
is a set, then
A_{*}(S)
is a sheaf if and only if
A
is
j
-continuous. By Lemma
3.9
, it is enough to show that for every
U\in\mathcal{C}
and every covering family
r\in j(U)
the canonical map

\mathbf{Pre}(\mathcal{C})(y(U),A_{*}(S))\to\mathbf{Pre}(\mathcal{C})(\overline{r},A_{*}(S))

is an isomorphism. Using the adjunction
A^{*}\dashv A_{*}
adjunction, we have the following commutative diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathbf{Pre}(\mathcal{C})(y(U),A_{*}(S))}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathbf{Pre}(\mathcal{C})(\overline{r},A_{*}(S))}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathbf{Set}(A^{*}(U),S)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathbf{Set}(A^{*}(\overline{r}),S)}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbf{Pre}(\mathcal{C})(i,A_{*}(S))}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cong}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cong}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbf{Set}(A^{*}(i),S)}

but by Remark
10.5
, if
A
is
j
-continuous, then
A^{*}(i)
is an isomorphism, so the bottom horizontal map is an isomorphism, so the top one is as well. Thus
A_{*}(S)
is a sheaf. Conversely if the top horizontal map is an isomorphism for every
S
, then by the Yoneda lemma
A^{*}(i)
is an isomorphism and hence
A
is
j
-continuous.
∎

Now given a site
(\mathcal{C},j)
, let
\mathbf{ConFlat}(\mathcal{C},j)
denote the full subcategory of
\mathbf{Flat}(\mathcal{C})
on those flat functors
A:\mathcal{C}\to\mathbf{Set}
that are also
j
-continuous.

Corollary 10.7

(
[
MM12
, Corollary VII.5.4]
)
.

The equivalence of Proposition
10.3
restricts to an equivalence

\mathbf{ConFlat}(\mathcal{C},j)\simeq\mathbf{Geo}(\mathbf{Set},\mathbf{Sh}(\mathcal{C})).

Definition 10.8
.

By a
point of a site

(\mathcal{C},j)
, we mean a
j
-continuous flat functor
A:(\mathcal{C},j)\to\mathbf{Set}
or equivalently a point
A_{*}:\mathbf{Set}\to\mathbf{Sh}(\mathcal{C},j)
.

Remark 10.9
.

Note that if
(\mathcal{C},j)
is a site, then a functor
A:\mathcal{C}\to\mathbf{Set}
is
\mathbf{Set}
-flat if and only if it is covering flat by Remark
9.29
. Therefore if we consider
\mathbf{Set}
with the jointly epimorphic coverage
j_{\text{epi}}
, then since it is a saturated coverage, the functor
A:\mathcal{C}\to\mathbf{Set}
is a point of
(\mathcal{C},j)
if and only if
A
is a morphism of sites.

Remark 10.10
.

Note that any point
A_{*}:\mathbf{Set}\to\mathbf{Sh}(\mathcal{C},j)
of a site
(\mathcal{C},j)
also determines a point
iA_{*}:\mathbf{Set}\to\mathbf{Pre}(\mathcal{C})
of the presheaf topos, by composition with the geometric morphism
i:\mathbf{Sh}(\mathcal{C},j)\to\mathbf{Pre}(\mathcal{C})
, and if
a
denotes sheafification, then its left adjoint is
A^{*}a
.

Proposition 10.11
.

Given a site
(\mathcal{C},j)
, if
f:X\to Y
is a epi/monomorphism of sheaves and
p
is a point of
(\mathcal{C},j)
, then
p^{*}f:p^{*}X\to p^{*}Y
is an epi/monomorphism of sets.

Proof.

This just follows from the fact that
p^{*}:\mathbf{Sh}(\mathcal{C},j)\to\mathbf{Set}
is a left adjoint and hence preserves epimorphisms, and since it preserves finite limits it preserves monomorphisms.
∎

Corollary 10.12
.

Given a site
(\mathcal{C},j)
, if
f:X\to Y
is a
j
-local epi/monomorphism of presheaves,
p
is a point and
a
denotes sheafification, then
p^{*}af:p^{*}aX\to p^{*}aY
is a epi/monomorphism of sets.

Proof.

This follows from Corollary
7.37
and Proposition
10.11
.
∎

Definition 10.13
.

Let
(\mathcal{C},j)
be a site. Let
P=\{p_{i}\}_{i\in I}
be a set of points of
(\mathcal{C},j)
. We say that
P
is a
set of enough points
for
(\mathcal{C},j)
if for every map
f:X\to Y
of sheaves the following property holds: if
p_{i}^{*}(f):p^{*}_{i}(X)\to p^{*}_{i}(Y)
is an isomorphism for every
i\in I
, then
f
is an isomorphism of sheaves.

If
P=\{p_{i}\}_{i\in I}
is a set of points on a site
(\mathcal{C},j)
, then we obtain a map

p^{*}=\langle p_{i}^{*}\rangle_{i\in I}:\mathbf{Sh}(\mathcal{C})\to\prodop\displaylimits_{i\in I}\mathbf{Set}\cong\mathbf{Set}^{I},

induced by the left adjoints
p_{i}^{*}:\mathbf{Sh}(\mathcal{C})\to\mathbf{Set}
of each of the points. Thus
P
is a set of enough points for
(\mathcal{C},j)
if and only if
p^{*}
reflects isomorphisms.

Note that
p^{*}
preserves finite limits since each
p_{i}^{*}
does. Furthermore,
p^{*}
has a right adjoint
p_{*}:\mathbf{Set}^{I}\to\mathbf{Sh}(\mathcal{C},j)
defined for
X\in\mathbf{Set}^{I}
by

p_{*}(X)=\prodop\displaylimits_{i\in I}(p_{i})_{*}(X_{i}).

In other words, a set of points
P=\{p_{i}\}
on a site
(\mathcal{C},j)
induces a geometric morphism
p_{*}:\mathbf{Set}^{I}\to\mathbf{Sh}(\mathcal{C},j)
.

The following result provides a convenient criterion for such a functor to reflect isomorphisms.

Lemma 10.14

(
[
Jar15
, Lemma 3.26]
)
.

Let
f_{*}:\mathcal{E}\to\mathcal{F}
be a geometric morphism of Grothendieck topoi. Then the following are equivalent:

(1)

The left adjoint
f^{*}:\mathcal{F}\to\mathcal{E}
is faithful,

(2)

f^{*}
reflects isomorphisms,

(3)

f^{*}
reflects epimorphisms,

(4)

f^{*}
reflects monomorphisms.

Proof.

(1)\Rightarrow(4)
Suppose that
f^{*}
is faithful, and
g:X\to Y
is a morphism in
\mathcal{F}
such that
f^{*}(g)
is a monomorphism. If
h,h^{\prime}:Y\to Z
are maps such that
hg=h^{\prime}g
, then
f^{*}(hg)=f^{*}(h)f^{*}(g)=f^{*}(h^{\prime})f^{*}(g)=f^{*}(h^{\prime}g)
implies that
f^{*}(h)=f^{*}(h)^{\prime}
, and since
f^{*}
is faithful,
h=h^{\prime}
.

A similar argument shows
f^{*}
reflects epimorphisms and therefore also isomorphisms. Thus
(1)\Rightarrow(3)
and
(1)\Rightarrow(2)
.

(3)\Rightarrow(1)
Suppose that
f^{*}
reflects epimorphisms, and suppose that
g,g^{\prime}:Y\to Z
are maps such that
f^{*}(g)=f^{*}(g^{\prime})
. Now in any category with equalizers, two maps
g,g^{\prime}
are equal if and only if for an equalizer diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Z}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces h}

the limit map
h
is an epimorphism. Since
f^{*}
preserves finite limits,
f^{*}(h)
is an equalizer of
f^{*}(g)
and
f^{*}(g^{\prime})
. Thus
f^{*}(h)
is an epimorphism in
\mathcal{E}
. But
f^{*}
reflects epimorphisms, thus
g=g^{\prime}
. A similar argument proves
(4)\Rightarrow(1)
and thus
(2)\Rightarrow(1)
.
∎

If
P=\{p_{i}\}
is a set of enough points for a site
(\mathcal{C},j)
, and
f:X\to Y
is a map of sheaves, then the following are equivalent:

(1)

p^{*}(f)=\langle p^{*}_{i}\rangle(f)
is an isomorphism,

(2)

p^{*}_{i}(f)
is an isomorphism of sets for each
i\in I
, and

(3)

f
is an isomorphism of sheaves.

It will be useful to characterize when a set of points
\{p_{i}\}
is a set of enough points using the actual functors
p_{i}:\mathbf{Pre}(\mathcal{C})\to\mathbf{Set}
. For this we must restrict the coverages we consider.

Now suppose that
(\mathcal{C},j)
is a subcanonical site (Definition
8.1
, so that the Yoneda embedding factors through
\mathbf{Sh}(\mathcal{C},j)
, and suppose that
P=\{p_{i}\}
is a set of points of
(\mathcal{C},j)
. If
P
is a set of enough points, then the induced functor

p^{*}:\mathbf{Sh}(\mathcal{C},j)\to\mathbf{Set}^{I}

reflects isomorphisms. Since
y:\mathcal{C}\hookrightarrow\mathbf{Sh}(\mathcal{C},j)
is fully faithful, it also reflects isomorphisms, so this implies that the composite functor

\mathcal{C}\xhookrightarrow{y}\mathbf{Sh}(\mathcal{C},j)\xrightarrow{p^{*}}\mathbf{Set}^{I}

reflects isomorphisms. Let us denote this composite functor by
p:\mathcal{C}\to\mathbf{Set}^{I}
, which is defined objectwise by

p(U)=(p_{i}(U))_{i\in I}.

We’ve shown that if
P
is a set of enough points, then
p
reflects isomorphisms. However the converse is also true. If
p
reflects isomorphisms, then since every sheaf
X\in\mathbf{Sh}(\mathcal{C},j)
is a colimit of representables and
p^{*}
is a left adjoint, this implies that
p^{*}
will reflect isomorphisms. Thus we have proven the following result.

Lemma 10.15
.

If
(\mathcal{C},j)
is a subcanonical site, and
P=\{p_{i}\}_{i\in I}
is a set of points of
(\mathcal{C},j)
, then
P
is a set of enough points for
(\mathcal{C},j)
if and only if the functor
p:\mathcal{C}\to\mathbf{Set}^{I}
given by the product
\langle p_{i}\rangle_{i\in I}
, reflects isomorphisms.

Lemma 10.16
.

Given a site
(\mathcal{C},j)
, and a set
P=\{p_{i}\}_{i\in I}
of enough points for
(\mathcal{C},j)
, a map
f:X\to Y
of sheaves on
(\mathcal{C},j)
is an epi/monomorphism of sheaves if and only if
p_{i}^{*}(f)
is a epi/monomorphism of sets for all
i\in I
.

Proof.

(\Rightarrow)
Since each
p_{i}^{*}
preserves finite colimits and limits, they each preserves epimorphisms and monomorphisms.

(\Leftarrow)
This follows from Lemma
10.14
.
∎

Corollary 10.17
.

Given a site
(\mathcal{C},j)
with a set
P=\{p_{i}\}_{i\in I}
of enough points, and a map
f:X\to Y
of presheaves on
\mathcal{C}
, the following are equivalent:

(1)

f
is a
j
-local epi/monomorphism of presheaves,

(2)

p_{i}^{*}(af)
is an epi/monomorphism of sets for every
i\in I
,

Proof.

(1)\Rightarrow(2)
is Corollary
10.12
.

(2)\Rightarrow(1)
follows from Lemma
10.16

and Corollary
7.37
.
∎

10.1.
Examples

10.1.1.
Points of the site of a topological space

In this section, we will show that if
X
is a topological space, then the site
(\mathcal{O}(X),j_{X})
as introduced in Example
2.9
, is a site with enough points.

For every
x\in X
consider the full subcategory
\mathcal{O}(X,x)
of
\mathcal{O}(X)
whose objects are those open subsets containing
x
. This category is finitely cofiltered. Indeed,
X\in\mathcal{O}(X,x)
, so
\mathcal{O}(X,x)
is nonempty. Suppose
U,V\in\mathcal{O}(X,x)
, then
x\in U\cap V
and
U\cap V\leq U
,
U\cap V\leq V
, and since
\mathcal{O}(X,x)
is a poset, this is sufficient to be finitely cofiltered. Now given
F\in\mathbf{Sh}(X)=\mathbf{Sh}(\mathcal{O}(X),j_{X})
, consider the set:

p_{x}^{*}F=\underset{U\in\mathcal{O}(X,x)^{\text{op}}}{\text{colim}}\,\,F(U).

In other words,
p_{x}^{*}F
is the set of equivalence classes of local sections
s\in F(U)
where
x\in U
and where if
x\in V
and
t\in F(V)
, then
s\sim t
if there exists a
W\subseteq U\cap V
with
x\in W
such that
s|_{W}=t|_{W}
. In other words,
p_{x}^{*}F
computes the set of
germs
of
F
at
x\in X
.

It is easy to see that this extends to a functor
p_{x}^{*}:\mathbf{Sh}(X)\to\mathbf{Set}
by the functoriality of colimits. Now suppose that
d:I\to\mathbf{Sh}(X)
is a finite diagram. Then we obtain a diagram
\widetilde{d}:\mathcal{O}(X,x)^{\text{op}}\times I\to\mathbf{Set}
with
\widetilde{d}(U,i)=d(i)(U)
. Since
\mathcal{O}(X,x)^{\text{op}}
is finitely filtered, by Proposition
C.15
, taking the
I
-limit and
\mathcal{O}(X,x)^{\text{op}}
-colimit of
\widetilde{d}
is commutative, and therefore
p_{x}^{*}
preserves finite limits in
\mathbf{Sh}(X)
. It is easy to check directly that
p_{x}^{*}
has a right adjoint given using the usual skyscraper sheaf construction, and hence defines a point
p_{x,*}:\mathbf{Set}\to\mathbf{Sh}(X)
.

Now if
U\in\mathcal{O}(X)
, then it is not hard to see that
y(U)
is a
j_{X}
-sheaf. So we can compute the
j_{X}
-continuous,
\mathbf{Set}
-flat functor
\mathcal{O}(X)\to\mathbf{Set}
corresponding to
p_{x}^{*}
by the composite functor

\mathcal{O}(X)\xhookrightarrow{y}\mathbf{Sh}(X)\xrightarrow{p_{x}^{*}}\mathbf{Set}.

So
p_{x}:\mathcal{O}(X)\to\mathbf{Set}
sends an open subset
V\subseteq X
to the set

p_{x}^{*}(y(V))\cong\underset{U\in\mathcal{O}(X,x)^{\text{op}}}{\text{colim}}\,y(V)(U).

Now
y(V)(U)
is empty unless
U\subseteq V
, in which case its a singleton. In other words, the functor
p_{x}:\mathcal{O}(X)\to\mathbf{Set}
sends an open subset
V\subseteq X
to the singleton set if
x\in V,
and to the empty set otherwise.

Given an element
s\in F(U)
, with
x\in U
, denote the image of
s
in
p_{x}^{*}F
by
[s]_{x}
. If
f:F\to G
is a map of sheaves on
X
, then it is easy to see that
p_{x}^{*}f:p_{x}^{*}F\to p_{x}^{*}G
is given by
p_{x}^{*}f[s]_{x}=[f(s)]_{x}
.

Lemma 10.18
.

Given a topological space
X
, the map

\langle p_{x}^{*}\rangle:\mathbf{Sh}(X)\to\prodop\displaylimits_{x\in X}\mathbf{Set}

constructed from the geometric morphisms arising from every point
x\in X
is a faithful functor.

Proof.

Suppose that
f,g:F\to G
are maps of sheaves on
X
such that
p_{x}^{*}f=p_{x}^{*}g
for all
x\in X
. Then if
s\in F(U)
, and
x\in U
, then we have
(p_{x}^{*}f)[s]_{x}=[f(s)]_{x}=[g(s)]_{x}=(p_{x}^{*}g)[s]_{x}
. Thus
f(s)|_{U_{x}}=g(s)|_{U_{x}}
for some
U_{x}\subseteq U
with
x\in U_{x}
. Thus if we vary over all
x\in U
, the collection
\{U_{x}\}
forms an open cover of
U
. Now
F|_{\mathcal{O}(U)}
and
G|_{\mathcal{O}(U)}
are sheaves on
U
, and
\{f(s)|_{U_{x}}=g(s)|_{U_{x}}\}_{x\in U}
forms a matching family on
U
. Thus
f(s)
and
g(s)
are both amalgamations of this matching family, and since amalgamations are unique for sheaves,
f(s)=g(s)
.
∎

Corollary 10.19
.

Given a topological space
X
, the set
\{p_{x}^{*}\}_{x\in X}
is a set of enough points for
\mathbf{Sh}(X)
.

Proof.

This follows from Lemma
10.18
and Lemma
10.14
.
∎

An alternative way we could have proven the above result is by showing that the functor
\langle p_{x}\rangle_{x\in X}:\mathcal{O}(X)\to\mathbf{Set}
reflects isomorphisms, by Lemma
10.15
. The only way that
\langle p_{x}\rangle_{x\in X}(U\subseteq V)
is an isomorphism is if
U
and
V
contain precisely the same points, i.e.
U=V
. Thus
\langle p_{x}\rangle_{x\in X}
reflects isomorphisms, and so
\{p_{x}\}_{x\in X}
is a set of enough points for
\mathcal{O}(X)
.

10.1.2.
(\mathbf{Set},j_{\text{epi}})
has enough points

Recall the coverage
j_{\text{epi}}
on
\mathbf{Set}
given by jointly epimorphic families from Example
2.8
. This site is subcanonical, and the identity functor
1_{\mathbf{Set}}:\mathbf{Set}\to\mathbf{Set}
is clearly
j_{\text{epi}}
-continuous and
\mathbf{Set}
-flat, and therefore defines a point. Since
1_{\mathbf{Set}}
reflects isomorphisms, by Lemma
10.15
,
\{1_{\mathbf{Set}}\}
is a set of enough points for
(\mathbf{Set},j_{\text{epi}})
.

10.1.3.
(\mathbf{Man},j_{\text{open}})
has enough points

Consider the category
\mathbf{Man}
of finite dimensional smooth manifolds, equipped with the open cover coverage
j_{\text{open}}
of Example
2.9
. We wish to show that this site has enough points. Let
p_{n}:\mathbf{Man}\to\mathbf{Set}
denote the functor defined objectwise by

p_{n}(M)=\underset{r\to\infty}{\text{colim}}\,\,\mathbf{Man}(B^{n}(1/r),M),

where
B^{n}(1/r)
is the
n
-dimensional ball of radius
1/r
, and for
r\leq r^{\prime}
the map
B^{n}(1/r^{\prime})\to B^{n}(1/r)
is just inclusion. The idea here being that we want to probe manifolds with vanishingly small
n
-dimensional balls. We want to show that
\{p_{n}\}_{n\geq 0}
is a set of enough points for
(\mathbf{Man},j_{\text{open}})
. Consider the corresponding functor
p_{n}^{*}:\mathbf{Sh}(\mathbf{Man},j_{\text{open}})\to\mathbf{Set}
given by

\displaystyle p_{n}^{*}(X)=X\otimes_{\mathbf{Man}}p_{n}

\displaystyle\cong\intop\nolimits^{y(M)\to X}X(M)\times p_{n}(M)

\displaystyle\cong\intop\nolimits^{y(M)\to X}X(M)\times\left(\underset{r\to\infty}{\text{colim}}\,\mathbf{Man}(B^{n}(1/r),M)\right)

\displaystyle\cong\underset{r\to\infty}{\text{colim}}\,\intop\nolimits^{y(M)\to X}X(M)\times y(M)(B^{n}(1/r))

\displaystyle\cong\underset{r\to\infty}{\text{colim}}\,X(B^{n}(1/r)).

Now since the colimit is obviously filtered, it is easy to see that
p_{n}^{*}
preserves finite limits. It also clearly has a right adjoint using the skyscraper construction. Thus we know that each
p_{n}
is
\mathbf{Set}
-flat and
j_{\text{open}}
-continuous. We now only need to show that
\{p_{n}\}_{n\geq 0}
jointly reflect isomorphisms.

To do this, we follow Schreiber’s proof
[
Sch13
, Proposition 4.3.1.7]
and introduce an auxillary site for each manifold
M\in\mathbf{Man}
. Let
\mathbf{Man}^{\text{open}}_{/M}
denote the full subcategory of
\mathbf{Man}_{/M}
on those maps
f:U\to M
that are open embeddings. Let
\pi:\mathbf{Man}^{\text{open}}_{/M}\to\mathcal{O}(M)
be the functor that takes the image of the open embeddings. Let
i:\mathbf{Man}_{/M}^{\text{open}}\to\mathbf{Man}_{/M}
be the inclusion functor. Equip
\mathbf{Man}_{/M}^{\text{open}}
with the restriction
j^{\text{open}}_{/M}
of the coverage
j_{/M}
. Let us show that
i:(\mathbf{Man}_{/M}^{\text{open}},j^{\text{open}}_{/M})\to(\mathbf{Man}_{/M},j_{/M})
is a dense morphism of sites. Since
i
is the inclusion of a full subcategory, conditions (D3) and (D4) of Definition
9.45
hold automatically. We need only check (D1) and (D2), but it is easy to see that these hold in this example. Thus by Theorem
9.52
,
i
induces an equivalence of sheaf topoi.

For each point
x\in M
and
r>0
, let
\varphi_{x}:B^{n}_{x}(1/r)\hookrightarrow M
denote a fixed open embedding of the
n
-dimensional ball of radius
1/r
into
M
such that
\varphi_{x}(0)=x
. Consider the functor
p_{n,x}^{*}:\mathbf{Sh}(\mathbf{Man}^{\text{open}}_{/M},j^{\text{open}}_{/M})\to\mathbf{Set}
defined objectwise by

p_{n,x}^{*}(X)=\underset{r\to\infty}{\text{colim}}\,X(B^{n}_{x}(1/r)).

It is easy to see that each
p^{*}_{n,x}
is a left adjoint that preserves finite limits. By practically the same argument as in Lemma
10.18
, it is not hard to see that
\{p^{*}_{n,x}\}_{n\geq 0,x\in M}
defines a set of enough points for
(\mathbf{Man}^{\text{open}}_{/M},j^{\text{open}}_{/M})
, equivalently
(\mathbf{Man}_{/M},j_{/M})
. By Lemma
9.41
, the projection map
\pi_{/M}:(\mathbf{Man}_{/M},j_{/M})\to(\mathbf{Man},j_{\text{open}})
is a comorphism of sites, and furthermore
\Delta_{\pi_{/M}}
sends sheaves to sheaves.

So if
X\in\mathbf{Sh}(\mathbf{Man},j_{\text{open}})
, then
\Delta_{\pi_{/M}}(X)\in\mathbf{Sh}(\mathbf{Man}_{/M},j_{/M})
and clearly

p_{n,x}^{*}(\Delta_{\pi_{/M}}(X))\cong p_{n,x^{\prime}}^{*}(\Delta_{\pi_{/M}}(X))\cong p^{*}_{n}(X),

for all pairs of points
x,x^{\prime}\in M
, simply because
B^{n}_{x}(1/r)\cong B^{n}_{x^{\prime}}(1/r)
.

So if
f:X\to Y
is a map of sheaves on
(\mathbf{Man},j_{\text{open}})
such that
p^{*}_{n,x}(\Delta_{\pi_{/M}})(f)
is an isomorphism for all
n\geq 0
and
x\in M
, then
f
is an isomorphism. But this is equivalent to
p_{n}^{*}(f)
being an isomorphism for every
n\geq 0
. Therefore
\{p_{n}^{*}\}
is also a set of enough points for
(\mathbf{Man},j_{\text{open}})
.

Remark 10.20
.

In fact, Schreiber proves
[
Sch13
, Proposition 4.3.1.7]
that the functor

p^{*}_{\infty}:\mathbf{Sh}(\mathbf{Man},j_{\text{open}})\to\mathbf{Set}
defined objectwise by

p_{\infty}^{*}(X)=\underset{n\to\infty}{\text{colim}}\,\underset{r\to\infty}{\text{colim}}\,X(B^{n}(1/r))

by itself is a set of enough points for
(\mathbf{Man},j_{\text{open}})
.

11.
Giraud’s Theorem

In this section, we will prove Giraud’s Theorem, which characterizes Grothendieck toposes entirely by exactness properties. We will also discuss how Giraud’s axioms are equivalent to what Rezk calls
weak descent

[
Rez10
, Section 2]
. We end this section with a discussion of what satisfying descent means, and how
\infty
-toposes repair this “weakness” of
1
-categorical Grothendieck toposes.

More precisely, we will be focused on proving the following meta-theorem:
Let
\mathcal{E}
be a category, then:

(D)^{\prime}\implies(G)\implies(T)\implies(D)^{\prime}

where

(1)

(D)^{\prime}
is the statement that
\mathcal{E}
is locally presentable and satisfies weak descent,

(2)

(G)
is the statement that
\mathcal{E}
is a Giraud category and

(3)

(T)
is the statement that
\mathcal{E}
is a Grothendieck topos.

Thus this will prove that
(D)^{\prime}\Leftrightarrow(G)\Leftrightarrow(T)
.
This will take a good deal of work, and in places we refer to the literature, but it is the basis for the theory of Grothendieck toposes. Namely giving three equivalent definitions for what a Grothendieck topos is.
(D)^{\prime}
provides a perspective that generalizes most efficiently to model topoi and
\infty
-topoi, and makes explicit the difference between Grothendieck topoi and model topoi, while
(G)
is an internal perspective, it gives a characterization of Grothendieck topoi based on their internal categorical structure.

11.1.
Giraud’s Axioms

In this section we briefly introduce Giraud’s axioms. These are a list of conditions whose conjunction is necessary and sufficient for a category to be equivalent to a Grothendieck topos. The first two are easy to state, while the third will need some preliminary definitions.

Let
\mathcal{E}
be a locally presentable category:

•

(G1):
(Disjoint coproducts) For any objects
X,Y\in\mathcal{E}
, the following diagram is a pullback:

(42)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varnothing}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{X+Y}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lrcorner}

•

(G2):
(Universal colimits) Given a morphism
f:X\to Y
in
\mathcal{E}
, the pullback functor

f^{*}:\mathcal{E}_{/Y}\to\mathcal{E}_{/X}

preserves small colimits,

•

(G3):
(Effective equivalence relations) equivalence relations in
\mathcal{E}
are effective.

Definition 11.1
.

We refer to the conditions
(G1)-(G3)
as
Giraud’s Axioms
. We call a category
\mathcal{E}
a
Giraud category
if it is locally presentable and satisfies Giraud’s axioms. Let
(G)
be the statement
\mathcal{E}
is a Giraud category.

Now we will explain what we mean by
(G3)
. Because equivalence relations are important in what follows, we dedicate a small section to them.

11.1.1.
Equivalence Relations

Definition 11.2
.

Let
\mathcal{C}
be a category with finite limits and
X\in\mathcal{C}
an object. An
equivalence relation
on
X
is a pair of morphisms
s,t:R\to X
such that:

(1)

(Relation) the map
R\xhookrightarrow{(s,t)}X\times X
is a monomorphism,

(2)

(Reflexivity) The diagonal factors through
R
:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{X\times X}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces R}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Delta_{X}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces r}

(3)

(Symmetry) There exists a map
i:R\to R
such that
s=ti
and
t=si
,

(4)

(Transitivity) There exists a map
c:R\times_{X}R\to R
such that if

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{R\times_{X}R}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces R}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces R}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces s}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces q_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces t}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces q_{2}}

is a pullback, then the following diagram commutes:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{R\times_{X}R}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{X\times X}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces R}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{(sq_{2},tq_{1})}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces c}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{(s,t)}}

The definition of an equivalence relation is a bit unwieldy, so it will be helpful to obtain another method to manipulate them. By a relation
R\subseteq X\times X
on an object
X
in a category
\mathcal{C}
, we mean a pair of maps
s,t:R\to X
such that
R\xhookrightarrow{(s,t)}X\times X
is a monomorphism.

Given a relation
R\subseteq X\times X
, we obtain a monomorphism of presheaves
y(R)\hookrightarrow y(X\times X)\cong y(X)\times y(X)
.

Lemma 11.3
.

Given a finitely complete category
\mathcal{C}
, a relation
R\subseteq X\times X
is an equivalence relation on
X
if and only if for every
U\in\mathcal{C}
, the relation of sets

y(R)(U)=\mathcal{C}(U,R)\subseteq\mathcal{C}(U,X)^{2}\cong\mathcal{C}(U,X\times X)=y(X\times X)(U)

is an equivalence relation in the classical sense.

Proof.

(\Rightarrow)
Given an equivalence relation
R\subseteq X\times X
, we want to show that for every
U\in\mathcal{C}
, the relation
\sim
on morphisms
f,g:U\to X
where
f\sim g
if the map
(f,g):U\to X\times X
factors through
R
, is an equivalence relation.

It is now easy to read off the properties of
\sim
being an equivalence relation diagrammatically from Definition
11.2
. For example, if
f:U\to X
, then
f\sim f
, since the following diagram commutes by Definition
11.2
.(1)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces U}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{X\times X}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces R}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(f,f)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Delta_{X}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces r}

The other conditions follow from similar arguments.

(\Leftarrow)
Suppose that
R\subseteq X\times X
is a relation, and suppose that for every
U\in\mathcal{C}
, the relation
\sim
on morphisms
U\to X
introduced above is an equivalence relation.

Since
\sim
is reflexive, this means that
1_{X}\sim 1_{X}
. In other words, the diagonal map
(1_{X},1_{X}):X\to X\times X
factors through
R
. This defines the map
r:X\to R
satisfying Definition
11.2
.(2).

Since
\sim
is symmetric, that means that the map
(t,s):R\to X\times X
factors through
(s,t):R\hookrightarrow X\times X
. This defines the map
i:R\to R
satisfying Definition
11.2
.(3).

Now first note that
sq_{2}\sim sq_{1}
, because
sq_{1}=tq_{2}
, so the following diagram commutes

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{R\times_{X}R}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{X\times X}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces R}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(sq_{2},sq_{1})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces q_{2}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(s,t)}

Similarly
tq_{2}\sim tq_{1}
, so the following diagram commutes

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{R\times_{X}R}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{X\times X}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces R}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(tq_{2},tq_{1})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces q_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(s,t)}

But since
\sim
is transitive, this means that
sq_{2}\sim tq_{1}
, so there is a map
c
satisfying Definition
11.2
.(4).
∎

Example 11.4
.

Recall the notion of kernel pair from Definition
A.59
. Every kernel pair provides an equivalence relation. Indeed, given
f:X\to Y
with kernel pair
p_{0},p_{1}:X\times_{Y}X\to X
, then for maps
g,h:U\to X
, we have
g\sim h
if and only if
fg=fh
. This is easily seen to be an equivalence relation.

Example 11.5
.

Given any
X\in\mathcal{C}
, the diagonal map
\Delta_{X}:X\to X\times X
is an equivalence relation. Indeed for maps
f,g:U\to X
,
f\sim g
if and only if
f=g
.

Lemma 11.6
.

Given equivalence relations
R\subseteq X\times X
and
S\subseteq X\times X
, on
X
, the union subobject
R\cup S\subseteq X\times X
is also an equivalence relation on
X
.

Example 11.7
.

Given any monomorphisms
f,g:A\hookrightarrow B
in
\mathcal{C}
, the subobject
A\xhookrightarrow{(f,g)}B\times B
is a relation. Suppose further that
A
satisfies Definition
11.2
.(4), i.e. it is transitive. Let
(g,f)
denote the subobject
A\xhookrightarrow{(g,f)}B\times B
, and let
(f,g)^{*}
denote the subobject

(f,g)^{*}=\Delta_{B}\cup(f,g)\cup(g,f).

Then
(f,g)^{*}
is an equivalence relation. This is really a special case of constructing the smallest equivalence relation containing
(f,g)
, see
[
Hen19
]
.

Definition 11.8
.

Recall the notion of quotient object from Definition
A.56
. Let
X
be an object in a finitely bicomplete category
\mathcal{C}
, and let
R
be an equivalence relation on
X
. Let
X/R
denote the coequalizer in
\mathcal{C}
:

R\overset{s}{\underset{t}{\rightrightarrows}}X\xrightarrow{q}X/R

i.e
X/R
is the quotient object of
X
by
R
.

An equivalence relation
R
on an object
X
is said to be
effective
if the following diagram is a pullback:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces R}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{X/R}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces s}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces t}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces q}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces q}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lrcorner}

11.1.2.
Technical Results

Now let us introduce a couple of technical results about categories satisfying Giraud’s axioms (G) that will be helpful in what follows. We owe much to Simon Henry and Michael Shulman’s guidance in
[
Hen24
]
. For the next result recall the definition of intersections and unions of subobjects from Lemmas
A.38
and
A.39
.

Lemma 11.9

(
[
Joh02
, Lemma A.1.4.8]
)
.

Given a Giraud category
\mathcal{E}
(Definition
11.1
), monomorphisms
f:A\hookrightarrow B
,
g:A\hookrightarrow C
and a pushout square

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces C}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces P}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces k}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces h}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lrcorner}

then the morphisms
h
and
k
are monomorphisms. Furthermore the square is a pullback square.

Proof.

Let
D=B+C
,
b:B\hookrightarrow B+C
and
c:C\hookrightarrow B+C
be the inclusion maps. Let
R=B+A+A+C
. Following
[
Joh02
, Lemma A.1.4.8]
we will write this as
R=B+A_{1}+A_{2}+C
to help keep track of which
A
we are talking about in the course of the proof. Let
r
and
r^{\prime}
denote the morphisms

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{(B+C)+A_{1}+A_{2}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{B+C}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces r=(1_{B+C},bf,cg)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces r^{\prime}=(1_{B+C},cg,bf}

Now the relation
A\xhookrightarrow{(bf,cg)}(B+C)\times(B+C)
is transitive. Indeed if
p,q,r:U\to(B+C)
are maps such that
p\sim q
and
q\sim r
, then there exist maps
\ell,\ell^{\prime}:U\to A
such that
p=bf\ell
,
q=cg\ell
,
q=bf\ell^{\prime}
and
r=cg\ell^{\prime}
. In other words we get an induced map

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces U}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varnothing}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces C}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{B+C}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\ell^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\ell}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lrcorner}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces c}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces b}

where
\varnothing
is initial by
\mathcal{E}
having disjoint coproducts (G1). Thus
U\cong\varnothing
, and it then follows that
p\sim r
. Thus
R\xhookrightarrow{(r,r^{\prime})}D
is the relation
(bf,cg)^{*}
from Example
11.7
and hence an equivalence relation.

Now let
P
be the coequalizer

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces R}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B+C}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces P}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces r}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces r^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces q}

and let us consider the following commutative diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{A_{1}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{B+A_{1}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{A_{1}+C}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces R}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{B+C}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces C}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{B+C}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces P}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(1_{B},f)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces b}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(g,1_{C})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces r^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces r}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces q}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces c}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces q}

where the unlabelled maps are inclusions. The bottom right hand square is a pullback by (G3), i.e. because
R
is an equivalence relation and therefore effective. All of the other squares can be verified to be pullbacks as well.

Now we wish to show that
qc
and
qb
are monomorphisms. So consider the commutative diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{B+A_{1}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{B+A_{2}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces R}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{B+C}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{B+C}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces P}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(1_{B},f)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces b}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(1_{B},f)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces r^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces r}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces q}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces b}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces q}

It can again be checked that each square is a pullback, so that the whole outer square is a pullback. But this is the same thing as saying that the square

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces P}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 1_{B}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 1_{B}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces qb}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces qb}

is a pullback, which is equivalent to
qb
being a monomorphism. Using the same trick again proves that
qc
is a monomorphism as well.

Now notice that this also shows that the intersection
B\cap C
of the subobjects
B\xhookrightarrow{qb}P
and
C\xhookrightarrow{qc}P
is
A
. Thus it is then not hard to see that the outermost square is also a pushout.
∎

Lemma 11.10

(
[
Joh02
, Lemma A.1.4.9]
)
.

Given a Giraud category
\mathcal{E}
, every monomorphism in
\mathcal{E}
is an effective monomorphism (Definition
A.45
).

Proof.

Given a monomorphism
f:X\to Y
in
\mathcal{E}
, by Lemma
11.9
, the cokernel pair (Definition
A.43
)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{Y+_{X}Y}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces i_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces i_{0}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lrcorner}

is also a pullback, which implies that

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{Y+_{X}Y}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces i_{0}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces i_{1}}

is an equalizer. Thus
f
is an effective monomorphism.
∎

Lemma 11.11
.

If
\mathcal{E}
is a Giraud category, then
\mathcal{E}
is balanced, i.e. if
f:X\to Y
is a morphism in
\mathcal{E}
that is both a monomorphism and an epimorphism, then it is an isomorphism.

Proof.

If
f:X\to Y
is a monomorphism and an epimorphism, then by Lemma
11.10
,
f
is an effective monomorphism, so it is the equalizer of its cokernel pair,

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{Y+_{X}Y}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces i_{0}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces i_{1}}

But
f
is also an epimorphism, and
i_{0}f=i_{1}f
, so
i_{0}=i_{1}
. But the equalizer of
i_{0}
with
i_{0}
is just the identity map

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{Y+_{X}Y}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 1_{Y}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces i_{0}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces i_{0}}

Since equalizers are unique up to isomorphism, this shows that
f:X\to Y
is an isomorphism.
∎

Lemma 11.12

(
[
MM12
, Appendix Lemma 2.2]
)
.

If
\mathcal{E}
is a Giraud category, then every morphism
f:X\to Y
in
\mathcal{E}
can be factored as

X\overset{e_{f}}{\twoheadrightarrow}Z_{f}\xhookrightarrow{\iota_{f}}Y

where
e_{f}
is an effective epimorphism and
\iota_{f}
is a monomorphism.

Proof.

Let
Z_{f}=\text{coim}_{\text{reg}}(f)
denote the regular coimage (Definition
A.60
) of
f
, i.e. the coequalizer of the kernel pair of
f

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{X\times_{Y}X}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{Z_{f}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p_{0}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces e_{f}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota_{f}}

Now by definition
e_{f}
is an effective epimorphism. We wish to show that
\iota_{f}
is a monomorphism.

So suppose there are maps
n,m:A\to Z_{f}
such that
\iota_{f}n=\iota_{f}m
. Then by the universal property of pullbacks we have a unique induced map

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{Z_{f}\times_{Y}Z_{f}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{Z_{f}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{Z_{f}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces h}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces m}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces n}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lrcorner}

Now by the pasting law for pullback diagrams we have a commutative diagram where each square is a pullback

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{X\times_{Y}X}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{Z_{f}\times_{Y}X}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{X\times_{Y}Z_{f}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{Z_{f}\times_{Y}Z_{f}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{Z_{f}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{Z_{f}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lrcorner}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lrcorner}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces e_{f}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lrcorner}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lrcorner}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces e_{f}}

Furthermore, since epimorphisms are stable in
\mathcal{E}
by Corollary
11.14
, the composite map above which we will denote by
q:X\times_{Y}X\to Z_{f}\times_{Y}Z_{f}
is an epimorphism.

Therefore the pullback map

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{A\times_{Z_{f}\times_{Y}Z_{f}}(X\times_{Y}X)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{X\times_{Y}X}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{Z_{f}\times_{Y}Z_{f}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(n^{\prime},m^{\prime})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces h^{*}(q)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lrcorner}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces q}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(n,m)}

is an epimorphism as well.

Now note that
p_{0}(n^{\prime},m^{\prime})=n^{\prime}
and
p_{1}(n^{\prime},m^{\prime})=m^{\prime}
. Since
e_{f}p_{0}=e_{f}p_{1}
we have

e_{f}n^{\prime}=e_{f}p_{0}(n^{\prime},m^{\prime})=e_{f}p_{1}(n^{\prime},m^{\prime})=e_{f}m^{\prime}

Therefore

nh^{*}(q)=e_{f}n^{\prime}=e_{f}m^{\prime}=mh^{*}(q).

But
h^{*}(q)
is an epimorphism, so
n=m
. Thus
\iota_{f}
is a monomorphism.
∎

Lemma 11.13
.

Given a Giraud category
\mathcal{E}
, a morphism
f:X\to Y
in
\mathcal{E}
is an epimorphism if and only if for the regular coimage factorization

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\text{coim}_{\text{reg}}(f)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces e}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces i}

the map
i
is an isomorphism.

Proof.

(\Leftarrow)
if
i
is an isomorphism, then it is an epimorphism, so
ie=f
is an epimorphism.

(\Rightarrow)
if
f=ie
is an epimorphism, then
i
is an epimorphism and a monomorphism, so by Lemma
11.11
,
i
is an isomorphism.
∎

Corollary 11.14
.

If
\mathcal{E}
is a Giraud category, then all epimorphisms in
\mathcal{E}
are effective epimorphisms.

Proof.

By Lemma
11.13
, if
f:X\to Y
is an epimorphism, then
f
is isomorphic to
e_{f}:X\to\text{coim}_{\text{reg}}(f)
. But coequalizers are unique up to isomorphism, so
f
is therefore the coequalizer of its own kernel pair and hence an effective epimorphism.
∎

Lemma 11.15
.

If
\mathcal{E}
is a Giraud category, then epimorphisms in
\mathcal{E}
are stable under pullback. In other words if
f:X\to Y
is an epimorphism in
\mathcal{E}
and
g:Z\to Y
is an arbitrary morphism and the following commutative diagram is a pullback

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{Z\times_{Y}X}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Z}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g^{*}(f)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lrcorner}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g}

then
g^{*}(f)
is an epimorphism.

Proof.

Suppose that
f:X\to Y
is an epimorphism and
g:Z\to Y
is an arbitrary morphism in
\mathcal{E}
. By Corollary
11.14
,
f
is an effective epimorphism, hence
f
is the coequalizer of its kernel pair. Thus

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{X\times_{Y}X}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p_{0}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}

is a coequalizer. Now by the dual of
[
Rie17
, Proposition 3.3.8]
, the projection functor
\pi_{Y}:\mathcal{E}_{/Y}\to\mathcal{E}
strictly creates all colimits. In other words, the diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{X\times_{Y}X}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p_{0}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}

is a coequalizer in
\mathcal{E}_{/Y}
. Now by (G2), the pullback functor
g^{*}:\mathcal{E}_{/Y}\to\mathcal{E}_{/Z}
preserves colimits, so the diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{g^{*}(X)\times_{Z}g^{*}(X)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{g^{*}(X)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Z}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Z}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces q_{0}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces q_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g^{*}(f)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g^{*}(f)}

is a coequalizer in
\mathcal{E}_{/Z}
. By the dual of
[
Rie17
, Proposition 3.3.3]
the functor
\pi_{Z}:\mathcal{E}_{/Z}\to\mathcal{E}
preserves colimits, and hence
g^{*}(f)
is a coequalizer and hence an epimorphism.
∎

11.2.
Giraud’s Axioms implies Grothendieck Topos

The goal for this section is to prove that if
\mathcal{E}
is a locally presentable category then
(G)\implies(T)
. This is a classical theorem of Giraud
[
AGV72
]
, who actually proves
(G)\Leftrightarrow(T)
, but we prefer to prove the one direction so that we may prove all of the equivalences at once. Our method of proof was inspired by
[
Bar19
]
.

For the rest of this section, assume that
\mathcal{E}
is a Giraud category, so in particular
\mathcal{E}
is locally presentable. This implies that there exists a reflective localization:

(43)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{E}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathbf{Pre}(\mathcal{C})}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces L}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\dashv}

where
\mathcal{C}
is a small full subcategory of
\mathcal{E}
whose objects are
\kappa
-presentable for some regular cardinal
\kappa
, and
\iota
is the yoneda embedding restricted to
\mathcal{C}
, i.e. if
U\in\mathcal{E}
, then if
j:\mathcal{C}\hookrightarrow\mathcal{E}
is the inclusion of the full subcategory, then
\iota
is the functor
\iota(U)=y(U)|_{\mathcal{C}}=\mathcal{E}(j(-),U)
and furthermore preserves
\kappa
-filtered colimits.

Let us define a coverage
j_{\text{Gir}}
on
\mathcal{C}
so that we can obtain an equivalence
\mathbf{Sh}(\mathcal{C},j_{\text{Gir}})\simeq\mathcal{E}
.

Given
U\in\mathcal{C}
, let
j_{\text{Gir}}(U)
be the set of families
r=\{r_{i}:U_{i}\to U\}_{i\in I}
in
\mathcal{C}
such that

\sumop\displaylimits_{i\in I}r_{i}:\sumop\displaylimits_{i\in I}U_{i}\to U

is an epimorphism in
\mathcal{E}
.

Lemma 11.16
.

The collection of families
j_{\text{Gir}}
forms a coverage on
\mathcal{C}
.

Proof.

Suppose that
U\in\mathcal{C}
,
r=\{r_{i}:U_{i}\to U\}\in j_{\text{Gir}}(U)
and
g:V\to U
is a morphism in
\mathcal{C}
. Then we can form the pullback

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{g^{*}(\sumop\displaylimits_{i}U_{i})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\sumop\displaylimits_{i}U_{i}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces V}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces U}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g^{*}(\sumop\displaylimits_{i}r_{i})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sumop\displaylimits_{i}r_{i}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g}

in
\mathcal{E}
. Now by Lemma
11.15
, epimorphisms in
\mathcal{E}
are stable under pullback. Thus
g^{*}(\sumop\displaylimits_{i}r_{i})
is an epimorphism in
\mathcal{E}
and furthermore by (G2) is isomorphic to

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\sumop\displaylimits_{i}g^{*}(U_{i})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\sumop\displaylimits_{i}U_{i}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces V}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces U}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sumop\displaylimits_{i}g^{*}(r_{i})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sumop\displaylimits_{i}r_{i}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g}

Thus
g^{*}(r)=\{g^{*}(U_{i})\to V\}
(note we are abusing notation here,
g^{*}(r)
just denotes the family of pullbacks) is a
j_{\text{Gir}}
covering family of
V
. Thus
j_{\text{Gir}}
is a coverage.
∎

Due to Lemma
11.16
, we call
j_{\text{Gir}}
the
Giraud Coverage
on
\mathcal{C}
.

Lemma 11.17
.

The Giraud coverage
j_{\text{Gir}}
is saturated.

Proof.

Let us show that
j_{\text{Gir}}
is refinement closed. Suppose that
U\in\mathcal{C}
,
t=\{V_{j}\to U\}
is a family on
U
,
r=\{U_{i}\to U\}
is a
j_{\text{Gir}}
-covering family on
U
and there is a refinement
g:t\to r
. That means that there is a map
\sumop\displaylimits_{j}V_{j}\to\sumop\displaylimits_{i}U_{i}
such that the following diagram commutes

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\sumop\displaylimits_{j}V_{j}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\sumop\displaylimits_{i}U_{i}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces V}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sumop\displaylimits_{j}t_{j}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sumop\displaylimits_{i}r_{i}}

But
\sumop\displaylimits_{i}r_{i}
is an epimorphism, which implies that
\sumop\displaylimits_{i}r_{i}\circ g=\sumop\displaylimits_{j}t_{j}
is an epimorphism. Thus
t
is a
j_{\text{Gir}}
-covering family on
U
.

Let us show that
j_{\text{Gir}}
is composition-closed. Suppose that
r=\{r_{i}:U_{i}\to U\}_{i\in I}\in j_{\text{Gir}}(U)
and
t^{i}=\{t^{i}_{j}:V^{i}_{j}\to U_{i}\}_{j\in J_{i}}\in j_{\text{Gir}}(U_{i})
, then
t^{i}:\sumop\displaylimits_{j}V^{i}_{j}\to U_{i}
is an epimorphism, and since coproducts of epimorphisms are epimorphisms, the map

\sumop\displaylimits_{i}\sumop\displaylimits_{j}V^{i}_{j}\xrightarrow{\sumop\displaylimits_{i}t^{i}}\sumop\displaylimits_{i}U_{i}

is an epimorphism. But the map
r:\sumop\displaylimits_{i}U_{i}\to U
is also an epimorphism, thus the composite

\sumop\displaylimits_{i}\sumop\displaylimits_{j}V^{i}_{j}\xrightarrow{\sumop\displaylimits_{i}t^{i}}\sumop\displaylimits_{i}U_{i}\xrightarrow{r}U

is an epimorphism. Thus the composite family
\cup_{i}(t^{i}\circ r)
is an epimorphism and hence in
j_{\text{Gir}}(U)
.
∎

We now wish to show that the functor
\iota:\mathcal{E}\to\mathbf{Pre}(\mathcal{C})
factors through
\mathbf{Sh}(\mathcal{C},j_{\text{Gir}})
. In other words we want to show the following.

Proposition 11.18
.

For every
V\in\mathcal{E}
the presheaf
y(V)|_{\mathcal{C}}=\mathcal{E}(j(-),V)
on
\mathcal{C}
is a
j_{\text{Gir}}
-sheaf.

Proof.

Suppose that
U,V\in\mathcal{C}
and
r=\{U_{i}\to U\}\in j_{\text{Gir}}(U)
. Then
r:\sumop\displaylimits_{i}U_{i}\to U
is an epimorphism in
\mathcal{E}
. By Corollary
11.14
,
r
is the coequalizer of its kernel pair, and by (G2), we can write this as the coequalizer

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\sumop\displaylimits_{i,j\in I}U_{i}\times_{U}U_{j}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\sumop\displaylimits_{i}U_{i}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces U}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p_{0}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces r}

Now each
U_{i}\times_{U}U_{j}
may not be an object in
\mathcal{C}
, but it can be written as a colimit
U_{i}\times_{U}U_{j}\cong\text{colim}_{k}B_{k}^{ij}
where each
B^{ij}_{k}\in\mathcal{C}
. Since we can write any colimit as a coequalizer, then the cocone map
\sumop\displaylimits_{k}B^{ij}_{k}\to U_{i}\times_{U}U_{j}
is an epimorphism. Therefore if we let

h:\sumop\displaylimits_{i,j}\sumop\displaylimits_{k}B^{ij}_{k}\to\sumop\displaylimits_{i,j}U_{i}\times_{U}U_{j}

denote the resulting epimorphism and let
q_{0}=p_{0}h
,
q_{1}=p_{1}h
, then the following diagram is still a coequalizer

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\sumop\displaylimits_{i,j}\sumop\displaylimits_{k}B^{ij}_{k}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\sumop\displaylimits_{i}U_{i}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces U}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces q_{0}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces q_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces r}

Indeed if
\ell:\sumop\displaylimits_{i}U_{i}\to Q
is a map such that
\ell q_{0}=\ell q_{1}
, then
\ell q_{0}=\ell p_{0}h=\ell p_{1}h=\ell q_{1}
, but since
h
is an epimorphism this implies that
\ell p_{0}=\ell p_{1}
. Since
r
is a coequalizer of
p_{0}
and
p_{1}
this gives a unique map
U\to Q
making the diagram commute. Thus
r
is also a coequalizer of
q_{0}
and
q_{1}
.

Now we apply
y(V)|_{\mathcal{C}}
to the above coequalizer diagram and we obtain an equalizer diagram

y(V)|_{\mathcal{C}}(U)\to\text{eq}\left(\prodop\displaylimits_{i}y(V)|_{\mathcal{C}}(U_{i})\underset{p_{1}^{*}}{\overset{p_{0}^{*}}{\rightrightarrows}}\prodop\displaylimits_{i,j}y(V)|_{\mathcal{C}}(U_{i})\times_{y(V)|_{\mathcal{C}}(U)}y(V)|_{\mathcal{C}}(U_{j})\xrightarrow{h^{*}}\prodop\displaylimits_{i,j}\prodop\displaylimits_{k}y(V)|_{\mathcal{C}}(B^{ij}_{k})\right)

but since
h
is an epimorphism,
h^{*}
is a monomorphism, which implies that the following diagram is an equalizer

y(V)|_{\mathcal{C}}(U)\to\text{eq}\left(\prodop\displaylimits_{i}y(V)|_{\mathcal{C}}(U_{i})\underset{p_{1}^{*}}{\overset{p_{0}^{*}}{\rightrightarrows}}\prodop\displaylimits_{i,j}y(V)|_{\mathcal{C}}(U_{i})\times_{y(V)|_{\mathcal{C}}(U)}y(V)|_{\mathcal{C}}(U_{j})\right)

thus
y(V)|_{\mathcal{C}}
is a
j_{\text{Gir}}
-sheaf.
∎

So we have managed to factor the reflective localization
L\dashv\iota
through
\mathbf{Sh}(\mathcal{C},j_{\text{Gir}})

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathbf{Sh}(\mathcal{C},j_{\text{Gir}})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathbf{Pre}(\mathcal{C})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{E}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces i}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces L^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces a}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces L}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota}

So
\iota=i\iota^{\prime}
and
L=L^{\prime}a
for adjoint functors
L^{\prime}\dashv\iota^{\prime}
.

Proposition 11.19
.

The adjunction
L^{\prime}\dashv\iota^{\prime}
is an adjoint equivalence of categories.

Proof.

Since
\iota
and
i
are fully faithful, so is
\iota^{\prime}
. So for every
V\in\mathcal{E}
, the counit
\varepsilon_{V}:L^{\prime}(\iota^{\prime}(V))\to V
is an isomorphism.

Now if
U\in\mathcal{C}
, then by Proposition
11.18
,
y(U)\in\mathbf{Sh}(\mathcal{C},j_{\text{Gir}})
and
L^{\prime}(y(U))\cong L^{\prime}(ay(U))=L(y(U))=U
. Thus the unit is isomorphic to

y(U)\to\iota^{\prime}(L^{\prime}(ay(U)))\cong\iota^{\prime}(U)\cong\mathcal{E}(j(-),U)\cong\mathcal{C}(-,U)\cong y(U)

and hence is an isomorphism.

Now for
X\in\mathbf{Pre}(\mathcal{C})
, by Lemma
C.35
,
X
can be written as a filtered colimit of representables
X\cong\underset{i\in I}{\text{colim}}\,y(U_{i})
. Thus if
X
is a sheaf then

X\cong aX\cong a\underset{i\in I}{\text{colim}}\,y(U_{i})\cong\underset{i\in I}{\text{colim}}\,ay(U_{i})\cong\underset{i\in I}{\text{colim}}\,y(U_{i})

where the last isomorphism holds by Proposition
11.18
. So the unit is isomorphic to

\displaystyle X\xrightarrow{\eta_{X}}

\displaystyle\iota^{\prime}(L^{\prime}(X))

\displaystyle\cong\iota^{\prime}(L^{\prime}(\underset{i\in I}{\text{colim}}\,y(U_{i})))

\displaystyle\cong\iota^{\prime}(\underset{i\in I}{\text{colim}}\,U_{i})

\displaystyle\cong\mathcal{E}(j(-),\underset{i\in I}{\text{colim}}\,U_{i})

\displaystyle\cong\underset{i\in I}{\text{colim}}\,\mathcal{E}(j(-),U_{i})

\displaystyle\cong\underset{i\in I}{\text{colim}}\,y(U_{i})

\displaystyle\cong X

where the third to last isomorphism holds because
I
is filtered and
j:\mathcal{C}\hookrightarrow\mathcal{E}
is the inclusion of subcategory of presentable objects. Thus the unit is an isomorphism. Thus
L^{\prime}\dashv\iota^{\prime}
is an adjoint equivalence of categories.
∎

Thus we have proven (G)
\Rightarrow
(T).

Theorem 11.20
.

If
\mathcal{E}
is a Giraud category
(G)
, then
\mathcal{E}
is a Grothendieck topos (T).

11.3.
Descent

In this section we define what it means for a category to have descent. This property is also known as having Van Kampen colimits. We show that
\mathbf{Set}
does not have all Van Kampen colimits, i.e. does not have descent, but satisfies a weaker property, identified by Rezk
[
Rez10
]
, and which he appropriately refers to as weak descent.

For the first part of this section we will be a little sketchier than usual in motivating descent, and we will use some terms that we will not define, as following the details would quickly lead us down quite an advanced rabbit hole, but we will return to a much more detailed discussion in Section
11.4
. We recommend the reader consult
[
Ane19
]
for more details on this advanced topic, which was one of our inspirations for this section.

Let
\mathcal{C}
be a category with all small colimits and finite limits (which we will refer to as a cocomplete lex category in what follows), and consider the following pseudo-functor
\mathbb{U}:\mathcal{C}^{\text{op}}\to\mathbf{CAT}
, called the
universe of
\mathcal{C}
, defined on objects by

X\mapsto\mathcal{C}_{/X}

and morphisms by

\left(f:X\to Y\right)\mapsto\left(f^{*}:\mathcal{C}_{/Y}\to\mathcal{C}_{/X}\right)

clearly for a composable pair
X\xrightarrow{f}Y\xrightarrow{g}Z
in
\mathcal{C}
, there exists a natural isomorphism
(g\circ f)^{*}\cong g^{*}\circ f^{*}
, and
(1_{X})^{*}\cong 1_{\mathcal{C}_{/X}}
, thus defining a pseudofunctor.

Given a cocomplete lex category
\mathcal{C}
, we can ask whether its universe
\mathbb{U}:\mathcal{C}^{\text{op}}\to\mathbf{CAT}
sends limits to (pseudo)limits. An equivalent way of asking this is to ask whether for a small diagram
X:I\to\mathcal{C}
there is an equivalence of categories

\mathcal{C}_{/{\underset{i\in I}{\text{colim}}\,X_{i}}}\simeq\lim_{i\in I}\mathcal{C}_{/X_{i}}

where the right hand side is a pseudolimit in
\mathbf{CAT}
.

There is a nice way of getting a handle on the above question. First let
\mathbf{Cart}(\mathcal{C}^{I})
denote the category whose

(1)

objects are functors
X:I\to\mathcal{C}
, and

(2)

whose morphisms are natural transformations
\alpha:Y\Rightarrow X
such that every component square is a pullback:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{Y_{i}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{X_{i}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{Y_{j}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{X_{j}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\alpha_{i}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\alpha_{j}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lrcorner}

We call natural transformations with this property
Cartesian
.

Lemma 11.21
.

Given a cocomplete lex category
\mathcal{C}
and a small diagram
X:I\to\mathcal{C}
, there is an equivalence of categories

\lim_{i\in I}\mathcal{C}_{/X_{i}}\simeq\mathbf{Cart}(\mathcal{C}^{I})_{/X}.

Thanks to the above lemma, we can now re-express our desire for
\mathbb{U}
to preserves limits as asking that the following adjunction be an adjoint equivalence:

(44)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{C}_{/\text{colim}_{i}X_{i}}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathbf{Cart}(\mathcal{C}^{I})_{/X}}}
colim
cst
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\dashv}

where
\alpha=\text{cst}(Y\to\text{colim}_{i}\,X_{i})
is the natural transformation given objectwise by pullback:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{Y_{i}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{X_{i}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\text{colim}_{i}\,X_{i}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\alpha_{i}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lrcorner}

and
\text{colim}(\alpha:Y\Rightarrow X)
is simply the induced map of colimits
\text{colim}_{i}\,Y_{i}\to\text{colim}_{i}\,X_{i}
.

Definition 11.22
.

Given a cocomplete lex category
\mathcal{C}
, we say that a small diagram
X:I\to\mathcal{C}
is
Van Kampen
if (
44
) exists and is an adjoint equivalence. If
S
is a class of diagrams in
\mathcal{C}
, then we say that
\mathcal{C}
has Van Kampen
S
-colimits, if for each
s\in S
,
s
is a Van Kampen diagram. We may also say that
\mathcal{C}
satisfies
descent
with respect to
S
.

Remark 11.23
.

Many kinds of categories that appear in applications are those that have Van Kampen
S
-colimits for various classes
S
. Such examples include lextensive categories, adhesive categories, exhaustive categories and exact categories. See
[
nLa23a
]
for more.

Now if both
colim
and
cst
are fully faithful, then the unit and counit will be isomorphisms and therefore they will form an adjoint equivalence.

Lemma 11.24
.

Asking that
colim
and
cst
are fully faithful is equivalent to the following two conditions holding:

(D1)

(Colimits are Universal) Given a map
Y\to\text{colim}_{i}X_{i}
, then

Y\cong\text{colim}_{i}\,\left(Y\times_{\text{colim}_{i}\,X_{i}}X_{i}\right),

and

(D2)

(Colimits are Effective) Given a Cartesian natural transformation with components
\alpha_{i}:Y_{i}\to X_{i}
, then

Y_{i}\cong\left(\text{colim}_{i}\,Y_{i}\right)\times_{\text{colim}_{i}\,X_{i}}X_{i}.

Example 11.25
.

In
\mathbf{Set}
, all colimits are universal. Similarly all colimits are universal in locally cartesian closed categories
\mathcal{C}
, since for every map
f:U\to V
the pullback functor
f^{*}:\mathcal{C}_{/V}\to\mathcal{C}_{/U}
has a right adjoint and therefore preserves colimits. In other words, all locally cartesian closed categories satisfy
(D1)
.

However
\mathbf{Set}
does
not
satisfy (D2), i.e. not all of its colimits are effective!

Example 11.26

(
[
Rez10
, Example 2.3]
)
.

Let
I
be the category
\{a\leftarrow b\to c\}
, and
\mathcal{C}=\mathbf{Set}
. Let
X=\{x,x^{\prime}\}
, and fix
\sigma:X\to X
given by
\sigma(x)=x^{\prime}
and
\sigma(x^{\prime})=x
. Let the functor
A:I\to\mathbf{Set}
be given by

X\xleftarrow{(1_{X},1_{X})}X+X\xrightarrow{(1_{X},\sigma)}X.

We let
X+X=\{(x,0),(x^{\prime},0),(x,1),(x^{\prime},1)\}
.
Similarly define the functor
B:I\to\mathbf{Set}
given by

*\leftarrow*+*\to*

where
*
is the singleton set. There is a cartesian natural transformation
\alpha:A\Rightarrow B
given by:

(45)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{X+X}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{*}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{*+*}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{*}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(1_{X},1_{X})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(1_{X},\sigma)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lrcorner}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lrcorner}

Indeed, given maps
q_{0}:Q\to X
and
q_{1}:Q\to*+*
, the composite

Q\xrightarrow{q_{0}}X\xhookrightarrow{i_{\ell}}X+X

where
i_{\ell}
is the inclusion into the left component, gives a unique map making the obvious diagram commute, and hence both squares are pullbacks.
The natural transformation is an object
\alpha:A\Rightarrow B
in
\mathbf{Cart}(\mathcal{C}^{I})_{/B}
. But now consider taking the pushouts of

and

\text{colim}\,B=*
. Let us compute the pushout of

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{X+X}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X}

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{*}}
