\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(1_{X},\sigma)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nabla}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces q^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces q}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lrcorner}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces h}

then

q\nabla(x,0)=q(x)=q^{\prime}(x)=q^{\prime}(1_{X},\sigma)(x,0),\qquad\text{and},\qquad q\nabla(x,1)=q(x)=q^{\prime}(x^{\prime})=q^{\prime}(1_{X},\sigma)(x,1),

so
q^{\prime}
is constant, and therefore
q
is constant since
q\nabla(x^{\prime},0)=q(x^{\prime})=q^{\prime}(x^{\prime})=q^{\prime}(1_{X},\sigma)(x^{\prime},0)
.
But now notice that the square

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A(a)=X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\text{colim}A\cong*}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{B(a)=*}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\text{colim}B\cong*}}

is not a pullback. In other words, we have a commutative cube

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{X+X}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{*}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{*}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{*+*}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{*}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{*}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lrcorner}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lrcorner}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lrcorner}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lrcorner}

where the two back faces are pullbacks, and the top and bottom faces are pushouts, but the two front faces are not pullbacks
35
35

35

This is often the way which effective pushouts are described in the literature, especially for adhesive categories
[
LS04
, Definition 2]
.
This implies that not all pushouts in
\mathbf{Set}
, and hence not all colimits, are effective.

Remark 11.27
.

In fact, the precise class of pushouts in
\mathbf{Set}
that are effective, i.e. Van Kampen, (which contains the class of pushouts along monomorphisms) has been classified in
[
Löw10
]
.

Remark 11.28
.

The (very vague) idea here is the following: colimits are universal if given a map
Y\to\text{colim}_{i}X_{i}
, then we can “break apart”
Y
into pieces
Y\times_{\text{colim}_{i}X_{i}}X_{i}
and glue them back together to get
Y
.

Colimits are effective if given maps
Y_{i}\to X_{i}
, we can “glue the pieces together”
\text{colim}_{i}Y_{i}\to\text{colim}_{i}X_{i}
, and then break them apart again to get the individual pieces.

In
\mathbf{Set}
, colimits aren’t effective because when we glue together sets, we lose information about the original sets that we cannot retrieve.

However, as one would hope given the Giraud axiom (G3), there are certain colimits that are effective in
\mathbf{Set}
, namely coequalizers by equivalence relations. Let
I=(a\rightrightarrows b)
, and let
A,B:I\to\mathbf{Set}
be defined as the following coequalizers, with their respective coequalizers shown,

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces R}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{Y/R,}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces S}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{X/S}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p_{0}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces h}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces q_{0}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces q_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces k}

and where we assume that
R
and
S
are equivalence relations. Suppose we have a cartesian natural transformation
\alpha:A\Rightarrow B
, i.e. a diagram of the form

(46)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces R}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces S}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\alpha}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p_{0}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lrcorner}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\alpha^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lrcorner}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\alpha}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces q_{0}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces q_{1}}

We wish to show that the resulting commutative diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{Y/R}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{X/S}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces h}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\alpha}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\alpha^{\prime\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces k}

is a pullback. Now the two squares commuting in (
46
) implies that

is a map such that if
yRy^{\prime}
then
\alpha(y)S\alpha(y^{\prime})
. The squares being pullbacks imply that
yRy^{\prime}
if and only if
\alpha(y)S\alpha(y^{\prime})
. Furthermore, it implies that there are bijections

\alpha^{-1}(x)\cong(\alpha^{\prime})^{-1}(x,x^{\prime})\cong\alpha^{-1}(x^{\prime})

of the fibers
36
36

36

This is an important property of pullbacks. They induce isomorphisms between the fibers of the two vertical (horizontal) maps.
for every
(x,x^{\prime})\in S
. So if
y\in Y
and
\alpha(y)Sx
, then there exists a unique
y^{\prime}\in Y
such that
\alpha(y^{\prime})=x
.

Now to show that (
11.3
) is a pullback, it is enough to show that the map

Y\to\left\{(x,[y]_{R})\,:\,\alpha^{\prime\prime}([y]_{R})=[\alpha(y)]_{S}=[x]_{S}\right\},\qquad\left(y\mapsto(\alpha(y),[y]_{R})\right)

is a bijection. Let us show it is injective. Suppose that
\alpha(y)=\alpha(y^{\prime})
and
[y]_{R}=[y^{\prime}]_{R}
. We want to show that
y=y^{\prime}
. Let
\alpha(y^{\prime})=x
. We have
\alpha(y)Sx
, so there exists a unique
y^{\prime\prime}\in Y
such that
\alpha(y^{\prime\prime})=x
. Thus
y=y^{\prime}=y^{\prime\prime}
. Now let us show that it is surjective. If
x\in X
and
y\in Y
such that

such that

(x,[y]_{R})=(\alpha(y^{\prime}),[y^{\prime}]_{R})

Proposition 11.29
.

The category
\mathbf{Set}
satisfies (G3). In other words
\mathbf{Set}
has effective equivalence relations.

In fact, there is a full characterization of effective colimits in
\mathbf{Set}
.

Proposition 11.30

(
[
Hoy
]
)
.

A colimit of a diagram
d:I\to\mathbf{Set}
is effective if and only if it is Van Kampen if and only if its category of elements
\intop\nolimits d
has the property that each of its connected components is simply connected.

Notice that conditions
(D1)
and
(D2)
from Lemma
11.24
can be reformulated as follows. Consider the following conditions on a cocomplete lex category
\mathcal{C}
:

•

(D1a)
-(Universal coproducts): Given a collection of objects
\{Y_{i}\}_{i\in I}
, let
Y=\sumop\displaylimits_{i}Y_{i}
. Let
f:X\to Y
be a morphism, and let
X_{i}=Y_{i}\times_{Y}X
. Then the induced map
\sumop\displaylimits_{i}X_{i}\to X
is an isomorphism,

•

(D1b)
-(Universal pushouts): Given a span
Y_{0}\leftarrow Y_{1}\to Y_{2}
, let
Y=Y_{0}+_{Y_{1}}Y_{2}
. Let
f:X\to Y
be a morphism and let
X_{i}=Y_{i}\times_{Y}X
. Then the induced map
X_{0}+_{X_{1}}X_{2}\to X
is an isomorphism.

•

(D2a)
-(Effective coproducts): Given a collection of maps
\{f_{i}:X_{i}\to Y_{i}\}
, let
X=\sumop\displaylimits_{i}X_{i}
, and
Y=\sumop\displaylimits_{i}Y_{i}
, and let
f:X\to Y
be the coproduct
\sumop\displaylimits_{i}f_{i}
. Then the natural maps
X_{i}\to Y_{i}\times_{Y}X
are isomorphisms for each
i
.

•

(D2b)
-(Effective pushouts): Given a cartesian map of spans:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{X_{0}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{X_{1}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{X_{2}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{Y_{0}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{Y_{1}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{Y_{2}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f_{0}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f_{2}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lrcorner}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lrcorner}

let
X=X_{0}+_{X_{1}}X_{2}
and
Y=Y_{0}+_{Y_{1}}Y_{2}
, and let
f:X\to Y
denote the induced map of pushouts. Then the natural maps
X_{i}\to Y_{i}\times_{Y}X
are isomorphisms.

since all small colimits can be computed as coequalizers (and therefore from pushouts and an initial object) of small coproducts,
(D1)\Leftrightarrow(D1a)+(D1b)
and
(D2)\Leftrightarrow(D2a)+(D2b)
. It is often easier to work with these other conditions.

\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f_{0}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f_{2}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lrcorner}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lrcorner}

let
X=X_{0}+_{X_{1}}X_{2}
and
Y=Y_{0}+_{Y_{1}}Y_{2}
, and let
f:X\to Y
denote the induced map of pushouts. Then the natural maps
X_{i}\to Y_{i}\times_{Y}X
are effective

epimorphisms.

Definition 11.31
.

We will call denote the logical conjunction by
(D2)^{\prime}=(D2a)+(D2b)^{\prime}
. We let
(D)^{\prime}
be the statement that
\mathcal{E}
is locally presentable and satisfies
(D1)
and

. We also say that
\mathcal{E}
has

or

.

11.4.
Weak Descent implies Giraud’s Axioms

In this section we will show that weak descent implies Giraud’s axioms, i.e.
(D)^{\prime}\implies(G)
.

Proposition 11.32
.

Let
\mathcal{C}
be a cocomplete lex category. Then
\mathcal{C}
satisfies
(D1)
if and only if for all maps
f:T\to S
in
\mathcal{C}
, the pullback functor
f^{*}:\mathcal{C}_{/S}\to\mathcal{C}_{/T}
preserves colimits (
G1)
.

Proof.

(\Rightarrow)
Suppose that
x:I\to\mathcal{C}_{/S}
is a small diagram. Since we can compute colimits in
\mathcal{C}_{/S}
by looking at their projections, let
X\xrightarrow{\hat{x}}S=\text{colim}_{i}\,(X(i)\xrightarrow{x(i)}S)
be the colimit in
\mathcal{C}_{/S}
. This implies that
X\cong\text{colim}_{i}\,X(i)
in
\mathcal{C}
. Consider the pullback

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces T}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces S}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f^{*}(\hat{x})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lrcorner}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hat{x}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}

We want to show that
f^{*}(\hat{x})\cong\text{colim}_{i}f^{*}(x(i))
. Now by since
\mathcal{C}
satisfies
(D1)
, if we set
Y(i)=X(i)\times_{X}Y
, then
\text{colim}_{i}\,Y(i)\cong Y
in
\mathcal{C}
. Since colimits in
\mathcal{C}_{/T}
are computed by their projections in
\mathcal{C}
, the object
f^{*}(\hat{x}):Y\to T
in
\mathcal{C}_{/T}
is the colimit
\text{colim}_{i}f^{*}(x(i)):Y(i)\to T
. Thus
\text{colim}_{i}f^{*}(x(i))\cong f^{*}(\text{colim}_{i}x(i))\cong f^{*}(\hat{x})
. Thus
f^{*}
preserves colimits, so
\mathcal{C}
satisfies
(G1)
.

(\Leftarrow)
Suppose that
\mathcal{C}
satisfies
(G1)
. Then if
X=\text{colim}_{i}\,X(i)
,
g:Y\to X
is an arbitrary morphism and
Y(i)=X(i)\times_{X}Y
, then

\text{colim}_{i}\,Y(i)=\text{colim}_{i}\,\left(X(i)\times_{X}Y\right)\cong\left(\left(\text{colim}_{i}\,X(i)\right)\times_{X}Y\right)\cong\left(X\times_{X}Y\right)\cong Y.

Thus
\mathcal{C}
satisfies
(D1)
.
∎

Proposition 11.33
.

If a cocomplete lex category
\mathcal{C}
satisfies
(D2a)
, i.e. has effective coproducts, then it has disjoint coproducts
(G2)
.

Proof.

Let
I
be a small, discrete category, and
\{X(i)\}_{i\in I}
a collection of objects in
\mathcal{C}
, then we wish to show that the following diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varnothing}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{X(k)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{X(j)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\sumop\displaylimits_{i\in I}X(i)}}

is a pullback square whenever
j\neq k
.

First, for every
j\in I
, define a functor
\delta^{j}:I\to\mathcal{C}
by

\delta^{j}(i)=\begin{cases}\varnothing,&\text{ if }i\neq j,\\
X_{j},&\text{ if }i=j.\end{cases}

There is an obvious natural transformation
\delta^{j}\Rightarrow X
given by inclusion or the identity. This natural transformation is cartesian vacuously. Thus by
(D2a)
the following diagram is a pullback:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\delta^{j}(i)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\sumop\displaylimits_{i\in I}\delta^{j}(i)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{X(i)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\sumop\displaylimits_{i\in I}X(i)}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lrcorner}

for every
i
and
j
. If
i\neq j
, then the above pullback diagram implies disjoint coproducts
(G2)
.
∎

Now let us prove the last implication
(D1)+(D2b)^{\prime}\Rightarrow(G3)
. First, let us prove a few technical results that we will need.

Lemma 11.34

(
[
Hen21
]
)
.

If
\mathcal{C}
is a cocomplete lex category satisfying
(D1)+(D2b)^{\prime}
, then it has weak descent with respect to coequalizers.

Proof.

This is morally true because
(D1)+(D2b)^{\prime}
is equivalent to
\mathcal{C}
having Van Kampen coproducts and having weak Van Kampen pushouts. Since coequalizers can be built from pushouts and coproducts, the result follows. Suppose we have a commutative diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{X_{0}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{X_{1}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{Y_{0}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{Y_{1}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p_{0}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\alpha_{0}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces h}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\alpha_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\alpha_{2}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces q_{0}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces q_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces k}

where the two squares

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{X_{1}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{X_{0}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{X_{1}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{Y_{1}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{Y_{0}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{Y_{1}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\alpha_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p_{0}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lrcorner}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\alpha_{0}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lrcorner}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\alpha_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces q_{0}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces q_{1}}

are pullbacks. We wish to prove that the induced map
\ell

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{X_{1}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{Y_{1}\times_{Y}X}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{Y_{1}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\ell}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces h}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\alpha_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\alpha_{2}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces k}

is an effective epimorphism.

First, let us note that
Y_{0}\rightrightarrows Y_{1}\to Y
being a coequalizer is equivalent to the commutative square

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{Y_{0}+Y_{1}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{Y_{1}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{Y_{1}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(q_{1},1_{Y_{1}})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(q_{0},1_{Y_{1}})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces k}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces k}

being a pushout, and similarly for
X
. Now we wish to show that the natural transformation
\alpha^{\prime}
given in components as

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{X_{1}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{X_{0}+X_{1}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{X_{1}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{Y_{1}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{Y_{0}+Y_{1}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{Y_{1}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\alpha_{1}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{(p_{0},1_{X_{1}})}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{(p_{1},1_{X_{1}})}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\alpha_{0}+\alpha_{1}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\alpha_{1}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{(q_{0},1_{Y_{1}})}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{(q_{1},1_{Y_{1}})}}

is cartesian, i.e. we wish to show that the two squares above are pullbacks. But since
\mathcal{C}
satisfies
(D1)
, and since
\alpha
is cartesian, we have

(Y_{0}+Y_{1})\times_{Y_{1}}X_{1}\cong(Y_{0}\times_{Y_{1}}X_{1})+(Y_{1}\times_{Y_{1}}X_{1})\cong X_{0}+X_{1}.

Thus the natural transformation
\alpha^{\prime}
is cartesian, and since
\mathcal{C}
has weak Van Kampen pushouts
(D2b)^{\prime}
, this implies that the resulting map
\ell
above is an effective epimorphism.
∎

Proposition 11.35

(
[
Hen21
]
)
.

If
\mathcal{C}
is a cocomplete lex category that satisfies
(D1)
and
(D2b)^{\prime}
, then it has effective equivalence relations
(G3)
.

Proof.

Let

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces R}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{X/R}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p_{0}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p}

be an equivalence relation on
X
. Consider the equalizer

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{R^{(2)}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{R\times R}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota=(\iota_{0},\iota_{1})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\pi_{1}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\pi_{2}}}

where
\pi_{1}
and
\pi_{2}
are the composite maps

R\times R\xrightarrow{\text{proj}_{0}}R\xrightarrow{p_{0}}X,\qquad R\times R\xrightarrow{\text{proj}_{1}}R\xrightarrow{p_{0}}X.

If
\mathcal{C}
were
\mathbf{Set}
, then

R^{(2)}\cong\{((x_{0},x_{1}),(y_{0},y_{1}))\in R^{2}\,:\,x_{0}=y_{0}\}\cong\{(x_{0},x_{1},x_{2})\in X^{3}\,:\,x_{0}Rx_{1},\text{ and }x_{1}Rx_{2}\}.

So
R^{(2)}
is a relation on
R
, and we want to show that it is an equivalence relation. Set-theoretically, this is easy to see.
R^{(2)}
is the relation
\sim
on
R
where
(x_{0},x_{1})\sim(y_{0},y_{1})
if
x_{0}=y_{0}
. The diagonal map
\Delta:R\to R\times R
induces a map
r:R\to R^{(2)}
, by the universal property of the equalizer, which is basically the diagonal map. Thus
R^{(2)}
is reflexive. The other properties follows similarly, so we will just show them set-theoretically, and trust the reader can translate them to diagrammatic proofs.
R^{(2)}
is symmetric, because if
(x_{0},x_{1})\sim(y_{0},y_{1})
, then
x_{0}=y_{0}
, and thus
(y_{0},y_{1})\sim(x_{0},x_{1})
. It is also transitive, because if
(x_{0},x_{1})\sim(y_{0},y_{1})
and
(y_{0},y_{1})\sim(z_{0},z_{1})
, then
x_{0}=y_{0}
and
y_{0}=z_{0}
. So
x_{0}=z_{0}
. Thus
R^{(2)}
is an equivalence relation on
R
.

Now we wish to show that the following commutative diagram

(47)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{R^{(2)}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces R}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota_{0}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p_{0}}

is a coequalizer. First, consider the map
s:X\to R
, such that
p_{0}s=p_{1}s=1_{X}
, which exists since
R
is reflexive. Then, since
\iota:R^{(2)}\to R\times R
is an equalizer, we obtain a map
s^{\prime}:R\to R^{(2)}
induced by the map
(1_{R},sp_{0}):R\to R\times R
. But note that the following relations hold

\iota_{0}s^{\prime}=1_{R},\qquad\iota_{1}s^{\prime}=sp_{0},\qquad rp_{0}=1_{X}.

Thus (
47
) is actually a split coequalizer diagram, and hence a coequalizer.

We have a cartesian natural transformation given as follows

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces R}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{R^{(2)}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces R}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces R}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota_{0}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lrcorner}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\ell}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lrcorner}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p_{0}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p_{1}}

where
\ell
is the map defined as follows. Let us define it set-theoretically first as
\ell(x_{0},x_{1},x_{2})=(x_{1},x_{2})
. To describe this diagrammatically, we consider the induced map

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{R^{(2)}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{R\times_{X}R}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces R}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces R}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces h}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces i\iota_{0}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lrcorner}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p_{0}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p_{1}}

Then by using the composition map
c:R\times_{X}R\to R
, we define
\ell=ch
.

Thus since
\mathcal{C}
satisfies
(D1)+(D2b)^{\prime}
, by Lemma
11.34
,
\mathcal{C}
satisfies weak descent with respect to coequalizers, hence the following induced map

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces R}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{X\times_{X/R}X}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{X/R}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces i}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p_{0}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lrcorner}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces q}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces q}

is an effective epimorphism. But since
X\times_{X/R}X
is a pullback, the following diagram is an equalizer

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{X\times_{X/R}X}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{X\times X}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{X/R}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces i^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces q\,\text{proj}_{0}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces q\,\text{proj}_{1}}

Furthermore, the following diagram commutes

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces R}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{X\times_{X/R}X}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{X\times X}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces i}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(p_{0},p_{1})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces i^{\prime}}

So therefore
i
is a monomorphism. But then
i
is a monomorphism and an effective epimorphism, and therefore an isomorphism. Thus
R
is an effective equivalence relation. Thus
\mathcal{C}
satisfies
(G3)
.
∎

Thus we have managed to prove that
(D)^{\prime}\Rightarrow(G)
.

Proposition 11.36
.

If
\mathcal{E}
is a locally presentable category and satisfies weak descent
(D)^{\prime}
, then it satisfies Giraud’s axioms
(G)
.

11.5.
Grothendieck Topoi Satisfy Weak Descent

In this section we prove that
(T)\Rightarrow(D)^{\prime}
. We will do this by first proving that
\mathbf{Set}
satisfies weak descent, which then immediately implies that presheaf topoi satisfy weak descent, and then using lex localization, show that this implies it for all Grothendieck topoi.

Lemma 11.37
.

\mathbf{Set}
satisfies
(D1)
, i.e. it has universal colimits.

Proof.

By Proposition
11.32
,
(D1)
is equivalent to
(G1)
, i.e. for every function
f:S\to T
, the functor
f^{*}:\mathbf{Set}_{/T}\to\mathbf{Set}_{/S}
preserves colimits. This is true of every cartesian closed category, hence
\mathbf{Set}
satisfies
(D1)
.
∎

Lemma 11.38
.

\mathbf{Set}
satisfies
(D2a)
, i.e. it has effective coproducts.

Proof.

Suppose we have a collection
\{\alpha_{i}:X_{i}\to Y_{i}\}
of morphisms in
\mathbf{Set}
, and let
X=\sumop\displaylimits_{i}X_{i}
and
Y=\sumop\displaylimits_{i}Y_{i}
. We want to show that the induced map

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{X_{i}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{Y_{i}\times_{Y}X}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{Y_{i}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces h}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\text{in}^{X}_{i}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\alpha_{i}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lrcorner}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\alpha}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\text{in}^{Y}_{i}}

is an isomorphism. But
Y_{i}\times_{Y}X\cong\{(y,x)\in Y_{i}\times X\,:\,\alpha(x)=y\}
, and of course
\alpha(x)=y
if and only if
x\in X_{i}
. Thus
h
is an isomorphism, with inverse given by
h^{-1}(y,x)=x
.
∎

Lemma 11.39
.

\mathbf{Set}
satisfies
(D2b)^{\prime}
, i.e. it has weak effective pushouts.

Proof.

In
\mathbf{Set}
, all epimorphisms are effective epimorphisms. So we need to show that if we have a cartesian natural transformation of pushouts

(48)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{X_{0}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{X_{1}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{X_{2}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{Y_{0}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{Y_{1}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{Y_{2}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\alpha_{0}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f_{0}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lrcorner}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\alpha_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lrcorner}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\alpha_{2}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g_{0}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g_{1}}

and if we let
X=X_{0}+_{X_{1}}X_{2}
and
Y=Y_{0}+_{Y_{1}}Y_{2}
, then the induced maps

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{X_{0}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{X_{2}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{Y_{0}\times_{Y}X}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{Y_{2}\times_{Y}X}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{Y_{0}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{Y_{2}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces h_{0}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sigma_{0}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\alpha_{0}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces h_{2}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sigma_{2}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\alpha_{2}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lrcorner}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\alpha}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lrcorner}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\alpha}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lambda_{0}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lambda_{2}}

are epimorphisms. Let us prove that
h_{0}
is an epimorphism, as showing
h_{2}
is an epimorphism follows similarly.

First, let us consider the set
Y=Y_{0}+_{Y_{1}}Y_{2}
. This is precisely the set
Y_{0}+Y_{1}/{\sim_{Y_{1}}}
, where
\sim_{Y_{1}}
is the smallest equivalence relation containing the relation
\sim_{Y_{1}}^{\prime}
, where for
y,y^{\prime}\in Y_{0}+Y_{2}
, we have
y\sim_{Y_{1}}^{\prime}y^{\prime}
if and only if there exists a
y^{\prime\prime}\in Y_{1}
such that
g_{0}(y^{\prime\prime})=y
and
g_{1}(y^{\prime\prime})=y^{\prime}
.

Note that
h_{0}
is the map
h_{0}(x_{0})=(\alpha_{0}(x_{0}),[x_{0}])
, which belongs to
Y_{0}\times_{Y}X\cong\{(y_{0},[x])\in Y_{0}\times(X_{0}+_{X_{1}}X_{2})\,:\,[y_{0}]=\alpha[x]\},
since
\alpha[x_{0}]=[\alpha_{0}(x_{0})]
. Now given
(y_{0},[x])\in Y_{0}\times_{Y}X
, we want to show that there exists a
x_{0}\in X_{0}
such that
h(x_{0})=(y_{0},[x])
. So choose some
x_{0}\in[x]
. This means that
x_{0}\in X_{0}+X_{2}
. Suppose that
x_{0}\in X_{0}
, for if
x_{0}\in X_{2}
, the argument is similar. Then
[y_{0}]=\alpha[x_{0}]
implies that
\alpha_{0}(x_{0})\sim_{Y_{1}}y_{0}
. Assume that
\alpha_{0}(x_{0})\neq y_{0}
, otherwise
h_{0}
is surjective. Then since
\alpha_{0}(x_{0})\sim_{Y_{1}}y_{0}
, and there exists a zig-zag of the form

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{Y_{1}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{Y_{1}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\dots}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{Y_{0}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{Y_{2}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{Y_{0}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{Y_{0}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g_{0}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g_{0}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g_{0}}

and elements
y^{1}_{0},\dots,y^{1}_{n}
such that
g_{0}(y^{1}_{0})=\alpha_{0}(x_{0})
,
g_{1}(y^{1}_{0})=g_{1}(y^{1}_{1})
,
g_{0}(y^{1}_{1})=g_{0}(y^{1}_{2})
,
\dots
,
g_{1}(y^{1}_{n-1})=g_{1}(y^{1}_{n})
and
g_{0}(y^{1}_{n})=y_{0}
. But if
g_{0}(y^{1}_{0})=\alpha_{0}(x_{0})
, then since the left-hand square in (
48
) is a pullback, there exists a
x_{1}\in X_{1}
such that
f_{0}(x_{1})=x_{0}
and
\alpha_{1}(x_{1})=y^{1}_{0}
. But then
\alpha_{2}(f_{1}(x_{1}))=g_{1}(\alpha_{1}(x_{1}))=g_{1}(y^{1}_{0})=g_{1}(y^{1}_{1})
. But since the right-hand square in (
48
) is a pullback, that means that there is a
x_{1}^{\prime}\in X_{1}
such that
\alpha_{1}(x^{\prime}_{1})=y^{1}_{1}
and
f_{1}(x^{\prime}_{1})=f_{1}(x_{1})
. So
g_{0}(y^{1}_{1})=g_{0}(\alpha_{1}(x^{\prime}_{1}))=\alpha_{0}(f_{0}(x^{\prime}_{1}))
. Furthermore
x_{0}\sim_{X_{1}}f_{0}(x^{\prime}_{1})
because
x_{0}=f_{0}(x_{1})
, and
f_{1}(x_{1})=f_{1}(x^{\prime}_{1})
. Continuing on in this way, we can show that there exists a
\widetilde{x}\in X_{0}
such that
y_{0}=\alpha_{0}(\widetilde{x})
and such that
\widetilde{x}\sim_{X_{1}}\alpha_{0}(x_{0})
. In other words, we have shown that
h_{0}
is surjective. Thus
\mathbf{Set}
has weak effective pushouts.
∎

We leave the next result to the reader, it follows straightforwardly from the fact that colimits and limits are computed objectwise in presheaf topoi.

Lemma 11.40
.

If
\mathbf{Pre}(\mathcal{C})
is a presheaf topos, then it satisfies weak descent.

Proposition 11.41
.

If
\mathcal{E}
is a Grothendieck topos, then
\mathcal{E}
satisfies weak descent, i.e.
(T)\Rightarrow(D)^{\prime}
.

Proof.

If
\mathcal{E}
is a Grothendieck topos, then by the Little Giraud Theorem
7.18
, there exists a site
(\mathcal{C},j)
and a lex localization

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathbf{Sh}(\mathcal{C},j)\simeq\mathcal{E}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathbf{Pre}(\mathcal{C})}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces a}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\dashv}

Let us show that
\mathbf{Sh}(\mathcal{C},j)
has universal colimits. Suppose we have a small diagram
X:I\to\mathbf{Sh}(\mathcal{C},j)
, and we abuse notation to let
X=\text{colim}_{i}X(i)
. Suppose there is a map
f:Y\to X
, and consider the pullback

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{Y(i)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{X(i)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sigma_{i}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f(i)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lrcorner}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lambda_{i}}

for every
i\in I
. We want to show that
\text{colim}_{i}Y(i)\cong Y
. Now
\iota
preserves the above pullback for every
i\in I
, and since presheaf topoi have universal colimits, we have
\text{colim}_{i}\iota Y(i)\cong\iota Y
. Then

\text{colim}_{i}Y(i)\cong\text{colim}_{i}a\iota Y(i)\cong a\left(\text{colim}_{i}\iota Y(i)\right)\cong a\iota Y\cong Y.

Thus
\mathbf{Sh}(\mathcal{C},j)\simeq\mathcal{E}
has universal colimits.

The proof for effective coproducts follows similarly.

Now let us show that
(T)\Rightarrow(D2b)^{\prime}
. So suppose we have a cartesian natural transformation of pushouts in
\mathbf{Sh}(\mathcal{C},j)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{X_{0}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{X_{1}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{X_{2}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{Y_{0}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{Y_{1}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{Y_{2}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\alpha_{0}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f_{0}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lrcorner}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\alpha_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lrcorner}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\alpha_{2}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g_{0}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g_{1}}

Then applying
\iota
to the diagrams

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{X_{0}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{X_{2}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{Y_{0}\times_{Y}X}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{Y_{2}\times_{Y}X}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{Y_{0}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{Y_{2}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces h_{0}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sigma_{0}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\alpha_{0}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces h_{2}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sigma_{2}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\alpha_{2}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lrcorner}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\alpha}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lrcorner}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\alpha}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lambda_{0}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lambda_{2}}

we see that
\iota(h_{0})
and
\iota(h_{2})
are effective epimorphisms, since
\mathbf{Pre}(\mathcal{C})
has weak effective pushouts. Since
\alpha
preserves finite limits and colimits, it preserves effective epimorphisms. Thus
\alpha\iota(h_{0})\cong h_{0}
and
\alpha\iota(h_{2})\cong h_{2}
are effective epimorphisms.
∎

Thus we have finally arrived at the major goal of this section.

Theorem 11.42

(Giraud’s Theorem)
.

Let
\mathcal{E}
be a locally presentable category, then the following are equivalent:

(1)

\mathcal{E}
satisfies weak descent
(D)^{\prime}
,

(2)

\mathcal{E}
satisfies Giraud’s axioms
(G)
, and

(3)

\mathcal{E}
is a Grothendieck topos
(T)
.

11.6.
Infinity Toposes

In this very brief section, we discuss the major difference between Grothendieck
1
-toposes and
\infty
-toposes.

Remark 11.43
.

By a Grothendieck
1
-topos, we mean what we have been calling a Grothendieck topos this whole time. For this section, we will reference
\infty
-categories, but will not define them, or motivate them. For that we recommend
[
Rez22
]
,
[
Lan21
]
,
[
Rie20
]
.

In the landmark book
[
Lur09
]
, Lurie defines
\infty
-topoi as follows: an
\infty
-category
\mathcal{E}
is an
\infty
-topos if there is a small
\infty
-category
\mathcal{C}
and an accessible
38
38

38

i.e. the right adjoint preserves filtered
\infty
-colimits
lex localization (in the
\infty
-category sense)
\mathbf{Pre}^{\infty}(\mathcal{C})\to\mathcal{E}
, where
\mathbf{Pre}^{\infty}(\mathcal{C})=\mathcal{S}^{\mathcal{C}^{\text{op}}}
is the
\infty
-category of
\infty
-functors from
\mathcal{C}^{\text{op}}
to the
\infty
-category
\mathcal{S}
of spaces (up-to-homotopy equivalence).

When Lurie writes “
\infty
-topos,” he means Grothendieck
\infty
-topos. So he takes the Little Giraud Theorem
7.18
as the starting point for his definition. He then proves the following theorem
39
39

39

building off of very similar work from
[
Rez10
]
and
[
TV05
]
in the context of model categories and model topoi

Theorem 11.44

(
[
Lur09
, Theorem 6.1.0.6]
)
.

If
\mathcal{E}
is an
\infty
-category, then the following statements are equivalent:

•

\mathcal{E}
is an
\infty
-topos,

•

\mathcal{E}
satisfies the
\infty
-categorical analogues of Giraud’s axioms:

(1)

\mathcal{E}
is a (locally) presentable
\infty
-category,

(2)

\mathcal{E}
has universal
\infty
-colimits,

(3)

\mathcal{E}
has disjoint
\infty
-coproducts, and

(4)

\mathcal{E}
has effective groupoid objects.

Now the
\infty
-categorical analogues of Giraud’s axioms are straightforward except for (4). The idea being that in the
\infty
-category context, when one “quotients,” one needs to “remember how one quotiented.” In other words, for sets if we glue together a bunch of points, we have lost the information of
how
the points were identified in the first place. Since equivalence relations are precisely internal groupoids with no automorphisms, suitably interpreted
[
Min21
]
, in the
\infty
-category context, internal groupoids are understand as certain kinds of simplicial objects. So in the
\infty
-category context, quotienting is a much more sophisticated operation, and hence effective equivalence relations become effective groupoid objects.

Now we can restate the conditions for descent, i.e. Van Kampen colimits, and here, using
\infty
-categories actually makes everything easier to understand. We say that an
\infty
-category
\mathcal{E}
satisfies descent if for every
\infty
-diagram the canonical
\infty
-adjunction

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{E}_{/\text{colim}_{i}X(i)}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\lim_{i}\mathcal{E}_{/X(i)}}}
cst
colim
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\dashv}

is an equivalence of
\infty
-categories, where now the (co)limits shown above are
\infty
-(co)limits in
\mathcal{E}
or in the (very large)
\infty
-category
\mathbf{Cat}^{\infty}
of large infinity categories.

Theorem 11.45
.

An
\infty
-category
\mathcal{E}
is an
\infty
-topos if and only if it is presentable and satisfies descent.

Notice the difference here with Grothendieck
1
-toposes. The analogue of the above adjunction is not an equivalence in that case. Only weak descent holds.

Appendix A
Category Theory Background

In this section, we provide basic material from category theory that will be important for these notes. We try to be detailed here without being overly pedantic. Much of this material is well-known, but we feel that it could be useful to the reader to gather all these results in one place.

A.1.
Set Theory

Set theory is a subtle and oftentimes difficult subject for mathematicians to wrap their heads around. Much of these notes will not need to be concerned with the subtleties of set theory, however there are several areas where a concrete understanding of certain notions of set theory is absolutely vital, namely the theory of localizations (Section
B
), functor categories (Section
A.1.2
), large sites (Section
8.4
) and locally presentable categories (Section
C
).

In this section we include a bare-bones account of those concepts of set theory that we will need in what follows. We fix here the foundations of set theory using the axiomatic approach
[
Wel20
]
,
[
Kun14
]
. We do not define what a set is, rather we use a formal system to manipulate symbols in a way that satisfies the Zermelo-Frankel axioms with choice (ZFC).

Let us say a few informal words on this. We build set theory using first order logic, which is a system for manipulating symbols. We introduce variable symbols
x_{1},x_{2},\dots
, logical symbols
\vee,\wedge,\to,\leftrightarrow,\neg,\exists,\forall
40
40

40

Typically we take a subcollection of such symbols and define the rest of them in terms of this subcollection, for example we can define
\forall=\neg\exists\neg
.
, and two relation symbols
=,\in
. With these we can construct formulae, such as
\exists x_{1}.\forall x_{2}.((x_{1}=x_{2})\vee(x_{1}\in x_{2}))
. Formulae have a notion of free variables, and those without free variables are called sentences. From such formulae we can construct what we’ll call
collections
, but are more often called classes
41
41

41

For our purposes, we want to fix what it means to be a set, then use Grothendieck universes to distinguish between small and large sets, and we’d rather reserve the terminology for class for large sets.
, expressions of the form
\{x_{1}|\phi\}
, but these are also merely abbreviations, for example, the formula
x_{2}\in\{x_{1}|\phi\}
is just the formula
\phi(x_{2}/x_{1})
, where we have replaced all occurences of
x_{1}
in
\phi
with
x_{2}
. We can use the abbreviation
\varnothing=\{x\,|\,\neg(x=x)\}
and
V=\{x\,|\,x=x\}
, which are meant to be the empty set and the collection of all sets, respectively. We state again that these are purely syntactic constructs, i.e. they are just symbols. From here we use a formal deduction system for first order logic, see
[
Joh87
, Chapter 3]
, which provides us with axioms and inference rules like modus ponens, and this allows us to make formal deductions of sentences from collections of sentences. Logicians typically write
S\vdash\phi
to show that there exists a formal deduction of the sentence
\phi
from the collection
S
of sentences. We then add the axioms of ZFC to this deductive system, which say things like
\forall x_{1}.\forall x_{2}.(\forall x_{3}.(x_{3}\in x_{1}\leftrightarrow x_{2}\in x_{1})\to(x_{1}=x_{2}))
, which is called the axiom of extensionality.

This kind of description is overly pedantic for our purposes. The reader must merely accept that there is a completely formal way of manipulating collections
\{x|\phi\}
, and that a collection is called a
set
if we can prove, using our deductive system, that
\{x|\phi\}\in V
. A collection that is not a set is called a
proper collection
.

We refer to
[
Kun14
, Chapter 1]
for a more in-depth and readable account of the foundations of set theory and the ZFC axioms. The notes
[
Shu08
]
provides a readable account for other choices for foundations of set theory. The ZFC axioms guarantee that there exists an empty set
\varnothing
, that we have at our disposal the usual operations of set theory like union
\cup
, power set
P
, cartesian product
\times
, and intersection
\cap
. Note that from the axioms of ZFC, one can prove that the collection
V
of all sets is not a set, and hence is a proper collection. We also work in classical mathematics, namely the law of excluded middle holds.

A.1.1.
Cardinals and Set Theoretic Considerations

In what follows, we assume that we have fixed the foundations of set theory using ZFC as in the discussion above and know what a set is. This implies that all elements of a set are themselves sets.

Definition A.1
.

A
total order
on a set
S
is a binary relation
\leq\,\subseteq S\times S
satisfying the following conditions:

•

(
reflexivity
) for all
x\in S
,
x\leq x
,

•

(
transitivity
) for all
x,y,z\in S
, if
x\leq y
and
y\leq z
, then
x\leq z
,

•

(
antisymmetry
) for all
x,y\in S
, if
x\leq y
and
y\leq z
, then
x=y
,

•

(
totality
) for all
x,y\in S
, if
x,y\in S
, then either
x\leq y
or
y\leq x
.

We say that
S
is a
preorder
if
\leq
satisfies reflexivity and transitivity, and a
partial order
or
poset
if it is a preorder that also satisfies antisymmetry.

Definition A.2
.

A
linear order
on a set
S
is a binary relation
<\,\subseteq S\times S
satisfying the following conditions:

•

(
irreflexivity
) for all
x\in S
,
x\nless x
,

•

(
trichotomy
) for all
x,y\in S
, exactly one of the following holds:
x<y
,
y<x
or
x=y
.

There is a bijection between linear orders on a set
S
and total orders. Indeed, if
\leq
is a total order on
S
, then we can define a linear order
<
on
S
by declaring
x<y
if and only if
y\nleq x
, and conversely
x\leq y
if and only if
y\nless x
.

Example A.3
.

Every set
S
has a linear order given by the membership relation
\in\,\subseteq S\times S
.

Definition A.4
.

A
well-order
on a set
S
is a linear order
<
with the property that every nonempty subset
T\subseteq S
has a
least element
, namely an element
t\in T
such that there exists no
x\in T
such that
x<t
.

Remark A.5
.

A well-ordered set could equivalently be defined as a totally-ordered set such that every nonempty subset has a least element.

Definition A.6
.

We say that a set
S
is
transitive
if whenever
y\in S
and
x\in y
, then
x\in S
.

Definition A.7
.

An
ordinal
is a transitive set
S
such that the membership relation
\in\,\subseteq S\times S
is a well order.

The collection of ordinal numbers can be obtained by
transfinite recursion
. Namely

•

the empty set
\varnothing
is an ordinal,

•

if
\alpha
is an ordinal, then the
successor ordinal
,
\alpha+1
, defined by
\alpha+1=\alpha\cup\{\alpha\}
, is an ordinal, and

•

given a set
I
of ordinals, the set
\bigcupop\displaylimits_{\alpha\in I}\alpha
is an ordinal.

We say that an ordinal
\alpha
is a successor ordinal if it is of the form
\alpha=\beta+1
for some ordinal
\beta
. We say that
\alpha
is a
limit ordinal
if it is nonempty and not a successor ordinal.

We use Von Neumann’s convention of labelling

0=\varnothing,\qquad 1=\{0\},\qquad 2=\{0,1\},\qquad 3=\{0,1,2\},\dots

Clearly for
n>0
,
n
is a successor ordinal. We let
\omega=\mathbb{N}=\{0,1,2,\dots\}
denote the first limit ordinal.

We say that two sets
A
and
B
have the same
cardinality
if there exists a bijection between them. Notice that there are many infinite ordinals with the same cardinality

\omega,\;\omega+1,\;\omega+2,\;\dots,\;2\omega=\bigcupop\displaylimits_{n\in\omega}\omega+n.

If
\alpha,\beta
are ordinals, we say that
\alpha<\beta
if
\alpha\in\beta
.

Definition A.8
.

A
cardinal number
is an ordinal number
\kappa
with the property that it is not in bijection with any ordinal number
\lambda
such that
\lambda<\kappa
.

All finite ordinal numbers are cardinal numbers, and the first infinite cardinal number is
\omega
. Since the collection of cardinal numbers is also well-ordered, we label them as
\aleph_{\alpha}
for an ordinal number
\alpha
. Thus we write
\aleph_{0}=\omega
for the first infinite cardinal number. If
\kappa
is a cardinal number, then we write
\kappa^{+}
to mean the smallest cardinal number that is greater than
\kappa
, and we call it the
successor cardinal
. Note that this is very different from the notion of successor ordinal. Indeed,
\omega^{+}\neq\omega+1
. However it is a tautology that
\aleph_{\alpha+1}=\aleph_{\alpha}^{+}
.

The question of the truth of the statement
\omega^{+}=2^{\omega}
, where
2^{\omega}
denotes the powerset of
\omega
, is precisely the Continuum Hypothesis. The question of the truth of the statement of
\aleph_{\alpha}^{+}=2^{\aleph_{\alpha}}
is called the Generalized Continuum Hypothesis. Note that
\mathbb{R}
is in bijection with
2^{\aleph_{0}}
.

The following result goes under the name of the
Well-Ordering Theorem
, it was proven by Zermelo in 1904, and can be found in any introductory text on set theory.

Theorem A.9
.

In ZFC, every set can be well-ordered.

It can be shown by transfinite induction that every well-ordered set is order-isomorphic to a unique ordinal number.

Corollary A.10
.

Every set is in bijection with a unique cardinal number.

Remark A.11
.

Let
S
be a set, and
\kappa
a cardinal. We will write
|S|<\kappa
to mean that if
\lambda
is the unique cardinal number that is in bijection with
S
, then
\lambda<\kappa
.

Definition A.12
.

An infinite cardinal
\kappa
is said to be
regular
if the following condition holds: If
I
is a set such that
|I|<\kappa
and
\{S_{i}\}_{i\in I}
is an
I
-indexed family of sets such that
|S_{i}|<\kappa
for all
i\in I
, then for
S=\bigcupop\displaylimits_{i\in I}S_{i}
, it follows that
|S|<\kappa
. We say a cardinal
\kappa
is
singular
if it is not regular. In other words, a cardinal
\kappa
is singular if it can be written as
\kappa=\sumop\displaylimits_{i<\alpha}\kappa_{i}
where
\kappa_{i}<\kappa
and
\alpha<\kappa
.

Remark A.13
.

Note that
\omega=\aleph_{0}
is a regular cardinal, as a union of finitely many finite sets is always finite. Every successor cardinal
\aleph_{0},\,\aleph_{1},\,\dots
is regular. The cardinal
\aleph_{\omega}=\sumop\displaylimits_{i<\omega}\aleph_{i}
however is singular.

Regular cardinals are well-behaved in the following sense. Let
\mathbf{Set}_{\kappa}
denote the category of all sets with cardinality strictly less than
\kappa
. Then this category is closed under unions indexed by sets in
\mathbf{Set}_{\kappa}
. This is not true for singular cardinals. In other words, if
\kappa
is singular, then
\mathbf{Set}_{\kappa}
is not
\kappa
-cocomplete (Definition
A.32
)! Hence for purposes of category theory, it is best that we stick to regular cardinals.

Definition A.14
.

A cardinal number
\kappa
is
strongly inaccessible
if it is uncountable, regular and for every
\lambda<\kappa
it follows that
2^{\lambda}<\kappa
.

The name strongly inaccessible cardinal is justified by the above definition, as it implies that if
\kappa
is strongly inaccessible, then it cannot be proved to exist using the ZFC axioms
[
Wel20
, Section 2.6]
.

Definition A.15
.

A
Grothendieck universe
is a set
\mathbb{U}
with the following properties:

(1)

it is a transitive set (Definition
A.6
),

(2)

if
x\in\mathbb{U}
and
y\in\mathbb{U}
, then
\{x,y\}\in\mathbb{U}
,

(3)

if
x\in\mathbb{U}
, then its powerset,
P(x)\in\mathbb{U}
,

(4)

if
x\in\mathbb{U}
, and
f:x\to\mathbb{U}
is a function, then
\bigcupop\displaylimits_{i\in x}f(i)\in\mathbb{U}
, and

(5)

the first limit ordinal
\omega\in\mathbb{U}
.

We take the following statement as an axiom. It was first postulated by Grothendieck and Verdier.

(49)

For every set
x
, there exists a Grothendieck universe
\mathbb{U}
such that
x\in\mathbb{U}
.

This is often called the
universe axiom
. The use of Grothendieck universes is pervasive in modern mathematics, as it is one of the quickest ways to couch one’s mathematical discussions in rigor without having to worry much about set-theoretic details. It is convenient to note that Grothendieck universes are equivalent to strongly inaccessible cardinals in the following sense.

Definition A.16
.

Define a set
V_{\alpha}
for every ordinal number
\alpha
by transfinite induction as follows.

(50)

\displaystyle V_{0}

\displaystyle=\varnothing

\displaystyle V_{\alpha+1}

\displaystyle=2^{V_{\alpha}}

\displaystyle V_{\beta}

\displaystyle=\bigcupop\displaylimits_{\alpha<\beta}V_{\alpha},\qquad\qquad(\beta\text{ a limit ordinal}).

The collection of sets
V_{\alpha}
is referred to as the
cumulative hierarchy
or
Von Neumann universe
. One of the axioms of ZFC, the axiom of foundation, is equivalent to the statement that every set is an element of some
V_{\alpha}
.

The
rank
of a set
S
is the smallest
\alpha
for which
S\in V_{\alpha+1}
. It can be proved by transfinite induction that the rank of an ordinal
\alpha
is
\alpha
. Thus
V_{\kappa}
is the set of all sets with rank
<\kappa
.

Theorem A.17

(
[
Wil69
]
)
.

If
\mathbb{U}
is a Grothendieck universe, then it is of the form
\mathbb{U}=V_{\kappa}
for
\kappa
a strongly inaccessible cardinal number.

Thus the universe axiom is equivalent to the statement: for every cardinal
\lambda
there exists a strongly inaccessible cardinal
\kappa
with
\lambda<\kappa
.

Remark A.18
.

It can be shown that for
\kappa
inaccessible,
V_{\kappa}
is a model of ZFC. Note that this means that all sets that are elements of
V_{\kappa}
have bounded rank, and not cardinality. This is an important distinction, see
[
Shu08
, Section 8]
for more information.

Definition A.19
.

Given a Grothendieck universe
\mathbb{U}
, let
\kappa(\mathbb{U})
denote the set of ordinal numbers that are elements of
\mathbb{U}
.

Lemma A.20

(
[
Low15
, Proposition 0.1.16]
)
.

If
\mathbb{U}
is a Grothendieck universe, then
\kappa(\mathbb{U})
is an ordinal number such that
\kappa(\mathbb{U})\notin\mathbb{U}
. Further, if
\mathbb{U}=V_{\kappa}
for a strongly inaccessible cardinal
\kappa
, then
\kappa(\mathbb{U})=\kappa
.

Definition A.21
.

Fix two Grothendieck universes
\mathbb{U}
and
\mathbb{V}
such that
\mathbb{U}\in\mathbb{V}
. We say that a set
x
is
small
if it is in bijection with a set
y
such that
y\in\mathbb{U}
,
large
if it is in bijection with a set
y^{\prime}
such that
y^{\prime}\in\mathbb{V}
, and
very large
otherwise. We will sometimes refer to a large set as a
class
.

Remark A.22
.

Some authors prefer to use a single Grothendieck universe
\mathbb{U}
, and say that elements of
\mathbb{U}
are small and
subsets
of
\mathbb{U}
are large. The following lemma shows that such large sets are still large in our context with multiple Grothendieck universes.

Lemma A.23
.

If
x\subseteq\mathbb{U}
, then
x\in\mathbb{V}
.

Proof.

First note that
x\in P(\mathbb{U})
since
x\subseteq\mathbb{U}
. But since
\mathbb{U}\in\mathbb{V}
, and
\mathbb{V}
is a Grothendieck universe, this implies that
P(\mathbb{U})\in\mathbb{V}
by Definition
A.15
.(3). Therefore
x\in\mathbb{V}
by Definition
A.15
.(1).
∎

A.1.2.
Small and Large Categories

Now with the convenient framework of Grothendieck universes, we can now turn to set theory used in category theory. We fix two nested Grothendieck universes
\mathbb{U}\in\mathbb{V}
. Any set mentioned below without disambiguation will always mean a small set, as will any cardinal.

Definition A.24
.

A
small category

\mathcal{C}
consists of a small set
\text{Obj}\,(\mathcal{C})
of objects and a small set
\text{Mor}\,(\mathcal{C})
of morphisms, equipped with the usual structure defining a category. A
large category
consists of a large set of objects and a large set of morphisms. When we say a category in these notes, we mean a large category, and use both terms interchangeably. A
very large category
consists of very large sets of objects and morphisms. A
locally small category
is a large category
\mathcal{C}
such that for every pair of objects
x,y\in\mathcal{C}
, the set
\mathcal{C}(x,y)
of morphisms from
x
to
y
is small. We say a category
\mathcal{C}
is
essentially small
if it is equivalent to a small category. In what follows we will treat essentially small categories as if they were small.

Definition A.25
.

Given a regular cardinal
\kappa
, we say a set
S
is
\kappa
-small if
|S|<\kappa
. If
\kappa
is a regular cardinal, we say that a category
\mathcal{C}
is
\kappa
-small if its set of objects and each hom-set is
\kappa
-small. Note that
\kappa
being regular implies that
\text{Mor}\,(\mathcal{C})
is
\kappa
-small, which provides another advantage to working with regular cardinals.

Example A.26
.

Let
\mathbf{Set}
denote the category of small sets. This is a large category that is locally small, as the set of functions between two small sets
A
and
B
is small since it can be encoded as a certain subset of
A\times B
. However
\mathbf{Set}
is not essentially small. Indeed, suppose it was, then there would be a small set
S\in\mathbb{U}
such that every set in
\mathbb{U}
is in bijection with a set in
S
. This would imply that
\mathbb{U}\in\mathbb{U}
, which is a contradiction.

Similarly, the category
\mathbf{Cat}
of small categories is locally small. However, the category
\mathbf{CAT}
of large categories is a very large category whose hom-sets are large sets.

Example A.27
.

The category
\mathbf{Man}
whose objects are finite dimensional smooth manifolds and whose morphisms are smooth maps is a locally small category, but it is also essentially small. Indeed, every smooth manifold can be embedded into
\mathbb{R}^{\infty}
by the Whitney Embedding Theorem. Thus we can consider the category whose objects are finite dimensional smooth manifolds embedded in
\mathbb{R}^{\infty}
, and this will be a small category equivalent to
\mathbf{Man}
. Similarly the category
\mathbf{Cart}
whose objects are finite dimensional smooth manifolds diffeomorphic to
\mathbb{R}^{n}
for some
n\geq 0
and whose morphisms are smooth maps, is a large category that is essentially small. In fact it is equivalent to the category whose objects are
\mathbb{R}^{n}
for
n\geq 0
with smooth maps between them.

While the above example is convenient, and the proof straightforward, it does not generalize well to other kinds of manifolds. Let us remedy that.

Example A.28
.

Let
\mathbf{TopMan}
denote the category whose objects are finite dimensional topological manifolds and whose morphisms are continuous functions. Let us show that
\mathbf{TopMan}
is essentially small.

For us a topological manifold
M
is a topological space that is Hausdorff, paracompact and locally Euclidean. Topological manifolds can only have countably many connected components, and each component is path connected.

By
[
S11
]
, every connected topological manifold has cardinality at most
\mbox{c}=2^{\aleph_{0}}\cong|\mathbb{R}|
. Hence by
[
Kar17
]
, assuming the axiom of choice, the cardinality of
M
is also at most
\mbox{c}=2^{\aleph_{0}}
.

For each set
X
of cardinality
c
, there are at most
2^{\mbox{c}}
topologies on
X
. Hence there can be at most
2^{\mbox{c}}
many non-isomorphic topological manifolds. Hence
\mathbf{TopMan}
is essentially small, by using the axiom of choice to choose one topological manifold from each isomorphism class.

Now a finite dimensional smooth manifold
M
is a topological manifold equipped with a smooth structure. We want to show that there is a set of isomorphism classes of smooth manifolds. A smooth atlas
\mathcal{A}
for
M
consists of a set of topological embeddings
\{\varphi_{i}:\mathbb{R}^{n}\to M\}_{i\in I}
such that the transition functions
\varphi_{j}^{-1}\varphi_{i}:\mathbb{R}^{n}\to\mathbb{R}^{n}
are smooth. We say that
\varphi_{i}
and
\varphi_{j}
are compatible. Two atlases
\mathcal{A},\mathcal{A}^{\prime}
are deemed equivalent if every pair
\varphi\in\mathcal{A}
and
\psi\in\mathcal{A}^{\prime}
are compatible. A smooth structure on
M
is an equivalence class of atlases. Using the fact that
M
is second countable, every atlas is equivalent to a countable atlas. Hence the isomorphism type of a smooth manifold is determined by an underlying topological manifold and a countable atlas. There are precisely
\mbox{c}^{\mbox{c}}
functions from
\mathbb{R}^{n}
to
M
. Hence there are at most
\sumop\displaylimits_{\omega}\mbox{c}^{\mbox{c}}
distinct smooth atlases on a topological manifold and hence there are at most
\sumop\displaylimits_{\omega}\mbox{c}^{\mbox{c}}\cdot\mbox{c}=\sumop\displaylimits_{\omega}\mbox{c}^{\mbox{c}}
distinct isomorphism classes of finite dimensional smooth manifolds, which is the cardinality of a small set. Hence the category
\mathbf{Man}
is essentially small.
42
42

42

We thank Kevin Carlson and John Baez for discussion around this example.

Example A.29
.

We will see in Section
B
that the localization of a locally small category
\mathcal{C}
at a class of morphisms
W
can lead to a large category
\mathcal{D}
that is not locally small. Finding ways to ensure that
\mathcal{D}
is locally small is a huge motivation for studying set-theoretical techniques in category theory.

Lemma A.30
.

Let
\mathcal{C}
and
\mathcal{D}
be categories, if

(1)

\mathcal{C}
and
\mathcal{D}
are small, then the category
\text{Fun}(\mathcal{C},\mathcal{D})
whose objects are functors and whose morphisms are natural transformations, is a small category,

(2)

\mathcal{C}
is small and
\mathcal{D}
is locally small, then
\text{Fun}(\mathcal{C},\mathcal{D})
is locally small,

(3)

\mathcal{C}
and
\mathcal{D}
are large categories, then
\text{Fun}(\mathcal{C},\mathcal{D})
is a large category,

(4)

\mathcal{C}
and
\text{Fun}(\mathcal{C},\mathbf{Set})
are locally small, then
\mathcal{C}
is essentially small.

Proof.

(1) The collection of functors
F:\mathcal{C}\to\mathcal{D}
can be encoded as a relation on
\text{Obj}\,(\mathcal{C})\times\text{Obj}\,(\mathcal{D})
, which is a small set. Similarly the collection of natural transformations can be encoded as a relation on
\text{Obj}\,(\mathcal{C})\times\text{Mor}\,(\mathcal{D})
, which is a small set.
(2) Using the same argument as (1), we see
\text{Fun}(\mathcal{C},\mathcal{D})
is locally small.
(3) Using the same argument as (1), we see
\text{Fun}(\mathcal{C},\mathcal{D})
is large.
(4) This is
[
FS95
]
.
∎

Remark A.31
.

If
\mathcal{C}
and
\mathcal{D}
are very large categories, then there is no guarantee that the functor category
\text{Fun}(\mathcal{C},\mathcal{D})
exists in the sense that even if we can construct its collections of objects and morphisms, we may not be able to prove that these collections are sets.

Definition A.32
.

Given a category
\mathcal{C}
, a
small diagram
in
\mathcal{C}
is a functor
F:I\to\mathcal{C}
from a small category
I
. Similarly for a regular cardinal
\kappa
, a
\kappa
-small diagram is a functor whose domain is a category that is
\kappa
-small. We say a category
\mathcal{C}
is
\kappa
-(co)complete
if it admits (co)limits over all
\kappa
-small diagrams. We say
\mathcal{C}
is
(co)complete
if it admits (co)limits over all small diagrams.

Being careful about set-theoretic issues in category theory is necessary, as the following result shows.

Proposition A.33

(Freyd’s Theorem)
.

Let
\mathcal{C}
be a small, complete category. Then
\mathcal{C}
is a preorder.

Proof.

Suppose that
\mathcal{C}
is small and complete. Let
\kappa
be the unique cardinal such that
|\text{Mor}\,\mathcal{C}|=\kappa
. Suppose that
\mathcal{C}
is not a preorder, namely given two objects
c
and
d
in
\mathcal{C}
, there exist two distinct morphisms
f,g:c\to d
. Consider the product
\prodop\displaylimits_{\kappa}d
. Then there are
2^{\kappa}
many morphisms
c\to\prodop\displaylimits_{\kappa}d
, since there are
2^{\kappa}
many
\kappa
-indexed sequences of the form
(a_{0},a_{1},\dots)
where
a_{i}\in\{f,g\}
. Since
2^{\kappa}>\kappa
, this implies that
|\text{Mor}\,\mathcal{C}|>\kappa
, contradiction.
∎

A.2.
The Zoo of Monos and Epis

A.2.1.
Monos

Definition A.34
.

A monomorphism
f:X\to Y
in a category
\mathcal{C}
is a morphism with the property that if
g,h:Z\to X
are morphisms such that
fg=fh
, then
g=h
.

Definition A.35
.

Let
\mathcal{C}
be a category and
U\in\mathcal{C}
. A
subobject
of
U
is an equivalence class of monomorphisms
i:V\hookrightarrow U
, where identify two monomorphisms
i:V\hookrightarrow U
and
i^{\prime}:V^{\prime}\hookrightarrow U
if there is an isomorphism
\varphi:V\to V^{\prime}
such that
i^{\prime}\varphi=i
. We typically denote an equivalence class
[i:V\hookrightarrow U]
by
V\subseteq U
.

Definition A.36
.

We say that a category
\mathcal{C}
is
well-powered
if for every
U\in\mathcal{C}
, the subobjects of
U
form a small set. Note that every small category is well-powered.

Definition A.37
.

Suppose that
\mathcal{C}
is well-powered, with
U\in\mathcal{C}
. Let
\text{Sub}(U)
denote the (small) poset (with order denoted by
\subseteq
) of subobjects
43
43

43

We can equivalently consider it as the posetal reflection of the category of monomorphisms over
U
.
of
U
, where for subobjects
V,W\subseteq U
, then
V\subseteq W
if some monomorphism
i:V\hookrightarrow U
representing
V
factors through some monomorphism
j:W\hookrightarrow U
representing
W
. It is not hard to see this is well defined and makes
\text{Sub}(U)
into a poset.

This extends to a functor
\text{Sub}:\mathcal{C}^{\text{op}}\to\mathbf{Set}
44
44

44

we can also put the codomain to be the category of posets
, if
f:W\to U
is a morphism in
\mathcal{C}
, then set
\text{Sub}(f)(V\subseteq U)=(f^{*}V\subseteq W)
to be the subobject given by the pullback

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{f^{*}(V)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces V}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces W}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces U}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lrcorner}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}

Note what we have written above is not technically correct, since we should have chosen a representative monomorphism for
V\subseteq U
in order for the pullback to make sense. This is something we will do often, as it can be easily checked to be well defined, because pullbacks are only defined up to isomorphism anyway, and pullbacks of monomorphisms are monomorphisms.

Lemma A.38
.

If
\mathcal{C}
is a well-powered category with finite limits, then for every
U\in\mathcal{C}
,
\text{Sub}(U)
is a meet-semilattice. Namely for every pair
V,W\hookrightarrow U
of subobjects of
U
, the pullback

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{V\cap W}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces W}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces V}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces U}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lrcorner}

is a subobject of
U
such that
V\cap W\subseteq V
,
V\cap W\subseteq W
and for any other subobject
X
such that
X\subseteq V
,
X\subseteq W
, then
V\cap W\subseteq X
. In other words
V\cap W
is the meet of
V
and
W
in the poset
\text{Sub}(U)
. We call
V\cap W
the
intersection
of
V
and
W
as subobjects.

Lemma A.39

(
[
nLa25a
, Proposition 0.4]
)
.

If
\mathcal{C}
is a well-powered category with finite limits and universal finite colimits (see Section
11
), then for every
U\in\mathcal{C}
, the subobject poset
\text{Sub}(U)
also has binary joins given as follows. If
V,W\subseteq U
are subobjects of
U
, then consider the pushout

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{V\cap W}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces W}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces V}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{V\cup W}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lrcorner}

Then
V\cup W
is the join of
V
and
W
in
\text{Sub}(U)
, and we call it the
union
of
V
and
W
as subobjects.

Remark A.40
.

Note that
\mathcal{C}
having finite limits and colimits is not enough for
Sub
to take values in lattices. For that to be the case we must also demand that images be preserved by pullbacks. As we ask for
Sub
to take values in more structured lattices (like distributive lattices, Heyting algebras, Boolean algebras), we must demand more structure on the category
\mathcal{C}
(like being a coherent category, Heyting category and Boolean category). The study of this correspondence between structure on subobjects and structure on the category is a part of
categorical logic
. We will not delve too far into this subject, but the reader should be aware of its deep connections with Grothendieck topos theory. See
[
AB09
]
,
[
Joh02
, Section D1]
.

Definition A.41
.

Given a category
\mathcal{C}
and a morphism
f:U\to V
, an
image
of
f
is a monomorphism
m:A\hookrightarrow V
that
f
factors through such that if
m^{\prime}:B\hookrightarrow V
is another monomorphism through which
f
factors, then there exists a unique monomorphism
i:A\hookrightarrow B
such that the following diagram commutes

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces i}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces m}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces m^{\prime}}

In other words,
[m]
is the smallest subobject of
V
through which
f
factors. If it exists, then it is clearly unique up to unique isomorphism.

Definition A.42
.

We say that a monomorphism
f:U\to V
in a category
\mathcal{C}
is
regular
if there exist maps
g,h:V\to W
in
\mathcal{C}
such that

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces U}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces V}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces W}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces h}

is an equalizer.

Definition A.43
.

Given a category
\mathcal{C}
and a morphism
f:U\to V
in
\mathcal{C}
, we call a pushout of the form

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces U}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces V}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces V}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{V+_{U}V}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces i_{0}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces i_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lrcorner}

a
cokernel pair
of
f
, if it exists.

Definition A.44
.

Given a category
\mathcal{C}
with cokernel pairs and equalizers and a morphism
f:U\to V
, we call an equalizer of the form

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\text{im}_{\text{reg}}(f)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces V}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{V+_{U}V}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota_{f}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces i_{0}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces i_{1}}

a
regular image
of
f
.

Definition A.45
.

We say that a monomorphism
f:U\to V
in a category
\mathcal{C}
is
effective
if the following diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces U}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces V}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{V+_{U}V}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{i_{0}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{i_{1}}}

is an equalizer, i.e.
f
is a regular image of itself.

Remark A.46
.

The differences between monos, regular monos and effective monos might seem abstract and arbitrary at first glance, but there are important differences. Regular and effective monomorphisms behave better in general than arbitrary monomorphisms. To not get ourselves too distracted on this point, we defer to
[
Yua12
]
and
[
Lowa
]
.

Lemma A.47
.

If
\mathcal{C}
is a category with equalizers and cokernel pairs, then a monomorphism in
\mathcal{C}
is regular if and only if it is effective.

Proof.

(\Leftarrow)
This is clear, all effective epimorphisms are regular.

(\Rightarrow)
Suppose that
f:U\to V
is a regular monomorphism, given as an equalizer

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces U}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces V}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces W}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces q}

Then by the universal property of pushouts we obtain a unique map

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces U}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces V}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces V}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{V+_{U}V}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces W}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces i_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces q}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces i_{0}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\exists!h}}

But if we let

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\text{im}_{\text{reg}}(f)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces V}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{V+_{U}V}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota_{f}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces i_{0}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces i_{1}}

denote the regular image, then

q\iota_{f}=hi_{1}\iota_{f}=hi_{0}\iota_{f}=p\iota_{f}

so
\iota_{f}
factors uniquely through
f
since it is an equalizer of
p
and
q
, and it is easy to see that this map provides an inverse to
h
. Thus
f
is an effective monomorphism.
∎

Lemma A.48
.

If
\mathcal{C}
is a category with pushouts and cokernel pairs,
f:U\to V
is a morphism in
\mathcal{C}
, and all monomorphisms in
\mathcal{C}
are regular monomorphisms, then the regular image of
f
is an image of
f
.

Proof.

If
f=me
for some monomorphism
m:A\hookrightarrow V
, then since all monomorphisms in
\mathcal{C}
are regular,
m
is regular, and by Lemma
A.47
,
m
is effective. So the following diagram is an equalizer

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces V}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{V+_{A}V}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces m}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces s}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces t}

But by the universal property of pushouts, we get a unique map
h:V+_{U}V\to V+_{A}V
making the following solid diagram commute

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\text{im}_{\text{reg}}(f)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces V}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{V+_{U}V}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces V}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{V+_{A}V}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces i}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces k}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces q}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces h}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces m}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces s}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces t}

Now
m
is an equalizer, and
si=hpi=hqi=ti
, so by the universal property of the equalizer we obtain a uniqued dotted map
k
making the above diagram commute. Since
i
is a monomorphism, so is
k
. Thus, every monomorphism factoring through
f
must factor through
i
, and hence
\text{im}_{\text{reg}}(f)\hookrightarrow V
is an image of
f
.
∎

Lemma A.49
.

In
\mathbf{Set}
all monomorphisms are regular and effective.

Proof.

Let
i:S\hookrightarrow T
be an injective function. We want to show that the following diagram is an equalizer

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces S}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces T}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{T+_{S}T}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces i}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces q}

where
p,q:T\to T+_{S}T
are just the quotient maps. Suppose that
f:A\to T
is a function such that
pf=qf
. Then for every
a\in A
, there exists an
x_{a}\in S
such that
i(x_{a})=f(a)
, and since
i
is injective, this
x_{a}
is unique. Thus the map
x:A\to S
given by
x(a)=x_{a}
defines a unique map such that
ix=a
, and hence
i
is an equalizer.
∎

Corollary A.50
.

Monomorphisms in any presheaf topos
\mathbf{Pre}(\mathcal{C})
are regular and effective.

Definition A.51
.

Given a category
\mathcal{C}
with finite limits, we say a monomorphism
t:1\hookrightarrow\Omega
out of the terminal object of
\mathcal{C}
is a
subobject classifier
for
\mathcal{C}
if given any object
U\in\mathcal{C}
and any subobject
V\hookrightarrow U
, there is a unique morphism
\chi_{V}:U\to\Omega
such that the following diagram commutes and is a pullback:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces V}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 1}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces U}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Omega}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\chi_{V}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces t}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lrcorner}

Lemma A.52
.

Given a (locally small) category
\mathcal{C}
with finite limits, then
\Omega\in\mathcal{C}
is a subobject classifier for
\mathcal{C}
if and only if it is a representing object for the subobject presheaf defined above, namely there is an isomorphism

(51)

\text{Sub}(U)\cong\mathcal{C}(U,\Omega),

natural in
U
.

Example A.53
.

In the category
\mathcal{C}=\mathbf{Set}
, it is easy to see that the subobject classifier is the set
2=\{0,1\}
, with
t:1\to 2
picking out
0
. Given a subset
V\subseteq U
, the map
\chi_{V}:U\to 2
is given by

\chi_{V}(u)=\begin{cases}0&u\notin V\\
1&u\in V\end{cases}.

It is then easy to check that
t:1\to 2
is a subobject classifier for
\mathbf{Set}
.

Example A.54
.

Every presheaf topos
\mathbf{Pre}(\mathcal{C})
has a subobject classifier
\Omega
, defined as the presheaf which sends
U\in\mathcal{C}
to the set of sieves
R\hookrightarrow y(U)
. The map
*\to\Omega
is given by picking out the maximal sieve
y(U)
.

Example A.55
.

Given a Grothendieck site
(\mathcal{C},J)
, we say that a sieve
R\hookrightarrow y(U)
is
J
-closed if it has the property that if for every map
f:V\to U
, the sieve
f^{*}R
is
J
-covering, then
R
is
J
-covering. The sheaf topos
\mathbf{Sh}(\mathcal{C},j)
has a subobject classifier
\Omega
, defined as the sheaf which sends
U\in\mathcal{C}
to the set of
J
-closed sieves on
U
. Again
*\to\Omega
picks out the maximal sieve.

A.2.2.
Epis

Definition A.56
.

Let
\mathcal{C}
be a category and
U\in\mathcal{C}
. A
quotient object
of
U
is an equivalence class of epimorphisms
q:U\twoheadrightarrow V
, where we identify two epimorphisms
q
and
q^{\prime}:U\twoheadrightarrow V^{\prime}
if there exists an isomorphism
\varphi:V\to V^{\prime}
such that
\varphi q=q^{\prime}
.

Definition A.57
.

Given a category
\mathcal{C}
and a morphism
f:U\to V
, the
coimage
of
f
is the largest quotient object
U\twoheadrightarrow\text{coim}(f)
through which
f
factors.

Definition A.58
.

We say that an epimorphism
f:V\to U
in a category
\mathcal{C}
is
regular
if there exist maps
g,h:W\to V
in
\mathcal{C}
such that

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces W}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces V}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces U}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces h}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}

is a coequalizer.

Definition A.59
.

Given a category
\mathcal{C}
and a morphism
f:U\to V
in
\mathcal{C}
, we call a pullback square of the form

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{U\times_{V}U}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces U}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces U}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces V}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p_{0}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lrcorner}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}

a
kernel pair
of
f
, if it exists.

Definition A.60
.

Given a category
\mathcal{C}
with pullbacks and coequalizers and a morphism
f:U\to V
, we call a coequalizer of the form

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{U\times_{V}U}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces U}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\text{coim}_{\text{reg}}(f)}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p_{0}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces e_{f}}

a
regular coimage
of
f
.

Definition A.61
.

We say that an epimorphism
f:V\to U
in a category
\mathcal{C}
is
effective
if the following diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces V}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces U}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{U+_{V}U}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{i_{0}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{i_{1}}}

is an equalizer, i.e.
f
is a regular coimage of itself.

Lemma A.62
.

If
\mathcal{C}
is a category with coequalizers and kernel pairs, then an epimorphism in
\mathcal{C}
is regular if and only if it is effective.

Proof.

By the dual of the proof of Lemma
A.47
.
∎

Lemma A.63
.

If
\mathcal{C}
is a category with pullbacks and kernel pairs,
f:U\to V
is a morphism in
\mathcal{C}
, and all epimorphisms in
\mathcal{C}
are regular epimorphisms, then the regular coimage of
f
is a coimage of
f
.

Proof.

By the dual of the proof of Lemma
A.48
.
∎

Lemma A.64
.

In
\mathbf{Set}
, all epimorphisms are regular and effective.

Proof.

By the dual of the proof of Lemma
A.49
.
∎

Corollary A.65
.

In any presheaf topos
\mathbf{Pre}(\mathcal{C})
, all epimorphisms are regular and effective.

Remark A.66
.

Note that given a morphism
f:U\to V
in a category
\mathcal{C}
with finite limits and colimits, by universal properties, there is a canonical morphism
\text{coim}(f)\to\text{im}(f)
. In
\mathbf{Set}
, and for presheaf toposes
\mathbf{Pre}(\mathcal{C})
, this map is always an isomorphism, as can be checked directly.

A.3.
Presheaf Toposes

Lemma A.67
.

Given a small category
\mathcal{C}
, its presheaf topos
\mathbf{Pre}(\mathcal{C})
is a locally small category with all small limits and colimits. Furthermore limits and colimits are computed objectwise in the following sense: given a (small) diagram
d:I\to\mathbf{Pre}(\mathcal{C})
and
U\in\mathcal{C}
, there is an isomorphism

\left(\underset{i\in I}{\text{colim}}\,d(i)\right)(U)\cong\underset{i\in I}{\text{colim}}\,d(i)(U),\qquad\left(\lim_{i\in I}d(i)\right)(U)\cong\lim_{i\in I}d(i)(U).

Proof.

That
\mathbf{Pre}(\mathcal{C})
is locally small is Lemma
A.30
. The fact that (co)limits are computed objectwise is a standard fact about functor categories, see
[
Rie17
, Proposition 3.3.9]
for instance.
∎

Remark A.68
.

The condition for a map being an (monomorphism) epimorphism can be characterized using (pullbacks) pushouts, thus Lemma
A.67
implies that a map
f:X\to Y
of presheaves is an (mono) epi if and only if it is objectwise an (mono) epi.

Lemma A.69
.

Given a functor
F:\mathcal{C}\to\mathcal{D}
between (small) categories, there is a pair of adjunctions

(52)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathbf{Pre}(\mathcal{D})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathbf{Pre}(\mathcal{C})}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Delta_{F}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Sigma_{F}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Pi_{F}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\dashv}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\dashv}

where if
Y
is a presheaf on
\mathcal{D}
and
X
is a presheaf on
\mathcal{C}
, then

(1)

for
U\in\mathcal{C}
,
\Delta_{F}(Y)
is the presheaf defined objectwise by
(\Delta_{F}(Y))(U)=Y(F(U))
,

(2)

\Sigma_{F}(X)
is the left Kan extension of
X
along
F^{\text{op}}
,

(3)

\Pi_{F}(X)
is the right Kan extension of
X
along
F^{\text{op}}
.

Thus for
V\in\mathcal{D}
we have

\Sigma_{F}(X)(V)\cong\underset{F^{\text{op}}(U)\to V}{\text{colim}}\,X(U)\cong\underset{V\to F(U)}{\text{colim}}\,X(U),

and

\Pi_{F}(X)(V)\cong\lim_{V\to F^{\text{op}}(U)}X(U)\cong\lim_{F(U)\to V}X(U).

Lemma A.70
.

Given a functor
F:\mathcal{C}\to\mathcal{D}
between small categories and
U\in\mathcal{C}
, we have

\Sigma_{F}(y(U))\cong y(F(U)).

Proof.

Given
V\in\mathcal{D}
, we can write

\Sigma_{F}(X)(V)\cong(\text{Lan}_{F^{\text{op}}}X)(V)\cong\intop\nolimits^{U\in\mathcal{C}^{\text{op}}}\mathcal{D}^{\text{op}}(F^{\text{op}}(U),V)\times X(U)

equivalently

\Sigma_{F}(X)(V)\cong\mathcal{D}^{\text{op}}(F^{\text{op}}(-),V)\otimes_{\mathcal{C}^{\text{op}}}X

and by Lemma
9.6
we have

\Sigma_{F}(X)(V)\cong X\otimes_{\mathcal{C}}\mathcal{D}(V,F(-)).

Now if
U\in\mathcal{C}
, and
X=y(U)
, then by Lemma
9.7
, we have

\Sigma_{F}(y(U))(V)\cong y(U)\otimes_{\mathcal{C}}\mathcal{D}(V,F(-))\cong\mathcal{D}(V,F(U)).

Thus for every
V\in\mathcal{D}
,
\Sigma_{F}(y(U))(V)\cong\mathcal{D}(V,F(U))\cong y(F(U))(V)
. Thus we have
\Sigma_{F}(X)\cong y(F(U))
.
∎

Definition A.71
.

Given a presheaf
X
on a category
\mathcal{C}
, let
\intop\nolimits X
, denote its
category of elements
, namely
\intop\nolimits X
is the category whose objects are pairs
(U,x)
with
U\in\mathcal{C}
and
x\in X(U)
, and whose morphisms
f:(U,x)\to(V,y)
are those morphisms
f:U\to V
such that
X(f)(y)=x
. There is a canonical functor
\pi_{X}:\intop\nolimits X\to\mathcal{C}
given by
\pi_{X}(U,x)=U
. Note that
\intop\nolimits X
is isomorphic to the comma category
(y\downarrow X)
, where
y:\mathcal{C}\to\mathbf{Pre}(\mathcal{C})
is the Yoneda embedding.

Lemma A.72
.

Given a (small) category
\mathcal{C}
and a presheaf
X
on
\mathcal{C}
, there is an equivalence of categories

\mathbf{Pre}(\mathcal{C})/X\simeq\mathbf{Pre}(\smallint X).

Proof.

Let us construct a functor
\varphi:\mathbf{Pre}(\mathcal{C})/X\to\mathbf{Pre}(\smallint X)
. Given a map
f:Y\to X
of presheaves, let
\varphi(f)
denote the presheaf on
\intop\nolimits X
where
\varphi(f)(x,U)
is the set of sections
s\in Y(U)
such that
f_{U}(s)=x
. If
g:(x,U)\to(y,V)
is a morphism in
\intop\nolimits X
, then let
\varphi(f)(g):\varphi(f)(y,V)\to\varphi(f)(x,U)
take an element
s\in f_{V}^{-1}(y)
to
f(g)(s)\in f_{U}^{-1}(x)
. Conversely, let
\psi:\mathbf{Pre}(\smallint X)\to\mathbf{Pre}(\mathcal{C})/X
take a presheaf
A
on
\smallint X
to the presheaf
\pi_{*}(A)
defined objectwise by
\pi_{*}(A)(U)=\sumop\displaylimits_{x\in X(U)}A(U,x)
. If
g:A\to B
is a map of presheaves on
\intop\nolimits X
, then let
\pi_{*}(g):\pi_{*}(A)\to\pi_{*}(B)
be defined in the obvious way. It is not hard to show that these functors are quasi-inverses to each other and hence define an equivalence of categories.
∎

Remark A.73
.

We note that the above result extends to sheaves, see
[
MM12
, Page 157]
.

Remark A.74
.

The following important result is known both as the
Density Theorem
