and the
coYoneda Lemma
. We will just refer to it as the coYoneda Lemma.

Lemma A.75

(coYoneda Lemma)
.

Given a small category
\mathcal{C}
and
X\in\mathbf{Pre}(\mathcal{C})
, there is an isomorphism

X\cong\text{colim}\left(\intop\nolimits X\xrightarrow{\pi}\mathcal{C}\xrightarrow{y}\mathbf{Pre}(\mathcal{C})\right)\cong\intop\nolimits^{(x,U)\in\intop\nolimits X}X(U)\otimes y(U)\cong X\otimes_{\mathcal{C}}y(U)\cong\underset{y(U)\to X}{\text{colim}}\,y(U),

where
X(U)\otimes y(U)=\sumop\displaylimits_{X(U)}y(U)
is the tensoring of
y(U)
by the set
X(U)
.

Proof.

This can be proven in a variety of ways, here we give a coend calculus proof. Let
Y\in\mathbf{Pre}(\mathcal{C})
, then

\displaystyle\mathbf{Pre}(\mathcal{C})\left(\intop\nolimits^{(x,U)\in\intop\nolimits X}X(U)\otimes y(U),Y\right)

\displaystyle\cong\intop\nolimits_{(x,U)\in\intop\nolimits X}\mathbf{Pre}(\mathcal{C})(X(U)\otimes y(U),Y)

\displaystyle\cong\intop\nolimits_{(x,U)\in\intop\nolimits X}\mathbf{Set}(X(U),\mathbf{Pre}(\mathcal{C})(y(U),Y))

\displaystyle\cong\intop\nolimits_{(x,U)\in\intop\nolimits X}\mathbf{Set}(X(U),Y(U))

\displaystyle\cong\mathbf{Pre}(\mathcal{C})(X,Y),

where the second isomorphism is the defining property of tensoring, and the third is the Yoneda Lemma.
∎

Lemma A.76
.

Given a small category
\mathcal{C}
, its presheaf topos
\mathbf{Pre}(\mathcal{C})
is Cartesian closed, with internal hom defined as follows. Given presheaves
X
and
Y
on
\mathcal{C}
, let
[X,Y]
denote the presheaf defined objectwise by

(53)

[X,Y](U)\coloneqq\mathbf{Pre}(\mathcal{C})(X\times y(U),Y).

For a fixed presheaf
X
, the functor
[X,-]
defines a right adjoint to the functor
-\times X
. Furthermore
\mathbf{Pre}(\mathcal{C})
is locally cartesian closed, i.e. all of its slice categories
\mathbf{Pre}(\mathcal{C})/X
are Cartesian closed.

Proof.

We leave it to the reader to check that the internal hom defined by (
53
) makes
\mathbf{Pre}(\mathcal{C})
cartesian closed. Now the fact that
\mathbf{Pre}(\mathcal{C})
is locally cartesian closed follows Lemma
A.72
.
∎

Definition A.77
.

Given a map
f:X\to Y
of presheaves on a small category
\mathcal{C}
, let
\text{im}(f)
be the presheaf defined as follows. For
U\in\mathcal{C}
, let
\text{im}(f)(U)
be the set of sections
s:y(U)\to Y
such that there exists a dotted map (called a lift of
s
) making the following diagram commute

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{y(U)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces s}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces s^{\prime}}

Clearly
\text{im}(f)\subseteq Y
. Equivalently,
\text{im}(f)(U)=\text{im}(f_{U})
where
f_{U}:X(U)\to Y(U)
is the component map.

Lemma A.78
.

Given a map
f:X\to Y
of presheaves, the presheaf
\text{im}(f)
is the image of
f
, and is isomorphic to both the regular image and coimage of
f
in
\mathbf{Pre}(\mathcal{C})
.

Proof.

This follows from Lemma
A.67
, Corollary
A.50
and Lemma
A.48
.
∎

Lemma A.79
.

Suppose that
f:X\to Y
is a map of presheaves on a small category
\mathcal{C}
, such that the right hand vertical map is its image factorization, and such that both squares are pullbacks

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{Z\times_{Y}X}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{g^{*}(\text{im}(f))}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\text{im}(f)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Z}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lrcorner}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g^{*}(f)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lrcorner}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g}

Then
g^{*}(\text{im}(f))
is an image of the map
g^{*}(f):Z\times_{Y}X\to Z
. We say that
\mathbf{Pre}(\mathcal{C})
has pullback-stable image factorizations.

Proof.

This can be proven pointwise, and thus follows from the same result holding in
\mathbf{Set}
, which is easy to see.
∎

Appendix B
Localization

In this section we deal in detail with the important concept of localization. This is a powerful way of “inverting” a class of morphisms
W
in a category
\mathcal{C}
to obtain a new category
\mathcal{C}[W^{-1}]
.

Definition B.1
.

Let
\mathcal{C}
be a locally small category and
W
a class (i.e. large set) of morphisms in
\mathcal{C}
. We say that a functor
F:\mathcal{C}\to\mathcal{D}

inverts

W
if
F(w)
is an isomorphism for every
w\in W
. Given a category
\mathcal{D}
, let
\text{Fun}(\mathcal{C},\mathcal{D})
denote the category whose objects are functors and morphisms natural transformations, and let
\text{Fun}_{W}(\mathcal{C},\mathcal{D})
denote the full subcategory on those functors that invert
W
.

Definition B.2
.

A
localization
45
45

45

This is sometimes called weak localization. See
[
GPP22
]
for a comparison of different kinds of localization
of
\mathcal{C}
at
W
consists of a functor
L:\mathcal{C}\to\mathcal{D}
such that

(1)

L
inverts
W
,

(2)

for any functor
F:\mathcal{C}\to\mathcal{E}
that inverts
W
, there exists a functor
\widetilde{F}:\mathcal{D}\to\mathcal{E}
along with a natural isomorphism
\eta:F\Rightarrow\widetilde{F}L
, and

(3)

for every category
\mathcal{E}
, the functor

\text{Fun}(L,\mathcal{E}):\text{Fun}(\mathcal{D},\mathcal{E})\to\text{Fun}_{W}(\mathcal{C},\mathcal{E})

is fully faithful.

Lemma B.3
.

Given a locally small category
\mathcal{C}
and a class
W
of morphisms, a functor
L:\mathcal{C}\to\mathcal{D}
is a localization of
\mathcal{C}
by
W
if and only if
L
inverts
W
and for every category
\mathcal{E}
the functor

(54)

\text{Fun}(L,\mathcal{E}):\text{Fun}(\mathcal{D},\mathcal{E})\to\text{Fun}_{W}(\mathcal{C},\mathcal{E})

is an equivalence of categories.

Lemma B.4

(
[
GPP22
, Proposition 4]
)
.

Let
\mathcal{C}
be a locally small category and
W
a class of morphisms of
\mathcal{C}
. Suppose that
L:\mathcal{C}\to\mathcal{D}
and
L^{\prime}:\mathcal{C}\to\mathcal{D}^{\prime}
are localizations of
\mathcal{C}
at
W
, equipped with natural isomorphisms
\widetilde{L}L\cong L^{\prime}
and
\widetilde{L}^{\prime}L^{\prime}\cong L
, then there exist unique natural isomorphisms
\varphi:\widetilde{L}\widetilde{L}^{\prime}\cong 1_{\mathcal{D}^{\prime}}
and
\psi:\widetilde{L}^{\prime}\widetilde{L}\cong 1_{\mathcal{D}}
, defining an equivalence of categories
\phi:\mathcal{D}\to\mathcal{D}^{\prime}
such that
\phi L\cong L^{\prime}
.

Remark B.5
.

The universal property of a localization of
\mathcal{C}
at
W
can be stated much more succinctly as a pushout

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{W\times I}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{C}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{W\times J}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{D}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces i}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces L}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lrcorner}

in the very large category
\mathbf{CAT}
of large (and not necessarily locally small) categories, where
W
is thought of as a discrete category,
I
is the poset
\{0\leq 1\}
, and
J
is the category with two objects
0
and
1
and a single isomorphism between them. The functor
i
takes a pair
(w,\leq)
to the actual morphism
w
in
\mathcal{C}
. See
[
Sim05
]
for more details.

Given a category
\mathcal{C}
and a class of morphisms
W
in
\mathcal{C}
, we can construct a (possibly not locally small) category
\mathcal{C}[W^{-1}]
, which we call the
Gabriel-Zisman localization
of
\mathcal{C}
at
W
. Its objects are the same as
\mathcal{C}
, but its morphisms
f:U\to V
consist of equivalence classes of finite zig-zags of morphisms in
\mathcal{C}
of the form

U\xrightarrow{f_{0}}V_{0}\xleftarrow{w_{0}}V_{1}\xrightarrow{f_{1}}V_{2}\leftarrow\dots\rightarrow V_{n}\xleftarrow{w_{k}}V

where each
w_{i}\in W
. See
[
GZ67
, Chapter 1]
,
[
Sim05
, Section 2]
or
[
nLa24
]
for more details. This implies the following result.

Proposition B.6

(
[
GZ67
, Lemma 1.2]
)
.

For any category
\mathcal{C}
and class of morphisms
W
, the (possibly not locally small) category
\mathcal{C}[W^{-1}]
is a localization of
\mathcal{C}
at
W
.

Unfortunately, Proposition
B.6
is not as useful as it might seem. The explicit description of
\mathcal{C}[W^{-1}]
is not amenable to concrete computations.

B.0.1.
Reflective Localization

In this section, we introduce reflective localizations, a very important and useful class of localizations.

Definition B.7
.

We say that a functor
L:\mathcal{C}\to\mathcal{D}
is
reflective
if it has a fully faithful right adjoint
i
:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{D}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{C}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces L}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces i}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\dashv}

If
i:\mathcal{E}\hookrightarrow\mathcal{C}
is a full subcategory with a left adjoint
L:\mathcal{C}\to\mathcal{E}
, we call
\mathcal{E}
a
reflective subcategory
, and we call
L
the
reflector
. Let
\mathcal{L}\coloneqq iL
, we call this the
localizer
functor.

In what follows we will be interested in reflective functors
L:\mathcal{C}\to\mathcal{D}
that are furthermore localizations, which we call reflective localizations. These are very important kinds of localizations because the category
\mathcal{D}
is equivalent to a certain full subcategory of
\mathcal{C}
on the
W
-local objects (Proposition
B.15
) and because reflective localizations inherit the (co)limits of
\mathcal{C}
in a certain sense (Proposition
B.17
).

Definition B.8
.

Let
\mathcal{C}
be a category and
W
a class of morphisms in
\mathcal{C}
. We say that an object
X\in\mathcal{C}
is
W
-local
46
46

46

This terminology is really unfortunate considering how over-used the term local is used in these notes, but this terminology is by now very standard.
if for every
w:A\to B
in
W
, the map

w^{*}:\mathcal{C}(B,X)\to\mathcal{C}(A,X)

is a bijection. In other words
X
is
W
-local if and only if
y(X)
inverts
W^{\text{op}}
.

We say that a map
f:A\to B
is a
W
-local equivalence
if for every
W
-local object
Z
, the map

f^{*}:\mathcal{C}(B,Z)\to\mathcal{C}(A,Z)

is a bijection.

Remark B.9
.

Since
i
is fully faithful, we know that the counit of the adjunction
L\dashv i
is an isomorphism. Thus for every
d\in\mathcal{D}
, the component
\varepsilon_{d}:Li(d)\to d
of the counit is an isomorphism.

Lemma B.10
.

Let
L:\mathcal{C}\to\mathcal{D}
be a reflective functor with right adjoint
i:\mathcal{D}\hookrightarrow\mathcal{C}
. Let
W
denote the class of morphisms in
\mathcal{C}
inverted by
L
. If
X\in\mathcal{C}
, then the following are equivalent:

(1)

X
is isomorphic to an object
iY
, with
Y\in\mathcal{D}
,

(2)

X
is
W
-local, and

(3)

the unit
\eta_{X}:X\to iLX=\mathcal{L}X
is an isomorphism.

Proof.

(1\Rightarrow 2)
Suppose that
X\cong iY
for some
Y\in\mathcal{D}
. If
w:A\to B
is a morphism in
W
, then naturality of the adjunction
L\dashv i
implies that the following diagram commutes

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{C}(B,X)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{C}(A,X)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{C}(B,iY)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{C}(A,iY)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{D}(LB,Y)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{D}(LA,Y)}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{w^{*}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{(Lw)^{*}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cong}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cong}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cong}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cong}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces w^{*}}

and since
w\in W
,
L(w)
is an isomorphism, so
(Lw)^{*}
is also an isomorphism. Thus
w^{*}
is an isomorphism. Thus
X
is
W
-local.

(2\Rightarrow 3)
The triangle identities of the adjunction imply that the composition

LX\xrightarrow{L(\eta_{X})}LiLX\xrightarrow{\varepsilon_{LX}}LX

is the identity on
X
, but
i
is fully faithful so
\varepsilon_{LX}
is an isomorphism. Therefore
L(\eta_{X})
is an isomorphism, and thus
\eta_{X}\in W
.

Now since
X
is
W
-local, the map

\eta^{*}_{X}:\mathcal{C}(iLX,X)\to\mathcal{C}(X,X)

is a bijection. Thus there exists a unique map
g:iLX\to X
such that
(\eta^{*}_{X})(g)=g\eta_{X}=1_{X}
. But by (1) above,
iLX
is
W
-local as well, and
L(g\eta_{X})=L(g)L(\eta_{X})=1_{LX}
, thus
L(g)
is an isomorphism, so
g\in W
. Thus the map

g^{*}:\mathcal{C}(X,iLX)\to\mathcal{C}(iLX,iLX)

is also a bijection. Thus there exists a unique
h:X\to iLX
such that
hg=1_{iLX}
. But then
\eta_{X}=(hg)\eta_{X}=h(g\eta_{X})=h
. Thus
\eta_{X}
is an isomorphism.

(3\Rightarrow 1)
This is clear.
∎

Lemma B.11
.

Let
L:\mathcal{C}\to\mathcal{D}
be a reflective functor and let
W
denote the class of morphisms in
\mathcal{C}
that are inverted by
L
. Then

(1)

the essential image of
iL
consists precisely of the
W
-local objects, and

(2)

the
W
-local equivalences are precisely the maps in
W
.

Proof.

(1) This follows from Lemma
B.10
.

(2) Suppose
f:A\to B
is a
W
-local equivalence. We want to show that
L(f)
is an isomorphism. Suppose that
Y\in\mathcal{D}
, then by the naturality of the adjunction
L\vdash i
, the following diagram commutes

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{D}(LB,Y)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{D}(LA,Y)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{C}(B,iY)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{C}(A,iY)}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(Lf)^{*}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cong}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cong}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f^{*}}

Now since
f
is a
W
-local equivalence, and
iY
is
W
-local by Lemma
B.10
, then
f^{*}
is an isomorphism, which implies that
(Lf)^{*}
is an isomorphism. Since
Y
was arbitrary, by the Yoneda lemma this implies that
Lf
is an isomorphism. Thus
f\in W
.

Now suppose
f\in W
, then
Lf
is an isomorphism, so
(Lf)^{*}
is an isomorphism so
f^{*}
is an isomorphism in the above commuting diagram. Since every
W
-local object is isomorphic to an object of the form
iY
by Lemma
B.10
, this implies that
f
is a
W
-local equivalence.
∎

Lemma B.12

(
[
nLa23
, Proposition 0.8]
)
.

Let
L:\mathcal{C}\to\mathcal{D}
be a reflective functor, and let
W
denote the class of morphisms in
\mathcal{C}
that are inverted by
L
. Then
L:\mathcal{C}\to\mathcal{D}
is a weak localization of
\mathcal{C}
at
W
.

Proof.

Let
F:\mathcal{C}\to\mathcal{E}
be a functor that inverts all the morphisms in
W
. We wish to show that it factors through
L
up to natural isomorphism. Consider the following diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{C}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{C}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{E}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{D}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 1_{\mathcal{C}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces L}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces i}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\eta}

where
\eta
denotes the unit of the adjunction
L\dashv i
. Let
\widetilde{F}\coloneqq Fi
. We want to show that
F\eta:F\Rightarrow\widetilde{F}L
is a natural isomorphism. Notice that
L(\eta_{X})
is an isomorphism for every object
X\in\mathcal{C}
as argued in the proof of Lemma
B.10
. That means that
\eta_{X}\in W
, so
F(\eta_{X})
is also an isomorphism. This implies that
F\eta
is a natural isomorphism.

Now suppose there is another functor
\widetilde{F}^{\prime}:\mathcal{D}\to\mathcal{E}
and a natural isomorphism
\alpha:F\Rightarrow\widetilde{F}^{\prime}L
. Consider the pasting diagram:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{C}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{E}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{D}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{D}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces i}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces L}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 1_{\mathcal{D}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\widetilde{F}^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varepsilon}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\alpha}

this provides a natural isomorphism
Fi\cong\widetilde{F}^{\prime}
. If we now paste the above diagram to the unit
\eta
we will get
\alpha
back by the triangle identities:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{C}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{C}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{E}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{=}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{C}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{E}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{D}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{D}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{D}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces i}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces L}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 1_{\mathcal{D}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\widetilde{F}^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces L}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 1_{\mathcal{C}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces L}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\widetilde{F}^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varepsilon}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\alpha}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\eta}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\alpha}

Thus
\alpha
factors through
F\eta
uniquely, showing that (
54
) is fully faithful, and therefore
L:\mathcal{C}\to\mathcal{D}
is a localization of
\mathcal{C}
at
W
.
∎

Definition B.13
.

Suppose that
L:\mathcal{C}\to\mathcal{D}
is a localization of
\mathcal{C}
at a class of morphisms
W
. We say that
L
is a
reflective localization
if
L
is furthermore a reflective functor.

For plenty of examples of reflective localizations, see
[
Rie17
, Example 4.5.14]
.

Corollary B.14
.

Every reflective functor
L:\mathcal{C}\to\mathcal{D}
is a reflective localization of
\mathcal{C}
at the class
L^{-1}(\text{iso})
of morphisms inverted by
L
.

Proposition B.15
.

Suppose that
L:\mathcal{C}\to\mathcal{D}
is a reflective localization of
\mathcal{C}
at
W
, with right adjoint
i:\mathcal{D}\hookrightarrow\mathcal{C}
. Then if we let
\iota:\mathcal{C}_{W}\hookrightarrow\mathcal{C}
denote the full subcategory of
\mathcal{C}
on the
W
-local objects, there is an equivalence
\phi:\mathcal{D}\to\mathcal{C}_{W}
making the following diagram commutes

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{D}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{C}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{C}_{W}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces i}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{\iota}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\phi}

Proof.

First note that
W\subseteq L^{-1}(\text{iso})
since
L
inverts
W
. Let us show that
i
factors through
\mathcal{C}_{W}
. In other words if
Y\in\mathcal{D}
, then we wish to show that
iY
is
W
-local. But by Lemma
B.10
,
iY
is
L^{-1}(\text{iso})
-local, and therefore
W
-local. Therefore
i
factors through the inclusion
\iota
, let us denote the corestriction of
i
by
\phi
. Let us show that
\phi
is essentially surjective.

Suppose that
X\in\mathcal{C}
is
W
-local. We want to show that
X
is also
L^{-1}(\text{iso})
-local. Now since
X
is
W
-local, that implies that
y(X)
inverts
W
. Therefore by the universal property of localization, we obtain a factorization of
y(X)
up to natural isomorphism

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{C}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathbf{Set}^{\text{op}}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{D}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces y(X)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces L}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces G}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\ell}

But this implies that
y(X)
inverts
L^{-1}(\text{iso})
. Thus
X
is
L^{-1}(\text{iso})
-local. Thus by Lemma
B.10
, this implies that there exists a
Y\in\mathcal{D}
such that
X\cong iY
. Therefore
\phi
is essentially surjective. Furthermore,
\phi
is fully faithful because
i
is. Thus
\phi
is an equivalence and clearly
\iota\phi=i
.
∎

Corollary B.16
.

Given a reflective functor
L:\mathcal{C}\to\mathcal{D}
, then
L
is a reflective localization at
L^{-1}(\text{iso})
and
\mathcal{D}
is equivalent to the full subcategory
\mathcal{C}_{L^{-1}(\text{iso})}
of
L^{-1}(\text{iso})
-local objects in
\mathcal{C}
.

The following result gives a big motivation for understanding reflective localizations. It can help us obtain (co)completeness results just by knowing a category is a reflective localization.

Proposition B.17

(
[
Rie17
, Proposition 4.5.15]
)
.

Given a reflective subcategory
i:\mathcal{D}\hookrightarrow\mathcal{C}
with left adjoint
L
then

•

if
d:I\to\mathcal{D}
is a (small) diagram, then a cone
\lambda:\Delta(V)\Rightarrow d
in
\mathcal{D}
is a limit cone if and only if
i\lambda:\Delta(iV)\Rightarrow id
is a limit cone in
\mathcal{C}
. Furthermore if there exists a limit cone
\lambda^{\prime}:\Delta(U)\Rightarrow id
in
\mathcal{C}
, then there exists a limit cone
\lambda:\Delta(V)\Rightarrow d
in
\mathcal{D}
such that
iV=U
and
i\lambda=\lambda^{\prime}
,

•

if
d:I\to\mathcal{D}
is a (small) diagram, and
id:I\to\mathcal{C}
admits a colimit
\text{colim}\,id
in
\mathcal{C}
, then
L(\text{colim}\,id)
is a colimit of
d
in
\mathcal{D}
.

Therefore if
\mathcal{D}\hookrightarrow\mathcal{C}
is a reflective subcategory, with
\mathcal{C}
(co)complete, then so is
\mathcal{D}
. Furthermore limits in
\mathcal{D}
agree with those in
\mathcal{C}
, while colimits in
\mathcal{D}
are computed by applying
L
to the colimit computed in
\mathcal{C}
.

Appendix C
Locally Presentable Categories

Directed posets and filtered categories are massively important objects in category theory, and show up often in these notes, especially in the theory of flat functors (Section
9
). In this section, we build up from these concepts to the theory of locally presentable categories. These constitute a huge class of categories that have very nice properties. We try to keep this discussion motivated and down to earth.

This section owes itself to the classic reference
[
Ros+94
]
, and the reader should consult there for more details. We also recommend the excellent introductory survey
[
Sar17
]
.

To motivate locally presentable categories, let us begin with the following elementary observation about sets: suppose that
S
is a set, and let
\text{Sub}_{\text{fin}}(S)
denote the poset of finite subsets of
S
. Then
S
is a colimit of the included diagram
i_{S}:\text{Sub}_{\text{fin}}(S)\hookrightarrow\mathbf{Set}
. Indeed,
S
forms a cocone
\lambda:i_{S}\Rightarrow\Delta(S)
over
i_{S}
by including every finite subset into
S
. If
T
is another set with a cocone
\lambda^{\prime}:i_{S}\Rightarrow\Delta(T)
, then we can define a map
h:S\to T
by sending
x\in S
to wherever
\lambda^{\prime}_{\{x\}}:\{x\}\to T
sends
x
in
T
. This is easily seen to be well-defined and unique, showing that
S
is a colimit of
i_{S}
. This shows that the large category
\mathbf{Set}
is generated in some sense by the essentially small full subcategory
\mathbf{FinSet}
. Large categories in general are unwieldy and can be hard to work with directly. Locally presentable categories are large categories that are controlled in a very strong way by a small full subcategory. Let us now try and abstract this important property of
\mathbf{Set}
.

C.1.
Directed and Filtered Categories

Definition C.1
.

We say that a poset
I
is
finitely directed
if:

(1)

it is nonempty, and

(2)

every pair of elements
x,y\in I
have an upper bound in
I
, i.e. there exists a
z\in I
such that
x\leq z
and
y\leq z
.

The second condition is equivalent to every finite subset
A\subseteq I
having an upper bound, i.e. for all
a\in A
there is a
z\in I
such that
a\leq z
. We say a diagram
d:I\to\mathcal{C}
is finitely directed if
I
is a finitely directed poset, and say that
\text{colim}\,d
is a finitely directed colimit.

Directed posets are those posets which “flow” in one direction, namely the direction of the upper bounds.

Example C.2
.

If
S
is a set, then
\text{Sub}_{\text{fin}}(S)
is directed, since if
A,B\in\text{Sub}_{\text{fin}}(S)
, then
A\cup B
is a finite subset of
S
and
A,B\subseteq A\cup B
.

Now note that if
S
is a countably infinite set, say
\mathbb{N}=\omega
, then there will be infinite subsets of
\text{Sub}_{\text{fin}}(\omega)
with no upper bound. For example, the set
\{\{0\},\{0,1\},\{0,1,2\},\dots\}
has no upper bound in
\text{Sub}_{\text{fin}}(\omega)
. This motivates the next definition.

Definition C.3
.

Given a regular cardinal (Definition
A.12
)
\kappa
, we say that a poset
I
is
\kappa
-directed
, if for every subset
A\subseteq I
with
|A|<\kappa
,
A
has an upper bound in
I
. We say that
I
is directed if it is
\kappa
-directed for some
\kappa
.

Thus being finitely directed is equivalent to being
\omega
-directed. Thus
\text{Sub}_{\text{fin}}(\omega)
is
\omega
-directed, but not
\aleph_{1}
-directed. However the poset of all subsets
\text{Sub}(\omega)
is
\kappa
-directed for all cardinals
\kappa
since
\omega
is an upper bound for every subset of
\text{Sub}(\omega)
. This same idea as above extends powerfully to all diagrams to give the following result.

Lemma C.4
.

Let
\mathcal{C}
be a category with all small colimits, and let
d:I\to\mathcal{C}
be a small diagram. Let
\text{Sub}_{\text{fin}}(I)
denote the poset of finite full subcategories of
I
ordered by inclusion. This is a finitely directed poset, and furthermore we obtain a functor
s(d):\text{Sub}_{\text{fin}}(I)\to\mathcal{C}
given by sending
J\in\text{Sub}_{\text{fin}}(I)
to
\text{colim}\,d|_{J}
. We have the following

\text{colim}\,d\cong\underset{J\in\text{Sub}_{\text{fin}}(I)}{\text{colim}}\,s(d)(J)\cong\underset{J\in\text{Sub}_{\text{fin}}(I)}{\text{colim}}\,\text{colim}\,d|_{J}.

In other words, every colimit is isomorphic to a directed colimit of finite “partial colimits”.

Proof.

Let us show that
\text{colim}\,d
forms a cocone over
s(d)
. Let
\lambda:d\Rightarrow\Delta(\text{colim}\,d)
denote the colimit cocone over
d
, and
\lambda^{J}:d|_{J}\Rightarrow\Delta(\text{colim}\,d|_{J})
the colimit cocone of the restriction. Then for
J\in\text{Sub}_{\text{fin}}(I)
, we get a cocone
\lambda|_{J}:d|_{J}\Rightarrow\Delta(\text{colim}\,d)
, and thus a unique map
\sigma_{J}:s(d)(J)\to\text{colim}\,d
such that
\Delta(\sigma_{J})\circ\lambda^{J}=\lambda|_{J}
. Now if
J\hookrightarrow J^{\prime}
, then its easy to see that these maps are compatible, i.e. the
\sigma_{J}
form a cocone
\sigma:s(d)\Rightarrow\Delta(\text{colim}\,d)
, again by the uniqueness part of the universal property of colimits. Now if
\tau:s(d)\Rightarrow\Delta(V)
is a cocone over
s(d)
, let us define a map
h:\text{colim}\,d\to V
, which is the same thing as defining a cocone
h:d\Rightarrow\Delta(V)
. Given
i\in I
, let
h(i):d(i)\to V
denote the map given by considering the finite full subcategory
\{i\}
of
I
given by the single object
i
. This defines a map
\tau_{\{i\}}:s(d)(\{i\})\to V
, but of course
s(d)(\{i\})=\text{colim}\,d|_{\{i\}}\cong d(i)
, with colimit cocone component
\lambda^{\{i\}}_{i}:d(i)\to\text{colim}\,d|_{\{i\}}
given by the identity. So we have a map
\tau_{\{i\}}:d(i)\to V
. So let
h(i)=\tau_{\{i\}}
. We still have to prove that this forms a cocone over
d
. So suppose that
f:i\to j
is a morphism in
I
. We need to show that
h(j)d(f)=h(i)
. But this follows by considering the following commutative diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces V}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\text{colim}\,d|_{\{i\}}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\text{colim}\,d|_{\{i,j\}}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\text{colim}\,d|_{\{j\}}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{d(i)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{d(j)}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces h(i)=\tau_{\{i\}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\tau_{\{i,j\}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces h(j)=\tau_{\{j\}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lambda^{\{i,j\}}_{i}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces d(f)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lambda_{j}^{\{i,j\}}}

In other words, the
\lambda^{\{i,j\}}
being compatible with
f
forces the
\tau_{\{i\}}
to be compatible with it as well. Thus we have defined a cocone
h:d\Rightarrow\Delta(V)
, and therefore a unique map
h:\text{colim}\,d\to V
such that
\tau_{\{i\}}=h\lambda_{i}
for every
i\in I
. Now all we have to prove is that
\Delta(h)\sigma=\tau
, or equivalently for each
J\in\text{Sub}_{\text{fin}}(I)
, that
h\sigma_{J}=\tau_{J}
. Now
h\sigma_{J}:\text{colim}\,d|_{J}\to V
and
\tau_{J}:\text{colim}\,d|_{J}\to V
are maps out of
\text{colim}\,d|_{J}
and therefore are equivalent to cocones over
d|_{J}
. Thus it is enough to check that that they are equal as cocones. But since
\Delta(\sigma_{J})\circ\lambda^{J}=\lambda|_{J}
, this implies that for every
i\in I
we have
\sigma_{J}\lambda^{J}_{i}=\lambda_{i}
, and
\tau_{J}
precomposed with
\lambda^{J}_{i}:d(i)\to\text{colim}\,d|_{J}
is just
\tau_{\{i\}}
. Thus since
\tau_{\{i\}}=h\lambda_{i}
for each
i\in I
, this proves that
\sigma
is a colimit cocone.
∎

Given a category
\mathcal{C}
with a full subcategory
\mathcal{C}_{0}
, let us say that
\mathcal{C}
is
generated by finitely directed colimits
from
\mathcal{C}_{0}
if every object
U\in\mathcal{C}
is isomorphic to
some
finitely directed colimit in
\mathcal{C}_{0}
. In other words, there is a finitely directed diagram
d:I\to\mathcal{C}_{0}
, such that
U
is a colimit in
\mathcal{C}
of the resulting diagram

I\xrightarrow{d}\mathcal{C}_{0}\hookrightarrow\mathcal{C}.

So what we have discovered is that the category
\mathbf{Set}
, which is a large category, is generated by finitely directed colimits from the essentially small full subcategory
\mathbf{FinSet}
of finite sets.

It turns out that we can actually strengthen the above description of how
\mathbf{Set}
is generated from
\mathbf{FinSet}
. Above we said that every set is a colimit of
some
finitely directed diagram. However, we can actually assign to every set a canonical diagram that it is the colimit of.

Definition C.5
.

Let
\mathcal{C}
be a category, and
\mathcal{C}_{0}
a full subcategory of
\mathcal{C}
. For an object
U\in\mathcal{C}
, consider the diagram
\pi:(\mathcal{C}_{0}\downarrow U)\to\mathcal{C}
, where
\pi(A\to U)=A
is the forgetful functor. We call this the
canonical diagram of
U
with respect to
\mathcal{C}_{0}
. We say that
\mathcal{C}_{0}
is
dense
in
\mathcal{C}
if every object
U\in\mathcal{C}
is a colimit of its canonical diagram with respect to
\mathcal{C}_{0}
,

U\cong\text{colim}\left((\mathcal{C}_{0}\downarrow U)\xrightarrow{\pi}\mathcal{C}\right).

The concept of density can be generalized as follows.

Definition C.6
.

We say that a functor
i:\mathcal{C}_{0}\to\mathcal{C}
is
dense
if
\text{Lan}_{i}i
exists and is naturally isomorphic to the identity functor on
\mathcal{C}
.

Lemma C.7
.

Given a category
\mathcal{C}
and a full subcategory
\mathcal{C}_{0}
, the inclusion functor
i:\mathcal{C}_{0}\hookrightarrow\mathcal{C}
is dense if and only if
\mathcal{C}_{0}
is dense in
\mathcal{C}
.

Proof.

The functor
i
is dense if and only for every
U_{0}\in\mathcal{C}
we have

\text{Lan}_{i}i(U)\cong\text{colim}\left((\mathcal{C}_{0}\downarrow U)\xrightarrow{\pi^{\prime}}\mathcal{C}_{0}\xrightarrow{i}\mathcal{C}\right)\cong U.

But the functor
\pi:(\mathcal{C}_{0}\downarrow U)\to\mathcal{C}
is equal to
i\pi^{\prime}
. Thus
\mathcal{C}_{0}
is dense in
\mathcal{C}
if and only if
i
is a dense functor.
∎

Note that in the above definition if we take
\mathcal{C}=\mathbf{Set}
and
\mathcal{C}_{0}=\mathbf{FinSet}
, then
\mathbf{FinSet}
is dense in
\mathbf{Set}
if every set
S
can be written as the colimit of the canonical diagram
(\mathbf{FinSet}\downarrow S)
of all maps from finite sets into
S
. This is different from
\text{Sub}_{\text{fin}}(S)
, which is a poset, but it is still the case that
S
is the colimit of its canonical diagram with respect to
\mathbf{FinSet}
, using practically the same argument.

So we have managed to strengthen
some colimit
above to
a particular colimit
, but we have lost something in the sense that these particular colimits are no longer directed. Thus we introduce the following notion, which is a generalization of directed posets.

Definition C.8
.

A small category
\mathcal{C}
is
finitely filtered
if for every finite category
I
and diagram
d:I\to\mathcal{C}
, there is a cocone
\lambda:d\Rightarrow\Delta(U)
in
\mathcal{C}
. More generally a small category
\mathcal{C}
is
\kappa
-filtered
if every
\kappa
-small diagram
d
admits a cocone in
\mathcal{C}
. We say that
\mathcal{C}
is filtered if it is
\kappa
-filtered for some regular cardinal
\kappa
. We say a diagram
d:I\to\mathcal{C}
is filtered if
I
is a filtered category.

We say a category
\mathcal{C}
is (
\kappa
-)
cofiltered
if
\mathcal{C}^{\text{op}}
is (
\kappa
-)filtered.

The following alternate description of finite filteredness is also very useful.

Lemma C.9
.

A category
\mathcal{C}
is finitely filtered if and only if

(1)

it is nonempty,

(2)

for every pair of objects
U,V\in\mathcal{C}
, there exists an object
W
and morphisms
U\to W
and
V\to W
,

(3)

for every pair of parallel morphisms
f,g:U\to V
, there exists a morphism
h:V\to W
such that
hf=hg
.

The category
(\mathbf{FinSet}\downarrow S)
is finitely filtered for any set
S
. Indeed, for every
A,B\in\mathbf{FinSet}
and every pair of maps
f:A\to S
,
g:B\to S
, then
f+g:A+B\to S
is a map such that the following diagram commutes

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{A+B}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces S}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f+g}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g}

Furthermore, suppose that
a:A\to S
and
b:A\to S
are maps, and suppose that there are parallel maps
f,g:A\to B
over
S
, with
A
and
B
finite. Then
bf=bg=a
, as can be seen in the following commutative diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces S}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces S}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces a}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces b}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces b}

So by Lemma
C.9
,
(\mathbf{FinSet}\downarrow S)
is finitely filtered.

We lose very little by abstracting from directed posets to filtered categories. Let us first recall the notion of final functor.

Definition C.10
.

We say that a functor
F:\mathcal{C}\to\mathcal{D}
between cocomplete categories is
final
if for every functor
G:\mathcal{D}\to\mathcal{E}
, the canonical map

\underset{U\in\mathcal{C}}{\text{colim}}\,(G\circ F)(U)\to\underset{V\in\mathcal{D}}{\text{colim}}\,G(V)

is an isomorphism. Similarly, we say that
F
is
initial
if the canonical map

\lim_{V\in\mathcal{D}}G(V)\to\lim_{U\in\mathcal{C}}(G\circ F)(U)

is an isomorphism. Equivalently,
F
is initial if
F^{\text{op}}:\mathcal{C}^{\text{op}}\to\mathcal{D}^{\text{op}}
is final.

Proposition C.11

(
[
Rie17
, Lemma 8.3.4]
)
.

A functor
F:\mathcal{C}\to\mathcal{D}
is final if and only if for every
V\in\mathcal{D}
, the comma category
(V\downarrow F)
is nonempty and connected. Similarly,
F
is initial if and only if for all
V\in\mathcal{D}
, the comma category
(F\downarrow V)
is nonempty and connected.

Lemma C.12

(
[
Ros+94
, Theorem 1.5 and Remark 1.21
]
)
.

For every small
\kappa
-filtered category
\mathcal{C}
there exists a small
\kappa
-directed poset
\mathcal{D}
and a final
47
47

47

The reference refers to these as cofinal functors, an unfortunate clash of terminology.
functor
F:\mathcal{D}\to\mathcal{C}
. In other words, an object can be written as a filtered colimit if and only if it can be written as a directed colimit.

Now before we go further, let us better understand filtered colimits in
\mathbf{Set}
. This detour will have a surprisingly large pay-off (Proposition
C.15
), but we must first muck about with set-level details.

Let us show that filtered colimits in
\mathbf{Set}
admit a convenient description. First, let us consider an arbitrary diagram
d:I\to\mathbf{Set}
. By the usual way of decomposing a colimit into a coequalizer of coproducts we have

\underset{i\in I}{\text{colim}}\,d(i)\cong\text{coeq}\left(\sumop\displaylimits_{f:i\to j}d(i)\underset{\psi}{\overset{\varphi}{\rightrightarrows}}\sumop\displaylimits_{j\in I}d(j)\right)

where
\varphi
takes a pair
(f:i\to j,\,x\in d(i))
to
d(f)(x)\in d(j)
and
\psi
takes
(f:i\to j,\,x\in d(i))
to
x\in d(i)
.

Thus we can describe
\text{colim}_{i\in I}d(i)
as the set
\sumop\displaylimits_{i\in I}d(i)
quotiented by the smallest equivalence relation
\approx
generated by the relation where if
x\in d(i)
, then
x\sim d(f)(x)
. We can describe
\approx
completely as the reflexive, symmetric, transitive closure of the relation
\sim
. Another way of saying this is that
x\approx y
if and only if there is a finite zig-zag in
I
of the following form (or of the form where we reverse the directions of the arrows)

(55)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces i}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{k_{0}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{k_{1}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\dots}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{k_{n}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces j}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces h_{0}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces h_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces h_{n-1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g}

and there exist elements
x_{0}\in d(k_{0})
,
x_{1}\in d(k_{1})
,
\dots
,
x_{n}\in d(k_{n})
such that

d(f)(x)=x_{0}=d(h_{0})(x_{1}),\;d(h_{1})(x_{1})=x_{2}=d(h_{3})(x_{3}),\,\dots,\,d(h_{n-1})(x_{n-1})=x_{n}=d(g)(y).

So far, this is just the general description of an equivalence class
[x]
of an element in a general colimit in
\mathbf{Set}
. Now if
I
is filtered, and
x\approx y
, then the zig-zag (
55
) admits a cocone

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces k}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces i}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{k_{0}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{k_{1}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\dots}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{k_{n}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces j}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\ell_{0}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\ell_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces h_{0}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces h_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces h_{n-1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\ell_{n}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces q}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g}

and since

d(p)(x)=d(\ell_{0}f)(x)=d(\ell_{0})d(f)(x)=d(\ell_{0})(x_{0})=d(\ell_{0})d(h_{0})(x_{1})=d(\ell_{1})(x_{1})=\dots=d(\ell_{n})(x_{n})=d(q)(y)

this implies that there is a
z=d(p)(x)=d(q)(y)
in
d(k)
such that
x\sim z
and
y\sim z
. We have thus reduced general zig-zags to spans
i\to k\leftarrow j
, and therefore have proved the following result.

Lemma C.13
.

Suppose that
d:I\to\mathbf{Set}
is a filtered diagram of sets, then
\text{colim}_{i\in I}\,d(i)
is isomorphic to the set
\sumop\displaylimits_{i\in I}d(i)
quotiented by the equivalence relation
\approx
where
x\approx y
if there exists a
k\in I
and maps
f:i\to k
,
g:j\to k
such that
d(f)(x)=d(g)(y)
.

Let
\mathcal{C}
be a category with
I
and
J
-shaped colimits and limits. Suppose that
d:I\times J\to\mathcal{C}
is a diagram. There is a canonical map

(56)

\sigma:\underset{i\in I}{\text{colim}}\lim_{j\in J}d(i,j)\to\lim_{j\in J}\underset{i\in I}{\text{colim}}\,d(i,j),

that can be described as follows. For every
i_{0}\in I
and
j_{0}\in J
, there are canonical maps

(57)

\lim_{j\in J}d(i_{0},j)\to d(i_{0},j_{0})\to\underset{i\in I}{\text{colim}}\,d(i,j_{0}),

given by the respective (co)limit (co)cones. Now the composite map forms a cone over the diagram
\text{colim}_{i\in I}\,d(i,-):J\to\mathcal{C}
for if
f:j_{0}\to j^{\prime}_{0}
is a morphism in
J
, then the following diagram commutes

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\lim_{j\in J}d(i_{0},j)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{d(i_{0},j_{0})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{d(i_{0},j^{\prime}_{0})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\text{colim}_{i\in I}\,d(i,j_{0})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\text{colim}_{i\in I}\,d(i,j^{\prime}_{0})}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces d(i_{0},f)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\text{colim}_{i\in I}\,d(i,f)}

So for every
i_{0}\in I
there is a map

\lim_{j\in J}d(i_{0},j)\to\lim_{j\in J}\underset{i\in I}{\text{colim}}\,d(i,j),

and a similar argument shows this provides a cocone over
\lim_{j\in J}d(-,j):I\to\mathcal{C}
, and thus gives the map
\sigma
in (
56
).

Definition C.14
.

We say that
I
-colimits commute with
J
-limits
in
\mathcal{C}
if the map
\sigma
in (
56
) is an isomorphism for every diagram
d:I\times J\to\mathcal{C}
.

Proposition C.15

(
[
Rie17
, Theorem 3.8.9]
)
.

Finitely filtered colimits commute with finite limits in
\mathbf{Set}
.

Proof.

Suppose that
I
is a (small) finitely filtered category,
J
is a finite category and
d:I\times J\to\mathbf{Set}
is a diagram. We want to show that
\sigma
is a bijection. First note that an element of
\lim_{j\in J}\text{colim}_{i\in I}\,d(i,j)
consists of a finite collection of elements
[x_{j_{0}}]\in\text{colim}_{i\in I}\,d(i,j_{0})
which are compatible in the sense that for every map
f:j\to j^{\prime}
in
J
,
\text{colim}_{i\in I}\,d(i,f)([x_{j}])=[d(i_{0},f)(x_{j})]=[x_{j^{\prime}}]
. Since
J
is finite and
I
is filtered, by Lemma
C.13
, this means that there exists a fixed
k\in I
, and elements
y_{j_{0}}\in d(k,j_{0})
such that
[x_{j_{0}}]=[y_{j_{0}}]
for every
j_{0}\in J
. In other words, we can represent an element of
\lim_{j\in J}\text{colim}_{i\in I}\,d(i,j)
by the collection
\{y_{j}\}_{j\in J}
, and they will be compatible on the nose,
d(k,f)(y_{j})=y_{j^{\prime}}
. But such a collection is the same thing as an element of the set
\lim_{j\in J}d(k,j)
. In other words,
\sigma
is surjective.

Now an element of
\text{colim}_{i\in I}\lim_{j\in J}d(i,j)
consists of an equivalence class
[\{y_{j}\}_{j\in J}]
of compatible families of elements
y_{j}\in d(i,j)
, where
\{y_{j}\}\approx\{y^{\prime}_{j}\}
if there are maps
f:i\to k
,
g:i^{\prime}\to k
such that
d(f,j)(y_{j})=d(g,j)(y^{\prime}_{j})
for every
j\in J
, by Lemma
C.13
. Now if
[\{y_{j}\}]
and
[\{y^{\prime}_{j}\}]
are elements such that
\sigma([\{y_{j}\}])=\sigma([\{y^{\prime}_{j}\}])
, then we know that there is a
k\in I
and a family
\{z_{j}\}
with each
z_{j}\in d(k,j)
such that
[\{y_{j}\}]=[\{z_{j}\}]=[\{y^{\prime}_{j}\}]
such that the
z_{j}
are compatible on the nose. But this implies that
\{y_{j}\}\approx\{z_{j}\}\approx\{y^{\prime}_{j}\}
. Thus
\sigma
is injective.
∎

In fact Proposition
C.15
has a converse.

Proposition C.16

(
[
Bje+15
, Lemma 2.1]
)
.

Suppose that
I
is a small category and
I
-colimits commute with all finite limits in
\mathbf{Set}
. Then
I
is a finitely filtered category.

Before proving Proposition
C.16
, let us prove the following.

Lemma C.17
.

Let
\mathcal{C}
be a small category with
U\in\mathcal{C}
and consider the representable presheaf
y(U)\in\mathbf{Pre}(\mathcal{C})
. Then

\underset{V\in\mathcal{C}^{\text{op}}}{\text{colim}}\,y(U)(V)\cong\lim_{V\in\mathcal{C}}y^{\text{co}}(U)(V)\cong*

where
*
denotes a singleton set.

Proof.

Given an arbitrary set
S
, we have

\displaystyle\mathbf{Set}\left(\underset{V\in\mathcal{C}^{\text{op}}}{\text{colim}}\,y(U)(V),S)\right)

\displaystyle\cong\mathbf{Pre}(\mathcal{C})(y(U),\Delta(S))

\displaystyle\cong\Delta(S)(U)

\displaystyle\cong S

\displaystyle\cong\mathbf{Set}(*,S),

where the first isomorphism is the definition of the colimit functor as representing the constant functor. Since
S
was arbitrary, by the Yoneda lemma, this implies that
\text{colim}_{V\in\mathcal{C}}y(U)(V)\cong*
. A similar argument proves that
\lim_{V\in\mathcal{C}}y^{\text{co}}(U)(V)\cong*
, where
y^{\text{co}}(U)=\mathcal{C}(U,-)
.
∎

Proof of Proposition
C.16
.

Suppose that
d:J^{\text{op}}\to I
is a finite diagram. Consider the functor
F:I\times J\to\mathbf{Set}
defined by
F(i,j)=I(d(j),i)
. Now by Lemma
C.17
, for each
j\in J
, we have

\lim_{i\in I}F(i,j)=\lim_{i\in I}y^{\text{co}}(d(j))(i)\cong*.

Thus
\text{colim}_{j\in J}\lim_{i\in I}F(i,j)\cong*
. But

\underset{j\in J}{\text{colim}}\,F(i,j)\cong\underset{j\in J}{\text{colim}}\,I(d(j),i)\cong I(\underset{j\in J}{\text{colim}}\,d(j),i)\cong\text{coCone}(d)(i),

where
\text{coCone}(d)(i)
is the set of cocones over
d
with vertex
i
. Thus if
\text{coCone}(d)(i)
is empty for all
i
, then
\lim_{i\in I}\text{Cone}(d)(i)\cong\varnothing
. So if
I
-shaped colimits commute with finite limits, then
d
must have a cocone. Since
d
was an arbitrary finite diagram, this proves that
I
is finitely filtered.
∎

Corollary C.18
.

Given a small category
I
,
I
-colimits commute with finite limits in
\mathbf{Set}
if and only if
I
is finitely filtered.

This result can be strengthened to the following.

Corollary C.19
.

Given a small category
I
and a regular cardinal
\kappa
,
I
-colimits commute with
\kappa
-small limits in
\mathbf{Set}
if and only if
I
is
\kappa
-filtered.

Corollary C.20
.

Given a small category
I
and a regular cardinal
\kappa
,
I
-colimits commute with
\kappa
-small limits in presheaf topoi if and only if
I
is
\kappa
-filtered.

Now with Proposition
C.15
under our belt, let us return to our new goal. We wish to abstract the following property of
\mathbf{Set}
: the category
\mathbf{FinSet}
is dense in
\mathbf{Set}
. First we want a notion similar to that of finiteness for an arbitrary category.

Definition C.21
.

Let
\mathcal{C}
be a category and
U\in\mathcal{C}
. We say that
U
is
finitely presentable
if for every finitely filtered diagram
d:I\to\mathcal{C}
, the canonical map

(58)

\tau:\underset{i\in I}{\text{colim}}\,\mathcal{C}(U,d(i))\to\mathcal{C}(U,\underset{i\in I}{\text{colim}}\,d(i))

is an isomorphism. In other words
U
is finitely presentable if and only if
\mathcal{C}(U,-)
preserves finitely filtered colimits. More generally we say that
U
is
\kappa
-presentable if
\mathcal{C}(U,-)
preserves
\kappa
-filtered colimits.

Remark C.22
.

If
U\in\mathcal{C}
is
\kappa
-presentable, then it is
\kappa^{\prime}
-presentable for any pair of regular cardinals
\kappa<\kappa^{\prime}
. This is just because
\kappa^{\prime}
-filtered categories are
\kappa
-filtered. In particular if
U
is finitely presentable, then it is
\kappa
-presentable for all infinite regular cardinals
\kappa
.

We can equivalently swap the word “filtered” with “directed” in the above definition thanks to the following result.

Proposition C.23

(
[
Ros+94
, Corollary of Theorem 1.5 and Remark 1.21]
)
.

A category
\mathcal{C}
admits
\kappa
-filtered colimits if and only if it admits
\kappa
-directed colimits. Furthermore a functor
F
preserves
\kappa
-filtered colimits if and only if it preserves
\kappa
-directed colimits.

From Lemma
C.13
, we can understand the map (
58
) explicitly. The left hand side is the set of maps
s_{i}:U\to d(i)
quotiented by the equivalence relation
\approx
, where
s_{i}\approx s_{j}
if there are maps
f:i\to k
and
g:j\to k
such that
d(f)s_{i}=d(g)s_{j}
. The right hand side is the set of maps from
U
to the colimit of
d
. If we let
\lambda_{i}:d(i)\to\text{colim}\,d
denote the colimit cocone components, then the map
\tau
sends an element
[s_{i}]
to the map
\lambda_{i}s_{i}
.

So
\tau
is surjective if and only if every map
U\to\text{colim}\,d
factors through some map
U\to d(i)
. Now
\tau
is injective if and only if whenever we have maps
s_{i}:U\to d(i)
and
s_{j}:U\to d(j)
such that
\lambda_{i}s_{i}=\lambda_{j}s_{j}
, then
s_{i}\approx s_{j}
. In other words,
\tau
is a bijection if and only if every map
U\to\text{colim}\,d
factors through some
U\to d(i)
uniquely up to
\approx
.

Lemma C.24
.

A set
S
is finitely presentable in
\mathbf{Set}
if and only if it is finite.

Proof.

(\Rightarrow)
Suppose that
S
is finitely small. Then
\mathbf{Set}(S,-)
preserves the colimit of the diagram
\text{Sub}_{\text{fin}}(S)\hookrightarrow\mathbf{Set}
. In other words, the identity function
1_{S}:S\to S
factors through some finite subset of
S
. Thus
S
is finite.

(\Leftarrow)
Suppose that
S
is a finite set and
d:I\to\mathbf{Set}
is a finitely filtered diagram. If we let
S=\{x_{0},\dots,x_{n}\}
, then
S\cong\sumop\displaylimits_{i=0}^{n}\{x_{i}\}\cong\sumop\displaylimits_{i=0}^{n}*
. Thus by Proposition
C.15
,

\mathbf{Set}(S,\underset{i\in I}{\text{colim}}d(i))\cong\prodop\displaylimits_{i=0}^{n}\mathbf{Set}(*,\underset{i\in I}{\text{colim}}d(i))\cong\prodop\displaylimits_{i=0}^{n}\underset{i\in I}{\text{colim}}d(i)\cong\underset{i\in I}{\text{colim}}\prodop\displaylimits_{i=0}^{n}d(i)\cong\underset{i\in I}{\text{colim}}\,\mathbf{Set}(S,d(i)).

So
S
is finitely presentable.
∎

Remark C.25
.

We offer a more hands-on proof of Lemma
C.24
.
(\Leftarrow)
here, we think this proof really gets the idea of finite presentability across. If
h:S\to\text{colim}\,d
is a function, then by Lemma
C.13
, we know that
\text{colim}\,d=\sumop\displaylimits_{i\in I}d(i)/{\approx}
. So since
S
is finite, if we let
S=\{x_{0},\dots,x_{n}\}
, then there are objects
i_{\ell}\in I
and elements
y_{\ell}\in d(i_{\ell})
such that
[y_{\ell}]=h(x_{\ell})
for all
0\leq\ell\leq n
. Since
I
is finitely filtered, there exists a
k\in I
with maps
f_{\ell}:i_{\ell}\to k
. So there is a function
h_{k}:S\to d(k)
that sends
x_{\ell}
to
d(i_{\ell})(y_{\ell})
, and furthermore
[d(i_{\ell})(y_{\ell})]=[y_{\ell}]=h(x_{\ell})
. In other words,
h
factors through
h_{k}
. Thus
\tau
in (
58
) is surjective. Making different choices of the
i_{\ell}
and
y_{\ell}
could result in different choices of
k
, but the resulting maps
h_{k}:S\to d(k)
and
h_{k^{\prime}}:S\to d(k^{\prime})
would have the property that
h_{k}\approx h_{k^{\prime}}
. Thus
\tau
is injective.

A very similar argument proves the following.

Corollary C.26
.

Given a regular cardinal
\kappa
, a set
S
is
\kappa
-presentable if and only if
|S|<\kappa
.

Let us also note that
\kappa
-presentable objects are closed under
\kappa
-small colimits.

Lemma C.27
.

Let
\mathcal{C}
be a category with all
\kappa
-small colimits, and let
c:I\to\mathcal{C}
be a
\kappa
-small diagram. If
d
factors through
\mathcal{C}_{\kappa}
, i.e. every
c(i)
is
\kappa
-presentable, then
\text{colim}\,c
is
\kappa
-presentable.

Proof.

Given a
\kappa
-small diagram
c:I\to\mathcal{C}
and a
\kappa
-filtered diagram
d:J\to\mathcal{C}
, we have

\displaystyle\mathcal{C}(\underset{i\in I}{\text{colim}}\,c(i),\underset{j\in J}{\text{colim}}\,d(j))

\displaystyle\cong\lim_{i\in I}\mathcal{C}(c(i),\underset{j\in J}{\text{colim}}\,d(j))

\displaystyle\cong\lim_{i\in I}\underset{j\in J}{\text{colim}}\,\mathcal{C}(c(i),d(j))

\displaystyle\cong\underset{j\in J}{\text{colim}}\,\lim_{i\in I}\mathcal{C}(c(i),d(j))

\displaystyle\cong\underset{j\in J}{\text{colim}}\,\mathcal{C}(\underset{i\in I}{\text{colim}}\,c(i),d(j)).

where the second isomorphism holds because each
c(i)
is
\kappa
-presentable and the third holds by Corollary
C.19
. Thus
\underset{i\in I}{\text{colim}}\,c(i)
is
\kappa
-presentable.
∎

Example C.28
.

There are many interesting characterizations of finitely presentable objects in various categories, see
[
Ros+94
, Example 1.2]
. As just one example, to see where the terminology comes from, a group
G
is a finitely presentable object in
\mathbf{Grp}
if it is literally finitely presentable, i.e. if it can be presented using finitely many generators and finitely many relations.

Lemma C.29
.

Let
\mathcal{C}
be a small category with
U\in\mathcal{C}
. Then the representable presheaf
y(U)
is finitely presentable in
\mathbf{Pre}(\mathcal{C})
.

Proof.

This follows from the Yoneda lemma. Let
d:I\to\mathbf{Pre}(\mathcal{C})
be a finitely filtered diagram, then

\displaystyle\mathbf{Pre}(\mathcal{C})(y(U),\underset{i\in I}{\text{colim}}\,d(i))

\displaystyle\cong[\underset{i\in I}{\text{colim}}\,d(i)](U)

\displaystyle\cong\underset{i\in I}{\text{colim}}\,d(i)(U)

\displaystyle\cong\underset{i\in I}{\text{colim}}\,\mathbf{Pre}(\mathcal{C})(y(U),d(i)).

∎

Remark C.30
.

In fact, it turns out that the finitely presentable objects in a presheaf topos are precisely those presheaves that can be written as finite colimits of representables, see
[
Cam
]
.

Given a category
\mathcal{C}
, let
\mathcal{C}_{\text{fp}}
denote the full subcategory on the finitely presentable objects. As we have seen, every set
S
can be written as the colimit over its canonical diagram with respect to
\mathbf{Set}_{\text{fp}}\simeq\mathbf{FinSet}
. In other words
\mathbf{Set}_{\text{fp}}
is dense in
\mathbf{Set}
. We can similarly define
\mathcal{C}_{\kappa}
to be the full subcategory on the
\kappa
-presentable objects. Note however, that technically speaking,
\mathbf{FinSet}
is not a small category. It is essentially small though. Indeed, it is equivalent to the full subcategory
\mathbf{FinSet}^{\prime}
on the sets
\{0,1,\dots,n\}
, which is small.

Definition C.31
.

We say that a (locally small) category
\mathcal{C}
is
locally finitely presentable
if:

(1)

it is cocomplete, and

(2)

the category
\mathcal{C}_{\text{fp}}
of finitely presentable objects is essentially small and dense in
\mathcal{C}
.

More generally we say that
\mathcal{C}
is locally
\kappa
-presentable if
\mathcal{C}_{k}
is essentially small and fully dense in
\mathcal{C}
. We say that
\mathcal{C}
is
locally presentable
if it is locally
\kappa
-presentable for some infinite regular cardinal
\kappa
.

We can actually give an equivalent definition for locally presentable categories that is easier to check in practice, thanks to the following pair of technical results.

Lemma C.32

(
[
Ros+94
, Proposition 1.22]
)
.

Let
\mathcal{C}
be a cocomplete category. Then an object
U\in\mathcal{C}
can be written as
some

\kappa
-filtered colimit of
\kappa
-presentable objects if and only if
U
can be written as the colimit of its canonical diagram with respect to
\mathcal{C}_{\kappa}
. Furthermore the category
(\mathcal{C}_{\kappa}\downarrow U)
is
\kappa
-filtered for every
U\in\mathcal{C}
.

Lemma C.33

(
[
Ros+94
, Remark 1.9, 1.19]
)
.

A cocomplete category
\mathcal{C}
has a set
S
of
\kappa
-presentable objects such that every object of
\mathcal{C}
is a
\kappa
-filtered colimit of objects in
S
if and only if every object in
\mathcal{C}
is a
\kappa
-filtered colimit of
\kappa
-presentable objects and there exists, up to isomorphism, only a set of
\kappa
-presentable objects.

Corollary C.34
.

A cocomplete category
\mathcal{C}
is locally
\kappa
-presentable if and only if there is a set
S
of
\kappa
-presentable objects such that every object
U\in\mathcal{C}
can be written as a
\kappa
-filtered colimit of objects in
S
.

Lemma C.35
.

Given a small category
\mathcal{C}
, its presheaf topos
\mathbf{Pre}(\mathcal{C})
is locally finitely presentable.

Proof.

By the coYoneda Lemma
A.75
, we know that any presheaf
X
can be written as a colimit
X\cong\text{colim}_{y(U)\to X}\,y(U)
. Now by Lemma
C.29
, we know that representables are finitely presentable objects in
\mathbf{Pre}(\mathcal{C})
. Unfortunately, the category
(y\downarrow X)\cong\intop\nolimits X
is not filtered in general. However, by Lemma
C.32
, if we let
d:\intop\nolimits X\to\mathbf{Pre}(\mathcal{C})
denote the diagram
d(x,U)=y(U)
, then
s(d):\text{Sub}_{\text{fin}}(\intop\nolimits X)\to\mathbf{Pre}(\mathcal{C})
is the functor defined by
s(d)(J)=\text{colim}\,d|_{J}
. Now
\text{colim}\,d|_{J}
is a finite colimit of representables, so by Lemma
C.27
, each
\text{colim}\,d|_{J}
is a finitely presentable object in
\mathbf{Pre}(\mathcal{C})
. Since
\text{Sub}_{\text{fin}}(\intop\nolimits X)
is a directed poset, it is filtered, so
X
can be written as a filtered colimit of finitely presentable objects. Finally by Corollary
C.34
, this implies that
\mathbf{Pre}(\mathcal{C})
is a locally finitely presentable category.
∎

Suppose that
\mathcal{C}
is a locally
\kappa
-presentable category for some regular cardinal
\kappa
. Thus by Corollary
C.34
, there exists a set
S
of
\kappa
-presentable objects that generate
\mathcal{C}
by
\kappa
-filtered colimits. Let
\mathcal{C}_{0}
denote the full subcategory of
\mathcal{C}
on the set
S
. For every
U\in\mathcal{C}
, we can consider the functor
y_{0}(U):\mathcal{C}_{0}^{\text{op}}\to\mathbf{Set}
that sends a
V\in\mathcal{C}_{0}
to
\mathcal{C}(j(V),U)
. In other words,
y_{0}:\mathcal{C}\to\mathbf{Pre}(\mathcal{C}_{0})
is the Yoneda embedding restricted to
\mathcal{C}_{0}
.

The functor
y_{0}
has a left adjoint defined as follows. Let
j:\mathcal{C}_{0}\hookrightarrow\mathcal{C}
denote inclusion functor. Consider the Yoneda extension of
j
, i.e. the left Kan extension

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{C}_{0}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{C}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathbf{Pre}(\mathcal{C}_{0})}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces j}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces y}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\text{Lan}_{y}j}

which exists because
\mathcal{C}
is cocomplete.

Let
L\coloneqq\text{Lan}_{y}j
. Then for any
X\in\mathbf{Pre}(\mathcal{C}_{0})
we have

L(X)=(\text{Lan}_{y_{0}}j)(X)=\intop\nolimits^{U_{0}\in\mathcal{C_{0}}}\mathbf{Pre}(\mathcal{C}_{0})(y(U_{0}),X)\otimes j(U_{0})\cong\underset{y(U_{0})\to X}{\text{colim}}\,j(U_{0}).

It is easy to see that this defines a left adjoint to
y_{0}
. Given
X\in\mathbf{Pre}(\mathcal{C}_{0})
and
V\in\mathcal{C}
, we have

\displaystyle\mathcal{C}(L(X),V)

\displaystyle\cong\mathcal{C}\left(\intop\nolimits^{U_{0}\in\mathcal{C_{0}}}\mathbf{Pre}(\mathcal{C}_{0})(y(U_{0}),X)\otimes j(U_{0}),V\right)

\displaystyle\cong\intop\nolimits_{U_{0}\in\mathcal{C}_{0}}\mathcal{C}(X(U_{0})\otimes j(U_{0}),V)

\displaystyle\cong\intop\nolimits_{U_{0}\in\mathcal{C}_{0}}\mathbf{Set}(X(U_{0}),\mathcal{C}(j(U_{0}),V))

\displaystyle\cong\mathbf{Pre}(\mathcal{C}_{0})(X,y_{0}(V)).

Thus
y_{0}
is fully faithful, and has a left adjoint. It is good to note here that since
y_{0}
is fully faithful, it is conservative, i.e. it reflects isomorphisms. So if
f:U\to V
is a morphism such that
y_{0}(f):y_{0}(U)\to y_{0}(V)
is an isomorphism of presheaves, then
f
is an isomorphism. This explicitly points out the philosophical idea that objects in
\mathcal{C}
are completely determined by the objects in
\mathcal{C}_{0}
.

Lemma C.36
.

For
\mathcal{C}
a locally presentable category, the functor
y_{0}:\mathcal{C}\to\mathbf{Pre}(\mathcal{C}_{0})
is fully faithful. In other words,
\mathcal{C}
is a reflective localization of a presheaf topos,

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{C}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathbf{Pre}(\mathcal{C}_{0})}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces y_{0}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces L}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\dashv}

and furthermore
y_{0}:\mathcal{C}\to\mathbf{Pre}(\mathcal{C}_{0})
preserves filtered colimits.

Proof.

Suppose that
\mathcal{C}
is locally
\kappa
-presentable for some regular cardinal
\kappa
with dense small full subcategory
\mathcal{C}_{0}\hookrightarrow\mathcal{C}_{\kappa}
. To prove that
y_{0}
is fully faithful, it is equivalent to show that the counit of the adjunction
\varepsilon:Ly_{0}\Rightarrow 1_{\mathcal{C}}
is a natural isomorphism. For
V\in\mathcal{C}
, we have

Ly_{0}(V)=(\text{Lan}_{y}j)(y_{0}(V))\cong\underset{y(U)\to y_{0}(V)}{\text{colim}}\,j(U)\cong\underset{Ly(U)\to V}{\text{colim}}\,j(U)\cong\underset{j(U)\to V}{\text{colim}}\,j(U)\cong\text{Lan}_{j}j(V)\cong V

where the third isomorphism follows from Lemma
9.7
and the last isomorphism follows from Lemma
C.7
. This shows that
\varepsilon
is a natural isomorphism and thus that
y_{0}
is fully faithful.

Now let us show that
y_{0}
preserves
\kappa
-filtered colimits. Suppose that
d:I\to\mathcal{C}
is a
\kappa
-filtered diagram, and
V\in\mathcal{C}_{0}
, then

\displaystyle\mathbf{Pre}(\mathcal{C}_{0})(y(V),\underset{i\in I}{\text{colim}}\,y_{0}(d(i))))

\displaystyle\cong\underset{i\in I}{\text{colim}}\,\mathbf{Pre}(\mathcal{C}_{0})(y(V),y_{0}(d(i)))

\displaystyle\cong\underset{i\in I}{\text{colim}}\,\mathcal{C}(j(V),d(i))

\displaystyle\cong\mathcal{C}(j(V),\underset{i\in I}{\text{colim}}\,d(i))

\displaystyle\cong\mathbf{Pre}(\mathcal{C}_{0})\left(y_{0}j(V),y_{0}\left[\underset{i\in I}{\text{colim}}\,d(i)\right]\right)

\displaystyle\cong\mathbf{Pre}(\mathcal{C}_{0})\left(y(V),y_{0}\left[\underset{i\in I}{\text{colim}}\,d(i)\right]\right),

where the first isomorphism follows because representables are finitely presentable in
\mathbf{Pre}(\mathcal{C}_{0})
by Lemma
C.29
and therefore
\kappa
-presentable, and the third isomorphism holds because
V
is
\kappa
-presentable. In other words,
\underset{i\in I}{\text{colim}}\,y_{0}(d(i))
and
y_{0}(\underset{i\in I}{\text{colim}}\,d(i))
are objectwise isomorphic as presheaves, and therefore isomorphic. Thus
y_{0}
preserves
\kappa
-filtered colimits.
∎

Theorem C.37
.

A category
\mathcal{C}
is locally presentable if and only if it is a reflective subcategory of a presheaf topos such that the right adjoint preserves filtered colimits.

Proof.

(\Rightarrow)
is the content of Lemma
C.36
.

(\Leftarrow)
Suppose that
\mathcal{C}
is a reflective subcategory of a presheaf topos

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{C}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathbf{Pre}(\mathcal{C}_{0})}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces j}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces L}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\dashv}

where
\mathcal{C}_{0}
is a small category, and
j
preserves
\kappa
-filtered colimits for some regular cardinal
\kappa
. We want to show that
\mathcal{C}
is locally
\kappa
-presentable.

By Proposition
B.17
,
\mathcal{C}
is cocomplete, so we need to show that every object
U\in\mathcal{C}
can be written as a
\kappa
-filtered colimit of
\kappa
-presentable objects in
\mathcal{C}
.

By the coYoneda Lemma (Lemma
A.75
) we have

j(U)\cong\underset{y(U_{0})\to j(U)}{\text{colim}}\,y(U_{0})

But since
j
is fully faithful, this means that the counit
\varepsilon:Lj\Rightarrow 1_{\mathcal{C}}
is a natural isomorphism, so for
U\in\mathcal{C}
, we have

(59)

U\cong Lj(U)\cong L\left[\underset{y(U_{0})\to j(U)}{\text{colim}}\,y(U_{0})\right]\cong\underset{y(U_{0})\to j(U)}{\text{colim}}\,Ly(U_{0})\cong\underset{Ly(U_{0})\to U}{\text{colim}}\,Ly(U_{0})\cong(\text{Lan}_{Ly}Ly)(U).

Thus the functor
Ly:\mathcal{C}_{0}\to\mathcal{C}
is dense, and if
\mathcal{C}^{\prime}_{0}
denotes the essential image of
Ly
, then
\mathcal{C}^{\prime}_{0}\hookrightarrow\mathcal{C}
is dense in
\mathcal{C}
and essentially small. Thus every object
U\in\mathcal{C}
can be written as a
\kappa
-filtered colimit (by Lemma
C.32
) of objects of the form
Ly(U_{0})
for
U_{0}\in\mathcal{C}_{0}
.

Let us show that each
Ly(U_{0})
for
U_{0}\in\mathcal{C}_{0}
is
\kappa
-presentable in
\mathcal{C}
. Let
d:I\to\mathcal{C}
be a
\kappa
-filtered diagram, then

\displaystyle\mathcal{C}(Ly(U_{0}),\underset{i\in I}{\text{colim}}\,d(i))

\displaystyle\cong\mathbf{Pre}(\mathcal{C}_{0})\left(y(U_{0}),j\left[\underset{i\in I}{\text{colim}}\,d(i)\right]\right)

\displaystyle\cong\mathbf{Pre}(\mathcal{C}_{0})(y(U_{0}),\underset{i\in I}{\text{colim}}\,j(d(i)))

\displaystyle\cong\left[\underset{i\in I}{\text{colim}}\,j(d(i))\right](U_{0})

\displaystyle\cong\underset{i\in I}{\text{colim}}\,\left[j(d(i))(U_{0})\right]

\displaystyle\cong\underset{i\in I}{\text{colim}}\,\mathbf{Pre}(\mathcal{C}_{0})(y(U_{0}),j(d(i)))

\displaystyle\cong\underset{i\in I}{\text{colim}}\,\mathcal{C}(Ly(U_{0}),d(i)),

where the second isomorphism holds because
j
was assumed to preserve
\kappa
-filtered colimits.

Thus every object in
\mathcal{C}
can be written as a
\kappa
-filtered colimit of a set
\{Ly(U_{0})\}_{U_{0}\in\mathcal{C}_{0}}
of
\kappa
-presentable objects, and thus by Corollary
C.34
, this proves that
\mathcal{C}
is locally
\kappa
-presentable.
∎

Now we turn to one of the most important applications of the theory of locally presentable categories.

Theorem C.38
.

Let
\mathcal{C}
and
\mathcal{D}
be locally presentable categories, and let
F:\mathcal{C}\to\mathcal{D}
be a functor that preserves small colimits. Then
F
has a right adjoint.

Before we prove this result, let us prove it in the case where the domain is a presheaf topos.

Lemma C.39
.

Let
F:\mathbf{Pre}(\mathcal{C})\to\mathcal{D}
be a functor where
\mathcal{D}
is a locally small and cocomplete category. If
F
preserves small colimits, then
F
has a right adjoint.

Proof.

Let
G:\mathcal{D}\to\mathbf{Pre}(\mathcal{C})
be the functor defined objectwise for
U\in\mathcal{C}
and
Y\in\mathcal{D}
by

G(Y)(U)=\mathcal{D}(F(y(U)),Y).

Let us show that this defines a right adjoint to
F
. Let
X\in\mathbf{Pre}(\mathcal{C})
, then

\displaystyle\mathcal{D}(F(X),Y)

\displaystyle\cong\mathcal{D}\left(F\left(\underset{y(U)\to X}{\text{colim}}\,y(U)\right),Y\right)

\displaystyle\cong\mathcal{D}\left(\underset{y(U)\to X}{\text{colim}}\,F(y(U)),Y\right)

\displaystyle\cong\lim_{y(U)\to X}\mathcal{D}(F(y(U)),Y)

\displaystyle\cong\lim_{y(U)\to X}G(Y)(U)

\displaystyle\cong\lim_{y(U)\to X}\mathbf{Pre}(\mathcal{C})(y(U),G(Y))

\displaystyle\cong\mathbf{Pre}(\mathcal{C})\left(\underset{y(U)\to X}{\text{colim}}\,y(U),G(Y)\right)

\displaystyle\cong\mathbf{Pre}(\mathcal{C})(X,G(Y)).

where the first and last isomorphisms follow from the coYoneda Lemma
A.75
, and the second isomorphism follows because
F
was assumed to preserve colimits.
∎

Proof of Theorem
C.38
.

Suppose that
\mathcal{C}
and
\mathcal{D}
are locally presentable categories and
F:\mathcal{C}\to\mathcal{D}
is a cocontinuous functor. Since
\mathcal{C}
is locally presentable, by Theorem
C.37
there is a small full subcategory
\mathcal{C}_{0}\hookrightarrow\mathcal{C}
such that
\mathcal{C}
is a reflective localization of its presheaf topos
j:\mathcal{C}\hookrightarrow\mathbf{Pre}(\mathcal{C}_{0})
, where
j=y_{0}
is the restricted Yoneda embedding, and with reflector
L:\mathbf{Pre}(\mathcal{C}_{0})\to\mathcal{C}
. The functor
FL:\mathbf{Pre}(\mathcal{C}_{0})\to\mathcal{D}
preserves small colimits, so by Lemma
C.39
, it has a right adjoint
G^{\prime}:\mathcal{D}\to\mathbf{Pre}(\mathcal{C}_{0})
.

Now by Proposition
B.15
,
\mathcal{C}
is also equivalent to the category of
W
-local objects in
\mathbf{Pre}(\mathcal{C}_{0})
, where
W=L^{-1}(\text{iso})
. Let us show that for any
V\in\mathcal{D}
, that
G^{\prime}(V)
is an
W
-local object.

Let
f:X\to Y
be a map of presheaves over
\mathcal{C}_{0}
that is a
W
-local equivalence (Definition
B.8
), by Lemma
B.11
, this is equivalent to being a map inverted by
L
. So suppose that
f
is inverted by
L
. We want to show that the map

\mathbf{Pre}(\mathcal{C}_{0})(Y,G^{\prime}(V))\xrightarrow{\mathbf{Pre}(\mathcal{C}_{0})(f,G^{\prime}(V))}\mathbf{Pre}(\mathcal{C}_{0})(X,G^{\prime}(V))

is an isomorphism. But since
G^{\prime}
is a right adjoint, we have the following commutative diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathbf{Pre}(\mathcal{C}_{0})(Y,G^{\prime}(V))}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathbf{Pre}(\mathcal{C}_{0})(X,G^{\prime}(V))}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{D}(FL(Y),V)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{D}(FL(X),V)}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbf{Pre}(\mathcal{C}_{0})(f,G^{\prime}(V))}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cong}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cong}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{D}(FL(f),V)}

But
f
is inverted by
L
, thus the bottom horizontal map is an isomorphism, so the top horizontal map is also an isomorphism. Thus
G^{\prime}(V)
is
W
-local for every
V\in\mathcal{D}
. Thus there exists an essentially unique
G(V)\in\mathcal{C}
such that
jG(V)\cong G^{\prime}(V)
. This can be shown to define a functor
G:\mathcal{D}\to\mathcal{C}
such that
jG\cong G^{\prime}
, or equivalently
G\cong LjG\cong LG^{\prime}
.

Then
G:\mathcal{D}\to\mathcal{C}
is a right adjoint to
F
, as we have

\displaystyle\mathcal{D}(F(U),V)

\displaystyle\cong\mathcal{D}\left(F\left(\underset{Ly(U_{0})\to U}{\text{colim}}\,Ly(U_{0})\right),V\right)

\displaystyle\cong\lim_{Ly(U_{0})\to U}\mathcal{D}(FL(y(U_{0})),V)

\displaystyle\cong\lim_{Ly(U_{0})\to U}\mathbf{Pre}(\mathcal{C}_{0})(y(U_{0}),G^{\prime}(V))

\displaystyle\cong\lim_{Ly(U_{0})\to U}\mathcal{C}(U_{0},G(V))

\displaystyle\cong\mathcal{C}\left(\underset{Ly(U_{0})\to U}{\text{colim}}\,U_{0},G(V)\right)

\displaystyle\cong\mathcal{C}\left(\underset{U_{0}\to U}{\text{colim}}\,U_{0},G(V)\right)

\displaystyle\cong\mathcal{C}(U,G(V)).

The first isomorphism holds since if
U_{0}\in\mathcal{C}_{0}
, then
y(U_{0})=j(U_{0})
and thus
Ly(U_{0})\cong U_{0}
, and
\mathcal{C}_{0}
is assumed to be dense in
\mathcal{C}
. The fourth isomorphism holds because
j
is fully faithful, with
j(U_{0})=y(U_{0})
and
G^{\prime}(V)\cong jG(V)
.
∎

In fact, the above result can be strengthened to the following result.

Theorem C.40

(
[
Ros+94
, Theorem 1.66]
)
.

Let
F:\mathcal{C}\to\mathcal{D}
be a functor between locally presentable categories. Then

(1)

if
F
preserves colimits, then
F
has a right adjoint, and

(2)

if
F
preserves limits and filtered colimits, then
F
has a left adjoint.

Remark C.41
.

Requiring
F
to preserve both limits and filtered colimits in order to have a left adjoint is necessary. A counterexample in the case where
F
only preserves limits is given in
[
AKT01
, Section 1]
.
